Introduction.

In two earlier writings I have already concerned myself with the critical situation of the modern theory of knowledge; in the present work, however, I mean to go a step further, and not only to trace the source of that crisis, but also to attempt to sketch the path that might lead to overcoming it. Accordingly this work falls into two parts: a negative or polemical part, in which I contest the dualistic theory of knowledge, whose chief representative we may take to be K a n t , and a positive, constructive part, in which I endeavor to outline a doctrine of the unity of cognition, at least in its main features.

The striving for a unified conception of things is stirring ever more vigorously in the philosophical movement of our day, and the thinkers are multiplying who believe that they can meet and do justice to this ideal urge of our time by reviving the doctrines of F i c h t e , S c h e l l i n g , and above all H e g e l . We do indeed owe these representatives of German idealism amends for much of the wrong that was done them, above all under the influence of S c h o p e n h a u e r ; but I nonetheless scarcely believe that the restoration of their philosophical systems could attain any higher significance than that of an interesting, because symptomatic, fashionable current. The gulf between

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                      1

[p. 2]

their thinking and ours may for the moment be veiled, but really bridged this gulf can never be.

The new generation has passed through the school of exact research, and the new philosophical spirit wells up directly from mathematics and the natural sciences. That gloomy time, when men believed they might deny philosophy in the name of natural science, has happily been overcome, and the great upheaval in prevailing views makes itself known in this, that it is precisely the natural sciences that have been seized by a longing for philosophy, and in particular for a scientific theory of knowledge. There is no need to wonder at this change in the state of things. The stronger a mind becomes, the more it strives after self-knowledge; and so the powerfully advancing spirit of natural science, too, shows itself ever more filled with the urge toward self-knowledge. It wishes to see itself mirrored in a theory of knowledge, for it feels that from self-knowledge it could draw unsuspected aids to further progress. It therefore hunts for a theory of knowledge, but it does not find one. This is the proper cause of the crisis in modern logic.

This crisis was sharpened by the circumstance that men believed they could satisfy the philosophical demands of the newer mathematics and natural science by reaching back to K a n t . The impulse to such a return was given by natural scientists who were fond of engaging with Kant, but most especially by S c h o p e n h a u e r , who, although he himself practiced a grim and ruthless criticism of the Kantian theory of knowledge, argued so energetically for the world-historical significance of Kantian thinking that every road was leveled and smoothed for a revival, indeed for a new triumphal progress of criticism. A Kant literature blossomed which perhaps

[p. 3]

surpassed everything that was ever accomplished in philosophical exegesis, and in which German thoroughness, in a manner of speaking, outdid itself. The several more or less hidden phases of development in the mind of the philosopher of Königsberg were observed under the microscope; every most delicate fiber of his thinking was followed along its whole line of development; the periodic fluctuations of his critical views on cognition were laid bare; and pains were taken to ascertain, with the most scrupulous care, all the influences that the Kantian spirit had received, as well as all the effects that radiated from it into world literature. Such Kant scholarship was without doubt well suited to enliven philosophical study; but as one decade after another went by in these labors, the real goal of the Neo-Kantian movement became ever more problematic, because it was itself that caused the opposition between Kantian and modern thinking — perhaps even against its will — to be felt ever more strongly.

This opposition springs from the dualistic character of the Kantian theory of knowledge. To be sure, the Critique of Reason creates the semblance that it meant to abolish the great historical opposition between the two main currents of modern philosophy, namely rationalism (Leibniz-Wolff) and empiricism (Locke, Berkeley, Hume), and to bring them to a reconciliation; but this semblance arises only because people commonly fail to look strictly at Kant’s peculiar position toward Aristotelian and scholastic logic. For when Kant set himself the lofty task of effecting a reconciliation between the two antagonistic currents of the newer philosophy, his position toward Aristotle was bound to be decisive for him in the first place, since it is utterly impossible to create a theory of knowledge without subjecting Aristotle’s Organon to the strictest criticism.

1*

[p. 4]

For it is obvious that the two opposed currents of modern philosophy are characterized also by their opposed estimation of Aristotelian logic. Rationalism remains well disposed toward the logic of antiquity; empiricism, on the other hand, has no small inclination simply to throw it overboard.

It is therefore no idle question what position Kant takes toward Aristotelian logic, for it strikes at the very nerve of life of criticism. For the syllogistic of the Stagirite Kant displays but little sympathy; in his shorter logical and metaphysical treatises he even tries to rebel against it, yet in spite of all his exertions he was unable to get beyond it. Like a mighty block of rock that barred every prospect, Aristotelian logic lay there on the path of his thought, and since it seemed, after all, to be vain labor to try to roll it from its place, Kant merely endeavored to hew it, as it were, and to remodel and restyle it into a portal or forecourt of his own logic. The reader of the Critique of Reason notices little of this, because Kant is always sparing of words when he has to touch upon his relation to Aristotle. It seems to me characteristic, too, that he did not himself publish his university lectures on general (Aristotelian) logic, but left their editing to the university lecturer Gottlob Benjamin J ä s c h e . He could, in short, derive little taste from the logic of antiquity, and yet allowed himself to be influenced by it in a decisive manner even in the shaping of his own logic. Permit me to characterize this peculiar state of affairs here and now in a few strokes.

To render account to himself of the significance of Aristotelian logic, Kant devised a highly original formula, which at the same time became the fundamental formula of his

[p. 5]

own transcendental logic. He assumed that human cognition consists of form and matter, and conceived Aristotelian logic, like every logic whatever, as a science that disregards all matter, that is, all content of cognition, in order to occupy itself entirely and solely with the form of this cognition. But what is one to think of a science that abstracts from every content of our cognition? I believe that such a science must be wholly devoid of content, unless it conceives the form of cognition as a content. But if the form itself can become content, what then is the difference between the form and the content of cognition? I shall be told that between these two concepts there is only a relative difference, and that what at this moment is form of cognition can in the next moment become its content. Granted. But if a form suddenly becomes a content, then this content must in turn have a form. What is one to do then with this new form? Is it perhaps to become once more a content of cognition? Is one permitted in science to entertain such a playing with words? Can anyone who has made friends with the spirit of mathematics and the natural sciences take pleasure in such concepts as form and matter of cognition? When the poet says that nature has neither kernel nor shell, one ought to transfer this classic thought to cognition also, for cognition too has neither kernel nor shell, and the logician above all may not enter upon a division by which the special sciences are assigned the kernel (content) of cognition, and logic merely its shell (form). Is logic to be a science of empty husks and hollow nuts?

Kant really takes the Aristotelian doctrine for a

[p. 6]

science of that kind, for in the innermost depths of his mind he is convinced of its barrenness. To him it is hollow and devoid of content. Nevertheless he would on no account admit this, and invents the flattering learned expression that this logic (indeed his own as well!) is merely a science of the form of cognition. What he thinks of it, however, comes to expression in his declaring Aristotelian logic to be a science complete in itself, which has no need of further discoveries at all. That perhaps sounds like praise and glorification; but at bottom it is only a solemn funeral oration on Aristotelian logic. For if a science need not concern itself with the content of our cognition, then it may very easily be complete, and one also understands very well that it can dispense with discoveries; what remains incomprehensible, however, is how such a science is able to exist at all. Only since Kant has Aristotelian logic been stamped as “formal,” and only since Kant has it become, properly speaking, one of the greatest riddles of philosophy. One no longer knows whether it belongs to this world or to the next, for its ghost walks abroad in broad daylight, if not in the open market-place, then at any rate in all the philosophical lecture-halls.

Now Kant, however, has interwoven the fate of his own transcendental logic in the most intimate way with the view that he arrived at concerning the significance of Aristotelian logic. The fundamental formula of his own theory of knowledge, according to which cognition consists of form and matter, is meant first of all to serve to establish a common basis for the logic of antiquity and his own. Both kinds of logic represent a doctrine of the forms of human cognition, and there was now needed only a second, supplementary formula in order to bring out the difference

[p. 7]

or the opposition between ancient and transcendental logic. This second fundamental formula of the Kantian theory of knowledge is by far the more ingenious of the two, for Kant’s own native philosophical individuality first comes to light in it. In the sense of this second formula one has to make a very significant distinction among the forms of cognition. For there are forms, or laws of function, of cognition that serve merely to lay out more distinctly, and to explicate, what is already known, and these are the analytic forms of cognition, which are unable to bring about any progress in cognition: they belong to general (Aristotelian) logic. Those more interesting forms or laws of function, however, by means of which we supposedly set out to conquer new cognitions, which therefore extend and increase our knowledge, in short the synthetic forms, Kant wished to discover himself, and he reserved them for his own transcendental logic. This is at bottom the principle by which Kant divides logic between himself and Aristotle. The empty husks and hollow nuts fall for the most part to the logic of antiquity; the supposedly living, fruitful forms of cognition, on the other hand, are to constitute the real heritage and main content of transcendental logic.

The position of the Kantian theory of knowledge is thus, at least in the main, completely determined already by its relation to Aristotelian logic. The first formula serves to establish a common foundation, the second, on the other hand, a complete opposition between the old logic and the new. Rationalists and empiricists can now be equally content, for Aristotle is on the one hand thoroughly acknowledged, and on the other wholly antiquated and superseded. It also stands out clearly that, in Kant’s coming to terms with the Stagirite, the eagerly desired formula for the reconciliation

[p. 8]

of the two modern main currents in philosophy has been found. For it needs hardly be said that the matter of cognition is to be nothing other than what we borrow from experience, that is, the a posteriori or empiricist element of our knowledge, whereas the form can mean nothing other than what, independently of all experience, originates from our own mind, that is, the a priori or rationalist element of our cognition. Form and matter are the two wonder-working words that are to solder rationalism and empiricism together.

But are these two words still able today to work this wonder? If there really were such forms of cognition as Kant demands, then the problem of the theory of knowledge would indeed be solved. We should then possess a modern logic as against the ancient, just as we possess, say, a synthetic geometry as against the Euclidean, and a modern dynamics as against the statics of Archimedes. But are there really such forms as Kant had in mind? This is a question that I have to answer in the polemical part of this work.

Without doubt there is hardly anything more poetic than to assume with Kant that our mind harbors forms and laws of function, or rather mystical powers, which bring it about in us that we perceive a world of phenomena. Our likeness to God cannot be brought home to us more impressively than by the doctrine that forms dwell in our mind which bring it about that a space and a time are there, and in them the boundless manifold of a lawfully connected world of phenomena. But are such forms not merely invented entities, mythological forces? Even supposing that they existed, it would have to be impossible for us to cognize them, for it is not themselves that they offer to our gaze, but only their work, only the picture of the world that they produce in

[p. 9]

our mind can be known to us. They would be, to express myself in Kantian fashion, things in themselves, enthroned in our mind and weaving from there the variegated, richly colored world that we perceive as lying outside us. It is only too comprehensible that so poetic a thought had to enchant all receptive minds, and we should have nothing at all to object to the poetic element in it, if only there were the slightest possibility of really getting to know those mystical forms that produce the world in us. People are wont not to make it clear to themselves that, even if there were forms in Kant’s sense, they would have to be forever incognizable. If, for example, one says with Kant that space and time are the forms of our sensibility, this can be interpreted only to mean that two secret powers dwell a priori in our mind, which produce the intuitions of space and of time in us. Those secret powers, however, would have to be something essentially different from space and time, because otherwise space and time would be producing themselves in us, which would amount to exalting them into gods. With the assumption of forms of cognition we have therefore gained no theory of knowledge, but merely a mythology of cognition.

But whoever wishes to hold fast to the cognizability of the forms nonetheless runs up against another insurmountable difficulty in them, which I wish to indicate here, in the briefest compass. Forms must, in Kant’s sense, act spontaneously, that is, they are free, sovereign, creative powers, with the assumption of which one is in danger of confusing the human mind with the divine. Kant believed he could meet this danger by conceiving the forms not only as producing cognition, but also as restricting, prohibitive powers, which prevent our gaze from being able to penetrate behind the world of phenomena. The forms

[p. 10]

seem at first to be friendly genii, which in unbridled strength freely let the world proceed from themselves, but they turn into sinister demons, who deprive our mind of air and light, and forever bar our view into true reality. Only through this does the Kantian theory of knowledge acquire its so-called critical character. Thus the Kantian forms of cognition show a Janus face, and the contradictions that result from this were recognized early. The forms are to be sovereign, but also not sovereign; between them and the things in themselves there is to be an infinite gulf, yet somehow they must nevertheless be connected; the things were to conform to the forms of cognition, but in the end it turns out that the forms have to conform to the things, etc. The whole apparatus of forms, which at the outset had so poetic an appeal, becomes in the end a sort of guillotine of cognition, destined to abolish from the world all metaphysical hopes of cognition. In short, the doctrine of forms is a metaphysical fiction whose peculiar task it is to make metaphysical fictions forever impossible.

These are roughly the points of view from which I should like, in the present work, to illuminate the formalist way of thinking, and most especially the doctrine of space and time. Opposed to the formalist trend of thought in our day stands the so-called (since Johann E. E r d m a n n ) psychologistic current; they are related to each other as rationalism to sensualism. Since I am striving for a doctrine of the unity of cognition, I must contest both neo-rationalism and neo-sensualism. As regards neo-sensualism, or psychologism, this too has a dualistic stamp: its basic dogma, namely, is that there are two classes of phenomena, physical phenomena, which are spatio-temporal, and psychical phenomena, which have a merely temporal

[p. 11]

character. If that were so, there would have to be two streams of time: a physical stream of time, intimately bound up with space, and making it possible for physical phenomena to occupy a position not only in space but also in time; and then, too, a psychical stream of time, which would be wholly separated from space, and would thus prevent psychical phenomena from passing over into space as well.

The assumption of two parallel worlds of phenomena, as it is taught in present-day psychology (psychophysical parallelism), seems to me the sorest point of our fashionable philosophizing. For such an assumption throws our thinking back upon D e s c a r t e s , indeed behind Descartes; for the dualism of two substances, an extended and a thinking substance, cannot become so dangerous as the dualism of two worlds of phenomena running parallel with each other. The Cartesian theory of two substances is merely a fiction of concepts, which as such must perish through its inner contradictions; but psychophysical parallelism seems to me to rest on a fiction of facts, which is far harder to combat than a mere fiction of concepts, because it suggests to its adherents the existence of a second, psychical shadow-world running parallel to the real world. One should not think it possible: yet in the midst of experimental psychology, and through it in the midst of modern biology, a kind of belief in ghosts has nested, according to which the so-called physical world has a double, and in consequence of which even the law of causality is supposed to suffer a doubling. I believe that such a way of conceiving things could come about only because the theory of knowledge subjected the relation of space and time to no adequate examination, and left open the possibility that it

[p. 12]

might be possible for there to be a world of phenomena that are altogether non-spatial and take place merely in time. Nothing seems to me, therefore, more topical for the modern theory of knowledge than to subject the relation of space and time to a searching examination, and to show into what contradictions we fall when we conceive space and time as two mutually independent forms of our sensibility. This leads me to work out a doctrine of the unity of space and time, which is fitted to set in an altogether new light even the doctrine of the unity and transformation of forces, as it was founded by Robert M a y e r , and which forms the foundation of all modern natural science.

Of the monistic logic, the sketch of which I offer in this work, I have, incidentally, little to report here. Ancient logic seems to me a static logic of concepts, which, because it starts from a doctrine of the concept, is unable to arrive at any genuine theory of judgment, and for that very reason remains, even in the doctrine of the concept, at mere relations of subsumption. In contrast to it, then, what I have in view is a dynamic logic of judgment, which is to be, as it were, a doctrine of the motion and progress of human cognition. I divide it into a general and a special logic, the latter of which is to unite with the main branches of the special sciences, in order, in the most intimate connection with them, to take an active part in the progressive labor of knowledge. The special theory of knowledge divides, for me, into a logic of the mathematical sciences (metageometry), a logic of the physico-chemical sciences (metadynamics), and a logic of the biological sciences (metabiology). I must, to be sure, ask for an indulgent judgment of my outline if only because within the narrow compass of this work I am able to develop only the guiding thoughts of a monistic logic, and even these often merely to indicate.

[PDF 16]

Part I.

The Dualistic Theory of

Knowledge.

[PDF 17]

Section I.

Formalism and the Theory of Space.

1. The Alchemy of Cognition.

Every dualistic theory of knowledge is characterized by the fact that it begins by dividing cognition into two factors, or else into two classes, and the faculty of cognition itself into two component powers or components. The most interesting and historically most important example of this is furnished by the critical philosophy, which, as is well known, splits cognition into a formal and a material factor, and the faculty of cognition itself, to begin with, into sensibility and understanding. It would be a mistake, however, to believe that there is only one kind of dichotomy of cognition, for the history of logic teaches that such bipartitions have been attempted in a great variety of ways, and have even been worked out into systems. Thus L e i b n i z gives us, if not a decomposition of cognition into two factors, at any rate a division of all cognition into two classes: into “vérités de fait,” that is, into factual truths, which relate to facts that actually take place, and into “vérités de raison,” or truths of reason, such as, for example, the theorems of mathematics.

Even the comparison of the Leibnizian and the Kantian logic thus lets us distinguish two kinds of dualistic

[p. 16]

theory of knowledge, of which the first sets out to make a mere classification of finished cognitions into two classes, while the second wants to decompose each single cognition into two factors, by whose union cognition is first to come about. Allow me to distinguish these two kinds of dualism from each other by the epithets “classificatory” and “chemical,” understanding by classificatory dualism that which divides the whole realm of our cognition into two countries or territories, whereas chemical dualism wants to let each single cognition proceed from the mysterious cooperation of two powers of cognition. It is characteristic of Kant that he lets both kinds of dualism play into each other, for he is not content to let cognition arise in a secret, transcendental retort out of a formal and a material element, but also divides the realm of cognition, after the manner of Leibniz, into the territories of cognition a priori and a posteriori. Only through this double dualism does his logic acquire its strangely iridescent, transcendental character.

People are not wont to raise the question whether the quasi-chemical decomposition of cognition into two factors, as Kant devised it, is the only conceivable one, or whether other logicians may perhaps have undertaken a similar chemical decomposition; although it would surely have to be very instructive to be able to compare two kinds of logical chemism with each other. So long as one has only the critical chemism before one’s eyes, one is wholly fascinated by it; but as soon as there is even a second similar attempt, our judgment emancipates itself, and we involuntarily arrive at the thought whether a third such attempt might not also be made, and, what is the main thing, whether any number of kinds of chemical bifurcation of cognition might not be devised. Since

[p. 1]

I had long inclined to this view, it was a very welcome find for me when the four-volume Wissenschaftslehre [Theory of Science] of the Austrian thinker of the first half of the nineteenth century — highly esteemed as a mathematician, but all the less known as a logician — B e r n a r d B o l z a n o — came before my eyes: because this logic, worked out with the highest scholastic thoroughness down to the smallest detail, positively undertakes to combat the chemism of matter and form, and to put in its place a new logical chemism. There is also already a neo-scholastic undercurrent in the modern theory of knowledge, which would like to correct the Kantian dualism with the help of the Bolzanian, which led me to furnish, in my writing “Kant and Bolzano,” a comparative parallel of the two dualistic conceptions, chiefly in order to show that both have the same sundering of cognition as their consequence.

Since I do not wish to repeat myself, I shall not go further here into Bolzano’s splitting of cognition into a purely logical and a psychological factor, and wish only to mention that dichotomous attempts and beginnings are to be found in profusion among empiricists too, such as L o c k e and H u m e . All these bipartitions are characterized by the fact that behind them lurks the opposition, as popular as it is mysterious, of sensibility and reason. In proceeding now to the investigation of this opposition, we enter upon the field of the great historical dispute between sensualists and rationalists in the theory of knowledge. The one party wanted to let the faculty of cognition, if possible, be absorbed in sensibility, the other, if possible, in understanding, and in this not even a tolerable mutual understanding could be reached. And why? I believe it is because those who laid stress on distinguishing t w o faculties of cognition unwittingly

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.              2

[p. 18]

came to assume only o n e , whereas those who would acknowledge only one faculty fell just as unwittingly into the assertion of a duality of faculties of cognition. Since each party held fast to a self-contradiction, neither could win the other over to its conviction.

No one doubts that the faculty of touching is a different one from that of seeing, of hearing, etc. Whence comes this great certainty in distinguishing different faculties? The reason, it seems to me, is not hard to discern. We are able to touch things without also looking at them, we are able to perceive sounds without reaching for the sounding instrument, or receiving a retinal image of it, etc. In short, we have the power to keep the one organ and its function inactive while we put the other organ and its function into activity. Were we in a position to eliminate our understanding while we let sensibility play, and conversely to eliminate sensibility while we exercise only the understanding, no one would any longer doubt that sensibility and understanding are t w o faculties of cognition; the dispute between sensualists and rationalists would then presumably never have broken out at all, and all the acuteness and profundity that one admires in the “Critique of Pure Reason” would appear to everyone wholly superfluous and wasted, because everyone would be capable from the start of isolating sensibility and understanding from each other and exercising them separately. But since the organism itself has not taken care to isolate sensibility and understanding in such a way and to localize them in independent organs, as is the case, for example, with seeing and hearing, the philosophers, and the theorists of knowledge in particular, wanted to attend to this afterwards, rather as if they had to make good an omission of nature itself.

[p. 1]

That they could not easily agree in this is all too understandable.

For let us try to imagine as vividly as we can that we had eliminated the understanding entirely: would there then remain to us, perhaps, the possibility of setting in operation, in any way, a wholly unintellectual sensibility? I hardly think so. For wherever we ascertain any activity of the senses, we presume at once that at least a faint trace of intelligence is also at play. Conversely, we are equally unable to imagine that, with our sensibility entirely excluded, we could still carry out operations of the understanding. Such a “pure understanding” would have to be wholly free not only from ideas of immediately present bodily things, but also from every memory-image of the real world, indeed from all possible images of fantasy, so that this “pure understanding” would not have the faintest inkling that a real world, or even merely a dream world, exists, and it would accordingly be inconceivable with what it could occupy itself, were it set in function in isolation. To be sure, one might say that this “pure understanding,” which has hermetically sealed itself against every intuition, every memory, and every phantasm, busies itself in its isolation with itself and its own activity, in order, in its absolute remoteness from the world, to fathom the laws of its very own thinking — and it seems that some logicians regard the procedure just described as the method of logic —: but whoever asserts this would first have to teach also the physiological methods by which one cuts one’s organism off not only from all sensible impressions of the environment, but also so lames memory and imagination that not even the faintest shadow of an image, or of any idea of fantasy, may rise from their depths, so that

2*

[p. 20]

the pure understanding could, unhindered by any sensibility, give itself over wholly to its pure reflections on itself and on its own laws of function.

These considerations should suffice to suggest why sensualists and rationalists (even with the best of will) are unable to come to an understanding with one another. For the sensualist does not use the word sensibility in an unambiguous way: at one moment sensibility is for him (as against rationalism) the mere negation of an understanding, at another he himself — quite unnoticed — puts an understanding into this sensibility, and so, through this ambiguity, he falls into an unavoidable self-contradiction. The rationalist fares no better; conversely, he exorcises the concept of sensibility out of reason, and then unnoticed boxes it back in. How, then — the reader will ask — are we to guard our thinking against this twofold self-deception?

Whoever wishes to bring the concepts “sensibility” and “understanding” into relation with the concept “faculty of cognition” could attempt this in two ways: 1. by conceiving sensibility and understanding as components of one and the same faculty of cognition; 2. by maintaining that they are two faculties of cognition wholly distinct from one another. Let us consider both conceptions more closely.

As for the first, it does not matter at all under what i m a g e one pictures the union of sensibility and understanding, whether one says, then, that they are two branches of one trunk or, say, two arms of a lever, and the like; for the essential point of this conception consists merely in assuming that sensibility and understanding, taken singly and by themselves, would be incapable of creating a cognition, and that only in union with each other could they produce cognition. For the rest, however, as was said, one is free to render this union

[p. 1]

of the two faculties sensible under any biological, mechanical, or chemical image whatsoever. — I shall now show that whoever wishes to work out for himself such a conception of the relation of sensibility and understanding cannot possibly stop at it, because it suffers from an inner contradiction.

For if one is to be entitled to distinguish two components in our faculty of cognition, one must try to demonstrate that each of these components has some share in our cognition, and in what, properly speaking, this share consists. Now in undertaking to furnish this demonstration one arrives either at the result that the share of each component is incognizable, or else that it is possible to cognize this share. In the first case there can no longer be any talk of components of cognition, since only the demonstration of some share of each component can entitle us to set up a theory of the composition of our faculty of cognition out of two components. But if we succeed in cognizing what share, for example, sensibility has in our cognition, then this cognized share is nothing other than cognition, and indeed that cognition which sensibility would be capable of producing by itself, without any assistance from the understanding. Sensibility would then be no mere component of the faculty of cognition, but an independent faculty of cognition in its own right. And exactly the same would hold of the understanding. — In short: if the share in cognition of the two components is incognizable, then, precisely because of this incognizability, there can be no talk of such components at all; but if the share in cognition of each component is cognizable, then one has to do, not with components, but with wholly independent cognitive powers.

Thus the first conception presses with irresistible

[p. 22]

necessity toward the second. Now it is an obvious absurdity to assume that we have two faculties of cognition existing independently side by side, which, without regard to one another, could each produce a world of its own. We should then live in two worlds, and, what would be the strangest thing, while immersed in the one world we should not have the faintest inkling that we have a second world as well. We should have two consciousnesses, that is, we should be two persons. To avoid such absurdity we should have to assume that we have, besides, a third faculty of cognition, which puts the first two into connection with each other; but this would be nothing other than the return to the first conception, in which sensibility and understanding are conceived merely as components of one faculty of cognition. Thus the second conception drives us back to the first with irresistible force, although we are already convinced of the untenability of the first. We recognize, then, that Scylla and Charybdis on whose rocks every theory that would compose our faculty of cognition out of sensibility and understanding must necessarily be wrecked, and has indeed been wrecked, as is exemplified most vividly of all by the Kantian Critique of Reason.

Kant’s conception is characterized by the following peculiarly wavering sentence: “So much alone seems necessary by way of introduction or preliminary remark, that there are two stems of human cognition, which perhaps spring from a common but to us unknown root, namely sensibility and understanding, through the first of which objects are g i v e n to us, through the second, however, t h o u g h t .” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 29.) This passage is the more interesting to us because it comprises in itself both the conceptions of the relation of sensibility and understanding that were characterized above as impossible. For if

[p. 1]

Kant holds that the two stems of our faculty of cognition have perhaps sprung from a common root, then there is contained in this image the conception that sensibility and understanding might be components of an original faculty; at all events, however, Kant inclines rather to the conception that the two “stems” (not “branches”) are, in a manner, two self-subsistent faculties of cognition, whereby it admittedly remains unclear how they could nevertheless make up merely one faculty of cognition. For us it may here be fairly indifferent how Kant’s interpreters may construe that passage, since it is equally untenable in the one sense as in the opposite. It is perfectly immaterial whether one presents sensibility and understanding as two faculties of cognition or as components of merely one faculty of cognition, for in both assertions one and the same contradiction finds expression.

I call every theory of knowledge d u a l i s t i c which operates with two faculties of cognition or with two components of a faculty of cognition, the expression dualistic being for me merely an abbreviation for “self-contradictory.” To this dualistic theory of knowledge there must be opposed a monistic one, which denies from the outset that “sensibility” and “understanding” could be two faculties of cognition, or even merely two components of a faculty of cognition. If, however, so epoch-making and profound a thinker as Kant nevertheless undertakes to distinguish two partial faculties of cognition and to base the system of a theory of knowledge on this distinction, then the temptation to such an undertaking must be deeply grounded in the nature of human thinking. Perhaps I shall succeed, in the positive part of this work, in showing whence this powerful temptation springs and how extremely difficult it is to escape it: a remark on this point I dare not

[p. 24]

suppress even here, because otherwise the standpoint I take toward the dualistic theory of knowledge could very easily be wholly misunderstood.

Even common understanding is very much inclined to separate a sensible cognition from a rational one, and accordingly to distinguish a sensible and a rational faculty of cognition. Whence does this come? I believe it comes from the fact that, during intuitive cognizing (of an animal or plant species, for example), we are aware that this could not take place without the use of a bodily organ (the eye, for example). In so-called “silent thinking,” however, where a man, as one is accustomed to say, turns away from his surroundings in order to give himself up to his thoughts: there one is scarcely aware of what organs must be active in order to make that silent thinking possible. Primitive man thus easily falls into the temptation to assume that there is a cognition which must be regarded wholly as a mere achievement of his organization, and that there is also a higher cognition with which his organization has nothing at all to do. Now it hardly needs saying that there can never and nowhere be an act of cognition in which our organization would not somehow be involved, and that one therefore may not found a distinction of modes of cognition on the principle whether our organization has a share in cognition or has none. For let us suppose that a person A performs some acts of cognition in such a way that his organization is absolutely not at play in them; then this person, too, will have no right at all to the assertion that it is p r e c i s e l y h e who exercises that cognitive activity. In order to be able to assert that it is I who perform this or that act of cognition, there must in any case have been performed in my organization some vital

[p. 1]

work, since otherwise anyone would have the right to regard my act of cognition as his own. Since, then, the distinction between sensible and rational cognition may not be made without raising the question how acts of cognition are related to our organization, every theory of knowledge that takes the difference between sensibility and understanding as its starting point is merely a game of hide-and-seek behind the mystery of our organization. Indeed, it positively demands that we should think of our organization as divided into two parts, of which the one provides for sensible, the other for rational cognition. The distinction between sensibility and understanding is therefore rooted in the dichotomy of our organization: it derives from a dualistic metaphysics. The dualistic theory of knowledge has a dualistic metaphysics secretly as its foundation, and, what is the strangest thing about this state of affairs, it undertakes to raise the question whether and how metaphysics is possible! At any rate, it neglects to discuss the relation of our cognitive activity to our organization, although it lies open to view that one cannot speak of sensibility and understanding at all without thinking of our organization.

But apart from this, there is already contained, as I have shown, a contradiction in the division of our faculty of cognition into two. This contradiction can be made intuitive by a simile — admittedly in a somewhat drastic manner. What would one think of a man who, cutting an apple into two halves, came to the view that this operation must also require two knives, since otherwise it would be unintelligible how two cut surfaces could have arisen? One likes to compare a critical understanding with a sharp blade that splits everything: is this blade now, perhaps, to split itself in two? The dualistic theory of knowledge

[p. 26]

appears to me a flashing sword which is able, in a wonderful way, to cut itself in two. How dualism manages this is a mystery, but far more mysterious still is the purpose for which it performs its wonders. For if we have gained two faculties of cognition instead of one, we must surely go on advancing in geometrical progression to four, eight, sixteen … faculties of cognition. At any rate we shall convince ourselves that the dualistic theory of knowledge does not stop at the single self-division; for the present, however, we wish merely to look more closely at the splitting of the faculty of cognition into sensibility and understanding, and to ask how Kant means to make the impossible possible and to separate sensibility from the understanding, in order to investigate the two apart.

2. The Elimination of the Understanding.

In order to investigate the sensible faculty of cognition first, it must, according to Kant, be “isolated” by itself, by separating off from it everything that the understanding accomplishes through its concepts. Once sensibility has been isolated in this way, one must then carry out a further splitting within sensibility, by detaching the matter of empirical intuition and retaining only its forms. Kant expresses this in the following way:

“In the transcendental aesthetic, therefore, we shall first isolate sensibility, by separating off everything that the understanding thinks in it through its concepts, so that nothing but empirical intuition remains. Secondly, we shall separate from this further everything that belongs to sensation, so that nothing but pure intuition and the mere form of appearances remains, which is the only thing that sensibility can supply a priori. In this investigation it will be found that there are two pure

[p. 2]

forms of sensible intuition as principles of cognition a priori, namely space and time, the consideration of which we shall now take up.” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 36.)

Now as regards the “isolating” of sensibility, this peculiar operation seems to me equivalent to the elimination of the understanding; for if the sensible faculty of cognition is to produce its achievements wholly unmixed with the concepts of the understanding, then the understanding must surely be put out of function in some way (to me, at any rate, unknown). But even if this were possible, I should not regard the bringing about of such a condition as the task of the philosopher, because philosophizing involves not the elimination of the understanding, but far rather its most energetic exercise. Moreover, I hold it to be impossible for anyone, by any procedure, to divest himself wholly of his understanding and still remain in a position to tell us even the least thing about the constitution of his sensible faculty of cognition. The isolating of sensibility makes the impression as if someone were to put out the light — because it is a troublesome appendage and a hindrance to seeing — in order to devote himself, in the darkness, wholly unhindered to the inspection of things.

It will perhaps be said that the “isolating” is not to be taken so “strictly”; Kant means by it only that in considerations of the sensible faculty of cognition one should disregard or abstract from the understanding and its concepts. But let us consider what it means to direct one’s activity of understanding upon the sensible faculty of cognition, and, while one thus develops as intensive an activity of understanding as possible, to abstract from this very activity. Of what use can it be to abstract from the activity of the understanding, since this activity is, in reality — despite the abstraction from it —

[p. 28]

to take place? It is as if one wished to expect of a hunter that, while he takes aim at the game, he should imagine that this aiming is not taking place, and that after he has brought down the game he should also be firmly convinced that it was brought down without any aiming at all. But why more similes here, which always suffer from a certain inadequacy: since it is evident even without similes that it is a comical self-deception to believe that one can settle anything about the sensible faculty of cognition without the concepts of our understanding having to flow in at every step, when it is precisely only by means of the concepts of our understanding that we are able to make determinations about anything whatever. Whatever we may investigate, it can be an object of our investigation only if it stands in that illumination which is created by our activity of understanding; and whoever wishes to remove this illumination of the understanding in order to grasp the unilluminated object, from him the object itself vanishes as well. It is precisely for this that the intellectual light is there, that in this light we may be able to grasp objects, and it must be counted among the most comical aberrations of profound thinkers that they wished to take possession of objects precisely by switching off the intellectual light. A classic example of this is Kant’s endeavor to isolate sensibility, that is, to grasp it unilluminated by the concepts of the understanding, hence unmixed with the light of the understanding, merely in its own light, whereas to grasp something cognitively means nothing other than to comprehend something with the help of the light of the understanding.

Since, now, the whole Critique of Reason is based on the effort to isolate two faculties of cognition and to grasp their achievements in their own light (to the exclusion of the other light), but then also somehow to bring both lights into connection with each other, then

[p. 2]

we ought once for all to make it quite clear to ourselves that this endeavor, because it is an impossible one, leads to a continuous series of nothing but impossible demands. We shall find later that Kant himself feels this, and for that very reason twists to and fro between questions of the possibility and of the impossibility of cognition; indeed, that the end of his undertakings is nothing other than that it becomes evident, at least to the unprejudiced, that the original demand from which he set out was an impossible one — because burdened with a self-contradiction.

As a result of the mighty influence that Kant’s profound inquiry has exerted on the epigones, the endeavor to think the understanding (or reason) eliminated has nested itself more and more firmly in the epistemological way of looking at things. This stands out most clearly, perhaps, in S c h o p e n h a u e r , who not infrequently regards the intellect as a hindrance in cognition and uses the peculiar expression “that between the things and us there still always stands the intellect,” which leads the reader to the strange thought that our cognition would be in better case at once if nowhere an intellect were to place itself between us and the things. Thus Schopenhauer furnishes us with an original philosophy of the reason that stumbles over itself and is therefore rather grim. He manages to tell us what a man who is looking at a landscape would still see if the understanding were suddenly withdrawn from him altogether, say through paralysis of the brain. “For what in seeing is supplied by s e n s a t i o n is nothing more than a manifold affection of the retina, quite like the sight of a palette with many-colored daubs of paint; and no more than this is what would remain in consciousness if one could suddenly withdraw the understanding entirely from him who stands before a wide, rich prospect, by, say, paralysis

[p. 30]

of the brain, suddenly withdraw the understanding entirely, yet leave him the sensation: for this was the raw matter out of which his understanding had just before created that intuition.” (Principle of Sufficient Reason, § 21.)

Truly, it is astonishing how well the understanding is here informed of precisely what takes place in consciousness during its absence. Were the understanding informed even a thousandth part as well about what takes place in consciousness during its presence, one might congratulate psychology, for it would be one of the most perfected sciences. Schopenhauer can tell us almost to the last hair how, by switching the various faculties or forms of cognition off and on, our cognition is resolved into its constituents, or built up again out of these constituents. For this building-up it is only necessary that some quantity of “raw matter of sensation” enter into the forms of intuition of space and time, as well as into the form of understanding of cause and effect (causality), and then the world is finished: then, instead of the meager matter of sensation, such as light and dark, blue and green, etc., one has before one the wonderful panorama of the real world, a blue sky, smiling meadows, rustling forests, plunging brooks, singing birds, etc. All this, now, is originally nothing but a wholly meager matter of sensation, which, to be sure, must enter into the forms of space, time, and causality held in readiness, in order to be able to present itself as a trim, richly colored, life-filled world. But we know all this so exactly only because we prudently eliminated reason first of all, since its presence would have prevented us from cognizing this secret constitution of our mental mechanism. Once we have eliminated reason (in Schopenhauer: the faculty of abstract concepts), we can, it seems, in this

[p. 2]

direction continue and eliminate the form of understanding of causality, whereby we are, no doubt, afforded the pleasure of beholding a world without causality, which can then, by the elimination of the form of time, be made timeless as well, and by the elimination of the form of space, no doubt, spaceless too, so that in the end nothing is left to us but a certain rather meager matter of sensation, without causality, space, or time, that is, the raw matter out of which our world is built up. One can now, probably, also put this procedure to the test by proceeding conversely from the raw matter of sensation and letting it enter, step by step, into the forms of sensibility and understanding, whereby the world then appears built up successively, or perhaps (with simultaneous employment of all the forms) at one stroke. From which it is to be seen that an altogether extraordinary magic power dwells in the forms that our mind harbors, indeed that they would have to be regarded as the actual creators of the world, apart, of course, from the “raw matter of sensation,” which must indeed be supplied to them, so that they may have a something upon which to exercise their creative power. — Must one not involuntarily think here of a physiologist who, in order to demonstrate the function of the individual organs, were to dissect his own body, that is, were to eliminate brain, heart, lungs, bowels, etc., in turn from his body, in order then, conversely, to build his own body up again nicely out of the pieces into which he has dissected himself, which feat of self-re-creation would have to be regarded as the finest test of the exact correctness of the first feat (the regular self-dissection).

This cursory excursion may serve only to make palpable, as it were, what an impossibility lies in putting together our faculty of cognition out of two — and soon also out of several — faculties or forms

[p. 32]

in thought. For if there is a sensible faculty of cognition of its own, and if it is to be investigated in an isolated way, then, to be sure, in such investigations the understanding can play only the role of an uninvited and most troublesome, indeed positively intolerable guest. One wants, after all, to be rid and free of all concepts of the understanding, in order to grasp the virginal sensibility in its virginity untouched by any understanding. In Kant this does not yet stand out so clearly as in Schopenhauer, who wanted to be more Kantian than Kant himself, and led the splitting of the faculty of cognition into two factors quite openly into the absurd. He proclaims quite unreservedly that nothing stands so much in our way in cognition as our own understanding, or even our own reason: for this understanding and this reason have to do only with the surface of the world of phenomena, and only a mystical intuition of genius is able to cognize the hidden essence of the world.

One sees whither the self-division of the faculty of cognition leads. Once the blade of cognition is broken, nothing more can be accomplished with it, and then oriental mysticism can enter victoriously into the bright halls of Western philosophy. Then the blind, unreasoning will can be raised to the world-principle, and then, to be sure, nothing remains but to deny it from the ground up and to aspire to the eternal nothingness of Nirvana. Then the compassionate, milk-and-water Buddha can be set upon the throne of truth, and soon this one, too, be put to flight by a dithyrambic-Dionysian Zarathustra. Whither this process of development leads, who can gauge? To all appearances, Western science and philosophy will also have to make friends with all sorts of woolly-haired, flat-nosed, and pointed-headed prophets of the dark parts of the world.

[p. 2]

Kant could not foresee such a process of development, for although it was he who, properly speaking, shattered the faculty of cognition, one would nevertheless do him the greatest injustice were one to impute to him the intention of such a shattering as well. Although he begins by splitting the faculty of cognition in two, he had not aimed at this self-division as his final purpose. For while in the transcendental aesthetic he was wholly filled with the aim of isolating sensibility, it mattered to him later, in the transcendental logic, chiefly to establish the possibility of a cooperation of the two torn-apart powers of cognition. (On this rests, in no small part, the difficulty of those expositions which Kant designated by the name “transcendental deduction of the concepts of the understanding.”) Kant’s endeavor to cement the breach between the two faculties of cognition led him later to such brilliant formulations as: concepts without intuitions are empty, intuitions without concepts are blind. “Hence it is just as necessary to make one’s concepts sensible (that is, to add the object to them in intuition) as to make one’s intuitions understandable (that is, to bring them under concepts).” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 75.) Indeed, Kant says outright that cognition can arise only through the union of the two faculties. “Only from their union can cognition arise.” (P. 75.) But all this is already labor lost. Once the faculty of cognition has been split into two components, it can no longer be helped, for what has once been killed one tries in vain to galvanize into new life. The faculty of cognition wants, from the very beginning, to be in itself one and indivisible; not first to be torn apart and then artificially patched together. If one has started from the logical contradiction that there

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                 3

[p. 34]

are two components of cognition, then it is only a new contradiction to want to paste together one power out of the two opposed powers. If an impossibility was contained in the demand to split cognition in two, then it is only a second impossibility to unite what has been torn apart into a whole. Scarcely has Kant stressed this united cooperation of the two powers of cognition when he must immediately recall that, despite the demanded union, they remain two separate powers. “Nevertheless, one must not mix up their shares, but has great cause carefully to separate and to distinguish each from the other.” So Kant admonishes himself, and with all the force of his profundity he strives to unite what is torn apart in such a way that it shall remain torn apart. He wants to make good one contradiction by a second, and achieves thereby only this much, that he has contradicted himself twice. He starts from the impossibility of wholly isolating sensibility and understanding, and arrives at the second impossibility, of wholly uniting them. But it is precisely this wrestling with two impossibilities that makes the Critique of Reason so exceedingly thought-laden a book. It is an imposing wrestling for truth that speaks out of this epoch-making work, but for just this reason, perhaps, there is no second work in the philosophical literature that brings the absolute untenability of the dualistic theory of knowledge so impressively before our eyes as the Kantian Critique of Reason. Whoever wishes to be fully convinced that cognition cannot be split into two factors, nor the faculty of cognition into two components, will do best to study the Critique of Pure Reason. It awakens — even if without intending to — the hottest longing for a doctrine of the unity of cognition, and in this lies its significance also for the development of the modern theory of knowledge. But in order to arrive at such a doctrine of unity

[p. 3]

we must subject the splitting and pasting together of cognition to an exact examination.

3. Matter and Form of Cognition.

One must distinguish well the faculty of cognition from its products or achievements, namely the judgments, which are presented linguistically in the form of sentences. The dualistic theory of knowledge splits not only the faculty of cognition into two components, but also its products into two constituents (or classes), and if the first undertaking was burdened with a contradiction, then the second, presumably, will be in no better case. I wish first to show that the splitting of the products of cognition into matter and form involves just as much an impossibility as the splitting of the faculty of cognition into sensibility and understanding.

To be sure, it is a most painful task to demonstrate the uselessness of such scholastic sham concepts as “matter and form” of cognition, for whoever concerns himself with these sham concepts must, at least for a time, perforce take upon himself the evil semblance of being himself a scholastic. I hope, however, that in the course of these investigations the reader will become convinced that what concerns me is not empty subtleties, but precisely the combating of them.

If cognitions are to be split into matter and form, then these constituents will be either cognizable or incognizable in themselves. If they are incognizable, then, precisely because of this incognizability, there can be no further talk of them; but if they are cognizable, then we have split one cognition into two cognitions. Since, now, every cognition is supposed to consist of matter and form, each of the two cognitions that we obtained must be composed both

3*

[p. 36]

of matter and of form. From this it is to be seen that our effort to produce the matter and the form separately was wholly in vain, for we obtained two cognitions, each of which contains constituents of both kinds. In vain should we continue the above procedure and try to split each of the two cognitions obtained: for we could obtain, again and again, only cognitions as products of the splitting, and these cognitions would again and again contain constituents of both kinds. — In the briefest compass this proof might be set out as follows. It is demanded that matter and form be distinguished in every cognition; but however one may divide cognition, each cognized constituent of it will in turn have to be cognition, therefore each constituent will have to contain both matter and form, therefore we shall never be able to arrive at constituents that consist exclusively of matter or exclusively of form. The splitting into matter and form is impossible. Q. e. d.

It will perhaps be objected to me that Kant has, after all, really split cognition into two factors, and that we must give more credence to this real achievement than to any theoretical counter-proof. To this I have only to reply with the poet: With words one can dispute right well, from words prepare a system. Kant gives the expressions matter and form, on every occasion, precisely such a meaning as seems to him to fit best at the place in question, for the clouds of Polonius are not richer in meanings than the terms: matter and form of cognition. In this connection I shall oblige at once with a few samples from Kant’s logic, and leave it to the reader to add others, or else himself to devise as many interpretations of matter and form of cognition as he pleases, for these expressions have, after all, a metaphorical character, and

[p. 3]

permit one to take them in whatever sense one pleases.

Thus Kant speaks, for example, of a matter and a form of concepts, and gives the following laconic explanation of them: “In every concept, m a t t e r and f o r m are to be distinguished. — The matter of concepts is the o b j e c t , their form u n i v e r s a l i t y .” This sounds strange enough, for matter and form are supposed to be distinguished in the concept itself, and so to be factors of the concept itself, whereas the object to which a concept is related is something other than the concept itself, and consequently cannot be obtained from a splitting of the concept. By the splitting of concepts nothing could arise but, at most, concepts, not those objects to which concepts are related. It sounds still more peculiar that universality should be the form of the concept, for “universality” is itself in turn a concept, which as such would again have to consist of form and matter, and so cannot be mere form, but would have to contain matter too. Does not Kant himself say that matter and form must be distinguished in every concept?

Another way of construing matter and form is given by Kant in the doctrine of judgment: “To every judgment there belong, as its essential constituents, matter and form. — In the given cognitions, connected into the unity of consciousness in the judgment, consists the matter; in the determination of the manner in which the various representations, as such, belong to one consciousness, the f o r m of judgment.” Whoever finds this explanation somewhat strained may keep to the other which Kant gives in the Critique of Pure Reason (p. 322): “In every judgment one can call the given concepts the logical matter (for the judgment), their relation (by means of the copula) the form of the judgment.” From this explanation it follows, however, that the

[p. 38]

copula would have to be a pure concept of form, devoid of all matter, which stands in contradiction with Kant’s demand that every concept must have matter. Indeed, if we take into account that according to Kant the matter of the concept is the object, then the copula, because it contains the concept of being —, would have to be the concept richest in matter, since all existing objects would belong to the matter of this concept. Moreover, I beg the reader to test Kant’s explanation of the matter and form of judgments on, say, the following example: “The form is a constituent of every cognition.” In this judgment, according to Kant, all concepts except the copula “is” would have to be counted as the logical matter of the judgment. But now the concept “form” appears in this judgment precisely as the subject; consequently form itself would have to belong to the matter of this judgment, from which it is to be seen what a game is being played here with the concepts of form and matter.

A third way of construing matter and form is found in Kant’s doctrine of inference: “In the antecedents or premises consists the matter, and in the conclusion, insofar as it contains the consequence, the form of inferences of reason.” This explanation is of great interest because it casts a characteristic light on Kant’s conception of logic. In the sense of his definition, logic is a science which abstracts from the matter of cognition and investigates merely its forms. Now Kant holds, however, that the premises of an inference constitute its matter, from which it follows that logic would never be permitted to concern itself with the premises of an inference, since these are, after all, of a material nature in relation to the inference. Here it stands out most clearly that logic, at least in the sense of the Kantian conception, must be a thoroughly contentless and impossible science. Or does one perhaps believe oneself to

[p. 3]

be in a position to furnish a doctrine of inference, when one may take no account of the premises (that is, the matter) of the inference? Admittedly Kant too, in his doctrine of inference, does take the premises into account, but he does so in full contradiction with his definition of logic.

But the ambiguities to which I have just pointed in the formulation of the concepts of form and matter are still not the worst feature of these scholastic metaphors. If only one could be sure that Kant always refers the concept of matter and form solely to products of cognition, that is, to judgments, concepts, inferences, one would still put up with all manner of contradictions, since one would at least know what is properly being talked about. But Kant refers the concept of form and of matter not only to the products of cognition, but to the faculty of cognition itself. The forms “lie ready in the mind”; they sit, they dwell in the faculty of cognition; indeed, for this very reason they are positively component parts of the faculty of cognition. They are thus now mystical powers of the mind, now laws of function according to which these powers operate, and now again they are merely component parts of the products of cognition generated by them (judgment, concept, inference). To all this is added the circumstance that the forms indeed sit in the “mind,” but that the same does not hold of matter: for whereas the former are at home in the mind, matter is only a sort of stranger or guest, who is received or not received by the forms, according as the faculty of cognition is in activity or has come to rest. The consequences of this almost endless confusion of concepts will become, so to speak, palpable in the doctrine of space.

With this I conclude the g e n e r a l considerations on the dualistic theory of knowledge, and wish only to stress that the proofs of the impossibility of a splitting of the faculty of cognition into two powers, or of the products of cognition

[p. 40]

into two factors, may claim validity not only in respect of the Kantian, but of every dualistic theory of knowledge whatever. Let one try to put in the above proofs, in place of the names “sensibility and understanding,” or “matter and form of cognition,” any other two names one pleases, and one will be convinced that it does not matter at all under what titles one chooses to introduce the deadly rift between two powers or factors into the theory of knowledge. One can give one and the same poison the most various names and mix the most various dyes into it, so that it will shimmer now in this light, now in that; but the insidiously corrosive nature, hostile to life, of it is not in the least altered by the loveliest colorings, nor even by the sweetest sugar-coatings.

This holds in particular also of the highly peculiar dualistic logic of B o l z a n o . He separates the content of our thinking from the acts of thinking, and relegates the truths to a cloud-cuckoo-land outside and beyond all thinking. He makes a deadly cut right through the middle of thinking, and turns our thinking activity into an action in itself wholly devoid of content and of truth, while on the other hand he exalts the truths into unthought thoughts, to which it is quite a matter of indifference whether they are grasped by a thinking being, and which in this way represent otherworldly heavenly lights that can, so it is alleged, shine into our thinking. At bottom, however, they are “cognitions in themselves” (quite analogous to Kant’s “things in themselves”) which can never be grasped by our thinking, because Bolzano tears asunder from the outset the bond between the act of thinking and its content of truth, and gives himself up to the singular illusion that there can be a science of pure logic which investigates contents of truth that are thought by no one, in this their unthoughtness. I mention this curious

[p. 3]

system of logic only because it is, to a still higher degree than the Kantian, suited to bring home the untenability of the dualistic theory of knowledge, and because even in our own day attempts are already being made to bring Bolzano’s Wissenschaftslehre—to be sure, refitted with an altered terminology and with modern psychologistic trimmings—into fashion. Will such an attempt be able to succeed? Who can foresee this, since in philosophy far worse attempts have been crowned with success. Bolzano is a thinker thoroughly filled with his conviction and, in this respect, most worthy of reverence, who, starting from the Leibnizian dualism, remodels the Kantian dualism, and carries this out with a thoroughness that recalls the highest flowering of scholasticism. The return to Bolzano—if one can speak of a return, since this solitary thinker has begun to take effect only in most recent times— would be the true leap into the Middle Ages, and such a leap has its good chances, because the Middle Ages lie not nearly so far behind us as many trustful devotees of progress would like to think; indeed, one may perhaps assert without exaggeration that the Middle Ages march at the head of our new age and, in doing so, carry themselves as the most modern thing in our modern endeavors.

Besides, Middle Ages and Modern Age, like all similar catchwords, are matters of complete indifference to us here, and if the truth lies in the medieval spirit, then let people return to it without delay and at the quickest tempo. What concerns us here is solely the question whether a decomposition and composition of cognition into and out of two factors is possible or impossible. What should we think of a chemist who announced to us that he had succeeded, out of the elements C, H, N, O, and perhaps also K, Na, Ca, Fe, P, by chemical

[p. 42]

synthesis in the retort, in creating the homunculus? And yet, in my opinion, this would be child’s play compared with the task of making the power of cognition emerge out of two powers, or cognition out of two factors that are not cognitions. It will be easier to solve how life can be kindled than how the divine spark of cognition is able to light up in a human mind.

To be sure, there is a decomposition of cognition, if one cares to call it decomposition, in that we express our knowledge in sentences of language, which in their turn consist of words. Of all the phenomena that nature offers us, this is the most wonderful, that there are beings who are able to set forth their cognition in signs. Astronomy is a sublime science, and one cannot think of the myriads of stars that pursue their courses in endless space without being overcome by wonder; but this wonder would not exist if there were not cognizing beings who use significant signs in order to represent eternal truths in them. Unfortunately, however, the human mind has misused these signs for so long, and has sinned so gravely against them, that it hears nothing with more distrust than the word that sounds from human lips. Confidence in the signs we use has been so shaken by scholastic and sophistical misuse of them that the modern theory of knowledge has become hostile to language, as finds a shocking expression in B a c o n , for he makes human speech responsible for the aberrations of cognition. L o c k e believes he can furnish an inquiry into the understanding without regard to speech, and becomes aware only in the third book of his work that language may not be regarded as nonexistent. English empiricism wants altogether, in order to penetrate to truth, to

[p. 4]

suppress the word and everywhere put in its place the intuitive idea. This is the anti-scholastic movement of minds, and it is hard to decide whether it is scholasticism or anti-scholasticism that has inflicted the graver wounds on the human mind.

At any rate, it is precisely modern anti-scholasticism (sensualism, empiricism) that has brought forth a dualistic theory of knowledge. People want at any price to disregard language and to devise decompositions by which our faculty of cognition is to be split. The consequences of this striving come to light most clearly in the Kantian doctrine of space.

4. The Formalist Theory of Space.

“The Transcendental Aesthetic,” says S c h o p e n h a u e r in his Criticism of the Kantian Philosophy, “is a work of such outstanding merit that it alone could suffice to immortalize Kant’s name. Its proofs have such full power of conviction that I count its theorems among the irrefutable truths, as they doubtless also belong among the most fruitful in consequences, and are therefore to be regarded as the rarest thing in the world, namely a real, great discovery in metaphysics. The fact, strictly proved by him, that a part of our cognitions is known to us a priori, admits of no other explanation than that these constitute the forms of our intellect: indeed, this is less an explanation than just the distinct expression of the fact itself.”

So it is supposed to be a fact that space and time are f o r m s of our sensible faculty of cognition, or else forms of our intuition, or, as the case may be, forms of the world of phenomena. “Form,” however, is an expression which we originally borrowed from our spatial intuition:

[p. 44]

must it not seem strange that precisely this expression, drawn from spatial intuition, is supposed to tell us what space really is? To be sure, the expression may not be taken in its proper sense, but only in some figurative sense; yet is it not suspicious that, for determining the essence of space, one makes use of just such a metaphor, rooted in spatial intuition? It seems, however, that precisely the metaphorical character of the expression “form” exerts a bewitching influence on minds. It tickles our fantasy to think of a kind of vessel that lies ready in our sensibility to take up into itself the material of sensations; and it flatters our vanity to know, besides, that this image may not be taken in its proper sense. For unfortunately we often care nothing about winning real knowledge, and wish only to be allowed to imagine that we have thought something profound.

We could make the refutation of the formalist theory of space quite easy for ourselves, for we should need only to appeal to the fact that in the preceding chapter, “Matter and Form of Cognition,” we have shown matter and form in general to be sham concepts. Since, moreover, we also hold the distinction of two faculties of cognition, such as sensibility and understanding are supposed to be, to be inadmissible, it can make no sense for us to speak of a form of sensibility or of intuition (or of appearance). Nonetheless we wish to set aside our general demonstrations and to try to judge the special theory of space before us, so to speak, out of itself.

To this end I should like first of all to remind you that we all bring with us from our pre-philosophical thinking a kind of theory of space: to be sure a quite childish one, of which one can speak only with a smile.

[p. 4]

By virtue of this primitive theory there is a something that looks very much like a nothing, and that can be regarded as a receptacle, a vessel, a form for the content of the whole corporeal world. This is space. The whole corporeal world might perhaps dissolve into nothing, but its form, space, would even then still remain, perhaps for this very reason, that it is a sort of nothing. In other words, space, despite its apparent nullity, is the precondition for all corporeal existence; for above all there must be a space, and only afterwards could a world of bodies also find room in it.—One sees, this “theory” too is a formalist one, indeed it may with full right be called an apriorist one, for in it space asserts precedence over the world of bodies: the universal vessel or all-embracing form must be there before any corporeal content could be there.

Perhaps, too, we ought not to deem ourselves all too superior to this naive way of thinking, for we stand under its influence where we least suspect and perceive it. It nests, namely, in the structure of our sentences, and, in that it molds our turns of speech, it governs our thoughts too, unnoticed. To convince oneself of this, consider, say, the wholly similar structure of the following two sentences:

The liquid is in the vessel,

The liquid is in the space.

In these two sentences the little word “in” plays linguistically exactly the same role, and yet in the two constructions it takes on radically different meanings, of which one is hardly any longer aware. One brings this difference of meaning most distinctly to consciousness, perhaps, if one raises the question what the reason may be that the liquid can well be poured out of the vessel, but not out of the space?

[p. 46]

One then commonly receives the answer: that is no question at all; the liquid cannot be poured out of space only because space is infinite; if the vessel in question were also infinite, the liquid could not be poured out of it either. From which it is clearly to be seen that the naive understanding really wants to conceive space as an infinite vessel, and can hardly become aware that between the liquid and a vessel there obtains an essentially different relation than between the liquid and space.

The liquid touches the vessel along a curved surface, which forms the inner surface of the vessel. There obtains accordingly between the liquid and the vessel a r e l a t i o n o f c o n t a c t ; everyone, however, will admit that we must think the relation of the liquid (or of any body whatever) to the part of space in which it is located not as a relation of contact, but as an absolute reciprocal r e l a t i o n o f i n t e r p e n e t r a t i o n . For whereas one body is wholly impenetrable for another (thus also the vessel for its content and vice versa), that is, arbitrary parts of the one cannot at the same time occupy the same place as arbitrary parts of the other: we think the relation of the body to the space filled by it in such a way that they wholly interpenetrate one another, that is, that parts of space coincide wholly with the parts of the body by which they are occupied.
