Precisely where the particles of the body are, there too are the particles of space; so that, if we may say the body is “in” space, we may also say conversely that space is in the body. And if the figure of speech is admissible that space is a vessel or a form for the body, then the converse figure of speech must also be permissible, according to which the body is a vessel or a form for space. Properly, however, none

[p. 4]

of these phrases ought to be used, since each of them is unsuited to characterize the relation of body and space. As to the question, finally, why a body cannot be poured out of space, our provisional answer is: for the same reason that space cannot be poured out of the body.

We can now characterize the naive conception of space in the following way. The complete coinciding of body and space is a relation that cannot be intuited, whereas the relation of vessel and content—because it is a relation of two bodies—has the advantage of intuitiveness. The naive man, whose poetizing mind insists on intuitiveness, therefore replaces the unintuitive relation of body and space by the intuitive one of body and body (vessel and content). He thus uses the little word “in” at one time to express the relation of two bodies, at another also the relation of body and space. In this the naive man shows a kind of ingenuity (genius); for he knows how to help himself, and knows how to put a suitable intuitive relation in the place of an unintuitive one. But now that the two such radically different relations have received the same expression, the mentally lazy understanding grows accustomed to setting the two relations themselves equal as well. Thus the common understanding, led in leading-strings by words, falls into the peculiar condition in which it makes no distinction between the relation of body and body and the relation of body and space; whereas the one is a relation of impenetrability, the other on the contrary a relation of penetrability. It looks as if this common understanding wanted to assert the following absurdity: A relation of impenetrability is a relation of penetrability.

[p. 48]

In short, the common understanding finds itself in a self-contradiction of which, however, it has no knowledge. To escape this self-contradiction we must make it clear to ourselves once and for all that the image of the vessel (or the form) and the content is only a poetic makeshift of our language to render sensible the unintuitive relation of body and space, and that as such a makeshift it also deserves to be called a work of ingenious fantasy; but considered as a l o g i c a l expression of the relation between space and body it is simply absurd and objectionable.

We must now put to ourselves the question whether the dualism or the contradiction which we have just exposed in the naive conception of the relation of space and the body located in it persists further in Kant’s doctrine of space, or is overcome and eradicated? In other words: does Kant reject the image of the vessel (or the form) and its content, or does he retain it? I believe that no one can hesitate over the answer: Kant too retains the image of the vessel and its content, although he no longer takes these expressions in their proper sense but in a figurative one. For whereas naive thinking speaks of bodies and space as if these lay outside consciousness, for Kant space and body are appearances within consciousness. This is, considered in itself, to be sure a difference of the highest significance: h e r e , however, this difference is of no moment for us. Essential for us is only the circumstance that Kant retains the image of the form and its content, even if he carries it—which lies remote from naive thinking—into the faculty of cognition. Just as a liquid is formed by the vessel in which it is located, so too, according to Kant, the

[p. 4]

material of our sensations is formed by space, which inheres in our sensibility as form. One sees that in Kant body and space have, so to speak, migrated into the faculty of cognition, so that in their place there is talk of a matter of intuitions (the sensations) and of a form of intuition (space).

In what, then, does the essential difference consist between the Kantian theory of space and that primitive conception of space which we all bring with us from the start? This difference consists in that naive thinking conceives space and the world of phenomena as existing independently of the human mind, whereas Kant relocates both into the human mind. Apart from this way of conceiving it, the two theories agree fully with each other, for the primitive theory of space too is formalist and apriorist, just like the Kantian doctrine of space.

It is formalist because it conceives space as an infinite vessel, that is, as a form in which the bodies are located. It arrives at this view because for it a space properly exists only where a human being is able to move, that is, it confounds the milieu in which we move with space. Space is for it essentially only a milieu, that is, a vessel or form, and this superstition sits so deep in the human mind that few can bring themselves, conversely, to grasp the world of bodies as a milieu for space, or, more correctly, to regard space and the world of bodies as so interpenetrating one another that they do not stand to each other in a relation of vessel and content at all. It is a kind of fetishism to regard space as the vessel of things; indeed this is perhaps the most widespread fetishism that is to be found among human beings at all. It is this fetishism now that forms the most essential component also of the Kantian conception of space.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.              4

[p. 50]

The primitive intuition of space is, however, also apriorist, exactly like the Kantian doctrine of space. The common understanding too accepts space as the precondition for the existence of corporeal things. God first created space, in order to be able to assign a place to the bodies that he created afterward, rather as one must first have a dwelling-space, and only then can fit out the furnishings in it. Kant too regards space as the apriorist condition of the world of phenomena, only he thinks of it as created from the outset by the form that inheres in our mind, so that afterward the sensations may spread out in it. The naive conception of space is objective (ontological) mythology; the Kantian doctrine of space, on the other hand, is subjective (gnoseological) mythology.

Nonetheless we must agree with Schopenhauer that the Transcendental Aesthetic has an epoch-making significance for the history of the theory of knowledge, for only since Kant has the doctrine of space and time stood in the foreground of epistemological considerations, only since Kant has there been, properly speaking, an interest in the problem of space and time, only since Kant do we know that a philosophical doctrine of the world of phenomena cannot be furnished at all without founding it on a theory of space and time; and when humanity one day—as I confidently believe—shall gain clearer concepts of space and time than it possesses today, the historian will have to point out that it was Kant who had foreseen the fundamental significance of the theory of space and time for the shaping of our whole conception of the world, and this, I think, suffices to immortalize the name of a thinker.

[p. 5]

5. The Mysticism of the Form of Space.

In the considerations of the preceding chapter I could emphasize only that basic feature of the Kantian doctrine of space which, in agreement with the generally prevailing primitive intuition of space, posits a dualism between space and the world of bodies, in consequence of which space becomes a sort of milieu, vessel, or form for the world of bodies, and exists independently of it, indeed precedes it as a condition of being. That space and the world of bodies mutually condition each other, that is, that a world of bodies can be there only because there is a space, and conversely a space can be there only because there are bodies that extend, so that therefore a priority of being belongs neither to space nor to bodies, I can set forth only in the positive part of this work: here, however, it is now a matter of working out clearly also what is peculiarly Kantian in the Kantian theory of space, whereby it differs in principle from the current conception of space, so that we may establish the high originality, but unfortunately also the untenability, of the Kantian conception.

What is original in the Kantian conception of space is the deadly cut by which it splits our intuitions, on the one hand, into a matter of intuition, that is, into sensations, and on the other into a form of intuition, that is, into the forms of space and time, which originally inhere in our sensibility. We have indeed already become acquainted heretofore with some samples of the ambiguity of the concept of matter and form, but the protean character of this pair of concepts is first brought into the brightest light by the bifurcation of intuitions. Had Kant contented himself with distinguishing a matter and a form in concepts, judgments, and inferences, his doctrine would probably never have penetrated beyond the narrowest professional circles of logicians

4*

[p. 52]

but since he split the whole world of phenomena, as it presents itself in intuitions, into a matter of sensation and into forms of sensibility, this novel kind of chemistry was bound to arouse the highest interest even in lay circles. To be sure, one already finds in L o c k e a peculiar prelude to the Kantian undertaking (it suffices to point to the doctrine of primary and secondary qualities), but in Locke’s mode of presentation even original points of view lose their philosophical significance, whereas Kant, who after all was no stylist, knows how to set his thoughts in a mystically monumental illumination. In that he divides our intuitions into matter of sensation and forms of sensibility, he furnishes an analysis of the world and a synthesis of the world that makes an incomparably fascinating impression. The world of phenomena is here really to be hewn in two by a miraculous cut, and to be created, so to speak, before our eyes out of the two factors thus obtained.

One must not let this mystical semblance be taken away if one does not wish to strip the Transcendental Aesthetic wholly of its charm. Nothing would be easier than to represent the Kantian undertaking as if it were a matter of a mere procedure of abstraction. We should then, in the case of a body, “disregard” its sensible qualities and retain merely its extension or its spatiality, and conversely, abstracting from this spatiality, obtain merely the sensible qualities as remainder. But may one place the Kantian doctrine in such a light? Was a Kant needed to instruct us that, in the case of a red ball, we are able to disregard its redness and its other qualities in order to think merely of its spatial character, or that, conversely, we are able to disregard this spatial character in order to think merely of its qualities? A greater distortion of the Kantian theory of space would hardly be

[p. 5]

conceivable. Kant wants not merely to “abstract” the matter of sensation from the form of space, he wants—I have no better expression for it—positively to isolate these two factors. For the forms subsist in our mind even if we happen, as for example in sleep, to exercise no cognitive activity: they subsist independently of any matter of sensation, and are for that very reason a priori forms. Only in that the matter of sensation enters into the a priori forms of sensibility of our mind do intuitions arise, according to Kant, from which it is to be seen that here a priori forms and a posteriori sensations appear as two isolated factors, which only through their mystical process of union let the intuitive world proceed out of themselves.

Now there is nothing easier, however, than to shift “isolating” over into “abstracting,” and thereby to set the Kantian theory of space in a twofold light, as the commentators too really are accustomed to do. At one time, namely, the decomposition into a priori form and a posteriori sensation is straightforwardly a method for creating an intuitive world out of two factors; at another time this mystical undertaking is rationally justified by the fact that by simple abstraction we really are able to distinguish extension from the other qualities of an appearance. If, however, we hold fast to this, that in Kant it is by no means a matter of such an abstraction, then we shall not only grasp clearly what is original in his theory of space, but also be able at once to demonstrate its logical impossibility.

For if we isolate the forms of space and time from the matter of sensation, this matter of sensation, since it has not yet entered into those forms, is spaceless and timeless! We should have to think sensations in such a way that, considered by themselves, they exist outside of space and time

[p. 54]

and so are eternal essences, and fall prey to transitoriness only through entering into the a priori forms of space and time of our mind. There would be—to imitate the Kantian terminology—p u r e sensations, which exist in unworldly seclusion outside of space and time, and so cannot yet form component parts of our intuitions, and there would be e m p i r i c a l sensations, which have already condescended to enter into the forms of space and time, and so have become component parts of our empirical intuitions. But what is one to think of “pure sensations” that dwell outside of space and time? Are they perhaps the celebrated “things in themselves”? And what have these pure sensations done wrong, that they must leave their unspatial and untemporal home in order to enter into the forms of space and time and thereby fall prey to transitoriness? Have they let themselves be guilty of some fall from grace? And how do they accomplish the miracle of crossing over from the realm of imperishability into the forms of space and time, whereby they are at once doomed to perish as well, since empirical sensations, hardly arisen, already have to pass away?—These and similar questions are unavoidable if one thinks the matter of sensation isolated from the forms of sensibility, and they show that a mythology of cognition lies hidden in the Kantian doctrine of space. To be sure, if one thinks the spatial extension and the qualitative intensity of intuitions as separated merely by abstraction, then all those questions fall away, but then it is also all over with the originality of the Kantian doctrine of space, for in order, say, to distinguish color from spatial extension by abstraction, one needs no a priori forms of sensibility and no transcendental idealism.

[p. 5]

Exactly as with the matter of sensation, so it stands also with the forms of sensibility, of which only the form of “outer sense,” namely space, interests us here. For since we must think this form at first as isolated, as it a priori “takes place in the mind,” we may not confound it with the form insofar as it already contains a matter of sensation, and so constitutes a component of our empirical intuitions. We must distinguish the pure form, which as yet contains no matter, from the empirical form, which is a component part of our intuitions actually taking place. Just as we have, for example, to distinguish carefully between the web of a spider insofar as it rests merely as a possibility in the spider, and the web insofar as it is finished for catching animals; so we must also make a strict separation between the form insofar as it still rests in the mind, and the form insofar as it has already been cast over the matter of sensation in order to enwrap it. In the first sense the form is a c o n d i t i o n for being able to have intuitions at all; in the second sense, on the other hand, the form is a c o m p o n e n t of the empirical intuition actually taking place. The mysticism of the Kantian concept of form thus comes about through one’s not distinguishing between the form insofar as it is a component part of our intuitions, and the form insofar as it is not yet a component of our intuitions, but merely an a priori condition for the possibility of their taking place. And yet everything depends here on this distinction. For if the form is merely a component part of our intuition actually taking place, then the form is found by a b s t r a c t i o n from the remaining component parts of the intuition; but if the form is an a priori condition for the taking place of intuitions, then it can be found only by Kant’s mystical m e t h o d o f isolation.

[p. 56]

Insofar as the form is a component part of our intuition, it is itself intuitive, for we do say of the form of a body that we s e e this form. Insofar, however, as the form is an a priori condition for the having of intuitions, the form must be something wholly unintuitive, incognizable. For if we were to assume that the form, as it lies a priori in the mind, is intuitive, this would mean that anyone could find and inspect in his mind two actual hollow vessels, space and time. The significance of the Transcendental Aesthetic would then have to be sought in this, that Kant had succeeded, by means of intuition, in discovering two vessels resting in his sensible faculty which had hitherto escaped every perception. Only a parodist of the Kantian doctrine, therefore, could assert that the forms of space and time, which inhere in our sensibility, could already in this a priori state be somehow intuited. Besides, one should also consider that for the coming about of an intuition two factors, form and matter, are always necessary. If, then, the form were to be intuitive in itself, it would have to serve as matter for itself: the form of space would have to be cast over itself and wrap itself up like a matter, in order to be accessible to perception. The spider’s web would have to serve to catch this web itself.

Curiously, however, Kant himself exchanges the form that is a component part of our intuition with the form that is supposed to be a precondition for any intuiting, and holds that this form too can be intuited as a priori form, for he speaks of a “pure intuition,” which is supposed to be possible in pure geometry and pure arithmetic. He himself confounds the procedure of abstraction by which we find the spatial in our intuitions with the procedure of isolation

[p. 5]

by which he wants to find the a priori condition of every intuition, and allows himself to be misled into making this a priori form itself into a “pure intuition.” Had he not fallen into this confusion of concepts—or, as the scholastics say, equivocation —he would not have devised his theory of space at all, for this rests on the thought that we have a “pure intuition” of the a priori condition of each and every empirical intuition; whereas this is—as I have shown—a sheer impossibility. A form a priori must necessarily be unintuitive, incognizable, that is, we should have gained nothing with it, even if it were present, for it would be merely a mythological entity that inheres in our mind in order to weave there a net of space, by means of which it catches and forms the matter of sensation.

The Kantian theory of knowledge thus demands two kinds of “things in themselves”: namely objective things in themselves, which are separated from our faculty of cognition by an infinite chasm, and subjective things in themselves, that is, forms of cognition, which properly make up our faculty of cognition. These are the two factors of cognition that Kant investigated, and one sees that both factors are incognizable, entirely in the sense of the general proof that I have already furnished for the indecomposability of cognition into two factors or components. Kant, however, perceived merely the incognizability of the one factor, for he held the forms to be cognizable. Had he also told himself that space and time in their isolation as a priori conditions of cognition must be incognizable, he would have given up the attempt to construct cognition out of two powers or factors.

Exactly as with the Kantian dualism, so it stands with every dualism in the theory of knowledge:

[p. 58]

but it seems that thinking humanity must still make many an ingenious attempt to split the power of cognition, until it finally arrives at the conviction that conjuring of spirits and fortune-telling contain a far more real endeavor than the decomposition of the faculty of cognition into two factors.

6. The Desensualized Sensibility.

In the preceding chapter I was at pains to demonstrate the double sense of the expression “form,” and thereby to dissolve the mystical charm of the Kantian theory of space. What “form” is, everyone knows who regards a body and perceives in it extension, shape, position; Kant, however, uses the expression form also for the purpose of designating that secret power of our sensibility by virtue of which we are able to perceive the world of phenomena only as ordered in space and time. Although we now learn not the least about this secret power of our sensibility, Kant nonetheless designates it by the name “form of sensibility,” and by this name awakens the semblance as if something were made known to us about the power that compels us to perceive the world of phenomena as a spatio-temporal one. At bottom, however, we have only a name for a wholly unknown power, and moreover a confusing (metaphorical) name, because the expression “form” deceives us with the show of something intuitive, whereas that “form” which is supposed to be a precondition of all intuiting is necessarily unintuitive. Of the expression “form,” then, the classic saying of the poet holds: “For where concepts are lacking, a word steps in at the right time.”

Matters stand almost worse with the expression “matter” (or material). For the latter, namely, it is characteristic

[p. 6]

that Kant refers it only to intuition (or appearance), but not also to the faculty of intuition, to sensibility itself. Since an intuition is supposed to consist of form and matter, one would expect that not only the form but also the matter of intuition is preconditioned by the nature of our sensibility. But this is precisely the characteristic feature of the Kantian doctrine of space, that for the form of our intuition an a priori condition in our sensible faculty is posited, whereas the matter of our intuitions must come away empty-handed, because for it, according to Kant, there are no a priori conditions in our sensibility. For what we sense as red, green, soft, hard, warm, cold, etc., there are no a priori conditions in our sensibility, and only the spatio-temporal character of appearances may enjoy a transcendental preconditionedness. Kant attributes “transcendental ideality” only to space and time; color, weight, etc., however, lack such an ideality.

One recognizes here at first glance that such an unequal treatment of the concepts of form and matter must be wholly untenable. If we perceive appearances as spatio-temporal only because in our sensibility there is, so to speak, a corresponding provision made for this, then the perceiving of color, weight, and other qualities must surely also be explained in such a way that for the perception of these too some provision is to be found in our sensibility. For if we assume that in our sensibility there is no arrangement at all by virtue of which we could perceive color, weight, etc., we should simply be wholly incapable of ever recognizing such qualities. With the same right and with the same necessity as we assume, for the spatio-temporal order of appearances, a ground of possibility in our sensibility, we must also admit,

[p. 60]

that such a ground of possibility will be found for the various sense-qualities. If such a ground of possibility is lacking for the sense-qualities, this means as much as that we lack the capacity to sense anything. Likewise it will be impossible for us to perceive appearances as ordered in space and time if in our sensibility no ground of possibility (no so-called “form”) for spatio-temporal perception were present.

If our sensibility were constituted as Kant characterizes it in the Transcendental Aesthetic, we could never have any intuitions whatever. In vain, for example, would a stimulus of red act upon us; a sensation of red would still never come about, because in our sensibility no provision a priori is made at all to answer the stimulus of red with a sensation of red. All possible sense-stimuli would storm in upon us in vain, for our impotent sensibility would find nothing in itself with which to react to the intruding effects with corresponding sensations. Our sensibility could only ever produce out of itself the “forms” of space and time, because in it rests the secret power to grasp everything intuitive spatio-temporally; but since no corresponding powers for the grasping of sense-qualities lie ready in it, there would still never be any intuition of any appearances. The Kantian sensibility is, in fact, no sensibility, for it contains only conditions a priori for the grasping of space and time, but it lacks conditions a priori for the perceiving of any sensations whatever. To be sure, one could say that Kant in the Transcendental Aesthetic wishes to speak only of “pure sensibility,” that is, of those conditions a priori by virtue of which we are able to apprehend appearances only as spatio-temporal. But Kant speaks in the Aesthetic also of sensations, and explicitly declares that for the various sense-qualities

[p. 6]

no conditions a priori are present in our sensibility, so that color, odor, taste, etc. are indeed of a subjective nature, but not in the sense in which space and time are, to which transcendental ideality belongs, whereas such ideality must be denied to color, odor, taste, etc. This, however, says nothing less than that there lies in sensibility nothing that would enable it to perceive sense-qualities.

Had he proceeded consistently, Kant would also have had to posit forms a priori in sensibility for the different modes of sensation. As universal and necessary as the principle is that all our intuitions have a spatio-temporal character, so universal and necessary is also the principle that only what is in some way sensed can furnish an intuition. As without space and time, so also without sensation there is no intuition. If it is therefore necessary that space and time be preconditioned in our sensibility, the same necessity holds for sensations in general. It was open to Kant merely to assume one form for sensing in general, or else to assign a particular form a priori to each mode of sensation, such as color, tone, pressure, warmth, etc. It is rather irrelevant for us whether there would be as many forms of sensation a priori as we have different senses, or whether a single form of sensation would correspond to all the fields of sense; but so much is certain, that provision for sensing in general would have to be made in sensibility by a form a priori, so that it may be capable of sensing anything, since without the capacity to sense it must necessarily cease to be a sensibility. In that Kant now holds in readiness one form each for space and time, but has not a single form in stock for sensing, he completely desensualizes our sensibility.

Of course he has his good motives for this peculiar procedure. The whole charm of the theory of space and

[p. 62]

time rests on this, that only space and time may have an original seat in sensibility, whereas the mob of our sensations has no claim to such a seat, and is thus excluded from the high council of our sensible faculty. Were Kant to grant to our modes of sensation one or several original seats in our sensibility, it would be all up with every charm of his Transcendental Aesthetic. And what is still worse, there would then have arisen a well-nigh endless multitude of highly irksome and captious questions, which it seemed advisable to evade if possible. Even the question whether a single form is to be assigned to all modes of sensation, or a particular form to each field of sense, would be highly inconvenient. For it draws after it a host of painful problems: for example, what kind of communication exists between a determinate form and the corresponding sense-organ. If there were, that is, in our sensibility a form for colors and a form for tones, one would have to ask of what kind the connection of the form for color with the eye, and of the form of tones with the ear, could be, etc.? It would then have emerged with the greatest clarity that the form for color cannot itself be colored (red, green, etc.), nor the form of tones itself a sounding form, but that these forms must be invisible and inaudible, that is, supersensible forms, as indeed it cannot be otherwise, since they are to have their seat in the mind (or in the soul) of man. With the same distinctness it would appear that the form too which is the condition of spatial perceiving cannot itself be extended, but must be of an unintuitive, non-sensible nature, and that the name “form” is wholly unsuitable for it as for the other forms. It would also be clear at a stroke that, because the “form” is unintuitive, it itself cannot

[p. 6]

be inspected, and so there can be no talk at all of a “pure intuition.”

As a consequence of the question how non-sensible “forms” can stand in communication with the bodily sense-organs, the problem of the connection of soul and body would have to be unrolled in its whole extent. It would turn out that in the Transcendental Aesthetic we are really engaged in metaphysics, and indeed of the most unfruitful kind, namely a metaphysics of occult qualities, with the difference that here the talk is not of occult qualities of bodily things, but of those of our mind. For it scarcely needs to be said that the “forms of sensibility” would simply reveal themselves as occult qualities of this sensibility, so that by assuming them nothing would be accomplished toward the explanation of our sensible activity. If someone wished to explain the yellow color of gold by saying that there lurks in the matter of gold an occult quality by virtue of which gold must necessarily be yellow, I believe that such an explaining scarcely still deserves the name. Of equally doubtful worth is it to say that there lurk in our mind forms a priori in consequence of which our intuitions must necessarily be spatio-temporal. Let one try, from the Kantian explanation that space is a form a priori of our sensibility, to derive by inference any property of space whatever, for example that it is three-dimensional, and one will be convinced that this is impossible. We can only ever invent and impute to the “form of our sensibility” something that we already know without the assumption of a form. Thus, when we already know that space is three-dimensional, we impute this to the form of our sensibility, and say that it is the secret author of this three-dimensionality. But were our space four-dimensional, we should also on this account

[p. 64]

hold the form of our sensibility responsible for this too. Nothing is so patient as a form a priori. If I know, for example, that the straight line is the shortest path between two points, I find it quite convenient to say that this could not be otherwise, because it is determined beforehand by the form of my sensibility; but were the shortest path between two points not the straight line but some arbitrary curve, I could with the same convenience represent this too as determined beforehand by the form of my sensibility. In short, the forms a priori have the laudable property of saying Amen to whatever we already knew before we received tidings of such forms. They are evidently there for the purpose of giving our knowledge a higher consecration, and we should be sincerely grateful to them for this service, if only they were willing to say Amen to everything that we recognize as truth. Thus we recognize, for example, as truth that no man can perceive phenomena without possessing some mode or other of sensation (such as seeing, hearing, etc.). Where all modes of sensation are lacking, there the perceiving of phenomena is necessarily lacking. Since, then, some mode of sensation (or modes) is unconditionally required for the perception of phenomena, we should unconditionally have to possess a form a priori at least for one mode of sensation, because without such a form the condition for the possibility of a sensing is lacking. At the very least Kant, in the spirit of his formalist way of thinking, would have had to conceive a form for sensing in general as well. The consequences of his neglect to provide also for an a priori form of sensation can be summed up in the following two points: 1. We might be absolutely paralyzed in every mode of sensation, that is, absolutely without sensation, and should still always have a “pure intuition,” since in our sensibility provision has in any case been made for forms of

[p. 6]

space and time. 2. In vain do we have nothing but healthy sense-organs; they are of no use to us at all, since our sensible faculty of cognition lacks a form for sensing that would make it possible for us also to sense. Eye, ear, nose, tongue, the whole nervous system function in vain in the most correct manner, since Kant did not think it necessary to provide the sensible faculty of cognition also with a form for sensing. From this, however, it may be seen how badly off we human beings would be, had we been created by a philosopher of a rationalistic cast of mind. He would have sent our sensibility on its way with the form of space and the form of time, by virtue of which we are supposed to have “pure intuitions” and to be able to pursue pure mathematics, as well as pure natural science, but he would have denied us that we should ever be able to sense anything at all.

Kant combines with the bipartition of intuitions into form and matter the tendency to grant an original home in our sensibility to form alone, but to deny one to matter. Thus there falls to the matter of our intuitions, the sensations, the heavy fate of utter homelessness. The sensations would, as has been shown, have to come over into the forms of space and time out of a world without space and time, which is yet impossible; on the other hand there are also no forms a priori for them from which they could derive their origin. So they hover between heaven and earth, midway between outer and inner things in themselves: a riddle of the Kantian philosophy, a symbol of the desensualized sensibility.

7. On “Things in Themselves.”

One can contest the formalist theory of knowledge from the ground up without having so much as to touch the romantic concept of the

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                  5

[p. 66]

things in themselves. One can furnish the proof that an isolation of sensibility from the understanding involves a twofold impossibility, and that the generation of cognition from a priori forms and an a posteriori matter of sensation contains a mythology of cognition which on the one hand contributes nothing to insight into the nature of our activity of cognition, but on the other hand is also a fiction which, in contradiction with its own intention, makes sensing, and therewith the whole activity of cognition, impossible. From this standpoint the famous Kantian question whether and how metaphysics is possible need then no longer be discussed, for a theory of knowledge which teaches a mythical-metaphysical dissection and generation of cognition has forfeited the right to raise the question of the possibility of a metaphysics, or even to want to establish limits for the human capacity for cognition at all. Were it possible—as the formalist theory of knowledge supposes it can accomplish—to isolate sensibility and understanding, to separate form and matter of cognition, then there would no longer be any impossibility at all, because the self-contradictory, the antilogical, would hereby be accomplished and raised to dominion over the world.

Once we have seen that the opposition of a priori forms and a posteriori matter of cognition is an impossible one, the whole system of formalist (or transcendental) idealism collapses, and then it is also no longer necessary to subject the concept of things in themselves to a special examination. Nevertheless I wish to conclude my considerations on formalist thinking with an elucidation of the concept of things in themselves, because thereby a light falls on the doctrine of forms itself as well. Kant, namely, devises an apparatus of forms, a sort of cognition-machine

[p. 7]

which must be fed by sensations in order to let the world of experience proceed out of itself. But since these sensations must ultimately after all stem from the things in themselves, the things in themselves really stand over against the Kantian cognition-machine as a correlate on the other side. It is now very instructive to set the cognition-machine and the things in themselves directly over against each other, because it then emerges clearly that the whole apparatus of forms too which Kant wants to devise consists of things in themselves, and indeed of subjective things in themselves, of which we ourselves, if they existed, would be unable to cognize anything.

One arrives at the concept of the things in themselves by the same deadly method of isolation by which sensibility was separated from the understanding. Only that now it is a matter not merely of eliminating the understanding, but of doing the same with sensibility as well, that is, of thinking our whole faculty of cognition eliminated, so that it may not place itself as a hindrance between us and the things themselves. Nothing, then, is more comprehensible than that Kant teaches that the things in themselves are incognizable: how should we, with an eliminated faculty of cognition, still be capable of cognizing them?

We now know, however, also what is demanded of us in order to arrive at the concept of the things in themselves. It is demanded of us that we should not think thinkingly: for only to a non-thinking thinking does the existence of things in themselves make itself known. Only when one divests oneself of one’s faculty of thinking, and thinks without a faculty of thinking, can one think of things in themselves. From this, however, it is evident that we should have to satisfy two demands which contradict one another in order to think things in themselves. Only he who commands a non-thinking activity of thinking can speak of things in themselves; but all those thinkers who possess only a thinking thinking are

5*

[p. 68]

forever prevented from attaching any sense to the word “thing in itself.” This also makes clear on what the great charm of the things in themselves rests: it is precisely the contradiction hidden away in this romantic concept that lends it an incomparable magnetic spell. For nothing seizes the human mind with such force as a contradiction, provided only that it is not presented so clumsily that it at once displays its own inner untenability.

On the other hand, however, the concept of the things in themselves is nothing other than the miscarried expression of an infinite longing for absolute cognition. Were we in a position to cognize things in themselves, this would, in Kant’s sense, have the significance that we possessed world-creating power. In that we cognized something, the cognized would already be called into existence; we should be in a position to create the things by mere thinking. A creating faculty of cognition we do not, to be sure, possess, and if the expression “thing in itself ” could be used only in the sense that we lack a world-creating faculty, then one would have nothing to object to it. We use in logic, after all, for example, such expressions as “square circle,” to indicate thereby that there are word-constructions which cannot be thought out. Contradictory formations of concepts are far-shining warning signs for thinking, not to plunge into the endless abyss of a contradiction, and to guard against being shattered on the logical impossibility. Regarded from this point of view the concept of the “things in themselves” deserves to be immortal, and the same holds of Kant’s whole apparatus of forms, for both together represent a fiction which has sprung from a boundless longing for absolute cognition. They show us clearly what we may not undertake

[p. 7]

if we want to press through to a theory of knowledge.

We must not isolate any cognitive powers and factors of cognition, for with such splittings we demand the impossible. If one isolates sensibility, then the understanding is eliminated, and all concepts of the understanding have become incognizable, because for mere sensibility there can be no concepts. If one isolates the understanding, then sensibility is eliminated, and then all intuitions of sensibility have become incognizable, for to the pure isolated understanding there can and may be no intuitions. For a pure understanding all sensible intuitions are things in themselves; for an isolated sensibility, on the other hand, all concepts of the understanding are things in themselves. The one faculty of cognition does not know the world of the other faculty of cognition, and exactly so it is with one form of cognition over against another. The form of space, for example, which produces in us the intuition of extension, can and may know nothing of the temporal succession that is brought forth by the form of time, just as conversely the form of time stands absolutely alien to every juxtaposition that is grasped by the form of space. So many forms, so many mutually exclusive powers of cognition! In vain does one seek to connect them with one another, and to set them up as components of one faculty of cognition, since I have furnished the proof that one cannot stop at components, because every component demands its share in cognition, and this share can be only that cognition which the component in question is able to bring forth for itself. Kant is in fact at pains to bring his forms to a unified cooperation, and he makes in this direction almost superhuman exertions, but in vain: for the forms that he devises behave like demons let loose, who

[p. 70]

go about their mystical work heedless of one another and shatter our cognition. Every form stands in the light of the other; all constrict one another, until finally the whole faculty of cognition, standing in its own light, robs itself of the possibility of cognition. Were there forms in Kant’s sense, we should cognize nothing at all, for every form is by itself already an absolute hindrance to cognition. It is also a quite singular thought to let cognition be generated by an a priori machine, and I believe that such a thought could have been occasioned only by the confusion of the faculty of cognition with our individual organization. Our individual organization does indeed consist of parts, organs, which perform peculiar functions, and this circumstance may lead a theorist of knowledge to invent an analogous organization of the faculty of cognition, but such an invention can serve only to deprive the faculty of cognition of its capacity to function. The faculty of cognition is thoroughly unified and indivisible, and to divide it means as much as to want to kill it.

It would certainly be an instructive undertaking to criticize the whole Critique of Reason from the standpoint just characterized and to show that the forms of the understanding are just such mythical impossibilities as the forms of sensibility. I shall indeed in the following investigations seize every opportunity to illuminate the remaining parts of the Critique, but in this work I must confine myself to setting forth the fundamental untenability of the formalist theory of knowledge in general, and then also specifically in examples, such as the theory of space (and later also the theory of time), because it is these constituent parts of formalism that still today exercise a mighty after-effect. I now turn to the psychologistic

[p. 7]

theory of knowledge, which in our day stands antagonistically over against the formalist theory of knowledge, although both, as I shall show, are related to one another in their innermost depths, because they have equally a dualistic character.

[PDF 74]

Section II.

Psychologism and the Theory of Time.

8. Symbolic and Impressionist Logic.

The critical situation of the modern theory of knowledge can perhaps best be characterized through the conflict of two parties, of which the one seeks the salvation of logic in the Kantian doctrine of forms, or else in Bolzano’s Wissenschaftslehre, while the other expects the laying of the foundation of a logic quite properly not from this science itself but from psychology. The representatives of this latter direction of thought are also customarily called “psychologicists,” but since this name seems to have originated in an ironizing intention, I believe I should replace it by the designation “impressionists,” because psychologism in the modern theory of knowledge may be conceived as a parallel phenomenon to impressionism in belles-lettres and the plastic arts.

The opposition of formalism and impressionism points back to the two main currents in modern philosophy, to rationalism and sensualism (empiricism). Impressionism in the theory of knowledge descends from English sensualism and empiricism, as it has been represented by B a c o n , L o c k e , B e r k e l e y ,

[p. 8]

H u m e , etc. Thus the crisis in modern logic can be traced back to Bacon and his great agitation against Aristotelian and scholastic logic; indeed, without this tracing back it is altogether incomprehensible. The spirit of the new age has for four to five centuries been pressing toward a new theory of knowledge, but the task to be solved is so excessively difficult that it will probably still require the most intensive thinking-work of several coming generations before one will be able to speak of a new logic that is to withstand scientific comparison with the new geometry, new analysis, new dynamics, physics, chemistry and biology, and to which our modern culture is to be able to point with just such pride as to the other scientific achievements of the last centuries.

The highly questionable vacillations and crises in the more recent theory of knowledge are to be traced chiefly to an all too violent, indeed passionate reaction against ancient logic. This logic has, like everything that we owe to the incomparable Hellenic spirit, a classical trait, that is, there lies in it an imperishable capital of truth which should not be lightly esteemed or rejected, but rather grasped in its living fertility and capacity for further development. As new poetry and plastic art have won their renaissance partly through opposition, but partly also through their most intimate contact with the Hellenic ideal of beauty, so too the new theory of knowledge can emerge not from mere opposition to ancient logic, but only from the union of modern impressionist thinking with the ancient symbolist way of thinking.

I call ancient logic a symbolic one, because, guided by its magnificent sense for truth, it saw that one cannot treat of cognition or knowledge without treating of the symbols in which we

[p. 74]

set forth palpably that which we know or suppose we know. If one lets oneself be prejudiced by Bacon from the outset against the symbols, which after all we shall never be able to dispense with in science, one enters upon the path of skepticism, which, pursued consistently, leads to an absolute despair of the human capacity for cognition in general. For if one regards linguistic symbols as, so to speak, bank-notes of our faculty of cognition, then with the discrediting of these bank-notes there is, strictly taken, discredited only the banker himself, namely the faculty of cognition. One readily grants to Bacon and his empiricist school that nothing is so much abused as symbols, particularly words, and that scholastic logic above all degenerated into a barren play with words and word-syllogisms; but may one on this ground call in question the fundamental significance of symbols for human cognition? Is logic to concern itself with deaf-mute cognition, and is it to become a deaf-mute science?

It was a fatal mistake of English empiricism to incite minds against the reliability of symbols merely because one was dissatisfied with Aristotelian logic, and to play out against them, as a trump, our impressions. As epochal as was the significance of bringing the impressionist side of our activity of cognition to the fore, so little was it necessary to treat the symbolic side of it with skepticism and disparagement. The Greek spirit was fully in the right when, in logical inquiry, it clung at first to the symbolic side of cognition, for the impressions that anyone has are in the first place his private affair, and only the symbols in which he declares his opinion make it possible for his views

[p. 8]

to be learned. The symbols in which we set forth our cognition are, so to speak, acts of cognition, and Hellenic logic grasped the cognizer on this acting, active side, quite as a judge, in the investigation of right and wrong, must first ascertain the acts of the person, and only then can also go into feelings or other motives of the acts. As comical as it would be if a public prosecutor were always to speak only of the feelings of the accused, never of his acts: so comical would be every theory of knowledge that had to do always only with impressions and their “elaboration,” but never with symbols, in which cognition must after all be set forth.

By this, however, I by no means wish to plead for a one-sided symbolic logic. Undoubtedly the neglect of the impressionist side of our cognition constitutes the fundamental defect of the Aristotelian Organon, and just as undoubtedly the bringing to the fore of this impressionism is the proper title to fame of the more recent, and quite especially of the English, theory of knowledge. But I believe that the impressionist thinking of modern times would not have made so sharp a front against symbolic logic if the logical teachings of Aristotle had at least brought symbolism to a really systematic development. Aristotelian logic can also, merely with regard to the symbolic side of our cognition, be faulted to a high degree, since at bottom it presents a one-sided logic of concepts, whereas in cognition what matters is acts of judgment and not the forming of isolated single concepts. Indeed Aristotle goes so far as to create an opposition between judgment and concept, and it is just this opposition that led to scholastic thinking and had to discredit ancient logic.

In the Hermeneutics Aristotle comes back again and again

[p. 76]

to explaining what a concept is as distinguished from a judgment. In three chapters (1, 4, 5) he repeats five times the same distinction, whose essential content is that there are in our soul two kinds of thoughts: first, those that fall under the viewpoint of the true and the false, these being the assertions or judgments; but second, those of which one cannot ask at all whether they are true or false, and these are the concepts. When I say, for example, ‘man is white,’ this is a thought that can be true or false; but if I say ‘man’ or merely ‘white’ taken by itself, then these expressions have indeed a meaning, but there attaches to them neither a true nor a false, which could come about only if those two symbols were connected with one another.

“As in the soul there is now a thought with which no assertion of the true or false is connected, now again one to which the one or the other must necessarily belong, so it is also in language; for it is connection and separation on which the false and the true rest. If, then, subject and predicate are each taken alone for itself, this corresponds to the thoughts without connection and separation, as when I say, for example, ‘man’ or ‘white’ without anything further being added; in this case there is as yet nothing true or false involved. A proof of this lies also in the fact that the ‘goat-stag’ [a fabulous animal] signifies something, to be sure, but nothing yet true or false, unless being or not-being is added to it, simply or with a certain determination of time.” (Hermeneutics 1.)

One can scarcely believe what mischief these explanations of the Stagirite have wrought by virtue of their apparent clarity and cogency. If, in the sense of Aristotle, we really have two kinds of thoughts in the soul

[p. 8]

and one class of them possesses that wondrous nature that they can be neither true nor false, then it would indeed be most conducive to our happiness if we always formed only such thoughts as—to use a modern turn of phrase—lie beyond true and false; and this happiness, too, would be, at least to all appearance, easy to realize, for we should have only to read dictionaries. It is then also quite incomprehensible why men take the trouble to connect one concept with another into an assertion, since in this way they expose themselves to the danger of also asserting falsehoods, whereas a thinking that consisted of nothing but isolated concepts could secure eternal inner peace of thought.

The contradictory opposition which Aristotle establishes between judgments and concepts seems to me the point of departure of every scholastic logic. If one begins to make so fundamental a distinction between thoughts and thoughts, as Aristotle does here, then one opens the way for every dualistic thinking, which wants to compose cognition of two kinds of opposed constituents. May I therefore be permitted to bring forward some objections to the Aristotelian distinction of judgment and concept: 1. One can also form sentences in such a way that they raise no claim to be considered from the viewpoint of logical truth or falsity, for example when one tells fairy tales. As the concept “centaur” is neither true nor false, so the fables that relate to centaurs will also have no claim to truth or falsity. In general one should not in logic treat of the concepts of the poetic or mythical spirit, since these concepts belong to the domain of aesthetic and artistic thinking. 2. In logic concepts fall quite

[p. 78]

like judgments under the viewpoint of truth and falsity. For one grasps the sense of concepts only when one understands how to apply them in sentences, and for every concept sentences must be found in which it can be applied. Whether a concept is correctly or incorrectly formed will very soon appear when one has recourse to the sentences that were used to unfold its meaning. Thus, for example, one will easily be able to establish that someone has made himself an incorrect concept of space if one learns that he imagines space as a vessel or a mold in which the bodies are located. 3. Aristotle goes beyond the facts when he holds that there are present in our soul two kinds of thoughts, of which the one kind must be true or false, the others not. I am not aware that anyone has found in his soul two kinds of thoughts before he has yet proceeded to the use of linguistic signs. Only when one sets forth one’s (judging) thoughts in symbols does it appear that, in order to make a mere assertion, one must bring several symbols into use. Now one can—and ought also—wonder in the highest degree that for the setting forth of a mere single assertion several signs are required: for this is precisely a fundamental problem of logic. Aristotle, however, does not raise this problem, and thereby bars his way to the most important basic question of symbolic logic. Without feeling the problem, he decides at once in dogmatic fashion that, corresponding to sentences and words, there are also two kinds of thoughts present in the soul: judgment-thoughts and concept-thoughts. There is, however, no greater danger for science than to solve problems dogmatically without having raised them, for in this way the most momentous errors can remain veiled. Even if it were true that there are in the soul two kinds of

[p. 8]

thoughts, as Aristotle demands, it must still be asked, how does one know this? Has one inferred it from the consideration of linguistic symbols, that is, concluded, on the basis of the difference between linguistic sentences and single words, that in the soul too two kinds of thoughts (judgments and concepts) must exist, or does one rely perhaps on a so-called “inner perception” (which plays so great a role in modern psychologism), which could find those two kinds of thoughts in the soul immediately, even without regard to the symbols we use? For my part I possess no such “inner perception,” and scarcely believe that anyone could possess it. 4. If concepts in Aristotle’s sense are indifferent to truth and falsity, then judgments too must be so. For judgments contain in themselves no other constituents than concepts; but since concepts lie beyond true and false, it is incomprehensible how from nothing but constituents that are indifferent to truth something can proceed that is true or false. 5. The preceding argument would lose its force only in the case that a judgment contained not only conceptual but also constituents of a different kind. Now there lurks in the Aristotelian explanation an ambiguity which seduces to the thought that judgments really do contain something else besides concepts. The true and the false, says Aristotle, rests on connection and separation. If that were so, we should have to conceive connection and separation as something that by its nature was opposed to concepts, since these latter are indeed indifferent to truth and falsity. Thus the Aristotelian conception of judgment contains a most dangerous ambiguity, in that it leaves undecided whether a sentence has to consist of two kinds of elements of opposed nature, or else merely of one kind of elements (concepts).

[p. 80]

6. If a judgment consists merely of one kind of thought-elements (concepts), then it must remain indifferent to truth and falsity, because all its elements have this indifferent nature. But if a judgment consists of two kinds of thought-elements (of concepts on the one hand, connection and separation on the other), then this would surely have to come to light in the symbols as well. Now we express connecting and separating, too, by words which represent concepts; so these concepts of connection and separation would likewise have to be indifferent to truth and falsity, from which it would only follow once more that the judgment would remain indifferent to truth and falsity. Herewith the latent contradiction in Aristotle’s fundamental explanations of judgment and concept is set out in tangible fashion.

Thus Aristotelian logic already harbors within itself a profound discord of thought. The nature of concepts is misjudged, for they are degraded, so to speak, to lifeless thought-stones that do not react to truth and falsity. Judgment itself is represented, in its essence, as a connecting and separating, though it lies open to view that one can connect concepts with one another without their making up a judgment, as for example when I say: space and time. The relation of concept and judgment is distorted, because concepts appear in the light of being dead thought-elements, out of which, by composition, judgments (admittedly again only dead ones) can arise. Herewith the way is paved for an alchemy of cognition, which can then assume the most various shapes. Thus, for example, the Kantian theory of knowledge is to be understood as one shape of logical chemism, for when Kant speaks of a matter and form of cognition, he is influenced by the Aristotelian conception of the relation of concept and judgment. For him concepts form the logical material for judgments, the

[p. 8]

manner of their connection and separation, however, he designates as form. The opposition between the two elements of judgment, or of cognition as well, which in Aristotle was still a latent one, becomes in Kant a fully executed system of the theory of knowledge. Only in this perspective does the epoch-making historical significance of the Kantian theory of knowledge come to light, for hardly any other thinker of modern times has spun on the dualistic thread that lay hidden in Aristotelian logic with such powerful energy as Kant. What did he not make of Aristotle’s two words (connection and separation)? He devised an entire a priori apparatus of forms, which was to produce the world of our experience, and even to establish once and for all the limits up to which speculation can and may penetrate without lapsing into a sham metaphysics.

Only through the Kantian critique of cognition does Aristotelian logic, too, stand in a light as new as it is interesting. For only the mystical magical glamour of Kantian logic shows us what dangers for our thinking arise from the circumstance that we neglect the fundamental questions of symbolic logic, such as the relation of judgment and concept, and pass indifferently by the wondrous fact that we set forth a single assertion in a plurality of symbols; indeed, what is still worse, derive from this fact, without misgiving, far-reaching dogmatic conceptions. May we really conceive concepts as something elementary as against the composite nature of judgments? If, for example, a chemist uses the word gold, then for him an entire treatise lies contained in the sense of this word; for he comprises all the experiences he has had of that metal, all the sentences he sets up about its behavior, in the one concept ‘gold,’ so that for him this word is an abbreviated symbol

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                6

[p. 82]

for a whole, exceedingly rich bundle of judgments. If, then, one may conceive judgment as though it were a composition of concepts, then in any case the polar-opposite standpoint is also justified, in which a concept presents itself as a composition of judgments.

Allow me to mention here in passing that in geometry a similar twofold way of conceiving geometrical figures has become prevalent. Everyone admits at once that nothing is more natural than to conceive a point as something simpler than a straight line, and that accordingly the straight line may be regarded as a continuous row of points. Modern geometry, however, proceeds also from the polar way of conceiving, in which a point is regarded as the representative of all the straight lines that pass through it. This twofold way of conceiving point and line has led to an altogether unforeseen enrichment of geometry, in that each way of viewing yields its own theorems, and from the one class of theorems the other class can be inferred at once, without further investigations. I mention this only to suggest that, if someone considers himself entitled to conceive concepts as elements of judgment, he is just as much entitled, conversely, to represent judgments as elements of concepts. What does one do, for example, when one defines a concept? One seeks out the judgments that are necessary to fix that concept unambiguously: so these judgments, too, can be conceived as though they were the constituent elements of the concept to be defined. To be sure, one may reply that it is not logically permissible to conceive now the concept, now the judgment as the elementary. I gladly concede this, if only because concepts can be put together without a judgment arising through the composition; only I hold that one

[p. 8]

the chemical principle of composition in logic altogether, since the faculty of cognition is no retort, and insights cannot be conceived like chemical ingredients.

In any case, however, it is a fact that we can set forth not only a judgment in a plurality of symbols, but conversely also the sense of a symbol in a plurality of judgments. This very possibility of inversion makes it, however, problematic in the highest degree whether from relations of composition among the symbols we may straightway infer relations of composition in our cognition. It is a main problem of symbolic logic how symbols are related to cognition itself, and since Aristotle in his Organon neglects precisely this question, indeed solves it in a dogmatic manner without even raising it, this was bound to act upon the development of symbolic logic in the most fateful way.

The first consequence of the Aristotelian confusion of the symbols with cognitions themselves was that ever more widespread self-deception, as though concepts and judgments could be found immediately in the cognizing soul—even without the slightest regard for linguistic symbols. There arose that peculiar concept of a “pure understanding,” which, sealing itself off hermetically from the sensible world, is capable of sinking into itself and finding pure concepts there, indeed of combining these also into pure (a priori) sentences. Thus symbolic logic became unfaithful to its own point of departure and despised the linguistic symbols, whose relation to cognition ought to have formed the object of its proper investigations. The more zealously men busied themselves with the elaboration of subtle technical terms, the more they abandoned themselves to the self-deception that symbols were to be regarded as an accessory of cognizing, whose sole task would be to communicate what is thought in the pure understanding in pure concepts and

6*

[p. 84]

pure judgments to other human beings as well. Since this whole procedure rested on self-deception, the necessary consequence of it was that one lost oneself in empty word-quibbling to precisely the same degree to which one believed oneself exalted above all symbols and able to draw pure thoughts from the pure understanding. Thus decadent symbolic logic smoothed the way for its arch-enemy, impressionist logic: that new kind of logic which really did regard all human symbols with the deepest mistrust, indeed represented them as the real canker of all science, as may be seen from the brilliant aphorisms of B a c o n ’s New Organon, borne by mighty eloquence.

9. Continuation.

What a profound influence B a c o n exerted on English thinking can be gauged from the contempt with which H o b b e s treats linguistic symbols, but perhaps still more from that discord into which L o c k e fell with himself when he finally arrived at the conviction that a consideration of linguistic signs cannot, after all, be omitted in an inquiry into the human understanding. This thinker, so extraordinarily amiable in his frankness, makes a formal confession of his change of opinion, which took place rather late, and since this confession positively constitutes a document for the history of the development of modern logic, may I be permitted to insert its wording here. Locke says in the third book of his Essay concerning Human Understanding (ch. 9, § 21):

“I must confess, then, that, when I first began this discourse of the understanding, and a good while after, I had not the least thought that

[p. 9]

any consideration of words was at all necessary to it. But when, having passed over the original and composition of our ideas, I began to examine the extent and certainty of our knowledge, I found it had so near a connexion with words, that, unless their force and manner of signification were first well observed, there could be very little said clearly and pertinently concerning knowledge: which being conversant about truth, had constantly to do with propositions . . .”

Thus speaks the father of modern psychologism, and his words attest that the symbolic logic of antiquity had long been regarded as dead and buried. A revolution had been accomplished in minds, which in the most radical manner set itself above the logic of concepts of the ancients, and with passion immersed itself in the impressionist conditions of our cognition. Thereby the new course in the theory of knowledge was created, and the impetus given to the flowering of new branches of knowledge, namely the biology of the life of sensation, in particular biological optics and acoustics (the doctrine of seeing and hearing). Much reason as modern logic has to be proud of these achievements, it is nonetheless deeply to be regretted—as is already evident from Locke’s confession— that men wished to tear down behind them all the bridges that could lead back to ancient logic. For such an overzealous craze for innovation had the sole consequence that men neglected to settle critical accounts with the old logic, and thereby fell quite unnoticed into the deepest dependence upon that old and underrated logic. Its errors could henceforth continue to work with unbroken, indeed with doubled, force, so that the new logic fell into the rut of a new scholasticism. In place of the scholasticism of

[p. 86]

concepts we have today a scholasticism of sensations or impressions. It is this scholasticism of sensation that one is accustomed to designate by the name psychologism.

We have seen that Aristotle, in a wholly unjustified way, posits a contradictory opposition between concept and judgment, and that in so doing he mixes “the thoughts in the soul” with the symbols, that is, confounds the constitution of our cognition in itself with the constitution of the signs we use. These fundamental aberrations are now propagated further in impressionist logic as well, with the sole difference that impressions have now taken the place of concept-symbols. As formerly the symbols were confounded with cognitions, so now impressions are confounded with cognitions, and as formerly judging cognition was represented as a “connecting and separating” of concepts, so now judging cognition was to become a connecting and separating of impressions. As formerly cognition was sought to be explained through a chemism of concepts, so now it is derived from a chemism of impressions. The alchemy of concepts is followed by an alchemy of impressions. Locke’s whole theory of knowledge stands under the sign of impression-chemistry, for there nothing else is spoken of than simple and composite ideas, and then again of a connection and separation of them, which is supposed to constitute the essence of judging.

But can cognition be conceived as a connection and separation of impressions? If, for example, I see a horseman sitting on his horse and then swinging himself out of the saddle, two ideas which were just now spatially connected are replaced in me by two new ideas, between which the preceding spatial connectedness no longer obtains.

[p. 9]

But has the truth of the assertion that the horseman a few moments ago sat on his horse been annihilated by his having dismounted? Ideas together with their connections perish, but the truth of their having been connected does not perish, and cognition has to do with this which does not perish amid the restless flight of impressions and their connections. From which it follows that impressions and their connections, because they are unceasingly doomed to perish, are not to be confounded with imperishable truth, which remains untouched by all the flight of impressions and their connections. Our cognition always has to do with the imperishable in the change of all phenomena, and there is no greater logical error than to confound the transitory with the cognition of the transitory, since cognition is precisely the grasping of the eternal in the transitory, as I shall illuminate more closely in the positive part of my work.

The contradictions of Aristotelian logic propagate themselves, as said, in impressionist logic. Just as, confronting the Aristotelian doctrine of judgment, we had to ask what is meant by the connecting and separating of concepts, since connecting and separating are likewise concepts: so we must also raise the question, confronting impressionist logic, what is meant by the connecting and separating of ideas, since to all appearance this connecting and separating can in turn be a forming of ideas. If that is so, then all judging and cognizing has been reduced to a mere flight of impressions, then cognizing is through and through nothing other than impression. Evidently the words connection and separation serve only to hide behind them, and to get the question of what cognition is out of the world

[p. 88]

altogether. For connection and separation already contain the whole concept of cognition in themselves, and one uses them only in order to divert attention from cognition and direct it to the impressions, of which one asserts that in their connections or separations they constitute cognition. Are we to let ourselves be deceived by such a scholastic playing with words? Would it not be more candid to say that our cognizing is absolutely nothing but impression, and again only impression, instead of hiding behind the words connection and separation, which create the semblance that something would still have to be added to the impressions which is not impression at all? But if one thinks of such another element, which first in union with the impression yields cognition: well then, let this element be shown, as Kant did, who added to the matter of cognition (namely the impression) also a priori forms. Then the dualism of this conception will come openly to the fore; admittedly it will then also have to be recognized as untenable in its contradictory nature.

The analogy between impressionist and symbolic logic can, however, be pursued still further. Just as the symbolic logician comes to assume a pure understanding, which, sinking into itself, is able to find pure concepts in itself: so the impressionist, too, falls into the predicament of conceiving sensibility in such a way that it sinks into itself in order to cognize the impressions contained in it. Verily one exerts oneself quite in vain to get around cognition or to exorcise it out of one’s own mind, for it demands a hearing with elementary and irresistible force. However much anyone may be filled with the striving to dissolve cognition into impressions, he nonetheless involuntarily comes to speak of a cognition,

[p. 9]

and then nothing remains but to assume in the mind a second sensible faculty, which busies itself solely with cognizing what goes on in the first sensible faculty. A sensible faculty this second one, too, must of course be, because cognition is to be reduced at any price to impressions. This peculiar theory of the two sensibilities attains classical expression in modern times first in L o c k e ; in the most manifold modifications later accorded to it, it forms the foundation of the modern scholasticism of sensation.

According to Locke our ideas spring from two sources; one of these sources is the objects of sense-perception. Our senses come into contact with palpable objects, through whose operation we arrive at such ideas as yellow, white, warm, cold, etc. The other source of our ideas is the activities of our own mind. For while our mind receives ideas from the first source, it comes in time also to observe its own doing, and thus its own doing becomes for it a second source of ideas, such as, for example, perceiving, thinking, doubting, believing, inferring, knowing, willing, etc., nothing but ideas which could not have been gained from the mere sensible observation of external things. “This [second] source of ideas every man has wholly in himself; and though it be n o t s e n s e , as having nothing to do with external objects, yet it is v e r y l i k e i t , and might properly enough be called i n t e r n a l s e n s e .” (Essay, Book II, ch. 1, § 4.)

What, then, is this inner sense? Are we perhaps to imagine that this sense, too, possesses a special organ, that thus some portion of the brain (perhaps the

[p. 90]

frontal lobe!) performs that vital work, without which we should have no knowledge of our own mental activity, and hence also no “psychology.” But that would amount to positing a special organ in the brain, which would furnish only the ideas of one’s own mental activity, thus only ideas such as the psychologist needs. There would be a brain-organ for psychology. But then probably mathematics, physics, chemistry, physiology, etc. might each lay claim to an organ or organ-complex of its own. Incidentally, Locke does not take the trouble to search for a brain-organ of the inner sense. Evidently he uses the word “inner sense” much as we in ordinary life speak of an “inner eye” or a “mind’s eye,” to indicate that there are objects which cannot be seen with the bodily eye. In short, he uses the word as a metaphor, which is to help him over a great logical embarrassment. For on the one hand he expressly declares that the inner sense is by no means to be of a sensible nature, on the other hand he would nevertheless, so to speak, stamp it as something sensible. The inner sense, though it is not sensible, is nonetheless to represent, in a way, a kind of sensibility, a second sensibility, an inner sensibility. Thus, in the twinkling of an eye, the non-sensible self-observation—Locke calls it reflection—becomes an inner sensibility. This non-sensible, which is nonetheless supposed to be very like a sensible, is one of those contradictions which Locke, with the amiability peculiar to him, quite guilelessly reveals, and in which his sensualist leaning is expressed most clearly.

If one considers more closely how the outer and the inner sense are to divide the mental work between them, one recognizes at once the impossibility of such a division. The outer sense is to have sensations, but no knowledge

[p. 9]

of its own doing; the inner sense is to supply such knowledge, but itself to have no sensations. For if the inner sense itself had sensations, it would not need to heed whether the outer sense has them, and then it would take knowledge of its own sensations, and not of the activity of the outer sense. Hence the outer and the inner sense must so divide the roles that the former has sensations without any knowledge whatever, the latter on the other hand has knowledge without sensation; which is evidently absurd, for how could the inner sense have knowledge of a sensing activity, since it itself senses nothing? It is just as if a blind man wished to borrow eyes in order to see something, and in doing so let himself be guided by the following speculation: the eye that I borrow has sensations, to be sure, but no knowledge of its doing; I, however, who am in possession of an inner sense, shall with its help look on while the borrowed eye senses colors and shapes. Thereby my taking cognizance is added to the unconscious sensations of the eye, which together yields a conscious seeing. Precisely this speculation, however, is what finds expression in the concepts of the outer and the inner sense. Unconscious sensation and sensationless consciousness add up—according to this theory—to a consciousness of sensations.

The two kinds of sensibility which Locke devised were appropriated by K a n t as well, and through him they became the common property of German psychologism. For since Kant distinguished two forms of sensibility, space and time, he was compelled to split sensibility itself into two faculties, namely into the outer sense, to which he assigned space, and the inner sense, to which he assigned time, as a priori form. Originally one thought that Kant intended to build his apparatus of cognition upon two faculties, upon the

[p. 92]

understanding and sensibility; now, however, it turns out that the apparatus must become more complicated.
