Sensibility itself must fall apart into two faculties, and it need hardly be said that from this circumstance precisely the same inconveniences arise as from the dissection of the faculty of cognition into understanding and sensibility. To the original dualism of the Kantian theory of knowledge there is added here a new one, the impressionist dualism, which demands the double impossibility of splitting sensibility into two components, and then of reuniting the two components again. It would have to be shown what share the outer sense, and what share the inner sense, has in cognition, and since both shares in turn could only be cognition, we should stand before two independent faculties of cognition which could never more be united with one another.

Kant himself incorporated the concept of the inner sense into his doctrine only with grave misgivings, and these misgivings come to clear expression in his follower B. G. E. S c h u l z e . “From the outer senses the i n n e r s e n s e , which is also called the h i g h e r , has been distinguished in more recent times. By this is understood the consciousness of everything that takes place within and belongs to the determinations of our I . . . But the consciousness of the inner was brought under the title ‘sense’ because we feel ourselves just as much constrained to cognize its objects as to sense the objects of the outer senses” (Psych. Anthropologie, pp. 114 f.) One sees that Schulze, feeling the weakness of the concept, defends it against possible attacks. He asks himself with what right the inner sense is called a sense. Has it, then, sensations? If this is affirmed, the question arises whether it has sensations of its own, or merely perceives the sensations of the outer sense. In the first case it is an

[p. 10]

independent sensibility on its own, and it becomes an absolute riddle how it communicates with the outer sensibility; in the second case, on the other hand, it is quite superfluous, for to what end should sensations of the outer sense be sensed twice?

When H e r b a r t and his school took a stand against the abuse that is practiced in psychology with the concept of the faculties of the soul, the “inner sense” too fell into disrepute. Admittedly only the faculty itself, or more correctly the mere word “inner sense,” for its achievements: inner experience, inner observation, and finally inner perception, enjoyed and enjoy the highest esteem, indeed a fundamental significance for the science of psychology. But what is the use of denying the faculty while acknowledging its activities? Thus the history of philosophy teaches us that the quarrel of the parties is very often directed merely against words. Certain words fall into discredit and are formally proscribed; but the error itself lives on under another name.

10. Physical and Psychical Phenomena.

The “inner sense” of L o c k e and K a n t was, as said, abandoned, and men began to speak merely of its achievements, of an outer and an inner observation; but when C o m t e attacked and ridiculed this distinction as well in energetic fashion, men withdrew to c h a n c e i n n e r perception, while renouncing the possibility of a s y s t e m a t i c inner observation. (Compare, e.g., Wu n d t ’s Psychology.) Nevertheless “inner perception” still counts today as the peculiar method on which the science of psychology rests, and without which it could not exist at all. It must, however, already

[p. 94]

strike one at first glance as strange that one is very faint-hearted also in regard to this “inner perception,” where one should surely suppose that an inner perception is also capable of perceiving itself, and so from the very beginning could have defended its cause victoriously. By its groping behavior, however, and by its retreat from systematic observation to merely occasional perception, it makes itself highly suspect.

The worst thing about this mystical inner perception, however, is not itself, but the circumstance that it urges the investigator to posit a class of its own of facts or phenomena, which are accessible solely to inner perception. For if there is an inner perception, there must also be a class of phenomena of its own assigned to it, to be well distinguished from that class of phenomena which is accessible to outer perception. To be sure, it is easy here too to import an ambiguity into the distinction, for one can assume, e.g., that it is the same phenomena that are perceived once outwardly and the other time inwardly. The phenomena would then have two sides: a conception which can in turn very easily be played over into the other, that there are after all two kinds of phenomena; for if a phenomenon can be regarded from two sides, then it furnishes two views, which may fairly also be regarded as two phenomena.

I wish, therefore, to illuminate first only that conception which with great decisiveness distinguishes two classes of phenomena: physical and psychical phenomena. As a chief representative of this tendency may be regarded the subtle-minded Franz B r e n t a n o , who in his “Empirical Psychology” deals very thoroughly with the method of this science, and who is so imbued with the significance

[p. 10]

of “inner perception” that he positively celebrates it as an infallible mode of perception, whereas he represents outer perception as one more or less deceptive. Accordingly he also draws a sharp cut through the whole world of phenomena, and wishes quite strictly to distinguish two classes of phenomena, of which the one is to be assigned to physics in the widest sense, the other, on the other hand, to psychology. He also gives us a firmly outlined instruction how we are to separate physical and psychical phenomena from one another, and thereby earns the merit of having frankly proclaimed the phenomenalist dualism which, despite all disguises, nevertheless prevails in modern psychology.

Brentano calls upon us to distinguish well the act of i m a g i n i n g from the i d e a itself. Hearing is not to be confounded with the heard tone, seeing not with the seen color, etc. Whoever makes this distinction will easily be able to understand what Brentano means by a psychical phenomenon as distinct from a physical one. The act of hearing, the act of seeing, the act of sensing (e.g., of warmth and cold) are psychical phenomena; but the act of judging, of remembering, of expecting, of inferring, of opining or doubting, etc. belong here too. No less are the acts in which we rejoice or are sad, fear or hope, hate or love, etc., etc., all of them psychical phenomena. Examples of physical phenomena, on the other hand, are the tone which we hear, the color which we see, the warmth which we sense, as well as the reproductions of them in our fantasy.

Every psychical phenomenon is—according to Brentano— characterized by what the scholastics of the Middle Ages have called the intentional inexistence of an object

[p. 96]

and what we moderns would call relation to a content or direction upon an immanent object. In the idea something is imagined, in the act of judging something is acknowledged or rejected, in the act of loving something is loved, etc. No physical phenomenon shows anything similar. Only hearing relates to a content, namely to the tone, but the tone does not in turn relate to a second content. The tone is a physical phenomenon, and as such the tone is the content of a psychical phenomenon, namely of hearing. H a v i n g contents belongs only to psychical phenomena, but not to physical phenomena, for these can only b e contents.

Can one content oneself with this distinction between physical and psychical phenomena? Can we perhaps allow a tone and the hearing of it to count as t w o phenomena, of which the one is physical, the other psychical? Do a color and the seeing of it perhaps make up t w o facts? The answer to these and similar questions should hardly be hard for anyone, for it is recognizable at first glance that a tone is a tone only insofar as it is heard, and hearing is a hearing only because tones are perceived through it. There is thus nowhere a tone without a hearing, and nowhere a hearing without a tone, so that the hearing and the tone make up merely one state of affairs. It is just the same with seeing and color, and in general with the act of sensing and with the content of it, that is, the sensation. We are able, to be sure, by our thinking to form two such concepts as hearing and tone, or seeing and color, or sensing and sensation, or act and content: but with this twofoldness of concepts no twofoldness of states of affairs or phenomena is yet given to us. If the tone is called a phenomenon, then the hearing of it can certainly not be called a

[p. 10]

phenomenon, for otherwise hearing itself would have to be a sounding, an inner sounding, and with the hearing of one tone we should have two tones at once in consciousness, namely an outer and an inner tone. Since now this inner tone, too, would have to be heard, but this hearing would in turn be an inner tone, we should have, instead of a twofold number of tones, at once a threefold number, and it is easy to see that one could stop no more at this threefold number than at any arbitrary finite number. In order that a tone might be heard, infinitely many tones would have to be heard, which would form an infinite series of ever greater inwardness. It is, in a manner, the curse of an error that it begets infinitely many errors, for it broods upon itself without end.

Incidentally, I should like to point here also to the highly interesting parallelism that obtains between the psychology of B r e n t a n o and the logic of B o l z a n o . While the latter wants to separate logic from psychology at any price, in order to obtain a pure logic, the former wants to divide psychology from physics at any price so as to be able to produce a pure psychology. In doing so the two thinkers use an identical method, for their separations rest on the conceptual distinction of act and content. Bolzano separates the content of a thought from the act of thinking and so produces on the one hand unthought contents, on the other contentless acts of thinking; Brentano, on the other hand, separates the act of sensing from the content of it, and produces in this way on the one hand unsensed contents of sensation, on the other contentless acts of sensation. Both thinkers nonetheless produce highly instructive theories, and furnish us classic examples of dualism in symbolic logic on the one side and in impressionist logic on the other.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                7

[p. 98]

Now our impressions can probably be split by many kinds of methods, and one need not divide the idea straight into an act and a content in order to obtain two kinds of phenomena, of which one class could be assigned to physics, the other to psychology. It is exactly the same with the splittings that one is accustomed to carry out upon cognition, and which can likewise be executed in manifold ways, as Kant and Bolzano show, of whom the one divides cognition into matter and form, the other into act and content. Like these rationalist or symbolic splittings, the impressionist isolations too always contain one and the same contradiction, as I have shown above in the case of Brentano’s scholasticism of sensation. It will not, however, be necessary to search for still other impressionist dissections, since it should suffice to point out the consequences which every twofold division of the phenomena into physical and psychical phenomena necessarily entails.

With such a twofold division of the phenomena philosophy returns quite imperceptibly to the dualism of D e s c a r t e s . For if there are two classes of phenomena separated from one another, one can quite confidently return to the doctrine of the two substances, the extended and the thinking substance. Indeed, phenomenalist dualism is by far more malignant than substantial dualism. For the latter can be overcome by laying bare the contradiction resting in it; but if there are two separate worlds of phenomena, which reveal themselves to two separate modes of perception, then such a doctrine appears with a claim to infallibility, because it rests on allegedly manifest facts. Phenomenalist dualism would be positively the empirical proof for the substantial dualism

[p. 10]

of Descartes. For if there are two worlds of phenomena separated from one another, then it would be mere obstinacy to refuse to lay two substances at the ground of them. Separate realms of existence demand separate fundamental principles, and a doctrine of two substances would be only the tersest manner of expression for the existence of two realms of phenomena.

Brentano presents this state of affairs in a truly ingenious manner. He raises the question whether psychology becomes the poorer by giving up altogether speaking of a “soul,” and arrives at the interesting result that little depends on the word and that it may be dropped without scruple. At the same time he admonishes the psychologists of the older school not to run headlong into their soul-substance, since one can get along quite well without it, and the problem of the immortality of the soul can be maintained even if one gives up the soul-substance. He seems to think: give me only a separate realm of psychical phenomena and an inner perception assigned to this realm, and then the gentlemen anti-metaphysicians may strike the word “soul,” or the soul-substance, from the philosophical dictionary at their pleasure. And in this respect he is quite right, for he sees through the so-called anti-metaphysicians, who are always harping on experience, but at bottom are the worst scholastics, because they are always concerned only with words they dislike. For if psychical phenomena can last seventy years in a man without a soul-substance lying at the ground of these phenomena, then it is nonetheless conceivable that those psychical phenomena—without the help of a soul-substance—could last as long as you please, and only a proof would be needed that psychical phenomena by virtue of their nature necessarily eternally

8*

[p. 100]

must endure. John Stuart M i l l seems to think very much the same (Examination of Sir W. Hamilton’s Philosophy, ch. XII): “As to immortality, it is precisely as easy to conceive that a succession of feelings, a thread of consciousness, may be prolonged to eternity, as that a spiritual substance for ever continues to exist: and any evidence which would prove the one, will prove the other.”

One ought not, I believe, to rail against words in philosophy, for all words without exception are equally excellent or equally worthless, according as the sentences which we form with their help are true or false. At bottom we are all metaphysicians, with the sole difference that some are vexed at this and others are not. We all want to cognize the abiding in the transitory, and it is of no use whatever to grieve over the fact that we can never get along with the fleeting without placing at its foundation something imperishable. The more one struggles against this, the more one confuses oneself. The question is not whether there is an Eternal on which everything fleeting is grounded, for if we know anything at all with certainty, it is precisely this; the question is only how we are to use the consciousness of the unity of the world of phenomena so as to be able to do justice to the facts and to their lawful connection. For if our thinking comes into conflict with the facts and their laws, then we flatter ourselves quite in vain about our metaphysics. Even in the question we discussed above, the only issue is whether there really are two such classes of phenomena as B r e n t a n o assumes; and I believe I have shown that there cannot be two such classes.

[p. 11]

11. Outer and Inner Perception.

Modern psychologism rests on an oppositional distinction between outer and inner perception, for it is this distinction first of all that misleads the investigators into speaking either of two separate worlds of phenomena or of an outer and an inner side of one and the same world of phenomena. We must therefore subject the so-called inner perception to a critical examination, in order to be able to press through to a judgment on modern psychologism at all. For this purpose it seems to me most fitting to raise such questions as could be decided only on the basis of an “inner perception”; if it then turns out that this celebrated inner perception is wholly incapable of perceiving anything just where one could most wish or demand that it do its duty, then we shall have gained a definite foothold for judging it.

With a view to this purpose I choose a question which is of far-reaching significance and plays a great part in the history of philosophy, and above all of the theory of knowledge, and which is at the same time suited to throw a bright light on modern impressionist logic. We have mentioned that L o c k e , in the course of his inquiries concerning the understanding, came to see that cognition cannot be examined if one stops merely at ideas and their composition, but that it is also necessary to take account of the symbols (words) which we employ for the presentation of our judgments. With this insight Locke made a turn which led him over from impressionist logic to symbolic logic, with the consequence that he now treated no longer merely of single ideas, but of general concepts (abstract ideas), as he

[p. 102]

had to. On this occasion the purely impressionist basic trait of his meditating emerges with a plastic clarity such as was possible only with the venerable candor of a Locke.

According to his view, namely, we could form a g e n e r a l idea, say of a triangle, only by forming an idea of such a triangle as would embrace all possible triangles, which would thus be right-angled as well as obtuse-angled and acute-angled, and no less at the same time equilateral, scalene, and isosceles, yet must at the same time exhibit none of these particular features, since otherwise it would not be a general idea. Curiously enough, Locke himself admits that by the general idea of a triangle he understands a nonsense of this kind. Here are his own words: “For example, does it not require some pains and skill to form the general idea of a triangle, (which is yet none of the most abstract, comprehensive, and difficult,) for it must be neither oblique nor rectangle, neither equilateral, equicrural, nor scalenon; but all and none of these at once” (Essay, Book IV, ch. 7, § 9). Locke thinks, too, that this skill is lacking in animals, and that our superiority over them rests on the ability to form the abstract ideas described above.

B e r k e l e y remarks upon this that he agrees entirely with this view, for it is certain that animals do not possess the ability to form the abstract ideas just described; the only fear is that men, too, could not accomplish such a thing; at most, he says, they might be found among learned men. Passing over the Lockean theory of abstract ideas thus with a gentle smile, he himself tries

[p. 11]

to furnish a theory of them, or more correctly to prove their non-existence. He draws our attention to the fact that if we want to establish general sentences, say about a triangle, we have no need at all to form such a self-contradictory idea of a universal triangle as Locke demands; it suffices completely that we assume a quite definite single triangle and regard it as the representative (as the symbol) of all possible triangles. Proceeding in this way, we need not at all take account, in the proof of any geometrical theorem, of how long or how large the sides and angles may be, whereby the theorem obtained would then present itself of its own accord as generally valid. He thus arrives at the result: “an idea which, considered in itself, is particular, becomes general by being made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort” (Principles, Introduction, § 12).

It hardly needs saying that Berkeley’s ingenious reflections, too, do not solve the problem with which we are here concerned. A single idea is simply a single idea, and it is not at all its merit that it can be used as a symbol for all single ideas of its kind, but it is the merit of the human faculty of cognition that it can accomplish something so wonderful. It is this accomplishment that is at issue when abstract ideas are spoken of, and this accomplishment would have to be explained, not simply ascertained, as Berkeley does. But when he says that the single idea “becomes general” through the use to which it is put, he returns to the Lockean contradiction, for a single idea is simply a single idea and cannot “become” a general one. When he finally denies the existence of abstract ideas, it also appears,

[p. 104]

as if he would deny to man the capacity for forming concepts.

Here, however, we do not wish to quarrel with Berkeley, but merely to call upon “inner perception”—since it possesses the capacity to perceive something in the interior of our mind—to decide whether “concepts” are really to be found there, or whether we are to deny with Berkeley that concepts exist. Inner perception cannot lack occasions for perceiving such concepts, for as often as we use symbols in everyday conversation, these symbols are supposed to be signs of concepts that take place in our mind. Inner perception could bring us a wholly unfailing conviction on this point; but has any human being ever gained, by means of a “perception,” insights into the activity of the cognizing mind?

One could raise many more similar and perhaps more interesting questions concerning the structure of our judgments and the constitution of our acts of will, which form the subject of endless controversies among philosophers, and one could convince oneself in a thousand and again a thousand cases that inner perception remains mute where one would need it most. But I believe that such an accumulation of examples is unnecessary, because one can see from the foregoing what an inner perception under no circumstances is able to accomplish. If, then, modern psychology is to be able to rest precisely on the capacity of an inner perception, it is in its highest interest to establish exactly what one may not demand of inner perception, and so to delimit its domain that one need not at every moment fear to hear oracular pronouncements as to what is really going on in the cognizing mind.

If, however, I try to restrict inner perception to a domain that rightfully belongs to it,

[p. 11]

then I must admit to myself that in this endeavor I find myself again and again in the dilemma of having either to deny it altogether, or else to designate as inner every perception of which a human being is capable at all. The perception of a color seems to me just as inner as the perception of a memory of it, or the perception of a pain, of an emotion, etc. For the perception which I have, for example, of the sunset glow is just as much my exclusive personal property as the pleasure which accompanies this sight, or the thoughts which may connect themselves with it. Just as the feelings, moods, and memories of a particular person can never be appropriated by another person, exactly so it is with the impressions of an individual; for insofar as an impression a belongs to a particular person A, the same impression a can never by any means belong to another person B. If by the innerness of a perception one understands its exclusive personal possession, then every perception is inner.

But it is hard to understand why expressions such as o u t e r or i n n e r perception are not avoided altogether, since both are metaphors borrowed from the intuition of space, and very easily mislead our thinking, in that they make us believe that inner perception perhaps relates to what goes on in the interior of our body. If one presented inner perception, as is fitting, as personal perception, one would also see at once that there can be no impersonal perception, and accordingly that an opposition between outer and inner perception cannot be defended with good means.

The origin of such distinctions as outer and inner perception I explain to myself in the following way.

[p. 106]

It belongs to the character of the new age that it is thirsty everywhere for observation, perception, etc., even where such a thirst must remain forever unquenchable. If, for example, someone has an insight into the truth of the Pythagorean theorem, his insight is something invisible, and there is no means of beholding it with armed or unarmed eyes. Modern logicians, however, were inclined to make concessions to the gaping crowd, and to give the semblance that there is a method of satisfying the eyes where they must remain unsatisfied. Out of dread of metaphysics, they let themselves be tempted into making such a metaphysical fiction as the opposition of outer and inner perception is. We do violence to the facts in order to avoid the semblance of being metaphysicians. One is accustomed to call someone a metaphysician who makes fictions where it would be a matter of observing facts, but more recent philosophy shows us that one may fall into fiction-making out of an anti-metaphysical tendency just as well as out of a metaphysical intention. I believe, therefore, that the catchwords “metaphysician” and “anti-metaphysician” should be dropped, for in the end it is never a question of party colors, but of the progress of our knowledge.

12. Psychophysical Parallelism.

There is a self-knowledge as opposed to a knowledge of objects, but there is no inner perception as opposed to an outer one, because the cognizing mind is unable to perceive itself and its cognition as it perceives, for example, any phenomena it pleases in space and in time. Our modern impressionist thinking, however, is characterized by the fact that it would like at any price to perceive even the imperceptible, and in

[p. 12]

this passionate urge it invents phantasms upon phantasms of the imperceptible for so long that it makes itself believe that there are two more or less independent worlds of phenomena, which open themselves to two kinds of perception. This finds a singular expression in the modern theory of psychophysical parallelism. Two chains of phenomena are supposed to run side by side, each is supposed to possess its own causality, so that one is accustomed to speak not merely of a physical but also of a psychical causality. For good measure one even contemplates a third kind, a psychophysical causality, which is supposed to connect the two aforementioned causalities with each other; but since one nevertheless sees that this third causality would have to dominate the two former ones and rob them completely of their significance, one prefers to stop at two causalities, and replaces the third by a mere “corresponding,” by a mere “correspondence,” according to which phenomena of the psychical series would, in general, have a vis-à-vis in the physical series. For words are never lacking when one is to express the impossible. The two chains of phenomena are supposed to be connected with each other and at the same time not connected; is there a more fitting expression for such a relation than “corresponding” or “correspondence” or “parallelism”?

Curiously enough, the phenomena of both series are described with such scientific precision that one feels a kind of flickering before the eyes, and begins to feel over the things in one’s own surroundings to see whether they might not all be present in two copies. It must be taken into consideration, however, that the parallelism of the two chains of phenomena is not supposed to be a complete one, since in the physical series there is a great deal for which the psychical series possesses no vis-à-vis, and conversely many members of the psychical series

[p. 108]

can find no partner in the physical. At times, therefore, this parallelism is also presented as if the two series actually “crossed” each other, so that parallel relations could take place only at the points of crossing. Often, however, one also gains the impression that the two dancing columns of phenomena melt together into a single column, so that it becomes questionable whether from the very beginning one did not have before one’s eyes merely a single chain of phenomena and merely regarded it from two sides, since psychophysical parallelism also lays great weight on the “double-sidedness” of psychical phenomena.

Modern philosophy once had to do not with two chains of phenomena but with two substances, an extended and a thinking substance, and then arose the problem of how these two substances are able to act upon each other, since they are fundamentally different in essence. As is well known, occasionalism taught an occasional divine assistance in every reciprocal action of the two substances upon each other, while S p i n o z a replaced the duality of substances by their singleness with the addition of two attributes, and finally L e i b n i z thought to improve the occasionalist theory by his doctrine of pre-established harmony. These philosophical speculations on the grand scale moved in a non-sensible domain, for the two substances were, after all, not accessible to sensible perception. But our impressionist age has rendered them sensible and translated them into the phenomenal, so that we now have two worlds of parallel facts, which possess their own causality. What was formerly speculation has now become perception, and one speaks with the same conviction of two realms of phenomena as one once spoke of two substances. Which of these two kinds of metaphysics is now the

[p. 12]

worse: that which treated in a rationalistic fashion of two substances, or this which speaks in an empiricist fashion of two worlds of phenomena?

Since, now, in more recent times very eminent investigators and representatives of an exact scientific method adhere to the opposition of inner and outer perception, and accordingly also to the parallelism of two chains of phenomena, we must subject the opposition of physical and psychical processes to a still closer consideration, in order to convince ourselves fully that no such opposition can be spoken of. Here we must once more go back to K a n t , who exerts a far greater influence on modern psychologism, too, than some psychologists would care to admit. L o c k e would never have been able, through his doctrine of outer and inner sense, to call forth so profound a movement as modern psychologism is, had not Kant made that distinction his own and reworked it in his mystical fashion. Only when Kant assigned space as form to outer sense, and time, on the other hand, as form to inner sense, was the foundation for psychologism properly laid. To be sure, it was not at all Kant’s intention to strengthen psychologism in any way; at bottom he had, after all, no very high opinion of the scientific significance of psychology, indeed the innermost tendency of his doctrine of forms was directed precisely against the psychologism, or impressionism, of the English theory of knowledge; but it very soon turned out that German logic, in contrast to the Kantian aspiration, drifted into the wake of impressionism. So long as the idealists Fichte, Schelling, and Hegel dominated German thinking, this did not, to be sure, come through distinctly, although psychologism very soon after the appearance of the Critique of Reason entered powerfully into the course of development of the

[p. 110]

German philosophy, and after the fall of the Hegelian panlogism ever more seized sole dominion over minds.

One could speak of two parallel chains of phenomena with ever-growing confidence only when one had grown accustomed to conceive space as the form of outer sense and time as the form of inner sense; K a n t gives us the following explanation of inner sense: “Inner sense, by means of which the mind intuits itself or its inner state, yields, to be sure, no intuition of the soul itself as an object; yet it is nonetheless a determinate form under which alone the intuition of its inner state is possible, so that everything that belongs to the inner determinations is represented in relations of time” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 37). Observe here how in this explanation the “mind” is opposed to the “soul”; for while the latter is unable to intuit itself, the “mind” has the capacity to intuit itself and its inner states, and indeed this takes place in relations of time. Through the indeterminate distinction between soul and mind, an intuitive psyche is here set over against the unintuitive spirit, and free way is made for psychological dualism. It can now be a question of physical and psychical processes or phenomena, and all the more because the psychical processes are supposed to be distinguished by the fact that they take place not in space but merely in time. On this rests the psychological actualism which comes to light, for example, in Wu n d t . The sense of this actualism is at bottom none other than that psychical events are supposed to take place only in time.

From the Kantian view that space is the form of outer, time on the other hand the form of inner sense, it follows directly that the physical phenomena

[p. 12]

can take place merely in space, the psychical merely in time. That this is an impossible way of separating two worlds of phenomena one recognizes at once, since the physical phenomena, too, have a temporal character: Kant therefore had to endeavor to remove this impossibility and to show that, although space is the form of outer sense and time the form of inner sense, the physical phenomena nevertheless do not merely dwell in space, but can also enter into the temporal stream. This was the great difficulty which he had to overcome in passing from the theory of space to the theory of time: a difficulty which is usually not sufficiently illuminated, in its whole magnitude, by the expounders of the Kantian doctrine.

Kant would gladly have held fast to the view that the outer world of phenomena is properly a merely spatial one; at least he lets this inclination show through in some of his utterances, for example in the following: “Time cannot be intuited externally, any more than space can be intuited as something in us.” In the sense of this utterance time would not be there for outer sense, that is, for outer intuition; conversely, there would be no space for inner sense, that is, inner intuition. Had Kant wished to be thoroughly consistent, he would have had to hold fast to this view too: to be sure, he would then have been compelled to assume two worlds wholly shut off from each other, standing outside any connection with one another. The physical world of outer sense would then be found merely in space, and nothing could happen in it, while the psychical world of inner intuition would be pure actuality, pure temporal flight. To these two hermetically sealed worlds there would correspond in us two I’s, of which the one, knowing nothing of the other, would possess its own world. This two-world system is, strictly speaking, a necessary consequence of the positing

[p. 112]

of two forms, such as space and time are supposed to be; for that independent secret principle which produces in us the intuition of time can and may not concern itself with the other principle, which conjures up the intuition of space, because, if the one principle wished to take notice of the other, it would also have to cease to be that particular principle which, according to its destination, it must be, as I have already emphasized more than once. For the doctrine of psychophysical parallelism too it would be most fitting if the physical phenomena nested merely in space and the psychical merely in time, for then there would be no doubt that one had to do with two classes of phenomena. To be sure, however, an opposition between a spatial world on the one hand and a temporal world on the other cannot be maintained, and so Kant too had to take care not to exclude the physical world from the form of time. He solves this difficult task in the following sentence: “Time is the formal condition a priori of all appearances whatsoever. Space, as the pure form of all outer intuition, is limited as a condition a priori merely to outer appearances. On the other hand, because all representations, whether they have outer things as their object or not, nevertheless belong in themselves, as determinations of the mind, to the inner state, but this inner state stands under the formal condition of inner intuition, and hence of time, time is a condition a priori of all appearance whatsoever, and indeed the immediate condition of the inner (of our soul) and precisely thereby mediately also of the outer appearances” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 50).

From this highly peculiar solution it now follows first of all that the form of time properly, that is, i m m e d i a t e l y , belongs merely to inner sense, but that it can pass over, on loan, that is, m e d i a t e l y , also to the phenomena of the

[p. 12]

outer sense. Now it hardly needs to be spelled out that no loan transactions can take place between outer and inner sense, and that the form of time must belong to the outer phenomena not merely in a borrowed way but also in an immediate way. For if the outer phenomena had first to become inner phenomena in order to share in the form of time, then they have precisely ceased to be outer phenomena, and one may henceforth conceive them only as inner phenomena, in which case they may indisputably participate in the form of time, but so that they can now no longer participate in the form of space. The ambiguous game that is played with outer and inner phenomena now emerges quite clearly. At one moment there is talk of exclusively inner phenomena, which belong merely to inner sense and are therefore purely temporal; at another, however, of such inner phenomena as have arisen in some enigmatic way out of outer phenomena, and can therefore share both in the form of space and in the form of time. But if it were possible for outer phenomena, becoming inner phenomena, to enter into the form of time, they would also have to bring their form of space along with them into the form of time! They surely cannot throw off their form of space like a worn-out garment in order to put on merely the garb of temporality; otherwise they would have to cease to be outer spatial phenomena. In other words: if it is possible for the outer phenomena, becoming inner states, to receive the temporal garb on loan, then it is also necessary that they carry their spatial garb into inner sense, and consequently all inner phenomena must now also participate in the form of space. If loan transactions can once take place between inner and outer sense, then such borrowing cannot remain one-sided. If inner sense lends the

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.              8

[p. 114]

time-form to outer sense, then conversely outer sense must also cede the form of space to inner sense. All phenomena must then be both spatial and temporal.

One can escape this fatal necessity only by positing not merely one stream of time, but two streams of time. Yes, if one were to assume that there is an outer and an inner stream of time, then it would be equally understandable how there can be two series of phenomena. The outer stream of time would be united in the most intimate way with space, so that the outer phenomena could be both spatial and temporal; the inner stream of time, on the other hand, would be cut off both from the outer stream of time and from space, whereby it would be made possible for the inner phenomena to flow along merely in time, without having to mix with the outer phenomena and share in their spatiality. Psychophysical parallelism must therefore, if it does not wish to be a theory fishing in troubled waters, quite openly proclaim a doctrine of two parallel streams of time, and it may do so without further scruples, for if it is possible to imagine that there are two parallel columns of phenomena, then one can just as well imagine that there are two streams of time, of which the one carries the physical, the other the psychical phenomena on its back.

From what has been said it may perhaps have become clear that it is the enigmatic relation of space and time that misleads people into assuming two parallel worlds of phenomena. Once the thinkers wondered how a non-sensible soul could stand in reciprocal action with an extended world of bodies; today a temporal soul has taken the place of the non-sensible soul, and one no longer asks after the reciprocal action of a non-sensible and a sensible principle, but

[p. 13]

after the connection of two parallel-running worlds of phenomena, of which the one is temporal, the other spatio-temporal. Rationalism had two metaphysical principles, empiricism has two intuitive worlds. In which of the two views is more fiction contained?

In order to be able to oppose psychological dualism, I shall discuss in the next chapter the question of the relation of space and time, and then characterize in a few strokes that doctrine of the unity of space and time which I intend to set forth more fully in the positive part of this work. I believe that all the questions of modern philosophical thinking converge in the great riddle of the relation of space and time.

13. The Relation of Space and Time.

The criticism which we brought against the Kantian concept of form in general retains its validity not only for the form of space, but also for the form of time. Just as it is untenable to regard space, whether in a proper or in a metaphorical sense, as a kind of vessel in which the bodies or our sensations are found, so it is equally impossible to represent time as a real or metaphorical vessel for events or changes. Between space and the world of bodies there obtains no such relation as that between a vessel and its content, for the latter is a relation of impenetrability, whereas the former can be designated as a relation of total reciprocal interpenetration. Accordingly we have exactly the same right to conceive the world of bodies as a vessel for space as for the converse view, in which space is the vessel and the world of bodies the

8*

[p. 116]

content. It is open to us to invert the Kantian manner of speaking and to designate the world of bodies as form, space as content, though with the remark that this phrase too, in its one-sidedness, is just as false as the inverted one, and that we therefore do best not to apply the pseudo-concepts of form and content at all. Exactly the same holds of time and its content, events. As space and body, so too time and what happens in it interpenetrate one another. Time cannot be poured out of the events, nor can the events be poured out of time: their connection is an inseparable one, their reciprocal interpenetration is so complete that we have no right to apply to them the relation of impenetrability that holds between form and content.

It is further to be noted that the expression “form” plays just as ambiguous a part in Kant’s doctrine of time as in his doctrine of space. The time that elapses in the actual events would have to be distinguished from the form as it lies ready in inner sense to receive events into itself. At one time there is talk of the time which we can measure by the clock, at another of the secret power which is supposed to be hidden in our inner sense, and which makes it possible for us to cognize a temporal course at all. Or, to render this distinction sensible: the form of time that is drawn over the events must not be confused with the form of time that has not yet approached the events and is merely the precondition of our being able to apprehend events as temporal processes at all. In the one sense time is the measurable duration in events, in the other sense time is a qualitas occulta of inner sense or of the soul, which m a k e s us experience changes as temporal. The one meaning may no more be confused

[p. 13]

with the other than one confuses the condition with the conditioned, the cause with the effect, the faculty with its performance, the law with the phenomenon in which it manifests itself. From this it is evident, however, that there can be no “pure intuition” of time any more than of space. For the time that elapses in the events is inseparably bound up with these events, which are sensible phenomena, and hence is never free from the sensations of which those events consist. But the time which is thought as the precondition of temporal intuiting is necessarily unintuitive; it is a mental thing in itself. Incidentally, Kant in his doctrine of time does not insist at all upon pure intuition of time; as indeed it is altogether most awkward to think time as a “form.” For this expression has a fascinating force only in the doctrine of space, chiefly because space is really perceived as form in bodies, and because one does not make clear to oneself that the “form,” insofar as it is supposed to be the condition a priori for the perception of shapes, is itself no shape, no form, but precisely a “condition,” a “law of function,” in short, something wholly unintuitive and also wholly incognizable, which we ascribe by invention to our sensibility. If the readers of the Transcendental Aesthetic kept this before their eyes, the deceptive semblance that something had been gained with the word “form,” as the condition of spatial intuition, would have to vanish completely. But because one does not make clear to oneself the ambiguity of “form,” the charm of the Kantian doctrine of forms evaporates only at the chapter on time, and to a still higher degree at the chapter on the categories. Transcendental logic as a whole remains without attraction for the greater number of readers. But even the doctrine of time already has no real charm any longer, and is even by adherents

[p. 118]

of Kant frequently sharply censured or even abandoned. This fact furnishes an interesting proof of how minds succumb to the power of metaphor. The metaphor “form” hypnotizes the reader so long as space is the subject, but it fades in the doctrine of time and becomes in transcendental logic a disquieting specter. Kant bears not the slightest blame for this, but only the reader, who does not already see through the mysticism of the word “form” in the doctrine of space.

What we said of the form of space, namely that it can contribute nothing to our cognition, holds also of the form of time. As the form in itself of outer sense is incognizable, so too is the form in itself of inner sense. We burden our thinking merely with superfluous incognizables if we assume them. What is the use to us of assuming a condition a priori for intuitive space, when that condition is unintuitive and incognizable, and so can give us not the slightest information about the nature of space? The same holds for the condition a priori of our intuition of time; it too is incognizable and betrays nothing about the nature of time. At most we can ascribe by invention to that condition a priori all that we already know of time in any case. If, for example, someone knows that time is one-dimensional, he can say that the form a priori of our inner sense makes us think of happening as running its course in one dimension. But if time had a dozen dimensions, we might with the same right seek the ground for this too in the form a priori of inner sense. If we represent the stream of time by a straight line, we can say that the form a priori compels us to it; but if the human race were to conceive time in the image of a curve, then the form a priori would have to furnish the ground of explanation for this fact as well. If time were in

[p. 13]

to run back upon itself, that is, if a past moment (as the same moment) could return once more, we should find it convenient to say: to be sure, the form a priori of our inner sense makes it necessary that a past moment should return in its selfsameness. And so on.

Let one try for once to make the simplest facts of our sense-activity comprehensible by the Kantian doctrine of forms. For example, everyone admits that hearing is an outer sense; if now the form of our outer sense is supposed to be space, how does it come about that we learn to refer tones to regions in space only in a quite uncertain way? Why does the form of outer sense not do its duty? Why does it let us down so easily when it is a matter of determining whether a tone comes from above or below, from right or left? Why does it not arrange tones as it does patches of color, namely so that they become shapes; in other words, why are masses of tone not pictures just as masses of color are? If the form of outer sense did its duty, it would shape all the sensations of the outer senses prettily into spatial pictures, as it does with the sensations of color. We should then have not only pictures of color, but also pictures of tone, pictures of warmth, pictures of taste and of smell. The art of music would be, in the strictest sense of the word, a painting, and there would then be an art of taste, an art of smell, indeed even an art of warmth, all of which would compete with painting, for they would all produce pictures, if not by means of colors, then by tastes, smells, and sensations of warmth. All this would really have to take place if space were the form of our outer sense. I do not wish, however, to pursue these questions further, for it is all too obvious that the Kantian doctrine of forms is wholly impotent in the face of all the facts of our life of the senses. It has, after all, at bottom precious little

[p. 120]

to do with this life of the senses; for it is a mere speculation upon the possibility of our mathematical cognition.

It would, however, have been quite in keeping with this speculation to subject the relation of the two forms to one another to a special examination. Natural science, after all, is always interested in relations between space and time. Now since Kant was occupied with the great question how “pure natural science” is possible, nothing was more obvious than to ask oneself how the form a priori of outer sense is related to that of inner sense. If one pictures to oneself quite vividly that two forms a priori lie hidden in our mind, then nothing can arouse our curiosity so much as the nature of the relation between the two forms. The question of this relation ought not to have been avoided, if only because this relation itself must have an a p r i o r i character (in the Kantian sense). For since both the form of space and the form of time lie ready in our mind before all experience, their relation to one another, too, must be fixed already before all experience. Here, then, the task was to discover a law which binds two forms a priori together, and thus to furnish the proof that the transcendental method does not consist merely in the invention of metaphors, but also lays down laws between the forms, which it imputes to our mind. Nothing would accord more with the most natural expectations than that the transcendental aesthetic, after its two chapters on space and time, should contain a third chapter on the relation a priori of the two forms a priori. L o c k e , after all, had already given the impulse to this procedure, in that he let the 13th and 14th chapters of his chief work (Volume I), which deal with

[p. 13]

space and time, be followed by a 15th chapter besides: “Of Duration and Expansion considered together.”

Now one tries in vain, however, to find out anything a priori about the relation of the two forms a priori. Consider the following two sentences: Space is a form a priori of sensibility. Time is a form a priori of sensibility. and try now to infer anything from these two sentences by deduction. I scarcely believe that one can arrive at anything other than the vague assertion that there must surely be some relation a priori between these two forms; but in what this relation consists, one will hardly be able to read out of the two sentences above. And why not?

The forms a priori that rest in our mind are, after all, nothing intuitive or cognizable; they are assumptions about two secret powers of the mind. How, then, is anything to be asserted about the relation of these two incognizable powers of the mind? Indeed, if a mathematician or philosopher had already established a law as to how the i n t u i t i v e space and the i n t u i t i v e time are related to one another, then the Kantian doctrine could confirm this law, for it could proclaim that that law must hold because this is presupposed by the relation a priori between the two forms a priori. But since no laws whatever concerning the connection of space and time have been established, Kant also neglects to concern himself with the relation of his two forms. Herewith, however, it is also proved that the doctrine of the forms a priori could only be good for giving a transcendental consecration to sentences that have been found elsewhere, for adorning them with a kind of otherworldly halo. I must now repeat that even this service could be of significance, if only the facts of our

[p. 122]

life of the senses could be brought into agreement with the assumption of those secret qualities of the mind.

To return to the question of the connection between space and time, let it be remarked that L o c k e already recognized, or at least divined, its great importance. He declares that the comparative consideration of the ideas of space and time might throw a new light on the mystical nature of both ideas; indeed one finds in him a remark on the relation of space and time which must be called truly profound: “Expansion and duration do mutually embrace and comprehend each other; every part of space being in every part of duration, and every part of duration in every part of expansion. Such a combination of two distinct ideas is, I suppose, scarce to be found in all that great variety we do or can conceive, and may afford matter to further speculation.” (Essay, Book II, ch. 15, § 12, closing remark.)

Without having known this highly interesting remark of Locke’s, I had already attempted years ago, in a Hungarian work, to put forward a doctrine of the unity of space and time as a necessary logical requirement of every conception of the world. If S p i n o z a allows only one substance to hold as the foundation of every change of phenomena, then only the first step toward the establishment of a unified conception of the world has thereby been made. But with this doctrine of the one substance one finds oneself in great embarrassment in the face of the facts, for the phenomena show a double order of space and time, which misleads thinkers into assuming either two substances or even two worlds of phenomena running parallel to one another. Therefore, in the next chapter, in a manner accessible even to the layman, I shall explain

[p. 14]

how one can accustom oneself not to tear the concepts of space and time apart, but to bring them into connection with each other. The more detailed working-out of this thought, however, follows only in the second part of this work.

14. New Theory of Space and Time.

Because space and time are two concepts which are expressed in two words, common understanding has grown accustomed to thinking of them as two independent entities. If, however, we remain mindful of the results of our preceding considerations, we shall draw from them the incentive always to bring our concepts of space and of time into connection with each other, so that in their separateness they may not wither and waste away, but may preserve their fitness to convey to us a correct conception of reality. In other words, we shall endeavor to make the belonging-together of space and time as clear as possible to ourselves, that is, to arrive at a unified conception of them. I have already attempted to furnish such a doctrine of the unity of space and time in my work Neue Theorie des Raumes und der Zeit [New Theory of Space and Time], but I wish to set forth the fundamental ideas of that work once more — since it was intended more for mathematicians — and this time in a generally intelligible way.

How do we go about bringing our concepts of space and time into a living connection with each other? Quite simply, by accustoming ourselves in our imagination to think of space not as a s t a n d i n g space, but as a space that is r e n e w e d at every moment, or that, so to speak, gives birth to itself anew at every moment.*)

*) This is, admittedly, a seemingly fantastic assumption, but it changes nothing whatever in the facts themselves, and by means of it one merely preserves thinking from a host of rusted-in contradictions.

[p. 124]

If, then, we think of space in such a way that it rejuvenates itself at every moment, in order to stand there as a newborn space, we have set it in a natural connection with time, so that we may be sure never to forget time when our thinking is occupied with space. For the ever-renewing space already comprehends time within itself, since its renewal takes place, after all, in time. Indeed, we have thereby also brought the concept of happening into connection with the concepts of space and time. For it may surely be described as a happening when we think of space as renewing itself without cease.

One can, with closed eyes, form a phantasm of space, and likewise a phantasm of time. What I intend can now also be explained thus: that henceforth one should not construct two fantasy-images, of space and of time, but should combine the two images with one another and work out for oneself a unified space-time image. In my work cited above I have called this unified space-time image “f l o w i n g s p a c e ,” in contrast to the image of “standing” space to which one has hitherto been accustomed. Whether the metaphor “flowing space” is well chosen or not, I leave to the taste of my readers; for my part I consider this expression acceptable because the added word “flowing” indicates that we have here united our phantasm of time (which is usually the image of a stream) with the phantasm of space. The expression has, however, in any case one drawback: it tempts one, namely, to think of space as flowing on along a stream, that is, as moving on in a straight line in its entirety. It hardly needs to be said, however, that I am far from thinking of such a space moving on, since in our fantasy we have at our disposal no fourth

[p. 14]

dimension, along which the three-dimensional space could move on. Whoever, on account of this circumstance, hesitates to make friends with the name “flowing space” might perhaps more easily grow accustomed to the expression “d y n a m i c s p a c e ,” in contrast to the s t a t i c s p a c e whose phantasm has hitherto dominated the thinking of men. By dynamic space, then, I understand nothing other than space insofar as one represents it to oneself as continually renewing itself. Whoever lacks such a phantasm may imagine static space in the image of a soap bubble; he will then obtain dynamic space by thinking of the soap bubble as bursting, but at the same instant already replaced by an exactly similar soap bubble, which in turn suffers the same fate as the first, and so on without end.

What use it can have to form such a combined phantasm of space and time is, to be sure, not immediately evident; all that I can say about it in advance is just this, that this seeming toying can become a means of freeing our thoughts about the actual world of phenomena from many a most painful inner contradiction. We have seen, after all, how the circumstance that we have two such concepts, or words, as space and time led to the assumption of two worlds of phenomena, a physical and a psychical, which, consistently pursued, necessarily yields two worlds standing out of connection with one another. As the substantial dualism of D e s c a r t e s made it incomprehensible how the extended substance could stand in connection with the thinking substance, so the phenomenal dualism of space and time creates a new impossibility, namely the impossibility that the spatial should be at once temporal and the temporal at once spatial.

[p. 126]

Wherever such pairs of concepts as soul and body, or time and space, make their appearance, we may be prepared in advance for the fact that the duality of the concepts or words which we use will give rise to endless confusions and contradictions, so that a good part of the business of philosophy consists in healing those wounds of our thinking which it inflicts upon itself through the use of pairs of concepts. Since these are positively mortal wounds for the understanding, we see philosophy (at least the genuine kind) constantly occupied with removing the rift which, through an uncounted multitude of pairs of concepts (dualisms), threatens to dissolve all our knowledge completely. One of the most dangerous dualisms, however, is the dualism between space and time, for it brings it about that natural philosophy (physics and chemistry) fairly bristles, in all its hypotheses and theories, with inner contradictions. Thus I should like to point here only in passing to the dualism of matter and force, which makes our entire mechanical conception of nature uncertain and self-contradictory. We commonly think of matter as a “dead mass,” of force, on the other hand, as that which acts, chiefly because our thinking clings not to the facts but to the words. Because we have two such words as “matter” and “force,” we accustom ourselves to tearing the phantasm which we connect with the one word away from the phantasm which accompanies the other word. Thus we systematically kill our fantasy, our living power of intuition, by tearing it into as many pieces as the dictionary places at our disposal in words or combinations of words. Our concepts or words become enemies of our intuitive conception of things, just as, conversely, our intuitions fall into an eternal war with our concepts, which comes to distinct expression in the deadly enmity between the parties of sensualism and rationalism. If, however, we

[p. 14]

accustom ourselves to setting in one, in our fantasy, what the pairs of concepts have separated, we gradually arrive at overcoming the opposition of sensualism and rationalism. So it has also been attempted — to return to the example above — to set the phantasms of matter and force in one, and to establish a so-called dynamic conception of matter; yet this could never succeed to any sufficient degree: in my view, because the dynamic conception of matter was not brought into connection with a dynamic conception of space. If I am not mistaken, the doctrine of space and time is of fundamental importance for the whole of natural philosophy; for that very reason it seems to me of some importance to pave the way for a doctrine of the unity of space and time.

Here, however, the doctrine of space and time interests us as the foundation of a theory of intuition. We want to construct a concept of intuition in general that is free from contradiction, whereby I must remark that I prefer to use the expression “intuition” instead of the term “appearance,” because the former reminds us that appearances cannot take place without appearing to someone. But one cannot form a concept of “intuition” free from contradiction if one stops at the doctrine of static space; as I shall show at once by an example.

I now have the intuition of a red ball lying here on the table. If I close my eyes, the intuition vanishes; if I open my eyes, I again have an intuition of the same red ball. But is the second intuition that I have of the ball the s a m e as the first intuition? Does the first intuition perhaps return? Certainly not. The second intuition

[p. 128]

is, however much it may resemble the first, a d i f f e r e n t one, from which it follows that I must conceive my intuition of the red ball as one continually repeating itself in time, as I shall establish at length in Part II. We can do justice to this continual repetition of our intuition only through the dynamic conception of space indicated above. For in thinking of space as renewing itself without cease, we have already thought all intuitions (or appearances) in space as renewing themselves without cease.

The assumption of a static space, on the other hand, entangles our thinking in countless contradictions, of which I wish to put together a few that are the most readily apparent, leaving it to the reader to add as many others as he pleases, which he must necessarily find if he closely surveys the whole field of his thinking insofar as it is connected with the concepts of space and time.

1. Whoever conceives space as standing separates it from

time, which can mislead him into assuming two worlds of

phenomena (an outer and an inner), which — as I have

shown — would necessarily be shut off from one another.

This one error alone engenders countless other errors in

psychology.

2. Whoever conceives space as standing separates it from

the phenomena that are thought of as changing in it.

Space becomes an eternal vessel, whose content is formed

by the fleeting phenomena. This conception is the one

that haunts naive minds, and leads to the question: how

such an eternal absurdity as space is possible at all. Such

an eternal absurdity must then in the end be taken for

substance (or for God).

3. Whoever conceives space as standing comes to think of

certain phenomena, too, as standing. There

[p. 14]

are, namely, phenomena which are produced anew, continually and in much the same manner, in the perception of the one who intuits them, as I have shown above by the example of the red ball. The concept of the phenomenon, however, is completely falsified if one believes that there are standing phenomena. One makes of the phenomenon a non-sensible substance if one believes that it can persist. Through the concept of the standing phenomenon one is misled into assuming absolute rest, which in turn leads to contradictions without number.

These few indications may suffice for the present to make us attentive to what fundamental contradictions of our thinking we can avoid if we learn to set our phantasms of space and time in one. Only the conception that space is a dynamic one shows us the world of phenomena as caught up in an eternal flux. The principle of H e r a c l i t u s , that everything flows, acquires, so to speak, an exact formulation which it did not originally possess. We must say that every phenomenon flows, because space itself is a flowing, or dynamic, space. There is no standing or resting phenomenon, that is, there is no absolute rest of the phenomenon; there cannot be any such thing, because time cannot stand still, and because one would call in vain to the fleeting moment with the poet: Tarry a while, thou art so fair.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                  9

[PDF 132]

Section III.

Appendix.

15. Analytic and Synthetic Judgments.

In this appendix I should like to discuss two questions which offer me an opportunity of casting light in advance on the standpoint I take up in the outline of a monistic theory of knowledge. The first of these questions concerns the Kantian distinction between analytic and synthetic judgments; the second, on the other hand, the Leibnizian distinction between factual and rational truths. As to the first question, modern logicians are accustomed to lay hardly any weight any longer on the distinction of analytic and synthetic judgments in Kant’s sense, and one can certainly only agree with them in this regard, at least insofar as by analytic and synthetic merely explicative and ampliative judgments are meant. It has long been seen through that hardly anything can be accomplished in logic with this opposition; nevertheless I believe I must enter into the Kantian distinction, because it offers an opportunity to open up the fundamental problem of the theory of judgment, and thus of logic in general. For in that Kant wishes to distinguish analytic and synthetic judgments, it comes out clearly, against his intention, how highly enigmatic the relation of the subject

[p. 15]

and predicate in a judgment is; and though Kant does not succeed in making his distinction acceptable, he does succeed, even if against his will, in laying openly before us that we do not, properly speaking, yet possess any theory of judgment, and that the relation of subject and predicate in every judgment is a logical mystery. We have seen how A r i s t o t l e , too, was unable to determine the difference between concept and judgment, and we shall find that it fares no better with Kant where he wants to fix the relation of subject and predicate: a problem which coincides entirely with the question of the relation of concepts to judgment.

In an analytic judgment, according to Kant, the predicate merely expresses something that was already thought in the subject. To form an analytic judgment it is therefore sufficient to have a mere subject-concept, for this already supplies the predicate as well: one need only analyze the subject-concept, that is, decompose it into partial concepts, because precisely one of these partial concepts can play the role of the predicate in the analytic judgment. One need not, therefore, as Kant puts it, go beyond the subject-concept in an analytic judgment, because one can, as it were, draw the predicate out of it itself. Through this drawing-out there comes more distinctly to light what one had thought in the subject-concept in a concealed or confused manner, so that the analytic judgment has merely the task of explicating our concepts. In essence every analytic judgment is an identical one, for the partial concept that constitutes the predicate is also already found in the subject, so that, if we designate this partial concept by A, the analytic judgment says nothing other than: A i s A.

In the synthetic judgment, on the other hand, the predicate has not already been thought in the subject; here, then, it is not sufficient to have a mere subject-concept, but one must

9*

[p. 132]

go beyond this concept A to a concept B which is not contained in A. Such judgments as A is B, where B is not contained in A, serve to extend our knowledge; they are therefore judgments proper; in them the predicate is connected with the subject without identity. Kant himself says: “A n a l y t i c judgments (the affirmative ones) are therefore those in which the connection of the predicate with the subject is thought through i d e n t i t y, b u t t h o s e i n w h i c h t h i s c o n n e c t i o n is thought without identity are to be called s y n t h e t i c j u d g m e n t s . ” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 10.)

This explanation is to hold in the first instance only for affirmative judgments, but according to Kant it can very easily be extended to negative judgments as well. If we now actually try to carry this out, we make the peculiar observation that in the above definition “analytic” can be replaced by “affirmative” and “synthetic” by “negative.” By this substitution one obtains the following sentence: “A f f i r m a t i v e judgments are therefore those in which the connection of the predicate with the subject is thought through identity, but those in which this connection is thought without identity are to be called n e g a t i v e judgments.” I scarcely believe that this transformation of the Kantian explanation could seriously be objected to; but to assure oneself of the correctness of our transformation, consider, say, the following example: “This goblet is of gold.” This is an affirmative judgment, and one readily recognizes that subject and predicate are here connected through identity. Now form the corresponding negative judgment: “This goblet is n o t of gold,” and one will surely admit that subject and predicate are here connected without identity. To be sure, Kant never thought in the least of equating analytic with affirmative and synthetic with negative

[p. 15]

but his definition turned out, against his intention, in such a way that he established between analytic and synthetic judgments a contradictory opposition, according to which they must be related to one another as affirmation and negation. One could therefore obtain from the analytic judgment a synthetic one by negation, and from the synthetic judgment an analytic one. If the analytic judgment is true, the derived synthetic judgment would have to be false, and conversely: if the synthetic judgment is true, the corresponding analytic judgment would have to be false.

The Kantian explanation is thus an utter failure, for it defines something which Kant had not in the remotest intended to define. Fortunately, however, he tells us what his intention was when he had in mind the difference between analytic and synthetic judgments. He wanted, namely, to set up a logical definition for the difference between e x p l i c a t i v e and a m p l i a t i v e judgments, that is, he wanted to establish what relation must obtain between subject and predicate in order that a judgment may be an explicative one, and what other relation must obtain in order that the judgment may be called an ampliative one. In this endeavor his thinking was derailed, for he supplies us with a definition which has far more to do with affirmation and negation than with explication and amplification. This derailment, moreover, was a necessary one, because the very problem to be solved had been wrongly formulated.

For one must not raise the question at all what mode of connection between subject and predicate must obtain in order that a judgment may be either explicative or ampliative. For the explicative or ampliative character of a judgment does not depend at all on how subject and predicate may be connected with one another in it. Whether, namely, a judgment exerts an explicative or an ampliative effect on the knowledge of any person lies

[p. 134]

not at all in the judgment itself, but in the person to whom we submit the judgment. If one stands before someone who knows, one strives in vain to explicate to him what he knows: and all such effort will be wasted. And if one has to do with an initiate, one tries in vain to introduce him by means of ampliative judgments into a field of knowledge in which he is already at home. The same judgment that counted today as explicative for me has already forfeited this character tomorrow, because it exhausted its explicative effect yesterday; and exactly the same holds of ampliative judgments. Since, then, it does not lie in the judgment itself whether it possesses explicative or ampliative force for someone, but this force is always dependent on the particular relation of a judgment to the whole history of the mental development of any persons whatever: it must also be a wholly mistaken way of putting the question, of what kind the connection of subject and predicate in the judgment must be in order that the judgment may possess explicative or ampliative force.

I must also confess that I am unable to find any difference in principle between explication and amplification. For if, for example, I do not understand something, and someone explicates it to me, I should have to be an extremely ungrateful person were I not to acknowledge that this explication is for me also an amplification of my knowledge. For if I was yesterday in obscurity about something, and today someone brings me clarity, he must surely have made me a present of something, and I must surely have become richer by something, since otherwise I should still persist in yesterday’s obscurity. Thus every explicative judgment acts somehow as ampliative, and conversely every ampliative judgment will carry with it some explication for my remaining previous knowledge. This one may therefore

[p. 15]

admit, because all truths are connected with one another, that is, form a single realm of truth, so that, if one conceives each single truth as a kind of single light, there are in the perfected realm of truth no single lights at all, because all single lights together make up precisely one universal light. If, therefore, one kindles for someone anywhere a little light of truth, however slight, one may rely on its redounding to the benefit of the whole of his insight. For this very reason one asks in logic always only whether a judgment is true or false, for truth and falsity belong to the judgment itself, and do not depend on the degree of education of the person to whom I submit the judgment. What is true is unalterably true for all times and in all worlds, and it is with this unalterable character of true judgments that logic has to do, but not with the transitory manner of effect which a judgment may exert at this or that time on this or that person.

The question of the explicative and ampliative force of a judgment belongs to the developmental history of individual intelligence and not to logic. Besides, we have recognized that the Kantian way of posing the problem is an entirely false one, for the connection of the subject with the predicate constitutes the unalterable essence, or the logical character, of the judgment; now it does not depend on this unalterable or logical essence of a judgment, for which person and at which time it may be desirable and beneficial for purposes of the explication or amplification of knowledge, or not. We may not, then, enter at all into the question: what connection between subject and predicate must obtain in order that a judgment may be explicative or else ampliative. Those who raise such a question and still want to answer it offer really

[p. 136]

the laughable sight of “one milking the he-goat while the other holds a sieve underneath.”

Although, however, there can be no question in logic of a division of judgments into analytic and synthetic (or explicative and ampliative), the abortive Kantian attempt to bring about such a classification of judgments nevertheless has an epoch-making significance for the h i s t o r y of logic. There are errors in science in which a lively, if unsuccessful, struggle for truth is contained, and to this kind of significant errors the distinction of analytic and synthetic judgments appears also to belong. For often the human mind finds itself in the position of being tormented by questions that are so extremely obscure that it is difficult even to formulate the question in any way correctly. But since the tormented mind would like to relieve itself at any price, it easily happens that the question itself miscarries, and that there can be still far less talk of a solution of it. On such an occasion one may speak of a logical explosion of the human mind, where mighty resistances did not permit the tortured impulse to know to discharge itself in regular fashion. This seems to me to be the case with the Kantian doctrine of the synthetic and analytic judgment. The whole alleged division of judgments is here something incidental, and the main thing is precisely that on which Kant himself lays no weight, or which he does not notice at all. Whatever the truth about synthesis and analysis, or about amplification and explication, may be: the significance of such a distinction vanishes before the great, properly logical question: of what kind is the connection of subject and predicate in a judgment at all?

Kant confounds two entirely unrelated questions with one another. The one question relates to the

[p. 15]
