difference between the explication of our knowledge and the amplification of it: a problem which, as said, belongs to the theory of the historical development of the individual intellect, since it always depends on the previous course of development and the momentary constitution of mind of an individual whether a judgment that we submit to him is able to gain for him an explicative or ampliative significance. This question, however, has nothing to do with the other: how subject and predicate are connected in a judgment at all? For if we ask about this connection, we open up the problem of the unalterable logical essence of a judgment in general. What concerns us then is the question: What is a judgment? Now Kant, quite involuntarily, has thrown this second, exceedingly significant question into the shade for the sake of the incidental first. For he believed that he could explain the difference of explication and amplification by the kind of connection of subject and predicate, which was precisely his error. Through this error the question of the proper essence of judgment in general retreated so far into the background that it no longer seemed to be a question at all. We must therefore draw this buried problem forth from the rubble of thoughts which Kant has heaped over it, in order to set its fundamental significance for logic in the brightest possible light.

Kant conceives the connection of the subject with the predicate in the judgment now as an identical one, now as a non-identical one. One should think he wished thereby to distinguish affirmative and negative judgments; for by affirmation one is accustomed to understand the identical setting-in-one of a subject-concept and a predicate-concept; whereas a negation is supposed to consist precisely in this, that a subject-concept and a predicate-concept are indeed connected into a judgment, but not set in one identically.

[p. 138]

Kant himself says: “In the affirmative judgment the subject is thought under the sphere of a predicate, in the negative it is placed outside the sphere of the latter.” (Logic, p. 113.) But he says something similar also with regard to the distinction of analytic and synthetic judgments: “Either the predicate B belongs to the subject A as something that is (in a concealed manner) contained in this concept A; or B lies entirely outside the concept A, though it stands in connection with it.” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 10.)

Now Kant, however, conceives synthetic judgments in the first instance as affirmative ones: so there are, according to him, affirmative judgments in which the predicate lies entirely outside the subject-concept, whereas in logic he wished to establish such a relation only for negative judgments. Thus it becomes a riddle how we are to conceive the connection of subject and predicate in the judgment. The logical essence of the judgment, the act of judgment itself, becomes a mystery, and it is this mystery that is concealed by the division of judgments into analytic and synthetic.

We must admit to ourselves that it is equally awkward to think subject and predicate of an affirmative sentence as connected t h r o u g h identity as w i t h o u t identity. Is one to assume, perhaps, that all affirmative assertion, according to its logical essence, would be nothing other than the statement that A is just A? When I say, for example, “This ball is red,” do I perhaps mean by it: “This red is red”? Is every affirmation, in its innermost core, to be nothing but a self-repetition? It would surely be strange if our assertions could in the end be reduced, in their essence, to such formulas as: Hard is hard; Round is round; Pleasant is pleasant, etc. What should we think of a musician who in a piece of music strikes every key twice, in order that at every tone he may make us attentive

[p. 15]

to the fact that it is just this tone that is in question? It is not readily comprehensible why we should say of an A “it is A”; for if the first A requires an explanation through the second A, why should the second not likewise require a third A, this a fourth, and so on, for its explanation? One would then have, in order to think an A, to repeat this A continually of itself, and one could never in all eternity get beyond the one concept A.

To be sure, no reasonable man would wish to assert that A is not A; and so nothing seems left but to take “A is A” for a truth, however much one may take offense at its uselessness. This fatal state of affairs has perhaps put no one so much into a rage as the otherwise so thoroughly harmonious L o c k e , as may be seen from the delightful 7th and 8th chapters (Of Maxims, Of Trifling Propositions) of the 4th Book of his Essay concerning Human Understanding. Locke here grows heated against the Scholastics, who make so much ado about the sentence “what is, is,” as if this were a fundamental principle of all knowledge, or all cognition were derived from it. He declares “that there are universal propositions which, though certainly true, yet add no light to our understanding and bring no increase to our knowledge, namely, first, identical propositions; secondly, when a part of a complex idea is predicated of the whole idea.” These are precisely the judgments which Kant designates by the name of analytic and presents as explicative judgments, whereas Locke, with evident disdain, calls them trifling propositions. To these trifling propositions he opposes the instructive or real truths, “which say something of a thing that follows from its complex idea, yet is not contained in it.” These are precisely the cognitions which Kant introduced into philosophy under the

[p. 140]

name of ampliative or synthetic judgments into philosophy.

One finds, then, the distinction between analytic and synthetic judgment already in Locke; only, with him this distinction has nothing at all of that fundamental significance which it attained in Kant. The two thinkers, moreover, take a very different position toward this same distinction, which rests on the fact that Locke is guided predominantly by empiricist inclinations, whereas Kant, in comparison with him, may be called a rationalist. The relative opposition of the two philosophers declares itself at once in this, that Locke would positively brand the analytic truths as trifling, whereas Kant, by way of a flattering gloss, would have them pass as explicative judgments. This opposition would stand out still more clearly if Kant had called the analytic truths “formal truths”; for then it would be evident at first glance why Kant regards with milder eyes the “trifling propositions” of which Locke speaks so ill. A truth devoid of import can, according to Kant, still always have a value, namely insofar as, by virtue of this very lack of import, it is a formal truth. Although, however, analytic truths in Kant’s sense are properly formal truths, he nevertheless did not name them so, which in his case has a well-concealed, deep ground, which I already touched upon in the introduction to this work.

For Kant takes the word “formal” in a twofold sense: at one time the formal belongs to general or Aristotelian logic, and then it is unfruitful or dead; at another time it belongs to transcendental or Kantian logic, and then it is fruitful or living. By analytic judgments Kant now wishes to understand merely those formal judgments which belong to Aristotelian logic, which are therefore unfruitful or dead. Those formal

[p. 15]

judgments, however, which carry our knowledge forward, which are therefore fruitful or living, he wishes to unite in one class with the material judgments that spring from experience, and for this union of judgments he coins the name synthetic. Thus under the name synthetic two classes of judgments of wholly opposite nature lie hidden: for there belong here both formal and material judgments, which are supposed to agree in this, that they are fruitful, that is, that they extend our cognition. From all this it is evident that it would not have been expedient to characterize the analytic judgments as formal, for by that the unwelcome state of affairs would have come out all too plainly, that the class of formal judgments was torn asunder, and only the unfruitful half of it received the name “analytic,” while the other, fruitful half was violently coupled into one class with the material judgments, which are different from it in essence.

It is now, then, still more clearly evident that the Kantian analytic judgments agree entirely with Locke’s trifling propositions. But Kant, as was said, is more mildly disposed toward these propositions, for to him they are formal propositions, and even if they are unfruitful, they stand to him nevertheless in blood-relationship with formal propositions which are supposed to have a synthetic, that is, fruitful character. Locke, on the other hand, as an empiricist, knows nothing of such formalistic subtleties, and “trifling propositions” are to him altogether intolerable. Through long columns he does not cease to ask what use there can really be in forming such propositions as “a man is a man,” “all white is white,” “red is not blue,” etc. And through this much questioning an excitement takes possession of him which is truly amusing. A monkey — he says — that amused himself by taking an oyster now into his right, now into his left

[p. 142]

hand would, if he were endowed with speech, address us thus: The oyster now in the right hand is the subject, the oyster now in the left hand is the predicate, but you see well enough: “the oyster is the oyster,” or, generally speaking, “what is, is.” “A is A.” Now just as the bulk of a monkey would increase little if he knew nothing better to do with the oyster than to take it from one hand into the other, so — Locke thinks — our cognition too would be little enriched if we knew nothing else to say than: “a soul is a soul,” “a fetish is a fetish,” “a horse is a horse,” and whatever more of such wisdom there is to be found. But however much he makes merry over the identical propositions, he does not get done with them, for he concedes truth to them and characterizes them only as useless trifling. To be sure, he remains in our debt for the answer how that which is true, to which we after all assign the highest value, can be something useless and ridiculous. Moreover he does not even stay with the view that the identical propositions are altogether useless; indeed, he credits them with a fundamental significance for cognition. “I grant as readily as any one that they are all true and self-evident (namely the identical propositions); I grant further that the foundation of all our knowledge lies in our faculty of cognizing the same idea as the same” . . . In spite of this fullest possible acknowledgment of the identical propositions, with which even the most zealous Scholastic could be content, he persists in holding that the identical propositions represent empty trifling. How empty trifling, however, can be the foundations of all our knowledge, is hard to comprehend.

The self-contradiction in which both Locke and Kant are inwardly entangled has, I believe, been sufficiently set forth. That all our judgments, in their innermost

[p. 15]

core were nothing but the statement of an A of that very same A, seems to be a monstrous assumption, which stamps all our judgments with the mark of an idiotic babbling. Both Locke and Kant shrink from this, and believe they gain something if they ascribe this idiotic character merely to one half of our judgments (the analytic ones); whereas both of them, with more or less reluctance, allow the principle of identity to stand as a foundation of a l l our knowledge. But even granting that only the one class of our judgments would suffer from the repetition of the subject in the predicate: are the synthetic judgments any better off, even by a hair? How are we to comprehend that a predicate lies wholly outside the concept of the subject, and nevertheless is to be a f f i r m a t i v e l y asserted of it? If a B lies outside the sphere of A, how may it be affirmed of that A? Does it not look as if we were affirming something where we ought to deny it? In other words: does it not look as if, by an unlawful affirmation, we meant to assert an absurdity? Great as was the offense we took at the identical connection of subject and predicate, it is perhaps still outweighed by the offense we must take at a non-identical connection of subject and predicate in the affirmative judgment. We are thereby brought into such straits that in the Locke- Kantian division of judgments into two classes, the one class seems to contain nothing but idiotic judgments, the other, on the contrary, nothing but absurd ones.

What follows from this consideration? I believe we ought first of all, in all modesty, to confess that we simply do not understand what sort of connection that is which constitutes the essence of judgment in general. We simply do not know how the so-called subject and the so-called predicate in a judgment

[p. 144]

are connected with one another. We do not know what it means to be “identically connected,” and therefore also not what it means to be “connected without identity.” We do not know what sense the so-called p r i n c i p l e of identity has. We do not know at all to what end a subject and a predicate may be present in a judgment. Is it necessary that they be there? Must the signs by means of which we express our judgments really be so constituted that a sign for the subject and one for the predicate may not be lacking; indeed, according to many logicians, a third sign also, for the connection of the parts of the sentence, that is, for the copula, must be present? We do not know whether this duality, or even triality, of the structure of the sentence flows from the nature of our judging, or is only a peculiarity — and then to be sure a quite wicked peculiarity — of our language. In a word, we do not know what it means: to judge.

There is nothing at all shaming in such a confession; far more shaming, it seems to me, would be the unwillingness to make it. Trillions upon trillions of judgments have been passed since mankind began to think, but no apt judgment has yet been passed on what a judgment is. This seems to me not at all astonishing, for had anyone succeeded in enlightening mankind about what it means to judge, then we should presumably also know already what cognition means, and what truth is, since we can have cognition of truth always only in our judgments. Whoever wished to explain what a judgment is in general would have — so at least it seems to me — to comprise all possible judgments in one, which one judgment would contain in itself precisely that by which every judgment becomes a judgment. If one now considers that it may be no slight task to comprise the common logical essence of all possible judgments in one judgment, one will perhaps be less surprised that such a

[p. 15]

problem is still unsolved, as rather that most logicians treat this problem as though it were a matter of cracking the first nut that comes to hand. It seems to me hardly more difficult to say what a judgment is than to show what truth is; indeed I believe that the two problems are probably only parts of one problem, since in judging it can only ever be a question for us of taking truth into possession. And if Kant does not hesitate to declare openly to us in his Logic that in the current explanation of what truth is one finds oneself in a circle, he could just as well have admitted that we do not know what role a subject and a predicate play in the sentence, and whether they are really necessary to the structure of a sentence. Furthermore he could also have admitted that we do not know what kind of connection of subject and predicate obtains in a judgment, or in other words, that we do not know what identity is, and what the famous Principium identitatis is supposed to mean. He could have laid before us the great embarrassment in which we find ourselves when we are to state of an A this very A, and the still greater embarrassment when we are to state of an A a B lying outside it. Instead of presenting this double embarrassment as though it were a classification of our judgments (into analytic and synthetic), it seems to me more expedient to set down this double embarrassment without circumlocution as a double embarrassment. We then have, to be sure, no s e e m i n g classification of our judgments, but we have at least a r e a l problem, namely the problem of judgment, which may also be regarded as the fundamental problem of the theory of knowledge. One could, it is true, set up as the principal question of the theory of knowledge the question what truth is, but the question as to the essence of judgment seems to me to have an advantage for this reason, that it is less pretentious, and at the same time

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                10

[p. 146]

contains the indication that in our judgments we often possess merely a supposed truth, that is, that in judging we are exposed to error. Thus with the problem of judgment there is raised not only that of truth, but also that of error.

Let it be remarked here, by the way, that B o l z a n o , in his Wissenschaftslehre, furnishes the detailed proof that we possess nowhere an explanation of judgment which would not, on closer consideration, prove to be circular. As he passes in review famous and obscure logicians of all ages from Aristotle to Hegel, he shows how into their definitions of judgment there creep such expressions as affirm, deny, attribute, refuse, combine, separate, see, reject, state, etc., which already anticipate the concept of judgment. A multitude of explanations suffer from the characteristic defect that they strive to abolish the peculiar nature of judging and to reduce it to the idea or the concept. To this belongs also the Kantian explanation, which would reduce judgment to a representation of a representation: “Judgment is therefore the mediate cognition of an object, hence the representation of a representation of it.” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 93.) The Kantian K r u g says it straight out, that every judgment is nothing other than a distinctly thought concept. S c h u l z e , to illustrate this, cites the example that “the judgment: the body is red, contains the same combination of concepts which occurs in the concept of a red body.” (Logic, § 85.) Such utterances about judgment show us best that perplexity in which logic has always found itself in explaining the act of judgment. Besides, Kant himself also admits that no explanation of judgment had satisfied him: “I have never been able to be satisfied by the explanation which the logicians give of a judgment in general; it

[p. 15]

is, as they say, the representation of a relation between two concepts.” (Crit. of Pure Reason, p. 140.) Now Kant holds, however, that precisely this relation is not determined by it. Had Kant taken this into account in his division of judgments into analytic and synthetic, he would presumably have put off the attempt at dividing until he had succeeded in making that “relation of two concepts” so clear that everyone would have to see that judgments can only be either analytic or synthetic. In short, he would have had to found the classification of judgments upon a theory of judgment.

But in the process it would have become evident that the nature of the act of judgment is to a high degree enigmatic to us, that therefore that general logic of which Kant tells us that it is a completed science, which need no longer advance, still stands at the beginning of the beginning. For where else are we to seek enlightenment about the logical essence of judgment than in general logic? And how can this general logic be completed, if, despite the efforts of great philosophers of all ages, it gives us so little enlightenment about the nature of judgment that even a Kant is close to letting judgment merge into the concept or the idea, indeed entangles himself in the contradiction that at one time he declares that the predicate lies outside the subject only in negative judgments, and at another time asserts this very same thing also of affirmative (synthetic) judgments.

To emphasize this seems to me to be of great importance for this reason, that hardly any greater hindrance can stand in the way of the progress of logical research than the authority of so influential a philosopher as Kant. There is nothing more convenient than to declare general logic a completed science, for thereby one spares oneself the trouble of seriously taking in hand those problems of

10*

[p. 148]

almost infinite difficulty which form the content of general logic. We may perhaps venture to say that we have as yet no systematic general logic at all, but that only the first beginnings of this difficult science are present in individual great thinkers.

16. Factual and Rational Truths.

Already in the first chapter I pointed to the difference between chemical and classificatory dualism in the theory of knowledge, and it now remains to consider this latter more closely, because it still exerts a powerful after-effect upon modern logic. The chief representative, indeed one may say the father, of classificatory dualism in the theory of knowledge is L e i b n i z , who joins to his distinction of “vérités de fait” and “vérités de raison” most ingenious metaphysical speculations. “There are,” he teaches, “two classes of truth: rational and factual. The rational are necessary and their opposite is impossible; the factual are contingent and their opposite possible.” (Monadology, p. 707.) It is this dualism which Kant combines with a chemical one (of form and matter) and in addition with an impressionist one (of outer and inner sense), and develops into a transcendental idealism. B o l z a n o too appropriates the Leibnizian classificatory dualism and combines it with a new chemical dualism of act and content of judgments, indeed he adds to it also a dualism of simple and composite ideas, which he developed after the analogy of Locke’s chemistry of ideas. We are concerned here, however, only with the classificatory dualism, which also in modern logic, in manifold turns of phrase, such as, for example, that of the evident and non-evident,

[p. 16]

of the apodictic and non-apodictic, the certain and the merely probable, the exact and the merely approximate, the deductive and inductive truths, makes its appearance.

If one considers that such concepts as necessity, possibility, impossibility play into the Leibnizian distinction, one will readily find it comprehensible that the dispute over his distinction can never come to an end. One will not, for example, be able to investigate the concept of necessity without also taking the concept of freedom into account; indeed this concept will also play into the concept of possibility, because possibilities always have meaning only for a being which — like man — takes itself to be a free being. Will one, however, ever be done with the concept of freedom? And what is one to do at all with the concept of contingency, which likewise cannot be excluded from consideration as soon as necessity is once under discussion? To be sure, such expressions as evident and non-evident, or certain and probable, or deductive and inductive, are very well suited to conceal those difficulties which are contained in the concepts of necessity, freedom, contingency, possibility, impossibility, but one will not let oneself be deceived by such a concealment, for those seemingly more innocent expressions have exactly the same content as the Leibnizian expressions, which openly display their metaphysical character. Take, for example, the expressions evident or certain: will one be able to investigate them without running up against the expressions true and necessary, and once one has stopped at these, then one is already wholly and entirely in metaphysics. Or is the expression probable more innocent than the expression contingent or possible, impossible, etc.? Truly, one strives quite in vain, by pushing forward this word or that, to keep logical investigation free from metaphysics.

[p. 150]

That one holds this to be possible is already a proof that one lets oneself be hypnotized by the sound of words, that is, that one is a Scholastic. Words are not there for the purpose of serving as blinders for the understanding, or of hiding oneself behind them. Hence it is still best today, too, to keep to the Leibnizian definition in distinguishing two classes of truths.

Leibniz holds that the truth of a fact is merely a contingent one and that its opposite is possible. Certainly we must agree with him in this, that in the face of any fact we can also think that it does not take place, or that in its place any other might be put. Did we not have this faculty, how could we intervene purposefully and methodically in the circumstances of our environment, in order to reshape them according to our vital needs, our claims of taste, or our tendencies of research? But the faculty of thinking another in the place of a fact changes not the slightest thing in the “necessary” taking place of that fact. Now Leibniz, however, holds the whole infinite realm of facts in a certain sense to be something contingent: in the sense, namely, that in the divine mind infinitely many worlds rest as ideal possibilities, and that He chose this one actual world for realization only because it is the most perfect among all the possibilities of worlds. However extraordinarily bold and poetic such a speculation may be, so little may the logician engage in it. It has, to be sure, a mystical charm for man to dream himself into the divine mind, that is, to imagine for a moment that he is a god; but such a reverie always has the evil consequence of poetically projecting one’s own human personality into the divine one, and of equipping the latter with all human weaknesses and vices. Because we human beings weigh different possibilities against one another when we are to make a

[p. 16]

decision, we have no right whatever to transfer this peculiarity, which follows from our limitedness, to an absolute being.

One forms a very low conception of an absolute being if one imagines it choosing between possibilities, and one forms, on the other hand, a far too high conception of one’s own faculty of cognition if one believes oneself able to put oneself in the position of a creative being. The poet is permitted to exalt himself so, for his task is to give expression to the storm of feelings and passions. Indeed, we shall admire the poet who knows how to present the human mind at the highest stages of its revolt against the absolute power of the world, because it is precisely in these revolts that human nature can break through wholly. A passion is passion only when it no longer knows any bounds, and when it rages in bright rebellion against the eternal order of the world; and a purification of passion too is possible only if the poet presents it in its highest excess and lets it be shattered against the absolute order of the world. But the logician, as logician, can never have the task of poetically projecting himself into an absolute being, and of constructing from there infinitely many ideal possibilities of worlds. Such a thing is done only by the inflamed poetic mind, which contemplates, for example, a Prometheus, a Titan who rises against Zeus in order to bring about a world-order better suited to man.

With the ideal possibilities of worlds of L e i b n i z one finds oneself in an optimistic mythology, in contrast to that more somber Promethean myth which I have just indicated. But whether one may speak of better worlds than the actual one, or would assume with Leibniz that the actual world is the best of infinitely many possibilities: such a manner of speaking has always

[p. 152]

an antilogical character. For whatever a man may devise, he himself, together with all his unlogical thoughts of ideal possibilities of worlds, belongs in this one actual world. Whoever wishes to assume another universe than the actual one gives himself the appearance of having already gained knowledge of the infinite infinities of the existing universe, and of being able to think, beyond this universe, other universes. But the more far-fetched such thinking is, the greater the testimony to poverty it contains for the concept which one forms of the universe. Whoever thinks seriously of this universe loses all desire to outstrip it with his thinking and to reach beyond its infinite powers of infinities, and he sees at once, too, that the attempt of thought to put another in the place of this universe is equivalent to the attempt to construct another absolute being in the place of an absolute being. Besides, the poet is not to be forbidden, in the intoxication of the passions he is to present, to storm the eternal walls of all these and similar impossibilities; for poetic extravagance can be beautiful, can be sublime, if behind it there hides a self-composed, harmonious view of the world. But whereas poetic extravagance is only a means of bringing eternal truth home to the human mind, logical extravagance is only a means of getting from one contradiction into another. Poetic extravagance is beautiful; logical is abominable.

Every fact is necessary, and there are nowhere any contingent facts. With the necessity of every fact, however, no more is meant than this, that if we were able to embrace infinite space and infinite time in one glance, such foolish thoughts as to whether all this could also be otherwise

[p. 16]

would not come to us at all. Only because we always survey a very small complex of phenomena, and this too only in an extremely inadequate way, is there connected with this limitedness of ours at once the faculty of positing in fantasy all sorts of possibilities, and, by this circumstance, of perhaps also going astray concerning the eternal order, because we imagine that our fantasying of all sorts of possibilities stands in contradiction to an eternally fixed order of nature; whereas the case is precisely the reverse: namely, that it is precisely an immutable order of nature which makes it possible for us to fantasy, to think, to cognize, to distinguish truth from falsity, and to act in the sense of truth, that is, freely. Every sound mind is fully aware that an immutable order prevails in nature, for the very circumstance that we place all phenomena at a determinate place and in a determinate interval of time is the clear proof that we cannot avoid assuming such a universal order. That we are never able to state the spatial and temporal position of a phenomenon with absolute exactness, as every physicist laments from the depth of his heart, is only another expression for the fact that we cannot survey infinite space and infinite time, for only if such an all-embracing view were given to us could we also fix the position of every phenomenon in an absolutely exact manner. It is therefore only a consequence of our limitedness that physics and chemistry are unable to carry out absolutely exact measurements; by no means, however, does it lie in the phenomena themselves that we cannot arrange them in an absolutely exact order. Every investigator of nature, however, proceeds from the presupposition of this absolutely exact order, and strives with all his powers to approximate it. He never doubts the order itself, but merely his ability and his means to be fully

[p. 154]

equal to it. This sense for truth and this genuine modesty of the investigator of nature are what stamp him a genuine philosopher.

In order to establish an immutable order of nature it is for the present not at all necessary to appeal to a law of causality. For no logician has yet succeeded in formulating a principle of causality in a scientifically clear manner, so that there is nothing easier than to doubt, with H u m e , the causality of phenomena. If one does not know what causality is, then it is also mere play to take up a skeptical attitude toward it. A serious skepticism is possible only where one already knows what one is to doubt, but to doubt that whose sense is as little fixed as the sense of the so-called principle of causality can occur only to those for whom truth is not at all a serious matter. But one can also, without appealing to any nebulous principle of causality, hold fast to the thought of an eternal order of the world, and for this purpose one needs only to point out that every phenomenon necessarily has a position in space and in time.*) No phenomenon is able to displace another from its spatiotemporal position, and each maintains, until the end of time, always the same spatiotemporal characteristic which belongs to it. Common understanding expresses this in the following formula: What has happened can never be made unhappened. In this truth the whole lawfulness of nature, the whole absolutely iron character of the order of the world, is already clearly expressed. One cannot blot out of the stream of time an event that has taken place and put another in its place.

*) In the sense of psychophysical parallelism there would, to be sure, be no lawfulness in the course of nature; for if purely temporal phenomena are assumed, this means as much as that phenomena exist which exist nowhere.

[p. 16]

Every event that has once taken place rigidly maintains for all eternity its spatiotemporal characteristic in the course of the world, and there is no power that could dispute this characteristic with it. This thought, which at bottom is nothing other than the so-called principle of identity (or also of contradiction), suffices fully to grasp that lawfulness which dwells in the course of the world, at least so far as is necessary in order to subject it to a further examination. Everyone admits that an event must take place at a determinate place and at a determinate time, and that the same event cannot lay claim to two different places in space or two different intervals in time. And whoever admits this has already admitted an eternal lawfulness of the course of nature. Leibniz’s principle that the opposite of every fact is possible would have meaning only if one could make a fact unhappened and conjure another in its place. Since, however, this is not possible, we may perhaps say that precisely the opposite of the Leibnizian conception must hold as the basis of a logic. If something is once a fact, then nothing can be altered in it, and to transform it into its opposite is an absolute impossibility.

One seeks in vain to set blue-blooded truths of concepts over against the plebeian factual truths and to establish an infinite chasm between them. All those inadequacies which are peculiar to the one kind of cognition cling also to the other kind of cognition, for they all bear the human stamp. Not only in the face of a fact can I imagine its opposite; in the case of any mathematical theorem, too, I can make the assumption that its opposite is true. Indeed, one uses this procedure as a method of proving theorems; one assumes

[p. 156]

namely the opposite of a truth, and draws conclusions from it until one arrives at a statement whose falsity is already known. Mathematical theorems are therefore not at all characterized by the fact that one could not assume their opposite; rather one can very often do nothing better than to assume their opposite until one is convinced of their truth: exactly as, in the face of facts, one can behave doubtingly until one has assured oneself of their truth.

If, for example, someone does not know whether the number 91 is a prime number or not, he takes up a doubting attitude toward it until, after a few trials, he has convinced himself that 91 = 7 × 13 and is therefore not a prime. But now think of a number consisting of a trillion digits, and set yourself the task of distinguishing whether it is a prime or not! (One can fix such a number, for example, by giving its first two digits and adding a law of formation by which from two digits the third following them can always be found.) How is one to go about establishing the prime character of a number of trillions of digits? Will a human life suffice for it? Mathematicians have not succeeded in establishing a general law of how primes follow one another in the series of numbers. One should, besides, tell oneself that mathematical complications can increase to such a degree that a human mind is too weak to see through them. The case is just the same here as with factual truths. In order to grasp factual truths with perfect clarity, we should have to be able to survey the infinite stream of time completely; but since this is not possible for us, we shall, in the face of any fact, when we investigate it more closely, finally get into the position of finding in it something that is still not sufficiently established, enigmatic,

[p. 16]

indeed unfathomable. Are we, however, to let what we know of the fact be embittered for us by what we do not know of it? That would mean as much as losing the joy in simpler mathematical truths because we have not yet seen through the complicated ones.

It must be emphasized, in general, that a human mind is never able to see anything with a b s o l u t e clarity: not even the mathematical truths. For one sees a truth with absolute clarity only when one has grasped its position in the infinite realm of truth, that is, its connection with all truths whatever. One would have to be omniscient in order to grasp a single truth in an absolute manner, that is, in all its connections. That is indeed the difference between a brighter and a less bright head, that for the former the single truth is as of glass, through which his gaze penetrates to other truths, whereas for the latter every truth is a wall which bars his view through to other truths. But however brilliant a man may be, there will be found in him a limit where he can no longer do justice to the infinite complication of truths; and this weakness sets in with “truths of concepts” just as much as with factual truths.

If someone considers, for example, the mathematical formula eⁱˣ = cos x + i sin x, he will admit that such a formula, even if understood, can still be understood in d i f f e r e n t d e g r e e s , although it may still count as an elementary formula. The deeper one penetrates into the theory of functions, the more clearly one also grasps the sense of that elementary formula, and the more brilliant mathematician understands the sense of the elementary formulas better too than the less brilliant, because his gaze embraces the realm of mathematical

[p. 158]

truths and their connections better and commands them. In short, with every so-called “truth of concepts” there is a certain “degree of understanding” with which one grasps it, quite as with every so-called “factual truth” there is a “degree of certainty” with which one constates it. The inadequacy of the human faculty of cognition, however, plays into both (so-called) classes of truths. Were there two such classes of truths, as Leibniz maintains, then in one kind of our cognition we should be infallible gods, in the other kind, on the contrary, senseless animals. But we are everywhere only human beings: in mathematics just as in physics or chemistry, and our cognition has everywhere the same character of inadequacy, as I shall set forth more precisely in the following outline of a logic.

[PDF 161]

Part II.

Outline of a Monistic Logic.

[PDF 162]

S e c t i o n I V.

General Logic.

17. Factual Truths.

1. Although in the discussions so far the critical or polemical tone has predominated, I have also hitherto availed myself, at suitable places, of the opportunity to let my own positive conception emerge ever more clearly. My concern has always been to contest dualistic logic, that is, for example, the rift between form and matter, between reason and sensibility, between outer and inner sense, etc.; but in order that this polemic may gain a firm footing, it is now unavoidable to present the principles of that monistic logic at which I was always aiming, at least in their main lines. Here, to be sure, I must impose very great restrictions upon myself, so as not to endanger the critical keynote of the whole work. Since, then, I merely sketch the main thoughts of a monistic logic, I reserve the detailed and systematic exposition of its principles for another occasion.

As has often been emphasized already, the question of the essence of judgment must be regarded as the principal problem of logic. For since we present everything true, as well as everything false, in judgments, the great question of

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.             11

[p. 162]

the distinction of truth from falsity must stand in the closest relation to the problem of judgment.

If, then, we wish to give ourselves some account of the essence of judgment, the most expedient course is to keep at once to a definite single judgment in which we fix a fact that we are perceiving at this very moment. Such a judgment is, for example: “Here on the table lies a book.” But since this fact is present only to me, and another person therefore cannot have the same conviction of the truth of the judgment cited that I have, let the reader pick out a fact from his own surroundings and put it into a judgment of whose truth he is fully convinced. There are, to be sure, skeptics who maintain that one can be convinced neither of the truth nor of the falsity of a judgment, neither of the occurrence nor of the non-occurrence of a fact; but it seems to me that a discussion of this doubt, or of other kinds of skepticism, is not yet in place here. In logic the first place belongs to being convinced, and only the second to doubt; for the goal toward which we strive is always conviction and not doubt, and the latter has its justification only where we start from a conviction that we already have in order to be able to arrive at a conviction that we do not yet have. But of this later.

To return now to the example I cited above (and which, for brevity’s sake, I shall designate by A), I ask myself what I really mean by saying that the judgment A is true. This can perhaps be made clearer if I take the book away from the table and accordingly form the true judgment: “The book no longer lies on the table,” which I shall designate by B. The fact which I expressed in the judgment A has ceased to exist; it is

[p. 17]

gone, vanished, or however one may care to express it. The question now is whether I have the right to say that the judgment A is a false judgment because the fact expressed in it no longer exists. I believe everyone will admit that the judgment A continues to maintain its truth, for it is true even now that there was a point of time at which the book lay on the table. As often as I return in my memory to that point of time and call to mind the fact cited, I must always hold to it that the judgment A is true. Indeed, even if I should have forgotten the fact expressed in the judgment A, on account of its triviality, so completely that I am no longer able to return to it at all, the judgment A will nevertheless be unable to lose its truth. It is eternally true.

2. If, then, I constate in a judgment a fact that is taking place at this very moment, I constate it for eternity. With regard to such constating judgments I can accordingly make the following statement: to constate a fact that is taking place at this very moment means as much as to e x p e r i e n c e s o m e thing imperishable in something transitor y. I do not mean, however, that I have thereby furnished a so-called definition of the true constating judgment; I do not raise so lofty a claim, if only for the reason that there is no agreement among logicians as to what they wish to understand by a definition. Some appear to assume that the definition of any object must contain all the true statements that can be made about the object in question at all. Others, on the other hand, believe it sufficient to make the essential true statements about the object, though it remains indeterminate what they understand by the essentially true statements. This is not the place to cite all the various formulas that have been set up for defining, for it would

11*

[p. 164]

fully suffice me if in the foregoing I had merely made a single true statement about the nature of constating judgments. One might perhaps take offense at my characterization of the constating judgment, in that I put the transitory and the imperishable together in one sentence in a paradoxical manner, and might find a contradiction in my saying that one experiences something imperishable in something transitory. By this, however, I mean only that, although in perceiving a fact we experience something passing, something vanishing, in our constating judgment we express the eternal truth of the occurrence of this transitory fact.

If one subsumes judging under the concept of experience, every true judgment can be called an e x p e r i e n c e o f e t e r n i t y , which says only that the truth one experiences is an eternal truth. This can also be put figuratively by saying that through the true judgment the fact is, as it were, lifted out of the stream of time and transitoriness into the supratemporal realm of eternal truth. In all these and similar turns of speech there is always expressed only the simple thought: the fact passes away, but its truth endures.

3. All true constating judgments are, as has been said, made for eternity. Now it may be objected to me, however, that in the everyday life of men countless true constating judgments are made without the persons judging thinking at all that their judgments lay claim to eternal truth. I should therefore have described quite incorrectly what is called a true constating judgment, since such judgments can also take place, and for the most part actually do take place, without being accompanied by the thought of an experience of eternity. To this I have to reply that we can do what we do, as is well known,

[p. 17]

with very different degrees of distinct awareness. In everyday life the persons who act are in general not permitted to become aware of the essence of judgment, since what matters there is commonly a swift judging and a nimble intervention in the facts. In everyday intercourse one passes judgments, but one does not write logical treatises.

The most unhappy of all unhappy thoughts is that of the logician who wants to make the measure of awareness in the life of everyday intercourse the measure of logical awareness. Logic is meant to be a science that strives aft e r t h e p e r f e c t i o n o f h u m a n a w a r e n e s s , and for that reason it resorts to all the means by which we are able to raise our awareness to a higher power. It therefore does not concern itself at all with whether in ordinary life the true constating judgment is at once accompanied by the thought of having been made for eternity; for it asks only whether our awareness can be raised to a higher power to such a degree that we see that in a judgment about a fleeting fact we have expressed not merely something fleeting but something eternally true. Logic is related to our judgments much as geometry is related to lines. In ordinary life, too, there is no talk of mathematical lines, which have no breadth, but this does not prevent the geometer from grasping the concept of the mathematical line, because this concept is at bottom nothing other than the clear awareness of what a line is really supposed to mean. Without this awareness a science of lines would be impossible; for if one could not abstract from two dimensions of space in order to consider only the third, we should never arrive at a concept of space, and hence at no geometry either. Likewise there can never be a logic if we are not permitted to set up ideals for the activity of judging. In logic we must first of all abstract from the

[p. 166]

deficiencies of awareness in everyday judging, since otherwise we should fare like a geometer who, over the doubt whether there are really any perfectly exact straight lines, would never get so far as to set up theorems about straight lines.

We therefore establish that in logic only such a judgment counts as true as is accompanied by the consciousness of its e t e r n a l validity; and we do not concern ourselves further with whether in everyday intercourse true judgments are at once set forth with the distinct consciousness of their eternal truth.

4. In order to become firmly established in the principle that the true judgment is an experience of eternity, it is well to render it as vividly sensible as possible, and here everyone may follow the method best suited to the nature of his fantasy. The simplest procedure for this purpose seems to me to be to imagine that the true judgment is repeated without cease through all future times, as though the judgment, by each repetition, wished to proclaim anew its claim to eternal truth. If, for example, we are speaking of the true constating judgment which I designated above by A, we have to think of this judgment as the representative of an infinite series of identically worded judgments:

A1, A2, A3, A4 . . . An . . . ad inf., all of which bring to expression entirely the same truth as the judgment A, and which follow one another in time immediately, at one another’s heels. Although the judgment A by itself already contains something eternally true and has no need of the support of repetition, that infinite series of identically worded judgments succeeding one another in time is nevertheless very well suited to represent symbolically the claim of the judgment A to eternal truth. This symbolism also has the advantage that we become clearly aware of the difference

[p. 17]

between truth and the act of judgment. For it is the same truth that can present itself in infinitely many identically worded acts of judgment. Acts of judgment are themselves transitory facts, but the sense that dwells in them, that is, the truth that comes to presentation in them, is imperishable. The acts of judgment of A, A1, A2, A3 . . . etc. are nothing other than the vital brain-work that I must perform in order to be able to think again and again the truth which forms the common sense or content of all these identically worded judgments.

This consideration teaches us that every truth has the character of u n i v e r s a l i t y . Even the singular truth that is expressed in the constating judgment A must be conceived as a universal truth; for the same truth that is contained in A also presents itself in the identically worded judgments A1, A2, A3, . . . ad inf.: it is therefore what is common to this infinite series of judgments, that is, it is a universal. Accordingly we can also characterize the true judgment of fact by saying that it is the experience of a universal in a particular fact. Moreover, this second characterization agrees with the first, according to which a true judgment is an experience of eternity. For if something is eternal, it is common to all points of time, that is, it is a universal.

In rendering our principle sensible, that the judgment of fact is an experience of eternity, we can go still further. If, for example, we represent the judgment A by a straight line, we can symbolize A1, A2, A3 . . . ad inf. by a system of rays parallel to A, somewhat in the following way:

etc.

[p. 168]

As is well known, the geometer conceives a system of parallel rays as though all the rays of this system intersected in one and the same point at infinity, so that this point at infinity represents for him, as it were, the whole system of parallel rays. For all the rays of the system point to the same point at infinity of the plane, that is, their common direction fixes the same point at infinity. If we were to proceed to another judgment of fact, say to the judgment U, we should have to represent it by a straight line having a different direction from A, and the judgments U1, U2, U3, . . . ad inf. identically worded with U would then be represented by something like the following system of parallels:

etc. which point to another point at infinity of the plane. Thus to every system of parallels, that is, to every direction of rays, there corresponds a point at infinity of the plane, and conversely to every point at infinity of the plane there corresponds a single system of parallels, that is, a single determinate direction of rays.

It is now easy to see that the point at infinity in which the rays of a system intersect is the symbolic representation of that truth which forms the common content of all the identically worded judgments that we symbolize by the system of parallel rays. Thus, for example, the rays A, A1, A2, A3, . . . etc. have a common direction, that is, a common

[p. 17]

point at infinity, which we shall designate by a. The identically worded judgments A, A1, A2, A3, . . . etc. have, however, one and the same truth as their common content; hence the point at infinity a is the proper symbol for the truth which we express in the judgment A.

The reader schooled in geometry will, I believe, be able to make friends with this symbolic representation of a judgment and its truth. Whoever finds such a symbolism uncongenial should consider how often he uses metaphors of this kind, saying that our mind is d i r e c t e d to this or that object, this or that thought, this or that truth. There is an almost endless number of metaphors which all speak of the d i r e c t i o n of our thoughts, and such metaphors are used without the least scruple. Who would take offense at the sentence that in a true judgment our thinking is directed to a truth, or that identically worded judgments run parallel to one another? We therefore represent an act of judgment by a straight line, and identically worded acts of judgment by parallel straight lines. The direction of these lines, or the point at infinity which is common to them, then symbolizes the common truth of those identically worded judgments.

I should like to remark here, further, that e v e r y s i n g u l a r t r u t h c a n a l s o b e c a l l e d a l a w . For we have seen that even such a truth as belongs to a simple judgment of fact lays claim to universality. For if we return to the occurrence of a fact in several judgments, the same truth prevails in all these judgments; that is, this truth is the law that is common to all these judgments.*)

*) Thereby the distinction between judgments a posteriori and a priori is abolished once and for all.

[p. 170]

5. Even on the basis of these considerations we may to a certain degree take up a position toward the three main tendencies of logic: empiricism, rationalism, and mysticism.

However great the weight the empiricist lays upon facts, and however fully we agree with him on this point, he is, oddly enough, little inclined to recognize the significance of the judgment of fact. He often seems to believe that the mere beholding of a thing is already a judging, and if he is nevertheless to draw a distinction between intuition and judgment, he holds that the judgment is a c o m b i n a t i o n of intuitions, or else a combination of ideas. Thus for him the judgment A, for example, is nothing other than a peculiar mode of combining the intuition of the book with the intuition of the table. He also speaks of the relation of these two intuitions or ideas. But if I now take the book away from the table, the combination or relation of the two intuitions is annulled, so that, if the judgment were nothing other than a mere combination or relation of intuitions, it would have to lose its truth at once by the cessation of this combination or relation. But it is precisely the character of judgment that, once it is true, it remains true also for eternity. The empiricist might wish to extricate himself from this predicament by understanding by a judgment of fact not merely the combination of two intuitions but also the memory of this combination. But the memory of the combination passes away, whereas the truth of the judgment is imperishable. In short, the empiricist labors to build up a judgment out of nothing but transitory elements. Sensations, intuitions, memories, complexes of sensations, intuitions, and memories, relations between such complexes: all this pleases him; only of the essence of judgment

[p. 17]

he will not hear, namely of its claim to eternal validity.

There is, however, no such combination of transitory elements, such as sensations, intuitions, or memories, out of which judgments could be built up. Where a judgment announces itself, there too the thought of the eternal or imperishable comes forward, and without this thought there are at bottom no judgments at all and no judging beings, although it easily happens that a judging being, a man, is so little aware of his judging that he does not notice how much the thought of the eternal plays an unavoidable part in each of his judgments, even in the humblest judgment of fact. True empiricists, however, with an unadulterated sense for truth, always struggle through in the end to the insight that without the concept of eternity one cannot be a man, that is, cannot judge. The majority of empiricists, however, try to contrive for themselves a “relativistic” concept of truth, according to which truth is something of relative validity, hence a truth merely for a certain age, or merely for a species and group of beings. This, however, is only a glossing over of the illusionism, skepticism, and nihilism lurking in the background.

As for the rationalist, he is already inclined in a high degree to do justice to the nature of judgment, but for him a proper judgment is only one that does not spring from experience. Only judgments a priori are aeternae veritates. In this nothing else is expressed than a disparagement of facts, a disparagement of those transitory elements, such as sensation, intuition, and memory, without which we should be as little able to judge as without the thought of eternity. The rationalist does not consider that judgments a posteriori are just as much judgments as judgments a priori are,

[p. 172]

and that therefore all the traits which make a judgment a judgment must belong in like manner to both classes. Now it belongs to the essence of judgment to be true or false; hence this belongs also to the essence of judgment a posteriori. It belongs, further, to the essence of truth to be eternal; hence the character of eternity belongs also to the essence of the true judgment a posteriori. If there are any true judgments a posteriori at all, they must be eternally true. Whoever denies this does not really believe at all that there can be true judgments of fact. The rationalist glosses over this lack of a sense for truth by attributing to facts merely probability. It is merely probable that he eats and drinks, breathes and speaks. Thus at bottom he arrives at the very standpoint of the empiricist, and behind his blue-blooded, inflated truths a priori there hides nothing but illusionism, skepticism, and nihilism.

As for the mystic, finally, he is inclined to underrate conceptual, and hence judging, thinking, and he believes that there are secret sources of cognition which have nothing to do with our everyday judging thinking. I am unable, however, to decide anything about clairvoyance, somnambulism, and the like, because I do not know this class of facts and their real significance. But I hardly believe that a mystic could give us anything better than to contemplate things under the aspect of eternity. For I am convinced that this constitutes the highest happiness of man; indeed I hold it to be the only possible happiness, since in all experiences that give us joy the thought must be present that this joy is taking place, hence that it is true. Since every value that there is can subsist only through there being a truth, truth is precisely the absolute

[p. 17]

value. But we have truth only in judgments; hence there can be for us no source of cognition that we could rate higher than judging thinking. Supposing that there are secret powers in us which we do not know, and by means of which the mystic arrives at illuminations that are denied to us, these illuminations are in the end nothing other than cognitions, which must be set forth in judgments. No one can give us more than truth; no one can teach us anything better than to contemplate things sub specie aeterni. And, what is the chief point here, everyone can say of himself that he has the capacity for this way of contemplation, since all judgments, even the most trifling, are made sub specie aeterni. No so-called “intuition of genius” is needed for it, to which many thinkers, such as Schopenhauer, appeal, who make of the idolatry of their own selves a philosophy. In order to see things under the aspect of eternity one needs only to be a man, and nothing more.

In conclusion it may be remarked that the logical theorem, which in this chapter is rather indicated than carried out, is really the fundamental principle of logic, which one is wont to designate as the p r i n c i p l e o f i d e n t i t y . For when I say that the fact may indeed pass away, but its truth is imperishable, what is meant is that this fact has an unshakable, eternal place in the realm of all happening, from which it cannot be dislodged by any other fact. Herein consists its identity. This must, to be sure, be discussed still more clearly; indeed we shall convince ourselves that the whole of logic is really nothing other than a discussion of the principle of identity. For the present it need only be emphasized that the passing away of facts can change nothing in their place in the flux of all happening. However trifling the fact may be, yet

[p. 174]

nothing so momentous can ever happen as could make that trifling fact undone. These negative turns of speech lead us over to the so-called p r i n c i p l e o f c o n t r a d i c t i o n , which at bottom is nothing other than the principle of identity in negative form of expression.

18. Impression-Judgment and Memory-Judgment.

6. In the preceding inquiry we arrived at a distinction between such judgments of fact as are made in the presence of the fact taking place, and such as are made merely on the basis of the memory of that fact: the former I call impression-judgments, the latter, on the other hand, memory-judgments. There now arises the significant question, in what the difference between an impression-judgment and a corresponding memory-judgment consists, since both are supposed to express one and the same factual truth. Only the discussion of this question can lead us to a deepening of the theory of judgment; and it is a theory of judgment that is our concern in all the following inquiries.

The answer to the above question will have a seemingly paradoxical character, which, however, is to be removed in the course of the considerations. For I maintain that e v e n t h e o r i g i n a l i m p r e s s i o n - j u d g m e n t i t s e l f, w h i c h i s made in the presence of the fact, is a memor y-judgment, and that its exceptional position rests on its being the very first among a l l p o s s i b l e m e m o r y - j u d g m e n t s . I mean by this that even the impression-judgment itself can be made only on the basis of a memory of the impression just experienced.

In order to see the truth of this logical theorem, we need only become aware of what is meant by a

[p. 18]

so-called impression. An impression never takes place at a mathematical point of time, never in a m a t h e m a t i c a l n o w . By the mathematical now, however, I understand the absolutely sharp boundary, without time or duration, between past and future. Now it is also evident that there are no impressions without time or duration. Every impression has a certain duration, however slight; it takes place in the e m p i r i c a l n o w , which is a small interval of time with fluid boundaries. In ordinary intercourse one puts the empirical now in place of the mathematical, or, to express it more exactly: one remains unclear about this difference, one confuses the two concepts with each other, one lets oneself be guilty—to designate it by a scholastic technical term—of an equivocation of two concepts, which for practical life, to be sure, very often has no further significance, but for science must entail the most ruinous consequences. For the logician (as also for the physicist) that fraction of a second which is called the empirical now is no negligible magnitude; on the contrary, this small interval of time plays a fundamental role in all the logician’s considerations. For since it is not a mathematical point but an i n t e r v a l , it falls in logic under the same point of view as any other interval whatever, for example a day, a year, or even a thousand years, etc.

For like a larger interval of time, the empirical now too, although it embraces only the fraction of a second, can be thought of as divided into as many parts as one pleases. It therefore encloses within itself a whole mysterious world of happening, the investigation of which is a chief task not only of the natural sciences but also of logic. We shall think of an impression, however slight its duration may be, as consisting of temporal sections, and indeed of boundlessly many temporal sections. If one holds

[p. 176]

this vividly before one’s eyes, one sees also that these different temporal sections cannot be simultaneous, and that therefore, when an impression is spoken of, mention must in any case be made also of p a s t sections of the impression. From this train of thought the sophist could, to be sure, at once make capital: he could assert, namely, that a l l temporal sections of the impression in question are already to be regarded as past, with the exception of its last section. Indeed, he could go further and assert that this last section of the impression too must already be regarded as past, and that only the m a t h e m a t i c a l e n d - p o i n t of the impression could count as an impression proper. But since impressions cannot take place at a mathematical point of time, the sophism just set forth could culminate in the assertion that impressions are through and through nothing but mere memories.

This sophistry rests on a confusion of the impression actually taking place with the concept which we form of an impression in general, and with the phantasm that accompanies our concept of an impression. To protect ourselves against such confusions, we must first of all distinguish well the judging awareness from that mode of awareness in which there is as yet no judgment, which therefore temporally precedes the judging awareness and is its precondition. I call that mode of awareness which is still without judgment, but is nevertheless the precondition of judging, the u n f r e e o r d e t e r m i n e d a w a r e n e s s , in contrast to the f r e e a n d d e t e r m i n i n g a w a r e n e s s in which we can pass judgments on the experiences of the former. As has been said, we must not confuse with each other these two modes of awareness, of which the one is the temporal precondition of the other.

That sophism set forth above, however, also draws our attention to the fact that our concept of an impression

[p. 18]

is a very inadequate one, and that it needs an essential correction. We must bring impression and memory into a far closer relation with each other than we are accustomed to do. For this purpose I permit myself to use the term “sensing,” and in such a sense that sensing embraces both the impression and the memory. In our sensing we must distinguish two opposed or antagonistic tendencies or currents, of which the one lends our sensing the character of impression, the other, by contrast, the character of memory. For in our sensing the tendency can prevail to let every temporal section of the sensation drop at once for the sake of the section just coming, as if it had not been there: we storm forward with our sensing into the future, we are fettered by the impression, it sweeps us along with it and does not let us arrive at a judging awareness. The antagonistic tendency in our sensing consists in each new section of the sensation being warded off, in order to preserve the past sections: it is the tendency of memory in our sensing, by which we free ourselves from the fetters of the impression, in order to be able to cross over into judging awareness.

We cannot succumb with mathematical simultaneity to both impression and memory: we pass from impression to memory and from memory to impression. Our sensing awareness is a rocking between two antagonistic states, a balancing between impression and memory. We can also express this by saying that our sensing awareness has a pulse or a breath, so that it can be represented by a wavy line whose wave-crests represent, say, the states of impression, and whose wave-troughs the states of memory. The more precise

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.             12

[p. 178]

investigation of this wavy line is a task for biology; here we are concerned only to emphasize that sensations are not such uniform states as they are commonly taken to be. In psychological textbooks one commonly speaks merely of the intensity and quality of our sensations, because in doing so one conceives sensation theoretically as though it took place at a mathematical point of time. That, however, amounts to speaking of a cube without extension or of a motion without time. One kills the sensation when one transposes it into a mathematical point of time; one makes it into a nothing. And one falls into this error because one succumbs to the equivocation of the mathematical and the empirical now.

But if one keeps in view that an impression must necessarily have temporal duration, one recognizes at once how intimately impression and memory belong together. One commonly takes for memories only those states in which, after a considerable time, we return to a past fact. But if one reflects that that capacity which is called memory must always be there when one wishes to constate an impression, since otherwise the impression would be forgotten in a trice, one sees at once that the transition from the impression to the judgment about it necessarily takes place on the basis of memory even when we make the judgment in the presence of the fact. It might be objected against this that we can, after all, look uninterruptedly at a body, for example at this red ball, and meanwhile also pronounce the judgment: “I see red”; so that something like a memory is not necessary at all, since the impression continues without interruption even during the judging. To this it need only be replied that it takes a quite imperceptibly short time for us to pass from the impression to the memory, from there to the judgment, and back again to the impression. Just as

[p. 18]

one does not notice one’s own pulse and breathing if one does not pay particular attention to them, so one does not notice the pulsation of one’s own sensing between impression and memory. To carry this out in greater detail is, as has been said, the task of biology; here we are concerned merely to show that an impression-judgment is only the first among all possible memory-judgments. The impression must go under if the memory is to rise, and if we are to be able to arrive at a judging awareness of the impression. Memory is the bridge between the impression and the experience of eternity, or the judgment.

7. It is of far greater importance than one would be inclined to believe at first glance to think of the impression as divided into boundlessly many temporal sections. For if one conceives the impression as though it took place at a mathematical point of time, one falls into the sensualist heresy of the s i m p l i c i t y of our impressions. The first chief representative of this doctrine in modern philosophy is L o c k e , who, as is well known, formulated the famous distinction between simple and complex ideas (or representations). Among the simple ideas he counts in the first place the impressions of hardness, softness, cold, warmth, whiteness, redness, sweetness, etc. He also speaks of simple ideas which can be conveyed to us not only through one sense-organ but through several channels of sense, such as extension, figure, motion, and rest. Through the so-called inner sense, too, we are said to receive simple ideas, such as representing, remembering, distinguishing, judging, knowing, willing, etc. Some other simple ideas, such as pleasure, pain, power, existence, unity, etc., are said to be conveyable to us both through all the channels of sense and through the inner sense. I will say nothing of how all the factors of mental life are here mixed up together in motley fashion, and ask only whether we

12*

[p. 180]

may allow sensible impressions to count as something simple? Since on our view a duration of time necessarily belongs to every impression, and since an interval of time must be thought of as divisible into as many sections as one pleases, there is for us no doubt either that we must think of an impression as consisting of boundlessly many sections.

To be sure, this view must not be misunderstood. An impression can never be divided, because one can never get at an impression. It is something so fleeting that when it is constated it is no longer there, so that one can refer to it only through a memory. May we therefore call it simple? I hardly think so. For an entire complex of various kinds of impressions escapes just as irretrievably as does each constituent of it. In general no operation can ever be performed on the impressions themselves; for if anything is to be operated upon in them, they must be taking place; but if they take place, then it is all over with them as well. Yet for this reason they must not be called simple; what belongs to them is not simplicity but uniqueness, by which we mean that one cannot have the same impression twice.

Nevertheless impressions are, as has been said, something composite. We find this first of all in our judging awareness of the temporal constitution of impressions. Indeed we can say in a certain sense that we can divide impressions not only in thought but also in reality. For two impressions can, under certain circumstances, stand for each other, for our theoretical insight, or be h o m o l o g o u s , as when we look at the stamen of a flower first with the naked eye and then under the magnifying glass. We then obtain homologous impressions, one of which is much richer in details than the other. What in the image obtained with the naked eye runs together

[p. 18]

and appears uniform is broken up, in the enlarged image, into a multiform manifold. Natural science will be able to furnish us with countless examples of such a decomposition of our impressions; indeed we may say that natural science accomplishes hardly anything else at all than to put, in place of our crude impressions, homologous images worked out with astonishing artifice, or else homologous phantasms (as in the hypotheses about light, about the atomistic composition of bodies, etc.), which afford us a surprising insight into the connections of nature. What is natural science but an eternal struggle against the narrowness and inadequacy of our sense-organs, and what does it want — to put it figuratively — but to endow us with a thousand new senses? In all its teachings, then, it is a living protest against the doctrine of the simplicity of our impressions. But it is not at all necessary, either, to appeal to the natural sciences. When two persons observe the same object, the one who has the sharper eye will be able to obtain an impression with more details than the other, who has weaker vision at his disposal. In the impression of the one person we find much broken up that in the impression of the other is a confluent uniformity; and this suffices fully to reject the doctrine of the simplicity of our impressions.

Curiously enough, many philosophizing physicists nowadays proclaim the principle of the simplicity of sensations, although this doctrine stands in direct contradiction to the spirit of natural science. Sensations, we are told, are the e l e m e n t s of the structure of the world. And one hears this said by physicists! It would be incomprehensible, were it not plainly evident that L o c k e and most especially B e r k e l e y are founding schools in modern

[p. 182]

natural science. Berkeley’s phenomenalism has received a paradoxical stamp in S c h o p e n h a u e r ’s sentence, “The world is my idea,” and positively dominates cultivated thinking. Now one can easily tell oneself that whoever had no faculty of judgment would, among other things, be unable to pass the judgment that the world is his idea. One finds a world only in judgment, and whoever is devoid of all judgment has no world either. What matters, then, is to pass true judgments, in order to be able to share in the true world; for with mere ideas one has as yet no world, and least of all the true world. The same holds of the sentence: the world is sensation, or its elements are sensation. In the face of this sensualist aberration logic must set up t h e p r i n c i p l e o f t h e c o m p o s i t e ness    of    i m p r e s s i o n s ; it runs: Every impression is boundlessly composite; or, more correctly: we must think every impression as consisting of boundlessly many parts.

With this principle, however, little has yet been gained, for we must ask ourselves why we merely t h i n k that the impression consists of parts, and why we do not also actually divide it? The answer is: b e c a u s e i t i s n o t g i v e n u s t o b e come aware in one second as often as we p l e a s e ; we cannot, for example, form a thousand judgments in one second. Were it possible to become aware in judging a thousand times in the second, then the second would be a c t u a l l y divided into a thousand sections, and we should then be able to perceive all that takes place in each thousandth of a second. Could we become aware in judging a trillion times in one second, then every trillionth of a second too would betray its secret to us. Since it is a truth that we cannot rouse ourselves to a judgment in one second as often as we please, it now remains only

[p. 18]

to divest this truth of its negative form, and thereby to present it more intelligibly. We must, namely, tell ourselves that within our sensing there exists a tendency not to let us come to awareness, and that it is this tendency which lends our sensation the character of impression. But if only this tendency existed in our sensing, we should never arrive at a judgment at all; we should be continually clapped in the slave’s fetters of the impression. Since, however, we are in the end nonetheless able to rouse ourselves to judgments about our impressions, we must of necessity assume in our sensing also a tendency running counter to the former, and this is memory, which helps us to a judging awareness of what we have experienced. The doctrine of the compositeness of the impression leads us, then, to the view that within sensing awareness two counter-running directions are to be distinguished, of which the one is the binding, and checks the arising of judgment, while the other, on the contrary, is the loosening one and is able to lead to judgment.

8.
