Since we thus recognize that our sensing awareness has an intermittent character, that is, is a play of waves between impression and memory, and represents a kind of mental breathing: we see also what great significance the speed of this breathing must have for the character of human intelligence. The whole world of our sensible perception would take on a different appearance if we could come 10 times, 100 times, 1000 times, etc., more quickly from the impression to the memor y and from there to the judgment t h a n w e a r e a c t u a l l y a b l e t o c o m e . Impressions which now seem to us simple, or more correctly uniform, would then be replaced by homologous impressions which could show what was formerly uniform

[p. 184]

resolved into a multiplicity. In the face of an ever-increasing speed of judging awareness, not a single one of our so-called simple sense-qualities could hold its own, and each would in the end have to break apart into qualities of different kinds. That fraction of a second which is needed for awareness of an impression is therefore of decisive significance for the whole sensible picture of the world that unrolls before us; and one may fitly call it t h e s e n s i b l e c h a r a c t e r i s t i c of our faculty of judgment. If one imagines the impression and the memory that follows it represented by a wavy line, the duration of one wave may be regarded as the measure of that characteristic.

These considerations might lead a thinker of fantastic bent to the question whether there are not actually also beings whose faculty of judgment possesses a higher (or lower) sensible characteristic than ours: whether there are not, then, beings who arrive at a judgment about the impression 10 times, 100 times, 1000 times faster (or slower) than we? Such fantasies, however, are good only for obscuring the positive side of our insight. We have quite enough to do in giving an account of the nature of our own faculty of judgment, and we are still so much at the ABC of this account that we cannot allow ourselves the luxury of speculating about the existence or nonexistence of fabulous beings. Whether they exist or not, this much is certain, that for these beings too there could be nothing better than to share in truth. In truth, however, we too can share, and the whole difference which a higher sensible characteristic could bring with it would be a quicker tempo in the enjoyment of truths of fact and a more effortless penetration into the connection of truths. Incidentally, nearly all our research aims at overcoming, as far as possible by artificial means, that limitation,

[p. 18]

which is indicated by the sensible characteristic. For it is the highest ambition of the investigator of nature, as of the logician, to cast glances into the secrets of that occurrence which takes place in the millionth and the trillionth part of the second.

Since we now conceive the impression as something boundlessly composite, we must also tell ourselves that it is i n e x h a u s t i b l e for our cognition. What we draw from the impression is always only a memory of the impression, and it hardly needs saying now that with a greater speed of our awareness we could draw more memories from our impressions. But however great the speed of our awareness might be, an exhaustion of the impression would never by any means be attainable. We can also express this by saying that our constating judgment is never able to replace the impression itself, for we should have to pass infinitely many judgments in order to do full justice to an impression; indeed a being of infinitely quicker, that is, supertemporal awareness would also have no impressions any more and would be relieved of every toil of any work of judgment. We, however, must have impressions in order to be able to arrive at memory and judgment, and must not imagine that we have replaced the impression by a judgment. We can report on our experiences in sentences, and such sentences count in intercourse from man to man as a surrogate for immediate experiences; but the immediate experience itself is and remains something irreplaceable, because the impression is something inexhaustible. This is the creed of genuine empiricism, and this creed rests on truth. Only one should not conceive the judgment as an act whose task it is to exhaust the impression; nor should one for that reason think lightly of the faculty of judgment and assail it with a foolish skepticism, because it does not set itself impossible,

[p. 186]

that is, from the human standpoint absurd, tasks. The judgment grasps of the impression as much as the memory following hard upon the impression offers it; and as soon as the judgment fulfills this task, it has also done justice to the impression, that is, it is true. A being that possessed a b s o l u t e truth would have neither impressions nor memory, nor would it need to concern itself with individual judgments. We, however, are beings who grasp truth only piecemeal in individual judgments, which also means that we are never able to exhaust our experiences in judgments.

Every sensible impression is comparable to a boundless ocean, on whose shore we stand in order to draw from it a handful of memory, which we preserve in the judgment. We have of the impression always only as much as we draw from it by means of memory. Even when the impression is described as “present,” we have in our judgment about it yet only as much as memory offers us. We are then merely, as a rule, in the advantageous position of being able to proceed at once to equal, repeating impressions. If, for example, I look at the red ball and say that it is red, then I have come from the impression to memory and to judgment, but am in the advantageous position of being able at once to return again to similar impressions; whereas, if the ball were no longer there, and I had to pass a judgment about its color from memory alone, the possibility would then also be taken from me of coming back from memory and judgment to an impression of the ball. The difference between impression-judgment and memory-judgment can thus be illustrated by the following simile. He who judges from memory alone is comparable to a man who has drawn water from a spring with his pitcher, and then goes away from the spring, and so cannot

[p. 18]

draw from it again. But he who judges on the basis of the “present impression” remains standing with his pitcher at the spring and can, if need be, fill the pitcher again and again. But even he is not relieved of the toil of drawing, and he always has at his disposal from the spring only as much as he has drawn from it with his pitcher at the time. The spring is not his, but what he takes from it at any time. So too of our impressions we possess only as much as we are able to draw from them by means of memory; and this holds even when the impressions are “present.”

9. These considerations furnish us also with a way of conceiving the mental development of the child in its first years of life. The infant does not lack impressions; what it lacks, however, is what we characterized above as a kind of mental breathing. It lacks the play of waves between impression and memory; or, more exactly speaking, this play of waves is so weak that the wavy line by which we represent sensing approaches in its case quite to the straight line. As the physical breath is lacking to the fetus in the mother’s womb, so the infant lacks mental breath. Only slowly and gradually does a rhythm between impression and memory announce itself, and it takes a year and more until this rhythm has grown so strong that the child is able also to form words, as signs of the fact that in it memory and impression already stand out from one another. It is not our task here to depict the mental course of development of the child, and I mention this subject only because I should like to suggest that the examination of our own activity of judgment furnishes us the means of making in some way comprehensible stages of mental development which stand below our own. Only to the extent that we learn to appraise our own activity of judgment logically do we have any prospect

[p. 188]

of being able to interpret the evolution of lower mental stages. Thus we find, for example, that what takes in the adult only a fraction of a second (namely the passing over from the impression to memory and to judgment) takes the child several years; so that we, so to speak, experience compressed into one second what the child is able to experience in years. We pass through in one second the analogous evolution which the child passes through in the course of years. And only from out of our own evolution of judgment are we able to cast any light at all upon the mental development of the child.

In short, a doctrine of the evolution of the human mind can be created only if one has first created a logic, that is, a doctrine of judgment. If someone has not yet worked out for himself a conception of what he calls a judgment, how does he propose to make intelligible to us that process whose whole significance culminates in its leading in the end to the capacity for judgment? If he is unable to become aware of his own activity of judgment, whence will he take the power to point out those preconditions which finally lead up to judgment? If he is unable to be our guide in that realm of the mind which is lit by the sunlight of judgment, how will he make that darkness which still lacks the faculty of judgment in any way intelligible to us? Only by examining our own activity of judgment do we obtain the logical means for founding a doctrine of the evolution of the mind. By which it is not to be said that these merely logical means suffice to deal with the lower stages of development; for without close observation of the lower stages a science of them cannot, of course, come into being.

Sensualist thinkers, however, generally go the reverse way; that is, they imagine that they can follow a reverse

[p. 18]

method. A doctrine of judgment, a developed science of logic, would not be necessary in order to grasp the evolution of the mind! They say that they are building up the science of the mind f r o m b e l o w , that they therefore wish to start from the s i m p l e s t elements: sensations, ideas, etc., in order to climb up to ever more composite structures, until at the summit of their inquiry they arrive at the act of judgment and at the syllogism. They wish to add impressions to impressions, that is, the transitory to the transitory, in order to let the eternal validity of an act of judgment emerge from such a sum. And that they call: building from below. They do not notice that they do not stand below, but had to climb to a certain mental summit before they came to occupy themselves with the science of the mind. They do not notice that only upon that summit did the sense for truth open to them, and that with the torch of truth they can now descend also into the twilights and darknesses of the depths. They do not notice that they are building up the science of the mind coming from above downward, that, starting from free judging awareness, they descend to bound sensing awareness. They imagine that one can extinguish one’s own torch of judgment, in order then to look about in the darkness of mere sensing to see how things really go on there. It is an error like that of someone who should believe that he could descend in a diving bell to the bottom of the sea without taking air along. We take the light of our judgment with us everywhere, into whatever we ponder, and see even the deeper regions of mental development only in the light that we have brought with us from the height of our region of judgment. There is, then, no “isolating” of sensibility, as K a n t thinks, but only a considering of this sensibility in the light of the theory of judgment, or logic. The higher now this

[p. 190]

theory of judgment is developed, the more prospect we have of illuminating also the darknesses of the sensing mode of awareness. But if someone with C o n d i l l a c imagines in his fancy a statue which he equips step by step with new senses, in order to show how from the night of unconsciousness the day of free awareness emerges, he only makes a fool of himself; for he carries, without noticing it, his own judgment into the statue, and while he makes himself believe that he is animating the statue only piece by piece, he has secretly and behind his own back — admittedly without knowing it — filled it wholly with his own mind from the very beginning. Instead of thus hoodwinking ourselves, it is better to tell ourselves from the outset that we can behold nothing in the world otherwise than in the light of our judgment, and that it therefore matters above all to fan this by logical inquiry to an ever brighter shining, so that it may become ever more serviceable for the exploration of the obscurities of our sensible life.

What I have just been at pains to set forth is nothing other than the fundamental methodological rule of philosophical thinking: t h e p r i n c i p l e o f t h e p r i m a c y o f l o g i c within the humanities and philosophy in gene r a l . For although logic is the last thing at which we arrive, it is the first thing that we must set forth in philosophy. And although the sensible impression and memory are the first thing that overwhelms our mind, they are in philosophy the last that we have to fathom. In this lies by no means a disparagement of our sensibility; on the contrary, one might rather speak, on this conception, of a disparagement of logic. For by beginning with logic and setting forth sensibility as the last of our inquiry, our thinking remains continually directed upon it, and for us the

[p. 19]

whole construction of a logic becomes a means of investigating sensibility.

Thus, for example, we have attempted in the foregoing considerations to cast some light on the relation of impression and memory. We could accomplish this in no other way than by starting from our judgments and comparing impression-judgments with memory-judgments. Their content is supposed to be the same; wherein, then, do they differ? We imagined the interval of time between the impression-judgment and the memory-judgment as becoming ever shorter, until finally their temporal distance became zero; and so we recognized that memory has already in the impression-judgment the same significance as in the memory-judgments. The logical theorem that an impression-judgment is nothing but the first among all possible memory-judgments also casts a light on the relation of impression and memory.

19. The Duality of Judgment.

10. We have recognized that we can come from the impression to judgment only by means of memory, that it is therefore a double experience from which we must start if we wish to pass a judgment of fact. Hereby, however, it is also explained why our judgments of fact in the first place, like all judgments in general, have a two-membered structure, that is, must consist of a subject and a predicate. For in judgment both the impression and the memory, that is, the whole double experience on which we rely, receive a conceptual character: they become, precisely, a conceived or conceptual double experience; and indeed to the impression and the memory as transitory experiences there correspond in judgment the subject and the predicate as experiences of concepts. In determined or sensing awareness we have

[p. 192]

an impression and a memory following hard upon it; in determining or judging awareness we have accordingly a subject-concept and a predicate-concept following hard upon it. With this conception the first step toward a theory of judgment has really been taken. Ancient logic had not even got as far as this first step: it accepted the dual articulation of judgment as something self-evident, and since it was not at all surprised that we judge in subjects and predicates, it did not even arrive at the proper problem of logic. The greatest of all wonders, namely that our linguistic sentences consist of a subject and a predicate: this proper logical wonder left minds indifferent. Men passed by the nature of their own faculty of cognition with dull senses.

Now we must tell ourselves, however, that our judging awareness rests upon our sensing awareness, and that the duality of our judgments cannot be plucked out of the air, but must be grounded in our sensing awareness. In the judgment of fact, to be sure, we let count only what we have just sensed; we let the impression count in particular, whereby it is raised to the subject-concept, and we let the memory count as drawn from the impression, whereby it is raised to a predicate-concept which refers to the subject-concept. We must not, however, so misinterpret this conception as if we could already within determined sensing awareness distinguish the impression from the memory and refer the latter to the former! Were this the case, our sensing would already be a judging. Only by pondering our judging and comparing impression-judgments with memory-judgments do we infer that our activity of judgment must already have been prepared in sensing by

[p. 19]

the opposition of impression and memory. In vain, however, would one try to sink oneself into one’s sensing and, thus absorbed in mere sensation, to wish to verify, or rather to investigate, the relation of impression and memory. It is among the most comical self-deceptions of psychologists that they suppose they can, by violently tearing themselves out of judging awareness, immerse themselves in sensing, in order to investigate sensing by means of so-called “inner perception.” For if sensation really comes over us, then we cannot yet behave judgingly, and if we behave judgingly, then at least in the moment of judging we are no longer immersed in sensation. When I therefore combat “inner perception” without cease, this has the sense that within states of sensation we can never investigate these themselves, but only through the investigation of our judging are we able to uncover the mysterious states of our sensing.

11. In order to escape the dangerous confusion of sensing and judging awareness, it is advisable to direct our gaze straight at the transition from sensing to judging. That there must at any time be a moment when we pass over from sensing awareness into judging awareness, anyone can tell himself; but what happens in that moment is a most tricky question. We speak of an occurrence only because we have the capacity of sensing, and quite especially also because our sensations are accompanied by feelings of pleasure and displeasure. In that moment, however, in which we pass from sensing to judgment, there cannot be added to the sensing again a new sensation or a new feeling of pleasure or displeasure, because we should then still be within sensing awareness. T h e

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.            13

[p. 194]

transition from sensing into judging cannot, then, be characterized by any newly added s e n s a t i o n s o r f e e l i n g s w h a t e v e r. Herein consists the wonderful peculiarity of that moment in which we begin to conduct ourselves cognizingly.

No sensation and no feeling announces this moment to us; for we may well take pleasure (or displeasure) in the fact that we cognize, but this pleasure or displeasure is not the cognizing. The moment of cognition is neither a sensation, nor a feeling, nor any experience akin to these. It is therefore easy to understand that inveterate sensualists are never able to do justice to this moment. For the sensualist only what is sensed and felt counts, but since the moment of cognition cannot be sensed or felt, the sensualist does not let it count. It is, to be sure, a self-contradiction to let what is sensed or felt count, yet not to let the fact of this letting-count count; nonetheless a man of sensualistic bent of mind will always demand that that moment of cognition be presented to him in the form of a sensation or a feeling. To accomplish this, however, is an impossibility, for should a sensation or a feeling be added in the moment of cognition to what is sensed or felt, this would be a falsification of that sensation which we are just supposed to cognize. We should therefore have to falsify the moment of cognition together with what is to be cognized, were we to wish to convince the sensualist that there is a moment of cognition. In vain too do we point out that from that moment of cognition there can spring an act of judgment, which presents itself linguistically in a sentence; for the sensualist will acknowledge the presence of the sentence only because it consists of sounds, which can be sensed. But these sounds are, after all, not there merely to be sounds:

[p. 19]

they are surely meant to represent a sense! This too he grants; for when we hear the sounds, we picture to ourselves in our sensing fantasy that fact which constitutes the sense of the spoken sentence. Thus all judging of the sensualist is directed to striking the moment of cognition, in which we neither sense nor feel, from our experiences, and declaring it null and void.

This, and nothing else, is the tendency of sensualism. Impressions, memories, feelings of pleasure and displeasure, etc., are to come together in great multiplicity, and so form, as it were, a dough of sensation, which is kneaded in the mental workshop, formed into a cake, and baked, in order then to be duly eaten and digested as cognition. For what is the use of truth, if it cannot be eaten? This is the deepest and most secret sense of sensualism. It will be objected to me that there are, after all, sensualists of various shades: crude materialist sensualists, subtler skeptical sensualists, refined pantheist sensualists, indeed also monotheist sensualists, such as B e r k e l e y . I, however, believe that there is no difference of principle between them. To the common sensualist truth is a sausage, to the subtler an oyster. The main thing remains that it must be edible.

However much one may balk at acknowledging the point of transition from sensing to judging, it cannot be denied by fair means. S i n c e t h i s m o m e n t i s n e i t h e r a s e n s i n g nor a feeling, it must be conceived as a mathe m a t i c a l p o i n t o f t i m e . In this mathematical now we take nothing away from the impression and the memory, which have just taken place; nor do we add anything to them but merely their validity, and indeed their eternal validity. For the impression has already passed, and the memory of it has just reached its final limit;

13*

[p. 196]

and our acknowledgment therefore applies to something already past. We find in the memory, and consequently in the impression, something imperishable, and this finding of the eternal in the transitory is what constitutes the content of the moment of cognition. We grasp the fleeting sub specie aeterni. If, however, this finding of the eternal in the transitory is to attain a significance for our further cognition, there must proceed from it a vital act, an act of judgment, which makes itself known as a speech-act or the like. Through such an act the mere knowledge of the cognizing moment becomes a sensibly graspable fact, and we have now, in accordance with the wish of the sensualist, a sensible fact before us which bears witness to that significant moment in which the sun of truth shone into our sensations.

One might easily misinterpret this presentation as if the moment of cognition were something subsisting by itself, and the whole double experience of impression and memory must now fall apart without connection. It must therefore be emphasized that we do not a d d the eternal to the impression and the memory as something foreign (for in that case our cognition would be a falsification of what is experienced), but that we f i n d the eternal in the impression and in the memory: and indeed find it as that which is in them before we had yet found it. In short, the transitory is a phenomenon of the eternal, and cognition is the finding of the eternal in the phenomenon; the whole process of impression and memory, however, is but a struggle toward that moment where the eternal veiled in it is revealed to us. Were this struggle a more powerful one, that is, one 10 times, 100 times, 1000 times quicker, we should have 10, 100, 1000 times more moments of cognition in one second than we are now capable of, and then we should, instead of

[p. 19]

the present impressions, have homologous impressions which contain multiplicities 10, 100, 1000 times greater than ours. To be sure, it seems to us, when we perceive any things, as if our cognizing or finding glance were continuously in activity; this semblance arises, however, only because, according to our concepts, the tenth part of a second is a very small interval of time, which may fitly be neglected. Because we keep the finding glance of our mind constantly in r e a d i n e s s , we believe that this readiness for cognition is already the finding glance itself. The fact, however, is that impressions and memories pass by our finding mind like a veil of cloud, as it were, and that our glimpse of eternity is able to shine rhythmically through it. That drift of clouds of impressions and memories, however, does not cease during the whole of our waking life, and there is no duration, however slight, in which the veil of impressions and memories would suffer an interruption. This is what the sensualists notice, and the kernel of truth in their error is that a state of mind is never by any means attainable for us in which the dark drift of clouds of our transitory experiences would not becloud our glimpse of eternity. Their error consists, however, in their denying our finding glance, which shines through the clouds of our experiences at mathematical points of time. Instead of denying it, they ought rather to become aware how weak that finding glance is, and how greatly the nature of our organization makes it impossible to accelerate it in its intermittent flashing. They would then recognize that it is unworthy of an investigator of truth to deny that glimpse of eternity, but that it must be his task to procure some substitute for the natural weakness of our human finding glance. That is what

[p. 198]

natural science really does, and it does so because there is contained in it an immanent logical striving, a genuine, unadulterated striving for truth, which, however, must not remain merely immanent but must be brought to self-consciousness in logic, in order that it may react fructifyingly upon the natural sciences. Thus in the name of this productive natural science and in the name of a productive logic I believe I must contest sensualism.

Admittedly I contest at the same time rationalism too, which supposes that that cloud of impressions and memories which veils the finding glance could for a while be torn quite apart. The rationalist extravagance believes it can push aside the curtain of our experiences and through the open gap behold the eternal face to face and draw truths from it. The rationalist misunderstands the nature of that “silent thinking” which he gladly practices, and believes that silent thinking can be free of sensibility; he overestimates it and falls into a conceit which can become as dangerous to science as sensualism. In particular the distinction of cognitions a priori and a posteriori rests on the fancy that man in “silent thinking” is free or independent of sensibility and can draw from the source of eternity a new class of insights.

12. In the above I have tried to set forth that nature of cognition which one calls its o b j e c t i v i t y . The glimpse of eternity is objective, because it alters nothing in the fact to be constated, takes nothing away from it, and fundamentally also adds nothing to it, since the eternal too is not added to the sensation, but is merely found in it. I must remark, however, that the term “objective” belongs among the expressions most abused and most ambiguous in philosophy. Every cognition can in the end

[p. 19]

also be called subjective, because it is the cognition of a subject. Nothing at all can be done with the expressions “subjective” and “objective,” and one is almost safe in asserting that wherever a philosopher uses these technical terms he is, knowingly or unknowingly, speaking in favor of a sophistry. With a judgment the question is never whether it is subjective or objective, but whether it is true or false. For subjective it is, as said, in any case, because it is passed by a person, and objective too it may well be in all cases, for some object or other even the erroneous or false judgment will after all have. The expressions objective and subjective have become naturalized in logic only because one wished to avoid the concepts of truth and error. The concept of truth in particular had to be avoided, because one secretly did not believe in it at all, and yet wanted to practice logic. But if one would not do without the expressions “subjective” and “objective,” one may call the transitory in our experiences subjective, the eternal in them objective. The finding glance, too, which finds the eternal in the transitory, may be called objective (or true).

In this sense, then, I say that in our cognition we have an objective attitude, and that our whole humanity rests on this wonderful capacity for an objective (truth-conforming) attitude. Through the objective cognizing glance we find in the transitory something eternal and therefore also set it down as holding eternally: as holding not only for us but for all men, indeed for all judging beings whatever. He who constates a fact does so in the name of mankind, which represents to him the totality of judging beings; at least in logic and in the other sciences one understands by the constating of a fact the

[p. 200]

constating in the name of the human race. Of this the natural scientists above all are well aware; they know that they represent, so to speak, the observing eye of mankind. To the true judgment of fact belongs universal validity; that is, it is passed as if all men and all judging beings whatever could share in it. I speak of all judging beings because, even if there were beings of quicker awareness than ours, this could change nothing in the truth of our judgments of fact. If other beings could find ten facts where we are able to find only one, it would be merely a question of translating our manner of intuition into theirs, and theirs into ours. An analogue of this we have, for example, in our being able to translate the mode of sensing of hearing into the manner of intuition of wave-motion, as acoustics teaches. Another analogue, still more easily understood, is when we translate the image which we have with the free eye into that which a moderately magnifying glass offers, and this again into the image of a solar microscope, etc. I cannot here go further into the logical character of the so-called “hypotheses of natural science” concerning light, heat, chemical combination, etc., and reserve this for a special work. The foundation for a logical theory of all hypotheses of natural science is contained in the principle that the speed of our judging awareness is a limited one and that greater speeds are conceivable, whereby that which we perceive as a simple quality can be thought of as resolved into a plurality.

I must here still mention a widespread error about the nature of our cognizing glance. One often takes cognizing for an i m a g e which we make for ourselves of the world, and supposes that truth consists in the correspondence

[p. 19]

of this image with the actual picture of the world. The world would then exist in two copies, once as an actual picture of the world, the other time as a picture of the world agreeing with the first. But if now the first picture of the world is cognized by a second picture of the world, then this second would have to be cognized by a third, the third by a fourth, and so on without limit; that is, we should never arrive at a cognition at all. An image or an imaging is no cognizing. A camera obscura is not yet a cognizing being because something is imaged in it. The confusion of cognizing with an image arises from the fact that one takes the memory-image for a cognizing already. Our sensing contains a double experience, and this double experience misleads thinkers again and again into taking cognizing for an imaging. Cognizing, however, takes place at a mathematical point of time and, as an experience of eternity, is to be well distinguished from the transitory double experience of impression and memory.

In conclusion it may be remarked that in our experiences we never have merely a single sensation, but simultaneously a great multiplicity of sensations. This multiplicity of simultaneous sensations is grasped by the cognizing glance all at once, and can then be set forth in a whole series of constating judgments. Many logicians and psychologists speak of a so-called n a r r o w n e s s o f o u r c o n s c i o u s n e s s ; yet it is not easy to understand what is meant by it. One seems to assume that a multiplicity of simultaneous sensations cannot be grasped simultaneously; but it remains unintelligible how one conceives the grasping of sensations, and why one expects of this grasping that it cannot do justice at once to simultaneously occurring sensations. One seems to imagine that the entrance gate to the cognizing

[p. 202]

mind were a narrow slit, which does not permit many simultaneous sensations to be taken cognizance of at once, but always grants access only to a few sensations. Now that slit is, to be sure, narrow enough, so narrow that it cannot possibly be any narrower; it is the mathematical point of time at which the light of cognition falls upon our experiences; but this is no hindrance to this light’s illuminating a great multiplicity of sensations simultaneously. If there were such a slit of cognition as one seems to assume, then, if only for a short while, all sensing would be overcome; the sensible curtain that conceals eternity would at least for a while be lifted, and each of us would be for that while no man, but a god. Then there would also be cognitions a priori which spring from an essentially different source from cognitions a posteriori; for if someone is able to be a god even for a short time, he can certainly bring out of that state all manner of cognition which remains denied to the rest of us children of men dwelling in this world. What is incomprehensible is only how such men, who are able to be gods for a time and have in this way acquired cognitions a priori, still think it worth the trouble to become men again, and even to mix their divine a priori cognition with our human a posteriori cognition into a hodgepodge.

13. If what we find with the cognizing glance is not to be lost, but is itself to become the occasion of a further-penetrating cognizing, we must rouse ourselves out of the cognizing glance to an action by which we set up a monument to that moment: this action is the judgment, which stands there in language as a sentence. In it we live through once more in a determining manner what we experienced in sensing in a determined

[p. 19]

way. Instead of impression and memory we now have a subject-concept and a predicate-concept, or rather subject-symbols and predicate-symbols. Indeed the finding glance itself presents itself in the sentence through a concept of its own (which, by its very nature, for that very reason remains the same in all sentences), through the so-called copula. The copula expresses what we find in every cognizing, namely the eternal, or that which is, in the fleeting phenomenon; but at the same time it also expresses the finding of this eternal, namely the knowledge of that which is; only this second signification of it is less emphasized. In the copula “is” we think predominantly of mere being, and only in an indistinct way of the validity of this being in our knowledge. Whence this comes will be explained later. In language the copula need not appear independently, but can fuse with the predicate as a suffix or (in some languages) occasionally even be omitted altogether. One need not regard it as a third member of the sentence, because at bottom it is a member common to all judgments and merely indicates that judgment is being passed.

The theory of judgment now leads us over to the theory of the concept. It is a chief characteristic of scholastic logic that it wants to make the theory of the concept the foundation of logic. In contrast to scholastic logic, dynamic logic sets up the fundamental methodological rule of the p r i m a c y o f j u d g m e n t . It is the act of judgment that creates concepts; indeed, properly speaking there are no concepts at all outside of judging. For if we direct our thinking to a single concept, e.g. the concept A, we ought also to say to ourselves “that we are thinking of the concept A,” and this is already a judgment. There are constating judgments in which we constate that we are thinking of a concept; only this kind of judgment is usually not noticed. Logic

[p. 204]

demands of us, however, that, when we think something, we should at once also become aware of it. If, then, we think of the concept A, we must in logic also become aware of the judgment by which we think of the concept A. From this it is evident that single concepts cannot occur isolated by themselves in our logically aware thinking. Only in judging do we have concepts, and it may then be that the concepts serve us merely as means for passing the judgment; but it is also possible that the judgment serves us only as a means for thinking of a concept. We cannot go more closely into this very important distinction, because that would require the construction of a systematic theory of judgment and of a systematic theory of the concept founded upon it. It suffices to have indicated here that concepts can play a twofold role in our logical thinking: either they are harnessed into the judgment in order to be bearers of a truth, and then they are called dependent; or else our judgment is harnessed in order to become the bearer of a concept, and then the concept is made independent. These two modes of judgment spring from two modes of awareness, which we shall later come to know as direct and contrary awareness.

Through the making independent of concepts, endless abuse has nested itself in logic; indeed, logic has been completely falsified by it, for in starting from independent concepts, which are the result of very far-advanced logical thinking, men stood the whole system of logic on its head. It is therefore expedient, for the time being, to speak of concepts only insofar as they are dependent, that is, harnessed into the judgment in order to bear its truth. The dependence of such concepts shows itself in the fact that, by virtue of their role in the judgment, they have a specific character, in consequence of which subject-concepts must be distinguished from predicate-concepts,

[p. 19]

which by their nature can be called just as opposed to each other as are impression and memory, from which they receive their character. But if we were to make concepts independent from the outset and make them the starting-point of a logic, it would be impossible to arrive at a theory of judgment; for once concepts are conceived from the outset as independent, they resist all combination, and the logician fares with them as a ruler fares whose subjects have all become anarchists. In short, with concepts made independent from the outset no theory of judgment, and hence no logic, can be built.

Concepts arise through the act of judgment, and indeed first of all through the judgment of fact. The cognizing glance places the eternal, which it finds in the transitory, beneath both the memory and the impression, and thus forms two concepts, in order merely to present a fact through the relation of the one to the other. Concepts are thus acts of the cognizing glance within determining or judging awareness. As for the subject-concepts first of all, we cannot yet do them justice here, because our investigation has been artificially narrowed from the very beginning, in that we purposely confine ourselves to a theory of the single judgment. Now, however, an impression never occurs as a single one, accompanied by no other impression; rather our sensing awareness is determined simultaneously by a great multiplicity of connected impressions, and the single impression is a definite impression only by belonging to the connected multiplicity of simultaneous impressions and occupying a definite position within it. To determine an impression, therefore, its position in the context of a simultaneous multiplicity of impressions must be characterized; a whole series of judgments must be passed upon it,

[p. 206]

which fix its position. All these judgments have the impression in question, or rather the subject-concept, as their center; so that the subject-concept is always the starting-point of a bundle of judgments connected in it. The theory of the subject-concept can be carried through only if one investigates connections of judgments.

Our task takes a simpler shape if for the time being we restrict ourselves merely to the predicate-concept. We can then dwell upon a s i n g l e impression and artificially confine ourselves to t h e single impression-judgment. As such, the predicate-concept does not wish to be the starting-point of a whole bundle of judgments, as the subject-concept does; it stands wholly in the service of the single judgment. Since in several memories we keep coming back to the same original impression, we shall place the same predicate-concept beneath this whole series of memories, and thus recognize that the predicate-concept contains what is common to all these memories. It is precisely the eternal content which our cognizing glance had already found in the first memory, and which it — if only our memory does not fail — is able to find again and again in repeated memories of the same impression. Predicate-concepts are thus eternal contents, which the cognizing glance finds in memories and which for that very reason it also ascribes to the impressions, that is, relates to subject-concepts.

14. We have represented the judgment of fact symbolically by means of a straight line, and can now conceive the subject-concept and the predicate-concept as two points by which that line is determined:

In the above figure the point a means the subject-concept, the point b the predicate-concept. If one takes a

[p. 19]

not as a mathematical, but merely as an empirical point, then it signifies the original impression from which the experience of judgment proceeds. Likewise b, as an empirical point, signifies the experience of memory which we relate to the impression a. The straight line itself which connects a and b represents the act of judgment, and indeed, as an empirical line, the vital act without which an act of judgment cannot take place; as a mathematical line, on the other hand, the imperishable sense or content of our judgment. The direction of the straight line is the symbol for the direction of our awareness toward the truth, and the infinitely distant point of the line: the truth itself, which we cognize in the judgment. Even for the length of the segment between a and b we can find a symbolic interpretation: it signifies that small interval of time which is necessary in order to arrive at awareness of the impression a; it signifies the human s p e e d o f a w a r e n e s s .

We must, however, point out at once a weakness of this symbolism. The subject-concept and the predicate-concept, which are so specifically different from each other a t l e a s t i n t h e j u d g m e n t o f f a c t , are represented uniformly by mathematical points a and b, so that our symbolism does not yet do justice at all to the nature of the judgment of fact. One can remedy this weakness of our geometrical mode of representation approximately in the following way. One fixes for the line a beginning-point, or, as the geometer would say, a zero-point, and gives it the meaning of the subject-concept; the predicate-concept, however, one can represent by a v a r i a b l e point on the line, since the memory of the original impression can take place at different times:

[p. 208]

In the above figure b, b1, b2, b3, b4 … represent the same predicate-concept, which forms the common content of several memories that all relate to the impression o. By letting the point b (in the direction of the arrow, which indicates the direction of the future) move on without limit, so that it passes through the points b1, b2, b3, b4, … ad inf., we obtain also a symbolic representation of the eternal validity of the act of judgment. For since the movable point b always moves on along one and the same straight line, this means, symbolically expressed, that the truth of the assertion will be maintained for all future times. — We represent, then, the subject-concept by a f i x e d point, whereas the predicate-concept by a v a r i a b l e point, and thereby indicate that we wish to see the highly significant specific difference between subject-concept and predicate-concept preserved, at least in the judgment of fact, under all circumstances. At the same time we recognize here that memory-judgments of identical wording need not be represented by parallel lines, as we did at the outset of our investigations, but can be represented on one and the same straight line, namely by regarding the predicate-point b as movable.

Here is perhaps the fitting place to recall also that logical symbolism which is gradually falling out of use, and which consisted in representing concepts by circles and relations of concepts by the mutual position of two or more circles. This symbolism is of great importance for the history of logic, for it furnishes the proof that the human mind, in logical investigations, feels impelled to resort to means that render things sensible, and gladly borrows these means from geometrical intuition of space. But the symbolism of circles has also the far more interesting significance,

[p. 19]

that by their great intuitiveness they reveal the fundamental errors of Aristotelian-scholastic logic better than mightily inflated learned treatises could do. One need only look at these symbolic circles to recognize at once that here we have symbols of their own for concepts, but not also for judgments. Had it been recognized that in logic everything depends on judging, and that concepts are there only because we are judging beings, one would certainly have endeavored first of all to find some symbol for the judgment. A second glance at those circle-figures instructs us that the specific differences between concept and concept are not taken into account at all, so that a subject-concept is likewise represented only by a circle, just like a predicate-concept. All manner of concepts have the circle for a symbol, so that the whole great wealth of sentence-structure dissolves into a complete sameness, and not even the polar opposition of subject and predicate (which I represent by a fixed and a movable point) is deemed worthy of being rendered sensible. This is connected with the fact that in this logic it is really not a question of judgments at all, hence also not of the differences between subject-concepts and predicate-concepts, but merely of uniform concepts and of the relations of their subordination and superordination. This disregard of the judgment and of the admirable articulated structure of linguistic sentences rests, however, ultimately on the fact that this logic is not concerned at all with j u d g m e n t s o f f a c t , in which we present our experiences, but always only with conceptual games. For the significant difference between subject-concepts and predicate-concepts comes to light, properly speaking, only in judgments of fact, whereas in such sentences (e.g. “Justice is a virtue”), which belong to the spheres of higher awareness, the specific difference between subject-concepts and predicate-concepts is blurred

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.             14

[p. 210]

and the subsumption of concepts under more general concepts comes into its own. Whoever starts from this subsumption of concepts under more general concepts builds logic from above, and is never able to cognize the essence of judgment; indeed, he will necessarily mistake it, and he will consequently also be incapable of doing justice to the nature of concepts. Against empiricism and sensualism we must stress that logic proceeds not from the impressions but from the act of judgment; but we confront the rationalists and scholastics by proceeding not from concepts and their subsumption but from the judgment in which we constate sensible impressions. Only so have we any prospect of being able to turn the true kernel of sensualism, together with the true kernel of rationalism, to account for the construction of a monistic logic.

20. The Principle of Identity.

15. The truth that a judgment must consist of a subject-concept and a predicate-concept has perhaps never been esteemed in logic according to its real significance, as is evident from the fact that it has never been ranked among the “laws” or “axioms” of logic. One speaks of a law of identity, of a law of contradiction, of a law of sufficient reason, etc., but of a law according to which a judgment must consist of a subject and a predicate there is no mention. This truth is treated altogether in an ambiguous manner, which can be characterized only with extreme difficulty. For in logic one speaks of the subject-concept and predicate-concept of a judgment as of a s e l f -e v i d e n t m a t t e r , but on the other hand one seems to want to assume that the dual articulation of the judgment is merely a dual articulation of its linguistic expression, so that

[p. 20]

the distinction of subject and predicate concerns not so much the judgment as the linguistic sentence, and therefore belongs rather to grammar than to logic. The great unclarity that prevails on this point has the consequence that logic is unable to raise itself to the rank of a genuine science.

For this very reason I endeavored in the previous chapter to furnish the proof that the duality of the structure of judgment is grounded in the deepest depth of human nature, of human awareness. It lies in human nature to have experiences of eternity, but these cannot take place if one does not start from vanishing experiences. These latter, however, cannot lead to experiences of eternity unless they are double experiences, of which the one takes the place of the other. We can grasp an original experience only through a vicarious one, and in doing so both experiences acquire a conceptual character, so that our judgment must present itself as the relation of a predicate-concept to a subject-concept. If, then, a schoolboy analyzes some linguistic sentence set before him, and points out in it the subject and the predicate, he is really analyzing human nature. For to be a human being means as much as to have experiences of eternity, and this again as much as to relate a predicate to a subject.

Since, however, we have learned from early youth to attend to the structure of sentences, the cognition of the dual articulation of the sentence has become so familiar to us that we no longer concern ourselves with the sense of this cognition. It has, so to speak, acquired an a u t o m a t i c character. Such automatic cognitions one is accustomed to accept as “self-evident matters,” in which one need not interest oneself further. If one does then, for once, wish to give oneself an account of

14*

[p. 212]

what sense it has that a sentence is dually built, one readily falls upon the thought that this articulation concerns only the linguistic structure of the sentence, and is a mere e x t e r n a l i t y . One who judges as though he were an automaton finds, to be sure, in the most significant truths merely externalities. Indeed, once one has reached the point of declaring the duality of sentence-structure to be a linguistic externality, one will soon be inclined to regard this externality as something more or less accidental, and to declare that a judgment may quite well be a judgment without being dually articulated, that is, without containing the relation of a predicate to a subject.

In fact some logicians seem to believe that a judgment can at times dispense with the subject or the predicate, or also that a single word which is neither subject nor predicate can stand there as a judgment. Indeed, a mere fleeting gesture, it is said, has often enough the meaning of a very clear and very definite judgment, which the person concerned, to whom the gesture is directed, can very well understand, without there being even a trace of a subject and a predicate. Further, it is also pointed out that the speech of primitive peoples can easily drive to despair anyone who seeks in it, under all circumstances, well-separated subject-concepts and predicate-concepts. Such and similar remarks, however, furnish only the proof of how very easily one misunderstands what is to be understood by a “logical law.” Indeed, it would perhaps be better to banish the word “law” altogether from logic, for precisely in this science it experiences the greatest misinterpretations. The logical laws are not such laws as those which are issued by the potentates of the earth, and for which, under threat of penalties of imprisonment and bodily punishment, observance is demanded. They are simply t h e o r e m s , which want to contain nothing but truth.

[p. 20]

When we say that a judgment necessarily contains the relation of a predicate to a subject, we mean by this only so much: that whoever becomes aware of his awareness, that is, judges about his judging, also finds that in the judgment he relates a predicate to a subject. In general, logical truths can be grasped only by one who becomes aware of his awareness, and hence hold only for such a one. Only in the sphere of this logical awareness does it hold that a judgment consists of a subject and a predicate. But for one who does not raise himself into this sphere of awareness, that theorem of logic does not hold. Since, however, the logician always understands by a judgment such a judgment as judgments are to be passed upon, he raises the judgment in general into the sphere of awareness of awareness, and there every judgment then has a subject and a predicate.

If, then, someone for the sake of brevity uses merely a single word in order to utter a judgment, this is no proof against, but for, the duality of sentence-structure. For whoever becomes aware of what is meant by the word finds in the word a judgment only because he is able to replace it by a dually articulated sentence. The like holds of a gesture which is used instead of a sentence. If, further, someone finds himself in such a state of mind that he cannot bring his thoughts into sentences of dual articulation, then he lacks precisely that degree of awareness which the logician requires for the formation of a l o g i c a l judgment. Thus, for example, a child at the beginning of learning to speak can utter only single words; but such words count for the logician neither as signs of concepts nor as signs of judgments, since these signs do not spring from that degree of awareness which we call logical awareness. This is present only where

[p. 214]

one who judges gives himself an account of the fact that he is judging. But if he gives himself such an account, he allows to count as judgments only those judgments which are accompanied by the consciousness that one is giving an account of them. Such judgments, however, always consist of a subject and a predicate.

16. The principle that our judgments must contain the relation of a predicate to a subject is, properly speaking, nothing other than the principle of identity. For what does it mean to identify something? It means as much as t o f i n d i n i t s o m e t h i n g e t e r n a l . For if this eternal were not to be found in it, it would be impossible to come back to it and to identify it. If, then, one identifies some impression, one must, although it is something transitory, nevertheless have found in it something imperishable, be it only the imperishable position which it occupies in space and time, and which can never be taken from it. From what has taken place somewhere and at some time, this having-taken-place there and then can never again be wrested. Thus at bottom we established the principle of identity right at the beginning of our investigations, for we said there that true judgments of fact are experiences of eternity, which can also be expressed by saying that they are experiences of identification; f o r o n e i d e n t i f i e s s o m e thing only by finding in it something imperi s h a b l e . A closer consideration has shown, however, that an impression cannot be identified without the help of a vicarious experience. By this insight it is now possible to grasp the principle of identity more exactly. One finds the identical or the imperishable in an impression only when one is able to find it in the vicarious experience. One identifies a fact, therefore, by relating a predicate-concept to a subject-concept. We may accordingly

[p. 20]

formulate the principle of identity (at least with respect to facts) as follows:

To i d e n t i f y a m e r e f a c t , w e m u s t h a v e a double experience, or rather relate two concepts to each other; and indeed we relate the vicarious experience to the original one, or rather the predicate to the subject.

Perhaps no theorem of logic has been so often and so thoroughly misunderstood as precisely this principle of identity, which of all the thoughts of man appears to be the “most self-evident.” Two most characteristic misunderstandings I must especially emphasize: I mean the sensualist and the rationalist misinterpretation of the principle of identity. Both have been occasioned by the familiar formula A i s A, for since men were unable to formulate the sense of the principle of identity in concepts, they gladly resorted to the above formula, which relieved them of speaking.

The sensualist interprets the formula A i s A approximately as follows. A in the subject is the sign for a sensible impression which one has; A in the predicate is again only a sign for the impression which one has already designated just before in the subject by A. Then A i s A means as much as: The impression which I have is the impression which I have. This would be the principle of identity in the sense of sensualism. One sees, it sounds rather comical, for it presents itself from the outset as the most superfluous thing in the world. Viewed in the light, a sentence is repeated twice in it, and instead of the sensualist simply saying: “I have an impression” — which of itself surely suffices fully — he finds it necessary to say the sentence twice, and so to give the appearance as if by this twofold reciting of a sentence more were gained for logic than by reciting it once.

[p. 216]

If I say: “This is red,” my impression is identified just as well and (linguistically surely still better) as if I say: “This, which is red, is red.” No identification requires the repetition of a sentence, and if anyone believes that by repetition and interlacing of the same thought he has stated the principle of identity, he is in a most comical aberration, which he easily sees through by considering that he could just as well recite a thought 3 times, 4 times, 1000 times, in order to give it seemingly still greater emphasis. He could thus, for example, form the following tapeworm of a sentence: “The impression which I have is the impression which I have, is the impression which I have, is the impression which I have, etc., ad infinitum.” Or expressed formulaically: A i s A i s A i s A … ad inf. One sees that L o c k e was not without reason vexed at the A i s A, for it is really the flattest of all that clever heads have ever devised.

And now let one recall how at the beginning of these investigations I pointed out that one can easily r e n d e r s e n s i b l e the eternal validity of a true judgment of fact by assuming that this judgment is repeated to the end of time, as it were to indicate that it never drops its claim to validity. I stressed, however, that this was a mere rendering sensible, and that the single assertion of a judgment of fact already includes the sense of its eternal validity. If one considers this, one sees at once how thoroughly ridiculous it is to render sensible the eternal validity of a fact by repeating the judgment of fact twice. If one used, instead of the formula A i s A, at least the formula A i s A i s A i s A … a d i n f . , this procedure would still have the sense that one would at least be aware that what is here in question is a r e p e t i t i o n of a judgment into the infinite, hence a mere rendering sensible

[p. 20]

of the insight that one wishes to constate something for all times. Now in the formula A i s A, however, there is precisely a lack of the insight that here the r e p e t i t i o n o f a j u d g m e n t takes place, and just because this insight is lacking, the formula A i s A is not only ridiculous, but, what alone has significance in logic: it is wholly absurd.

For if one understands by A the sign for a sensible impression which one has, and by the second A again the sign for this sensible impression, then one declares that one regards a sensible impression as subject and as predicate in one judgment. One falls into the delusion as if an impression were constated or identified by itself. This is the sensualist aberration. An impression can be constated or identified only through the act of judgment of our awareness. Impressions do not constate themselves; they are not human beings who have a judgment. Only when the judging mind of man finds something imperishable in the transitory impression does the impression receive a sameness, an identity, that is, is it constated or identified. Whoever therefore believes that the impression can at the same time play the role of the subject and of the predicate in the sentence, means that an impression constates itself, that is, that no awareness is necessary in order to constate it: he means that one can become aware of something without awareness. This absurdity, then, is what lies in the formula A i s A, when one believes that the two A’s are the signs for a sensible impression. The principle of identity demands that t w o c o n c e p t s d i f f e r e n t i n k i n d be present in the judgment, which by their relation to each other are to state the eternal validity of merely o n e fact. But if one conceives the formula A i s A in such a way that each of the two A’s represents a sensible impression, then this formula is not the formula of identity, but

[p. 218]

r a t h e r t h e f o r m u l a f o r s e n s u a l i s t a b s u r d i t y.

It lies in the nature of sensualism that it cannot do justice to the act of judgment, and that it supposes that the transitory refers to itself, and thereby becomes judgment. It thus interprets judging in such a way that the sensible impression which one has is at the same time the subject and the predicate in the sentence. Against this peculiar aberration it must always be stressed that i n j u d g m e n t t h e t r a n s i t o r y i s g r a s p e d n o t t h r o u g h t h e t r a n s i t o r y, b u t through the eternal in it.

17. Rationalism too conceives the principle of identity erroneously, and its aberration is in a way the reversal of the sensualist absurdity. For whereas the sensualist supposes that the transitory is grasped through what is transitory in it, the rationalist gives himself up to the illusion that he can grasp the imperishable through itself, hence without the help of the transitory. On this illusion every apriorism rests. The apriorist speaks of a “pure reason,” from which eternal truths can be drawn without the help of sensibility. This, however, is only the reverse side of the sensualist error: for whoever admits the absurdity that an impression can grasp itself can also admit the absurdity that awareness draws truths out of itself, without resting upon impressions. Sensualism and rationalism form at bottom only the two reverse sides of one and the same dualistic doctrine, and only in union with each other represent the sad dissension of the human mind. It is at bottom all one whether someone has a predilection for sensibility or is enthusiastic about conceptual thinking, for both inclinations of thinking have only one and the same consequence: they make logic into an impossibility.

[p. 20]

If logic is to become a science, it must therefore combat both inclinations of thought with equal ruthlessness. Here, then, the first thing is to furnish the proof that rationalism misunderstands the principle of identity, which forms the foundation of logic, just as much as sensualism does.

First of all, the rationalist stands in a similar error with respect to the identity formula as the sensualist, with the difference, however, that with him A i s A is a formula not for sensible impressions but for conceptual contents. The two A’s are each by itself to be the sign of one and the same concept, so that the formula translated into words runs: “The concept which I have is the concept which I have.” One is thus confronted here with that comical repetition of a thought which I have already characterized above. This repetition would be to some extent pardonable if it took place with consciousness, and if it were not merely a twofold repetition but wished to suggest the thought of an infinite repetition. But there is a lack of such awareness and purpose, and for that very reason the so-called identity formula is simply ridiculous. One can demonstrate this ridiculousness in what may be called a mathematically exact way. The identity formula is supposed, namely, to be the short, concise expression of a theorem in which is set forth what one is to understand by the principle of identity. I propose, then, to substitute the word “identity” for the sign A in the formula, in order to see what becomes of the formula in this case. It then takes the form: “Identity is identity.” And we must now be convinced that the identity formula says nothing about identity, that is, does not explain to us in the least what identity is. One may, however, reply to me that the law of identity is not called upon at all to give an explanation of what is to be understood by

[p. 220]

identity. Let even this be granted; but there can be no doubt about this, that the law of identity must tell us what the content of the law of identity is. In order, then, to learn this content, I put in the formula A i s A, instead of the sign A, the words “law of identity,” and obtain the following pronouncement: “The law of identity is the law of identity.” I believe this suffices.

The question now presses upon us, what we are to think at all of the formula A i s A? To get clear about this, let the reader imagine that he is engaged in a conversation with a Mr. X. In the course of the conversation let the significant moment now arise when Mr. X, stopping before the reader, repeats a sentence twice, or even three times. What is to be thought of this? I believe it depends entirely on the special case. It could be that Mr. X takes very great pleasure in the sentence, and repeats it on account of its euphony; it could be that he is astonished at the content of the sentence, and repeats it in order to make friends with it somehow; it could be that he repeats the sentence in order to impress it upon his hearer, etc. In short, there can be countless different situations of life in which a man finds occasion to repeat a sentence; so that one is entitled to formulate the sentence: What is meant by the mere repeating of a sentence is in general unfathomable, for such a repetition can have the most various meanings in different cases. This now holds also of the repetition A i s A, which gives itself the appearance of being no repetition, but of connecting a subject with a predicate. In reality, however, what is meant by this formula is: What A is, is A. A sentence is repeated in it, but in such a grammatical connection of a subordinate clause with a main clause as if it were a matter not of a repetition, but of an

[p. 20]

explanation. Thus, for example, in the sentence-combination: “What is lighter than water floats in water,” the first sentence is an explanatory subordinate clause of the second; but in jest one may allow oneself to put the refusal of an explanation into the grammatical form of an explanation, and to say: “What floats in water, that floats in water.” Language permits this, for it serves not only the purposes of logic but also the purposes of jest. Where one merely repeats a sentence twice, one can thus give this procedure, in jest, the appearance as if it were a matter not of a mere repetition, but of an explanation. This is the case also with the formula A i s A. It is an abbreviation of the formula W h a t A i s , i s A ; and this in turn is the jocular dressing-up of a repetition of a sentence in the form of an explanation. Since such jesting is out of place in logic, the identity formula is taken as what it does not seem to be in jest, but what it really is: the repetition of a sentence. Of such a repetition, however, we have established that it is unfathomable, since a repetition can take place from intentions of the most various kinds. Herewith it is proved that the formula A i s A can have absolutely no place in logic.

18. From the standpoint of the history of philosophy, however, one may now ask how it comes about that a formula which at bottom only indicates a repetition could count as the expression of the principle of identity. The answer is that this is easily intelligible and explicable. Like every repetition of a sentence, the identity formula too sets us a riddle: we are namely to guess for what purpose the sentence has been repeated. This riddle, however, has, as I already indicated, infinitely many solutions, since one can repeat a sentence with the most various intentions.

[p. 222]

Among the infinitely many solutions of the riddle thus set, however, there is one that can come into consideration precisely in logic. A sentence may, among other things, be repeated also for the purpose that the hearer, in the manifold repetition, should realize the i d e n t i t y of the thought, and should now busy himself with the question what identity actually is. The repetition of a sentence, and so also the identity formula, can serve to stir our mind to concern itself with the concept of identity. The logician, who feels this, can then easily fall into the error of supposing that he has also already explained to us what identity is, and what is to be understood by the principle of identity. In this error lies the comedy of the identity formula. We learn absolutely nothing about what is meant by the law of identity, and the logician believes he has told us. It is an error similar to that of a physicist who, for example, let a pendulum swing to and fro for a while before his audience, and then, breaking the deep silence, suddenly turned to his listeners with the words: And now, gentlemen, you know the laws of pendulum motion!

Just as pendulum oscillations can give an impulse to reflecting on the laws of these oscillations, so too the oft-repeated saying of a sentence can set one thinking what identity is. But just as little as pendulum oscillations betray of themselves which laws they obey, so little do sentences uttered repeatedly betray what is to be understood by the law of identity. What makes this analogy limp, however, is the essential circumstance that pendulum oscillations are at least honest, whereas the sentence “What A is, is A” is thoroughly rotten. Pendulum oscillations give themselves out as what they are, and one need only look at them in order to feel a lawfulness in them. But the

[p. 20]

sentence “What A is, is A” gives itself out as an explanation and is in reality only repetition. As a jest it may stand; but if it makes its entrance gravely, it becomes a lie and may play no part in a science such as logic is supposed to be. I hold it a great merit of L o c k e ’s that he rebelled against the identity formula; it is only to be deeply regretted that he did not brand its flat and insipid mendacity for all time; for no sentence could do greater harm to the standing of logic than this one.

19. The principle of identity is no repetition in which, making ourselves into an echo, we degrade ourselves to an automaton, and so ape ourselves. It is as full of content as any truth whatever can be; indeed, if it is permitted to speak of a rank among truths, the principle of identity may be set forth as the most significant of all the truths that man possesses. One can say, in a certain sense, that the principle of identity is logic, indeed that it is philosophy, or science as such. To be sure, one must not wish to misconstrue this assertion as though the principle of identity by itself alone could replace all other truths. No truth can replace another; for the significance of every truth lies in this, that it is absolutely irreplaceable. If we nevertheless attribute a quite special significance to the principle of identity, this is because this principle refers to the power of our awareness, or also to our power of judgment (understanding, reason), that is, to that power by virtue of which we are human beings and have cognition of truth. The principle of identity is, indeed, nothing other than an awareness of this power, by which we constate that we have this power, and characterize it: as the power by virtue of which we have experiences of eternity, and are thus able to identify something. Since, then, the

[p. 224]

principle of identity is a self-revelation of our reason, it comes over us at this revelation as though only through it had we come into possession of a reason: in this principle we enjoy our own rationality, and are filled with the indescribable happiness that we are truly judging beings, human beings. It is accordingly comprehensible, or at least pardonable, that we rank especially high the truth that we have in the principle of identity, for it is indeed the herald of the fact that we are capable of truth. It is also, properly speaking, the first revelation of human reason about itself, for above all we must know this about reason, that it relates the transitory to an eternal, and thereby identifies the transitory, that is, grasps its truth. All the remaining revelations of reason about itself already lie in the sense of this first revelation; so too the one that makes us know that we cannot relate the transitory to the eternal otherwise than by relating a predicate to a subject, which is only a more detailed execution of the principle of identity.

But the greater the weight we lay on the principle of identity, the more unreservedly must we also admit to ourselves that we are infinitely far from having exhausted the sense of this principle. With us human beings insight into any truth has quite a peculiar condition, which comes to light in the fact that, as is well known, two persons, although they have understood the sense of a theorem perfectly well, nevertheless have no equal insight into it. Why is the one insight not worth just as much as the other? This is an exceedingly difficult question, which has been little discussed in logic, and which we wish to touch on here only in passing. All truths hang together with one another, and they form for us human beings an infinitely

[p. 20]

complicated network, whose nature has been investigated only to an extremely small extent. In logic we are still at the ABC, and what we are able to accomplish is shamefully little. For with truths it always depends on grasping their connections; but since these connections are of an immeasurable intricacy, it is no wonder, either, that we as yet hardly have a logical science of these connections. If, however, a truth presents itself to us merely as a single one, we are, as it were, imprisoned in it, for in accordance with the nature of our awareness truths are for us always only points of passage to other truths, and in particular from those that we already know to those that we have not yet cognized. If, then, someone has insight into a theorem, one will still have to ask whether, and to what extent, this theorem can serve him as a bridge to other known and, if possible, still unknown theorems. On this rests the penetrating power of insight, or also its depth, as well as many another nuance in the activity of the understanding. With this it is at least indicated why a difference of value can exist between insight and insight.

These considerations are meant merely to turn our attention to a principle which may be called the p r i n c i p l e o f t h e r e l a t i v i t y o f h u m a n c o g n i t i o n or also the principle            of    the     infinite         capacity       for p r o g r e s s o f o u r a w a r e n e s s . This principle relates to the manner in which we possess truth. For when we speak simply of the possession of truth, this phrase gives the impression of an extravagance of our thinking, because one is easily reminded by it of an absolute possession. Now, however, as was said, every truth is a point of passage for our thinking and a summons to other

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.                 15

[p. 226]

truths, so that we are, as it were, nomads in the realm of truth, and our possession in it consists in being able to come from one truth to another, that is, our possession is a dynamic possession. It is extremely difficult to characterize the nature of this possession, and it is possible only to the extent that we have become aware of the nature of our awareness. Here I wished to set up the principle of the relativity of our cognition merely as a t a s k , namely as the question or the problem of what kind our possession of truth is.

The starting point for such inquiries, as indeed for all further logical inquiries, is formed, however, by the principle of identity. We have formulated this principle only with regard to sensible impressions, that is, we have merely established what it means to identify a sensible impression. But the answer to this question already contains at the same time the answer to the other question: namely, what it means to identify an eternal content, that is, the content of a true judgment, or even merely the content of a concept. For the latter question is an inversion of the former; and if we identify a sensible impression by relating it to something imperishable, we must conversely identify an eternal content of truth by relating it to something transitory. In other words, we must make the content of truth into a sensible fact in order to be able to identify it. The content of truth becomes a sensibly graspable fact, however, through the vital act of judgment by virtue of which we perform the act of judgment.
