This vital act of judgment, when it appears with the necessary vividness, makes itself known in g e s t u r e s o f j u d g m e n t , which in deaf-mutes have a mimic character, but in the normal person stand out from the various accompanying mimic acts as speech-acts proper. Only by virtue of these speech-acts are we able to identify a

[p. 20]

content of judgment. For a truth of which we become aware contains nothing in itself that would enable us to distinguish its present being-thought from its earlier being-thought. Whether we have a content of truth in mind now, or whether we had it in mind earlier, is irrelevant for this truth itself, for it is and remains forever unalterably the same. For us, however, where truth is concerned it is always a matter of having it at a certain point of time, for if we do not grasp it at a certain point of time, then we have not grasped it at all. Precisely for this reason a temporal doing is necessarily bound up with the grasping of a truth, without which the truth could not be grasped, and without which we should also be unable to return to it. Only by attaching one and the same content of judgment (identical) to several speech-acts of like sound, that is, to sentences of like sound, do we become conscious that the content which we thought is a content thought at different times and nevertheless identical. The identity of a content dawns on us only when we have grasped the non-identity of those speech-acts of like sound in which we present one and the same content. If, then, we were unable to recognize speech-acts as being of like sound, we could not identify the contents of these speech-acts either. Just as the identification of a sensible impression can take place only through an experience of eternity, so conversely this experience of eternity cannot be identified without being tied to sensibly graspable gestures of judgment. Starting from sensible impressions, our awareness awakens, and to sensible gestures of judgment we cling in turn, in order to become aware of awareness. Thus our judging has a twofold foundation: 1. the foundation of sensible impressions, without which we could never arrive at judgments of fact, 2. the foundation of sensible

15*

[p. 228]

(logical) symbols, without which we should be unable to become aware of awareness, that is, unable to judge about judging.

In seeing thus how all our musing and thinking is immersed in impressions, we free ourselves from every rationalistic extravagance in philosophizing. Against the sensualist we insist that we are beings who judge sub specie aeterni, and that the most ordinary observations, of which we become aware in everyday life through our judgments, are made under the aspect of eternity. Against the rationalist we point out that we could carry out no act of awareness about our awareness if we did not rely in doing so on sensible symbols, that we therefore, without the help of impressions, can draw absolutely nothing from the understanding or from reason. This follows from the principle of identity. As certainly as we are able to identify something, that is, as certainly as we cognize anything at all: so certain is it that we may not distinguish “empirical” and “pure” cognition, as K a n t does. Both kinds that he wishes to distinguish are pure and empirical at once, for both kinds are experiences of eternity, and both kinds are impossible without the cooperation of impressions. The principle of identity rightly conceived refutes every dualistic theory of knowledge which, assuming two sources of cognition, distinguishes two kinds of cognition as separated from one another by an unbridgeable gulf. There are stages in our cognition, because we can proceed from primary awareness to a secondary awareness: this, however, does not abolish the unity of our cognitions; rather the higher awareness is there only in order to establish, if possible, the unity which was not yet found through the lower.

The principle of identity, however, is not only a refutation of the division of our cognitions into a priori

[p. 21]

and a posteriori, but also a refutation of the division of our judgments into analytic and synthetic. In all judgments that are true, identity prevails, and only in false judgments is it lacking. If, then, K a n t means that in synthetic judgments subject and predicate are combined without identity, this comes about because by identity he understands that contentless repetition of a sentence which we characterized above. Such a conception of identity leads, to be sure, to a complete disorganization of logic.

21. The Double Judgment.

20. Logical investigations are afflicted with a peculiar, painful difficulty, which I have already touched on often before, but of which one is reluctant to give oneself an account, because it has a strongly humorous aftertaste. The logician, that is, must confess that nothing comes harder to him than, when he speaks about judgments, also to adduce a suitable example of a judgment. This is strange enough, since in everyday intercourse we form countless sentences or judgments, and in logical exposition do nothing else at all than pass judgments. But when it is a question of adducing a suitable example of a judgment, one is at once in great embarrassment, not so much because of the banality of such an example to be adduced, as rather because it is extremely difficult to find such an example in which the judgment adduced also makes the definite impression of a s i n g l e j u d g m e n t . If I say, for example, “Here on the table stands a lamp,” the reader learns thereby that here is a table, further also that here is a lamp, as well as that the lamp stands on the table; indeed it is quite impossible to foresee how many judgments there may be that stand in an intimate connection with the one adduced and accordingly give rise to a most disturbing

[p. 230]

effect, because in the example it is above all a matter of holding fast a single judgment and not several. The isolating of a single judgment is such a painful business that one is never done with it.

Similar difficulties, moreover, arise in every science. If the physicist, for example, wishes to demonstrate some phenomenon, he must for the most part, by means of highly complicated apparatus, exclude if possible all disturbing circumstances, and realize if possible all those conditions through which the phenomenon is able to appear with an approximate purity and exactness. Absolute purity and absolute exactness are never attained at all. Indeed, even in mathematics things do not go quite as some laymen believe. When the mathematician wishes to prove a theorem, it often seems to him that between a sentence A and a sentence B a mediating intermediate sentence ought still to be inserted, in order to secure full rigor for the proof. And if he is a scrupulous thinker, he finds hooks and little hooks everywhere, so that he has to insert ever new and ever new mediating intermediate sentences, and the doubt sometimes seizes him whether anything at all could be proved with a b s o l u t e r i g o r .

However that may be, the logician has no reason whatever to be ashamed that the isolating of a single judgment will not go well, and that there are no such blinkers by means of which our awareness could be directed with absolute rigidity to one and only one truth. Our awareness is by its innermost nature restless or dynamic, and in this respect comparable to a flickering flame, which even when it seems to burn with the utmost calm must renew itself in continually vibrating unrest. If we wished to direct our awareness with absolute rigidity to one thought, we should soon lose our consciousness, for such is the nature of our awareness, that it grasps a

[p. 21]

truth in order to come to the other, and that it can be kept alive only through such transitions, as the great fighter for truth, L e s s i n g , so beautifully expressed it. Only in coming from truth to truth do we have the truth. And we may count ourselves fortunate if we have it in this way, and our progress does not consist in our passing from one error into another.

Indeed, what is meant to signify still more than what has been said so far: it is in logic excluded on principle that a judgment which is before us could be before us merely as a single judgment. If I adduce it as an example of a judgment: “Just now someone knocked at the door,” then the judgment “I heard this” is also meant thereby. If I say: “The magnetic needle has now experienced a deflection through the electric current,” it is also meant that I have seen this. If I say: “This equation of the third degree has only one real root,” the sentence “I know this” is also understood along with it. One can multiply such examples at pleasure; for every judgment there will always have to be found the judgment that supplements it into a pair. This follows from the nature of our awareness. If I have become aware of a sensible impression and have passed a judgment on it, I can, proceeding, become aware also of the judgment just passed, and constate it as a fact by itself in a second judgment. To be sure, in ordinary life we pass judgments without adding at once: Now I have passed a judgment, or now I have asserted something, etc.; but in logic every judgment must be thought of as judged, because it is precisely the task of this science to reflect on our judging with judgments.

In the sphere of logical awareness every judgment is supplemented into a double judgment. The primary judgment may be called the object-judgment, the secondary, on the other hand, may be

[p. 232]

called the I-judgment. We have thereby gained a theorem, which may be formulated as follows: Wi t h o u t o b j e c t -judgment no I-judgment, and conversely w i t h o u t I - j u d g m e n t n o o b j e c t - j u d g m e n t . Or with regard to the awareness which performs the judgment: Without object-awareness no self-awareness, and conversely without self-awareness no object-awareness. This is the p r i n c i p l e o f j u d g m e n t p a i r s and is basically nothing other than a further development of the principle of identity; for it says that an identification of the object cannot take place without an identification of the I, just as conversely identifications of the I cannot take place without identifications of the object.

21. The logical theorem which I have just set up is also confirmed by the spirit of language. For language allows us to fuse the object-judgment with the I-judgment into a single judgment, hence to present a judgment pair as a unity, and to make a single content out of a double content. At the same time, however, language also offers us means not only to bring about the unification of judgment pairs, but also to bring before our view the opposed kind of awareness that prevails in the two members of the judgment pair. Compare, say, the following three sentences, of which the third, to be sure, sounds somewhat barbarous:

α) I see that the ball rolls.

β) I see the rolling of the ball.

γ) The rolling of the ball is seen by me. In α) the I-judgment is separated from the object-judgment, so that they both still retain the character of an independent judgment, although they are obviously both dependent on each other, and form a whole only in union. A double construction of this kind absolutely demands that we should also form another, in which the duality of the judgment pair appears abolished, and the object-judgment as

[p. 21]

a mere conceptual member is taken over into the I-judgment, where it then forms the so-called “complement” or “object” of the I-judgment, as can be seen from β). This fact of syntax has a very high logical significance, for it is not only the expression of the unified content of judgment pairs, but also the expression of the l o g i c a l p r i n c i p l e o f t h e m e r e l y relative difference between judgment and concept. What stands there as judgment in the one act of awareness can appear as concept in the other act of awareness, and this has also the converse consequence, namely that the conceptual member of one act of awareness can come to light in another act of a w a r e n e s s a s j u d g m e n t . This is a theorem which is most familiar to grammarians and stylists, and perhaps for that very reason seems to be disregarded by logicians, although it is the foundation for the doctrine of the relation between judgment and concept. This relation is among the greatest riddles of logic; but now we see that between judgment and concept there is only a relative difference, and recognize that this difference has its source in the nature of our awareness. Because our judgments supplement one another in pairs, that is, because our awareness is a double awareness, judgment and concept can pass into one another, whereby, however, their position in the judgment at hand need not become ambiguous, because it is always another act of awareness that can lend them a new character, as is to be shown yet more clearly.

If we now compare with one another the sentences β) and γ), which have one and the same content, it becomes clear to us that we may rightly speak of two modes of awareness, one of which may be called the d i r e c t and the other the c o n t r a r y or i n v e r s e awareness.

[p. 234]

The direct awareness is represented by the judgment β): it proceeds from the I in order to arrive at the object, its goal is the object; it is thus object-awareness. The contrary awareness is symbolized in language by the passive; it proceeds from the object in order to come to the I, it represents self-awareness. To be sure, it is also highly characteristic that precisely the passive behaves so refractorily that one gladly abstains from its use. Object-awareness prevails in language, as, by virtue of the practical and artistic services which it is meant to render, cannot be otherwise. The contrary awareness, or self-awareness, is not popular, and language very often refuses it its services, so that logicians of all party shades are fairly agreed in the complaint how much, under certain circumstances, the word can become a hindrance to thinking.

It will therefore be permitted here to apply, alongside the symbolism of language, the geometrical as well, especially since this latter is distinguished by exact intuitiveness. Our geometrical symbolism, already made use of, is well suited to bringing before the eye the oppositeness of awareness that prevails in the two judgment pairs:

I-judgment / Contrary awareness ←

→ Object-judgment / Direct awareness One and the same straight line represents to us both the object-judgment and the I-judgment, whereby it is indicated that both judgments have the same content; their opposed mode of awareness, however, is symbolized by the fact that we can traverse the straight line in two senses: once from left to right, the other time from right to left.

[p. 21]

Let us designate the memory of an impression by b, the original impression itself by a; then the direction from b toward a is the direction of the object-judgment or of direct awareness, whereas the reverse direction from a toward b represents the direction of the I-judgment or of contrary awareness.

22. Supported by the principle of judgment pairs, we are now in a position to remove an obscurity which has hitherto weighed on our investigations. For from the very beginning we have turned our attention to judgments of fact, and have treated them as single judgments, without regard to the I-judgments by which they would have to be supplemented. This most dangerous one-sidedness of our mode of consideration, however, could not be avoided, for it is impossible to turn attention at once to the double awareness, because thereby both direct and contrary awareness would come off short, and the course of the inquiry would call for a continual running to and fro of the understanding, by which only a giddiness of awareness could be produced. Just as in life we must above all be devoted to object-awareness, so too the logician must proceed from this, in order later to do justice to contrary awareness as well. It is impossible to become aware directly and contrarily at the same time, and however imperceptibly small the time interval may be that it takes to come from the object to oneself, this still cannot take place in a mathematical point of time. Just as we, when we travel from the place a to the place b, cannot at the same time make the journey from b to a, so too it is impossible, with mathematical simultaneity, to become aware directly and contrarily, of the object and of oneself.

Although, then, logic sets up the theorem that object-awareness and self-awareness cannot exist without each other, the thinking of the everyday

[p. 236]

life will nevertheless contradict this logical theorem in countless cases. There is always the possibility and probability of coming from one fact to another in such a way that awareness about awareness is thereby shortchanged and neglected. Were it not so, and were contrary awareness practiced just as much as direct, there would be no need of a science of logic.

Precisely because the natural understanding is far more devoted to direct awareness than to contrary awareness, it also arrives at no distinct differentiation of the two opposed modes of awareness. One does indeed speak much of a turning inward upon oneself, and especially in our days much nonsense is perpetrated in the aesthetic literature of the day with “psychological subtleties”: but fundamentally direct observation and self-awareness are thereby continually stirred together and mixed into a curious alloy. That state of awareness in which direct and contrary awareness are not distinctly differentiated is wont to be designated by more recent philosophers as n a i v e r e a l i s m : an expression which seems to me conceited, because with every thinker (be he ever so great and celebrated a philosopher) it is questionable whether he can give himself an account of the difference between object-awareness and self-awareness to the degree that would be necessary. For no mortal gets so far in logic that another could not get farther than he, and so we all together are and remain “naive realists.” But apart from the rationalistic self-exaltation which this nasty expression breathes, I also do not know why we should call natural awareness precisely naive r e a l i s m : for one knows quite well from the history of philosophy that realism and idealism feud with each other until, in the melee, they exchange positions

[p. 21]

and finally no one any more knows to which party he belongs.

A characteristic of natural awareness is, as was said, that it does not distinctly separate the opposed modes of awareness and consequently cannot penetrate to their unity either. Since we have proceeded hitherto from this natural awareness, there could be no distinct separation between what we called “impression” and “fact.” What is called fact for direct awareness is for contrary awareness: impression. What is fact for the physicist is for the logician an impression; the former, namely, speaks in the name of primary awareness, the latter has to do with awareness about our awareness, and in this one finds that the fact, with regard to the I, is impression. It is, as I set forth above, the same content that one grasps in the opposed modes of awareness. But as one who makes a journey from a to b gains a different aspect of the region than if he makes it from b to a, so too contrary awareness, or the judgment about judgment, gives us a new view of the world.

It is now a question, however, how matters stand with m e m o r y , and in particular whether it too presents two aspects, one for the physicist and another for the logician? It is only this question by which we arrive at an essential supplement to our considerations so far.

23. We are unable to become aware of our memory in a direct way, and it is this circumstance that has occasioned a great confusion in logic. Memories serve our awareness in order to arrive at facts, with the result that direct awareness can always be only an awareness of facts. In contrary awareness we recognize that the facts

[p. 238]

are impressions, and so arrive at the insight that we possess a twofold interpretation of what we immediately experience; we speak once of w h a t i s e x p e r i e n c e d , that is, of the fact, but another time of the e x p e r i e n c e , that is, of the impression. In relation to memory, however, we are not in this position, because memory is only a means, not an end, for direct awareness. We are accordingly not accustomed to interpret memory in a twofold way, as we do the original impression, and so easily arrive at the thought that it m a y n o t b e i n t e r p r e t e d i n a t w o f o l d w a y a t a l l . From such a way of thinking there then results a very peculiar distinction between impression and memory: it is said, namely, that the impression is both physical and psychical, whereas to memory an exclusively psychical character belongs. The impression then has t w o s i d e s , memory on the other hand only o n e s i d e . This statement is the expression of what I called psychological dualism. For one comes to find in the psyche, as it were, a forecourt and an interior space: in the forecourt the impressions bustle about, which, because they are turned toward the so-called external world, still have an outer side, and are therefore physical; but because they are already in the forecourt of the psyche, must also have an inner side, that is, are psychical structures. In the interior space of the psyche, which is admittedly very mysterious, among many other structures the memories too are at home: these have, then, no outer side, but only an inner side, they are no longer physical, but purely psychical. The chief difference between the forecourt of the psyche and its interior space, however, is that the forecourt is in space as well as in time; while the interior space of the psyche floats merely in time. How the psyche manages it that

[p. 21]

it is lodged with one half in space as well as in time, while with its other half it dwells merely in time, that is indeed enigmatic enough, but one simply passes over such enigmas. To be sure, one has given up speaking of an outer and an inner sense, because these expressions point too plainly to the forecourt and the interior space of the soul, hence to a mere phantasmagoria; but one persists in the distinction of t h e o u t e r a n d t h e i n n e r p e r c e p t i o n , of which the one is supposed to be directed to the physical, the other to the psychical. Now since outer perception perceives that which is found in the forecourt of the psyche, it is incomprehensible why it should be directed merely to the physical and not at the same time to the psychical. If the impressions are both physical and psychical, then the perception directed to them must likewise have two sides. Thus outer perception shimmers over into inner, or rather it is both kinds of perception at once. Only inner perception is really merely inner, whereby, to be sure, it remains incomprehensible why it should be set apart, since outer perception already has an inner side. Alongside these ambiguities many others run parallel: thus in particular the distinction of outer and inner, or also p h y s i c a l a n d p s y c h i c a l p h e n o m e n a , of which the physical in turn must also have a psychical side, while to the psychical phenomena only one side belongs. The oddest thing about this matter is that one wants to “d e s c r i b e ” the psychical phenomena, whereas one can describe only that which one grasps with direct awareness. Thus it acquires the appearance as if our direct awareness could take possession of memories just as of any facts whatever, and in psychological works one actually speaks with such

[p. 240]

definiteness of “psychological structures” as if one wished to describe, say, fog and clouds, sunset glow and polar lights, or other meteorological phenomena. Hereby the “purely psychical” plays quite over into the physical, and it is quite impossible to foresee how this Babylonian confusion of concepts could come to an end.

There is, as I set forth in the first part, no o u t e r , as opposed to an i n n e r perception. There are impressions and memories. There is a judging, and also a judging about judging, that is, there is a direct and a contrary awareness. Since, however, from L o c k e onward an impressionist logic won predominance over minds, men became accustomed in logic to make the impression and not the judgment the starting point of logic, whereby it became impossible to penetrate to a theory of judgment, that is, to a genuine logic. Thus it came about that the opposition and the unity of direct and contrary awareness were not recognized, but in the place of these logical concepts there came the sensualist concepts of outer and inner sense, and of outer and inner perception. Outer perception grasps what is spatio-temporal, inner perception grasps only what is temporal, consequently they both grasp only what is transitory, and between them there is no genuine opposition, and for that very reason no genuine unity either. One finds in them merely the semblance of an opposition and the semblance of a unity, in reality however only confusion and contradiction. They are model examples of a thoroughly unclear and hybrid formation of concepts. Such a hybridization of concepts was possible only because the m e t h o d o f l o g i c was not made the subject of inquiries. Logic has always wanted, and quite especially since Bacon, to be a methodology of the other sciences, but neglected above all to set forth in what its own method consists. This method

[p. 21]

is none other, however, than awareness about awareness. No other science sets itself the task of developing precisely this contrary awareness, for all the remaining sciences are devoted to direct awareness and use contrary awareness as a mere skill, which, because we are beings who have self-awareness, can develop in a natural way, without any specific cultivation of its own being bestowed upon it, that is, without its being cultivated scientifically.

There are, then, in science only two methods, a method of direct awareness and a method of contrary awareness, and a third could exist only if the mystics would once set forth, or could set forth, in what their method actually consists. Are they able to become aware otherwise than through a direct judging and a judging about judging? To this one receives no intelligible answer, and so it remains with the two methods named above, of which the one may be called the physical, the other the metaphysical or logical method. The one sets itself the task of developing direct awareness by the means of observation and experiment; the other, on the contrary, practices awareness about awareness, in order to develop it into a science of its own, which is admittedly so difficult a task that it has often appeared hopeless. Now there is no object-awareness without self-awareness, and this logical principle, which anyone easily sees, contains the imperative demand always to keep self-awareness at least on a level with object-awareness, because otherwise we lose the balance of our mind, that is, fall into error. One may also give to the method of direct awareness the name induction and call contrary awareness deduction, but these names awaken false sensualist and rationalist ideas, and

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.           16

[p. 242]

therefore seem to me little to be recommended. The method of logic or metaphysics has been expressed by no one so clearly as by the father of logic, by S o c r a t e s , who in his “Know thyself ” established for all time what the task of logic is.

This logical or metaphysical method of self-knowledge, or self-awareness, has been utterly corrupted by sensualist thinkers since Locke. The misunderstood contrary awareness, which manifests itself in judging about judging, appears disfigured and burdened with a self-contradiction, as inner sense, inner observation, inner perception, and gives itself out as a “psychological method,” indeed as the method of “empirical psychology,” for in its obscurity about itself it takes itself to be a second seeing, a second hearing, a second touching: so to speak, a viewing of seeing, a hearing of hearing, a touching of touching, etc. The oddities of this “empirical psychology,” which at bottom was a fashionable current produced by the flowering of natural science, are, by the way, smiled at by the leading psychologists of our time. Only people still believe that in self-awareness, borrowed from logic, misunderstood, and given the singular name of “inner perception,” they possess a psychological method.

In the principle of judgment pairs the logician sets forth the method of logic or metaphysics and renders Kant’s question, whether metaphysics is possible at all, an idle one. So long as there is a judging about judging, and so long accordingly as the expressions true and false exist in our dictionaries, so long will there be a logic or metaphysics: if in no other form, then as logical or metaphysical skill in the moral judgments of men, as logical or metaphysical mother wit in the natural

[p. 21]

sciences, etc., etc. To be sure, logical skill and mother wit can replace the science of logic itself only in a highly inadequate way. One needs a science that instructs us to regard the facts under the aspect of eternity, as the admirable S p i n o z a had taught. For w h e r e a s n a t u r a l s c i e n c e a l w a y s r e gards the eternal under the aspect of the t r a n s i t o r y, c o n t r a r y a w a r e n e s s p r o c e e d s t o a regarding of the transitor y under the aspect o f e t e r n i t y. In physics and metaphysics it is a matter of the same content of cognition; only physics would necessarily fall into the most ruinous errors and finally become wholly bogged down, were there not a complementary science that reverses the standpoint of physics and surveys the facts with the glimpse of eternity. But in logic, or metaphysics, too, it is always a matter only of facts, for these are precisely what are to be grasped sub specie aeterni. If logic loses sight of this, it becomes a formal, that is, contentless logic, or a speculative extravagance. In short, all physics and all metaphysics have equally to do always with facts and only with facts, and only the point of view from which the regarding takes place distinguishes the two complementary sciences from each other.

Since contrary awareness is a method peculiar to logic, the semblance may arise that on this conception there is no room left for psychology, and that it would have to be struck from the roll of the sciences. This would be, I believe, the greatest misunderstanding that my foregoing considerations could provoke. Psychology is a science which, it is true, has no method of its own differing from the two methods characterized above, but

16*

[p. 244]

its quite peculiar and high significance rests on the fact that it combines the two methods of direct and contrary awareness in a fruitful way. I cannot enter here at length upon the characterization of psychology as a science, because in this context it would lead me away from my proper aim. But so much I must remark, in order to forestall misunderstandings, that psychology also, just like logic, is a science of awareness, and indeed it investigates not only direct but also contrary awareness; and just because it does this, it must rest first and foremost upon logic or metaphysics: because without the theorems and the method of logic it can accomplish nothing. The difference between the psychological and the logical point of view consists, however, in this, that psychology has to do not with a w a r e n e s s i n g e n e r a l , but with a w a r e n e s s h e r e a n d n o w in this or that particular person. It asks, namely, what changes direct or even contrary awareness undergoes when the circumstances under which the awareness takes place suffer a change; it seeks to ascertain the lawful connections that obtain between the changes of the circumstances and the changes of personal awareness, and for this purpose employs all the means of observation and experiment, which it partly borrows from the natural sciences and partly, in keeping with its peculiar purpose, procures for itself by ingenious invention. What the logician has grasped sub specie aeterni (namely, the general theorems about judging), that it turns about and regards under the aspect of the transitory; and just because it does this, it must unite the metaphysical method with the physical. Hereby it obtains an exceedingly significant intermediate position between biology and logic (both in the widest sense), so

[p. 21]

that it holds, in a certain sense, the most responsible position within the circle of all the sciences. For on it, namely, the harmony of the whole conduct of science depends to a high degree, since if it lets itself be guided by a one-sided sympathy for biology or for metaphysics, then all the sciences are set at variance with one another.

24. If one admits that logic is the general science of awareness, one will also recognize the question of the significance of memory for our awareness as a logical one. We saw above that the impressionist way of thinking ascribes to the impression a physical and a psychical side, while it concedes to memory merely a psychical side, and we recognized that such a conception must generate an endless series of contradictions. To escape these contradictions, nothing remains for us but to place memory, too, wholly under the same double conception as the original impression itself. Memory, to be sure, is inaccessible to direct awareness, but this inaccessibility has its own explanation. It stands with it in a way similar to that of retinal images: we cannot, namely, look at our own retinal images, and only a second person can view them with the ophthalmoscope. Admittedly no such ophthalmoscope has been invented as would make it possible to constate memory-phantasms that someone has; nor do I mean that a person could ever by any means be put in a position to perceive memory-phantasms in which another person is absorbed. That would run counter to the principle of contradiction. But consider that the situation with respect to original impressions is exactly the same as with respect to memory-phantasms. If a person A has some impression a, a person B cannot have identically the same impression a.

[p. 246]

My impression cannot be your impression: this is as certain as that I and you are not one person. It is possible that the person B has an impression b which relates identically to the same body or to the same property, to the same change of a body; but the impression b can never be identical with the impression a. Exactly so it is also with memory. If a person A has a memory-phantasm a, a person B cannot have this memory-phantasm a. But it is quite possible i n p r i n c i p l e that the person B has a perception b which relates to identically the same nerve-process that the person A grasps as memory-phantasm a. The nerve-process x, which for the person A is a memory-phantasm a, could — if we possessed the physical apparatus necessary for it — furnish for the person B a perception b. The factual possibility of observing the nerve-processes of a person does not exist; the p o s s i b i l i t y i n p r i n c i p l e of such observation must, however, be admitted, because nerve-processes are spatiotemporal occurrences, and because spatiotemporal occurrences must be regarded as in principle open to inference. With whatever happens in space and time we always approach with the demand that it present itself to observation as motion, as chemical process, as heat, light, electricity, or some form of energy still unknown to us. Only in the face of the nonspatial and the nontemporal do we give up all direct observation in principle, because an eternal can never be grasped immediately as eternal, but always only through a transitory (spatiotemporal) thing.

There is therefore no reason whatever not to place memory under the same double point of view as the original impression. Only for the person himself who is absorbed in the memory is this memory always there only one-sidedly, because for that person it serves only as a means,

[p. 22]

relating itself by direct awareness to a fact. Now memory, however, so long as it is memory, can be only a means and never an end for the direct awareness of a person; so for that very person it must always offer only a one-sided aspect. For the direct awareness of a second person it would have to become a spatiotemporal fact just as much — if one had the physical means for it — as any arbitrary physical occurrence to which we assign a position in space and time.

22. The Doctrine of Unity.

25. The human mind is, as has been said, thoroughly steeped in sensibility, and strives in vain to shake it off. Judgment is indeed an experience of eternity, but we are unable to pass it without the aid of impression and memory; nor is it possible to judge about a judgment unless the first were present, rendered sensible by signs. Thus our cognition goes about on two crutches: on the crutch of the sensible impression, and on the crutch of the sensible symbol; and is accordingly always exposed to a twofold danger of stumbling, for on the one side it is threatened by the deceptive semblance of impressions and memories, and on the other side by the misunderstanding of symbols. But one misunderstands not only the symbols (words) that a second person uses: one misunderstands — what is far worse — one’s own word. One misunderstands it because ambiguity adheres to the word just as much as semblance does to the sensible impression. Instead of concealing this, it is far better to acknowledge it, and to procure means for combating the ambiguity to which we may succumb with every word. The Scholastics have indeed directed their attention to this

[p. 248]

subject, but their considerations on e q u i v o c a t i o n seem to me clumsy, because they always keep to the single word and furnish no theory of equivocation, which is what chiefly matters in logic. Just as natural science develops a formal doctrine for compensating errors of observation, so it is one of the most important duties of logic to furnish a systematic doctrine of equivocation. For to proceed as B a c o n and his empiricist successors did, in that they merely played themselves up as accusers of human language, and gave themselves the appearance as if scientific thinking without the use of signs were possible, seems to me a procedure as if someone, because it is possible that our eyes may flicker and that we may see an object double, were to have his eyes put out.

We have seen that we can grasp the eternal in a twofold way, once through direct awareness in the object-judgment, the other time through contrary awareness in the judgment about the judgment. In direct awareness we grasp the eternal by means of a memory referring back to an original impression; in contrary awareness we grasp the eternal by means of sensible symbols (linguistic sentences, which consist of words). Because we can grasp the eternal in a twofold way, and could not set this forth immediately at the outset of our inquiries, our whole exposition hitherto has suffered from a grave equivocation. We said, namely, in the doctrine of the duality of judgment that through our object-judgment we find in the memory, as also in the impression to which it is referred, an eternal, whereby our transitory experiences acquire the character of conceptual experiences, and our judgment presents itself as the relation of a predicate-concept to the subject-concept. This, however, is a conception of judgment by a judgment, hence a

[p. 22]

conception that could be won only by contrary awareness; whereas the object-judgment itself was no act of contrary but of direct awareness. Hereby, however, we fell into an equivocation, as I shall presently set forth.

When we refer ourselves by a memory to an original impression, we do indeed find in this double experience an eternal, but this eternal is at first s o m e t h i n g a c t u a l . Only when we make the experience grasped in a judgment the object of a second judgment by means of contrary awareness do we find in the symbols of that first judgment an eternal “expressed,” and may say that this eternal is nothing other than: c o n c e p t (or more correctly, a concept that is related to a concept). In the direct judgment we found the eternal as s o m e t h i n g a c t u a l , in the contrary judgment we found the eternal as c o n c e p t (as truth through the relation of concept to concept). This distinction, however, could not be made, because we had not yet separated direct and contrary awareness. Only in the principle of judgment pairs did the opposition of our modes of awareness come to the fore. At the same time, however, we recognized that it is impossible to become aware directly and contrarily at once, with the consequence that we are continually in danger of falling into error, since logic requires of us that we supplement direct awareness by indirect.

Now it is also apparent in what that error can consist. In order to develop our conceptual thinking we must supplement direct awareness by contrary, and thereby recognize the content of our direct judging in a new, in a contrary light. In this contrary light we bring forth, for the concepts which direct awareness produced, the supplement to paired concepts. T h e principle of judgment pairs

[p. 250]

necessarily entails the principle of concept pairs. There are concepts which are related to one another as direct awareness is related to contrar y: they have the same content, yet p r e s e n t i t i n o p p o s e d c o n c e p t i o n s . These are the well-known double concepts or concept pairs, which play a quite distinguished role in all philosophizing. Their nature, however, remained unelucidated; now we see how at least a certain class of double concepts is produced by the twofold manner of our awareness. In such concepts it is a matter of recognizing both their opposition and their unity, for whoever keeps in view merely their opposition but neglects their unity, or merely stresses their unity and does not acknowledge their opposition, falls into a contradiction with himself and becomes a d u a l i s t . (Whoever emphasizes the unity of those concept pairs but denies their opposition must likewise be called a dualist, because he too is in a contradiction; he is a monist merely in appearance.)

We have an example of concept pairs ready to hand; for we are concerned just now with the double concept of r e a l i t y a n d t r u t h . Throughout the course of our considerations hitherto we have not separated these twin concepts from one another, and so were bound to fall into a ceaseless equivocation of them. Now these two concepts are of opposed nature, because they spring from an opposed awareness; but they also form a unity of concepts, because direct and contrary awareness together first constitute human awareness. It is interesting to observe how natural awareness behaves toward the concept pair before us. Actual and true are used promiscuously in ordinary speech: natural awareness

[p. 22]

presses toward their unity. Of an opposition between the concepts reality and truth, however, one can scarcely speak before the nonlogician without being accused of sophistry. What is actual is surely true, and conversely what is true is surely actual: so he will tell us, and it cannot be denied that with these words he has calmly uttered a truth whose scope is such that he for the most part cannot become aware of it at all. For it is precisely we here who assert the unity of the actual and the true; only we are also conscious that we cannot and may not assert this unity logically without also stressing the opposition of the twin concepts before us. To bring this opposition out clearly, only a few explanatory words are needed.

Since we have truth in concepts that are related to one another, let us be permitted, instead of truth, to speak of the concepts by which it is grasped. Then we must set c o n c e p t over against r e a l i t y , so as in this way to make natural awareness also attentive to the fact that between reality and truth there is precisely that opposition which everyone readily recognizes when we use the word concept instead of the word truth. In direct awareness we have the eternal as something actual, in contrary awareness we have the eternal as concept. To be sure, the nonlogician will now be inclined to let reality and concept pass only as opposites, and will maintain that he does not understand at all how one can speak of a unity of reality and concept, for if there can be any opposition at all, it is the opposition between the actual and the concept of it, between being and the knowledge of being. Thus natural awareness, in the case of every double concept, wavers to and fro between the unity

[p. 252]

and the opposition of the halves of the concept, and usually passes unnoticed from the one contradiction into the other. With this I wished to characterize the most important class of equivocations, against which one for the most part struggles in vain even with the greatest exertion of the mind.

For if one puts together such a concept pair as, for example, r e a l i t y and c o n c e p t (or, more correctly, truth), one is easily confused by the thought that the word r e a l i t y too is only a sign for a c o n c e p t . Misled by this, one may come upon the thought that what we have in direct awareness as reality is nothing other than a concept. This equivocation leads to f a l s e i d e a l i s m . Since P l a t o one speaks in philosophy of Ideas, and by them one often understands such concepts as, to use the expression, are not d i s c u r s i v e , but are the eternally actual itself. Now we human beings have only discursive concepts, that is, concepts which we present through linguistic symbols, and which we are able to develop logically only in such a way that in contrary awareness we come back to what we experienced in direct awareness as reality.

The concept is never the actual, and the actual is never the concept: at least not for us human beings. Between reality and concept there is the same opposition as between object-awareness and self-awareness. Now we have seen, however, that object-awareness and self-awareness cannot take place at the same time, although we could not possess an object-awareness without supplementing it by self-awareness. Both modes of awareness point to the same eternal, but they nonetheless remain opposed modes of awareness. The one is not the other, and only the eternal is the same that we grasp in the opposed modes of awareness. This eternal itself is the unity of the actual, which we have in direct

[p. 22]

awareness as impression and memory, and of what we grasp in contrary awareness as truth (through concepts). To grasp the eternal as eternal without the aid of a transitory is, however, impossible for us human beings, and just because this is impossible, the unity of reality and concept is in us only a knowledge of this unity, not this unity itself. We know that reality and our concept are one in the eternal, but we k n o w it merely; that is, we have such knowledge only in contrary awareness, and not in the direct. Were we able to have this knowledge through direct awareness as well, both modes of awareness would have to take place mathematically at once: our object-awareness would already be self-awareness, reality would be its own concept, and our judgment would be the fact itself and not a judgment about it.

The eternal as the unity of the actual and the true we call God, and indeed the transcendent or extramundane God, as distinguished from the immanent or intramundane God, whom we grasp as an eternal now in direct, now in contrary awareness. The eternal of direct awareness is called substance, the eternal of contrary awareness is called the I. The clear working-out of these concepts is beset with the highest difficulties, because with them we move at every moment on the hairbreadth edge of equivocation, where to right and left we are equally in danger of falling victim to a bottomless absurdity. A scientific doctrine of these concepts can be furnished only on the basis of a doctrine of equivocation (that is, of the ambiguity of human speech) carried out to the most painstaking detail. The doctrine of equivocation is nothing other than that chapter of logic in which we endeavor, to the best of our powers, to destroy the sophistical semblance that arises from our having double experiences, double judgments and double

[p. 254]

concepts. This task, however, must not be conceived as merely negative, for it is most intimately bound up with the positive task of logic. Logic is also a science of human error, and indeed not of this or that special aberration, but of that general aberration which adheres to the nature of our awareness and is the reverse side of our faculty of cognition. One may say outright that the value of a logic depends on the degree to which it furnishes a g e n e r a l theory of error, not only because a systematic doctrine of error would make superfluous what is called skeptical philosophy, but because without such a doctrine the theory of truth, too, remains a mere sham theory, which is overthrown by every breath of skepticism. The more boldly we set forth the fundamental weaknesses of the human mind (for example, that unavoidable weakness whereby what has just passed seems to us to be present, so that it is impossible to distinguish strictly between impression and memory), the more occasions we create for reason to win new scientific victories. A recognized weakness is a strength. A defeat seen through is a victory. At least it is so in logic, whose power consists in establishing a doctrine of unity precisely by uncovering the divided nature of our awareness and the fundamental ambiguity of our immediate experiences and concepts. The greater the original weaknesses of our mind, the more brilliant can the positive victories of science become. Since our awareness is, so to speak, limitlessly weak, it makes possible for us a limitless progress in science. Instead, then, of despairing over the weaknesses of our awareness, we have every reason to regard these weaknesses as the necessary conditions of our progress, and to turn them to account in such a

[p. 22]

sense. No greater mine of cognition than our own weakness! In seeking to hold fast to this, we overcome skepticism, which is the proper enemy of science. Skepticism has hitherto for the most part been the parasite of science; it sucked out its marrow and its life-blood. Let us reverse the relation! Every doubt that stirs shall be welcomed, for as surely as it is there, so surely there must be a victorious cognition that will do it to death. Thus not the fear of doubt, but the strongest possible exploitation of doubt is the watchword of logical inquiry.

Had there been no semblance that the earth is at rest, there would have been no Copernicus. It is similar with all the discoveries of natural science; if one investigates, one will always find the deceptive semblance that gave occasion for the discovery of a truth. Now error, too, has the same Janus-face as truth, for because our awareness has a double nature, we grasp truth in a double way, or in this striving can also fall into error in a double way. To make this state of affairs at least palpable is the purpose of this sketch of a logic. Our human erring in science springs from a twofold source: 1. from the circumstance that our direct awareness proceeds from a double experience, namely from the impression and the memory, which play over into each other and back again, and never permit an absolutely sharp separation; 2. from the circumstance that our most important concepts are double concepts, because they are produced by a double awareness, of which the one continually squints toward the other, and one usually does not know which of the two wants to do the talking.—Our human erring has thus an impressionist and a symbolic source. The chief logical or symbolic errors are the errors of equivocation.

[p. 256]

We combat equivocation by seeking out its occasion in the nature of our awareness and by setting forth the laws of this awareness. Every true logical theorem is a victory over equivocation. But in order not to fall again and again into the deceptive double semblance of our symbols, we must also seek sensible means to free ourselves from equivocation. In other words, we must be concerned with the improvement of our symbols. In this direction logic is a critique of human language. To be sure, it criticizes language by means of language. This is no circle, for we criticize direct awareness by means of contrary awareness and contrary awareness by means of direct, which likewise is no circle. The critique of human language must gradually lead to the development of a logical symbolism, which admittedly cannot stand finished from one day to the next, and which also is not found by going straight to the construction of symbols. Every theorem of logic is a support for a symbolism that is to be created, and if the theory of judgment is led toward so rich an unfolding as, for example, geometry, its theorems will lead quite imperceptibly to a symbolism. Enlightened thinkers, such as D e s c a r t e s and L e i b n i z , thought of such a symbolism; but as usually happens with great thoughts, this one too was at first conceived utopianly. The brilliant L e i b n i z , in his excess of zeal, wanted at once to bring about a perfection of human thinking by means of a calculus philosophicus, and for that very reason his grandiose plan could not succeed.

For the present it seems to me sufficient if one tries to illustrate the theorems of logic geometrically. Such an illustration appears unpretentiously; for the time being it wants nothing other than to relieve the wearisome contrary awareness and to afford it, in geometrical intuition,

[p. 22]

a resting-cushion. Thus we can, for example, make the unity of the actual and the true, that is, the supreme theorem of logic, readily comprehensible to geometrically trained thinking by means of the straight line and its point at infinity. If one traverses the straight line in thought in the two opposed directions, one arrives, according to the geometrical conception, at one and the same infinitely distant point of the straight line. This is easily seen if one considers that the straight line can be conceived as a circle described with an infinitely great radius.

Truth                                  Reality

But in order not to let this symbolism degenerate into a mystical plaything, we must recognize its principle. That is, we must ask ourselves on what it rests that there are profound analogies between logic and mathematics. We can solve this question only if we first seek to fathom the scientific character of mathematics. For as clear as the content of this science is, so obscure is its logical position in the circle of the other sciences. It is as if an exceptional position belonged to it among all the sciences, and the more one reflects on this, the more one marvels that man is in possession of so sublime a science. We shall therefore try to cast at least a ray of light on the logical position of mathematics in the circle of the sciences.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.            17

[PDF 259]

S e c t i o n V.

Special Logic.

23. Neutral Awareness.

26. Logicians or metaphysicians have always paid to mathematics an admiration mixed with envy, for they felt their own striving to be most intimately akin to that of mathematics, yet at the same time had to admit with inner shame how far logic — as regards scientific development — lags behind mathematics, indeed how it is quite the counterpart of mathematics, for whereas the latter represents, so to speak, an endless solemn triumphal procession of truth, logic or metaphysics offers the dismal spectacle of an endless confusion and an eternal strife. So it is comprehensible that emulation of mathematics and a wish to do likewise has become quite a tradition in logic or metaphysics. Hardly any thinker was more powerfully gripped by this tradition than K a n t . The character of his entire Critique of Reason is not only deeply influenced but quite determined by the conception he forms of the position of mathematics in the circle of the sciences. When he thinks of the task of logic and metaphysics (for these are for him two essentially different sciences), he always brings them into

[p. 23]

relation to the character of mathematics; indeed, he is able to establish the significance of logic and metaphysics as sciences in no other way than by measuring it against the significance of mathematics. All his fundamental divisions, such as the distinction of cognitions a posteriori and a priori, and likewise the division of judgments into synthetic and analytic, are at bottom so conceived that by means of them the position of mathematics among the sciences, and thereby also the position of logic and metaphysics, may first be fixed. In his two fundamental distinctions there lies what could be called a classification of the sciences, and indeed a classification that is oriented by the model position of mathematics.

If we free his divisions from the misleading semblance of an ambiguous terminology, there lies at the foundation of them the cleavage between material and formal cognitions, or also sciences. Now he does not, to be sure, use the expressions formal and material cognition in his fundamental definitions, for it would indeed be far too harsh to demand cognitions or sciences that have matter and no form, or form and no matter; but this impossible demand nonetheless lies at the foundation of the Kantian theory of knowledge, if in a cloaked and blurred fashion. Such expressions as empirical and pure, a posteriori and a priori, are there only to gloss over and conceal the impossible opposition between thoroughly material and thoroughly formal cognition. An “empirical” cognition must at least be a little formal too, while the pure must in any case be quite formal. A cognition a posteriori is not merely material, and it too has a little form; conversely, a cognition a priori is not always wholly formal, but at times also a little material. Thus these dualistic terms squint across at one another in a shamefaced

17*

[p. 260]

way, which, however, cannot prevent us from recognizing the impossible opposition between formal and material cognition as the fundamental defect of the Kantian Critique of Reason.

Material cognition is supposed, however, to be the domain of physics in the widest sense; whereas formal cognition is supposed to belong to metaphysics in the widest sense. To the formal sciences belongs in the first rank mathematics, for its theorems are the model of universal validity and necessity. Now when Kant compares formal (or a priori) mathematics with the formal (or a priori) logic of Aristotle, he finds that the former is a fruitful science, engaged in steady progress, whereas the latter essentially still stands where Aristotle left it. On the strength of this impression the thought becomes fixed in him that mathematics is a science that advances and extends our cognition, whereas general logic is a science incapable of progress, which must be regarded as completed, and which at bottom only explicates and makes distinct what one knows even without it. In order now to characterize this opposition between mathematics and logic, he contrives for himself an opposition between synthetic and analytic judgments, of which only the first are real judgments, whereas the latter, because they are merely explicative (repetitive), can be called judgments only in God’s name. Among the formal (a priori) sciences, then, mathematics represents the fruitful, ampliative, synthetic science, whereas its counterpart, logic, is supposed to be the unfruitful, merely explicative, analytic discipline, and, if one speaks without euphemism: the science of nothing. Thus Kant’s fundamental distinctions (a priori and a posteriori, analytic and synthetic) serve quite properly the purpose of bringing his views

[p. 23]

on the position of the principal sciences relative to one another into logical formulas. The impossible opposition of formal and material separates the metaphysical from the physical sciences; and further, the impossible opposition of analytic and synthetic is there in the first place in order to divide unfruitful logic from fruitful mathematics. The question that Kant, on the basis of these singular distinctions, put to himself was whether there could not also be a second kind of logic, a fruitful logic, which would then be the proper metaphysics and would not need to be so ashamed in the face of mathematics as general or Aristotelian (formal) logic. And he actually devised a second logic, a transcendental logic, a kind of metaphysics that wants to demonstrate by means of metaphysical speculations that metaphysics is impossible. Metaphysics demonstrates its own impossibility; but since it is that which carries out the demonstration, it proves at the same time its own possibility. This impossible possibility of metaphysics forms the proper content of transcendental logic.

27. We reject both fundamental distinctions of Kant and found the opposition (and the unity) of physics and metaphysics on the opposition (and the unity) of direct and contrary awareness, which with us takes the place of the distinction of a posteriori and a priori. The untenability of the distinction between analytic and synthetic judgments we have also set forth, and since this Kantian distinction has the purpose of separating logic and mathematics from one another, we must now replace it by another, that is, we must fix the logical position of mathematics in the system of the sciences.

Let us first set aside geometry and kinematics and direct our attention to arithmetic, so

[p. 262]

we find that the fundamental arithmetical act is that judging which we call counting judging, or, in short, counting. If we are now able to characterize that kind of awareness from which counting judging springs, then we have set forth the logical nature of arithmetic, and thereby fundamentally of mathematics in general. To be sure, I do not mean that we shall thereby have gained a detailed logical characterization of mathematics, but what is at issue here is merely the first and most important step toward such a characterization, and not at once a detailed philosophy of mathematics. We ask, then, what the most essential characteristic of counting judgment is, whereby it differs from every other judging; for if we succeed in discovering such a distinguishing characteristic, then we have grasped the logical position of mathematics within the circle of all the sciences, or at least have gained the foundation for the logical characterization of mathematics.

Now counting judging begins with that act of judgment in which we assert of any object of our judgment whatever that it is o n e . It suffices, as we shall see, to grasp in its nature the first counting judgment, in which we form the concept of one, in order to understand at once every other counting judging (at least so far as positive whole numbers are concerned). He who explains to us how a man is able to take a step has explained to us the whole of walking; and he who shows us the logical nature of that judgment in which we say of something that it is one has disclosed to us the logical nature of all counting judging. We therefore reduce the question as to the logical nature of counting judging to the problem of what the logical essence is of those judgments in which we say of something that it is one.

Now it has the appearance as though among all our

[p. 23]

judgments no act could be found more superfluous than when we assert of something that it is one; as, for example, when we form the judgment, “This thaler is one,” or, “This ball is one.” Had Kant, instead of his example, 7 + 5 = 12, set before himself one of the examples just cited, nothing would have been left for him but to admit that it already lies in the sense of the expression “This thaler” that it is one; for if I form, say, the judgment, “This thaler bears the stamp of the year 1893,” everyone who hears this judgment knows that the talk is only of o n e thaler, even if I do not add, “This thaler is one.” Kant would then have had to say to himself, in the sense of his terminology, that the judgment “This thaler is one” is analytic, for the predicate “one” already lies in the sense of the expression “This thaler,” that is, in the sense of the subject. (The subject stands, as the grammarian says, in the singular.) Thus the very first step that we take in counting would be an analytic judgment: an ill omen for all the following steps; for, as I shall show, the very first step that we take in counting is a counting judging, which must already contain within itself all the essential characteristics of the later counting judging. In any case, however, we already recognize here that the concept of one is one that, in the sense of the Kantian terminology, springs from an analytic judgment. Everyone also sees that in the formation of the number-concepts 2, 3, 4 . . . etc. the concept of one must necessarily cooperate, for it is impossible to arrive at the concept of two unless we form the concept 1 + 1. Since, then, the concept of one plays into every counting judging, every counting judging is burdened with that analytic judging by which we arrived at the concept of the number one. In short, we see without much trouble that the first step we take in counting would, in Kant’s sense, have to be an analytic

[p. 264]

judgment, and that all the rest of counting involves that first analytic judgment over and over again. We see that the separation of the alleged synthetic judgment from the analytic is impracticable; for even if the judgment 7 + 5 = 12 had a synthetic character, it would at the same time have to have an analytic character as well, because the concept of one, which was gained through an analytic judgment, plays into all the rest of counting.

Moreover, I have already demonstrated the error that lies in distinguishing between analytic and synthetic judgments. This error could arise only because a theory of judgment was lacking. The principle of identity, which forms the foundation of the theory of judgment and hence of logic, was entirely misunderstood; the automatic repetition of a sentence was taken for the principle of identity. The consequence of this perversity was that Kant was able to find identity only in the empty (repeated) sentences, whereas he believed that the proper or synthetic sentences were formed without identity.

Every true judgment is an identical one, and it is true only because it is identical, which sentence can also be reversed. By means of memory we grasp something eternal or identical and recognize it as belonging to the original impression. But it had not been recognized that our object-judgments proceed from double experiences, and so it was also unintelligible how a predicate-concept could stand with a subject-concept in a relation of identity, since the predicate is indeed a different concept from the subject. The whole structure of a sentence seemed to be a living contradiction, for two very distinguishable concepts (subject and predicate) were supposed to stand in a relation of identity. Men had not the courage to bring this apparent contradiction openly to speech, though it made itself felt anew in every sentence. Subject and predicate are supposed to be identical and yet

[p. 23]

also not identical. How does that come about? Is every sentence to overthrow a fundamental truth of logic — the principle of contradiction — and is every assertion to be a living absurdity? The answer to this justified question is contained in the doctrine of the double experiences from which our judgment proceeds. The impression, like the memory, is something transitory, but we find — provided only that the judgment is true — in both transitorinesses the same eternal thing, and this grounds the identity of the subject-concept and the predicate-concept. But since this identity was recognized on the basis of a double experience, and the impression is not identical with the memory, we have, on the occasion of the recognition that both experiences contain a common eternal thing, to form two concepts, which are related to one another precisely by the judgment. Herewith the duality of the structure of the sentence and, at the same time, the identical relation of subject and predicate in every true sentence is set forth, and the semblance that the predicate could be the same concept as the subject is overcome once and for all. The identity of the true judgment does not rest on the predicate-concept being identically the same as the subject-concept; on the contrary: in order to be able to pass an identical judgment, two different concepts must be grasped under the aspect of identification, that is, of judging. In the object-judgment we have the aspect of identification only in the relation of the memory to the impression; if this relation is lacking, no true object-judgment can be passed. By virtue of our contrary awareness, however, we can also pass such judgments in which one or several judgments form the object of our judging, and by means of this higher judging we discover something identical in the underlying judgments, whereby sentences can then arise in which we express not facts, but symbolic (the so-called a priori) truths.

[p. 266]

They always rest on object-judgments, because contrary awareness cannot subsist without object-awareness.

28. Proceeding from the general nature of the judgment of fact, we must try to make intelligible also that judgment in which we say of something that it is one. If we have had any impression whatever, we can by means of memory say of that impression that it is one; indeed, we can say that u n i q u e n e s s belongs to the impression. For the same impression can never return: as certainly not as the same point of time cannot emerge anew in the course of time; in general, as certainly not as the principle of contradiction is a truth. Uniqueness belongs to all impressions, which can also be expressed thus: every immediate experience (e.g. every intuition) is unique, or again, everything transitory is unique. Our direct awareness is always eager for uniqueness, and it wishes by means of object-judgments to exhaust the uniqueness of the impression received, which, to be sure, can never succeed, because, as I have already shown, the impression is inexhaustible, so that one could do justice to it only by an infinite series of judgments. Arithmetic now helps itself in the face of this state of affairs in a very simple and ingenious way: it declares, namely, that what has just been experienced is u n i q u e , whereby it has exhausted the inexhaustible in the impression after its own fashion. For by declaring that what is experienced is inexhaustible, it has precisely mastered this inexhaustibility and grasped it through the concept of uniqueness. Whereas, then, the empiricist toils endlessly in an impression to grasp, if possible, many points of departure for object-judgments, and can never satisfy himself in this, the arithmetician has solved the whole infinite task of the empiricist with a single word, playfully; for he simply declares that that impression is inexhaustible and u n i q u e .

[p. 23]

For the arithmetician, namely, the impression has nothing to offer but its uniqueness, and as an arithmetician he does full justice to every impression if he declares it to be unique. What attributes belong to that impression, the empiricist may trouble himself about: for the arithmetician, or more correctly for the person who passes counting judgments, the impression has only the attribute of uniqueness and is exhausted with that.

But because the arithmetician has made empirical matters so easy for himself, he grasps in the impression only something that is common to all impressions. He acknowledges that the impression he receives is something quite particular, something unique; but since he does not trouble himself further about that quite particular thing whereby the impression has the character of uniqueness, he grasps in every impression nothing other than its uniqueness. Herein it is expressed that he well distinguishes the particular uniqueness from the general uniqueness of impressions, or at least would have to distinguish them well if he wished to give himself a clear account of what he does when he says of something that it is one. He grasps in the particular unique thing the general one. In that he relates the memory to the original impression, he grasps the general one (which is laid at the basis of the memory) in the particular unique thing of the original impression. He expresses this by the defining equation:

1 = 1, where the 1 on the left is the sign for the particular uniqueness of what is experienced, the 1 on the right, on the other hand, the sign of the general one. Incidentally, I will remark that the general one also plays a role in language, namely in the so-called indefinite article, whose somewhat obscure nature can perhaps be explained thus: that it is meant to represent not the particular uniqueness but merely the general one. If one considers, say, the

[p. 268]

following sentence, spoken by someone who points to several coins: “Only this o n e gold piece is a Roman coin,” then we have in the subject the particular unique thing as a numeral, and in the predicate the general one as the indefinite article.

The distinction of the particular uniqueness as subject-concept from the general one as predicate-concept forms the foundation for the logic of counting judgments. To show this, it suffices to reflect on the number 2, because its logical elucidation clearly contains within itself that of all the other numbers. The difficulty which the number 2 presents for logical explanation consists in this, that it designates at once the connection and the separation of a unique thing and another unique thing, and thus seems to contain a contradiction within itself. For the union of two units seems to demand that they be joined into a new one, while the holding apart of the two units seems to contain a protest against their union. The solution of this apparent contradiction is found by going back to the concept of one. This concept, namely, as we showed above, is a d o u b l e c o n c e p t , for as subject-concept it signifies the unique thing, and as predicate-concept the general one, which is to be found in every unique thing. If, now, one unit is to be joined with the other, this happens with the help of the general one, while the holding apart of the two units takes place through the unique thing in each unit. He, then, who has grasped the double concept in the concept of one will not be surprised that in counting we join the units with one another and yet also hold them apart. The general one is the binding agent, the particular one the separating agent. To be sure, the presentation of these logical relations demands a most painstaking elaboration, which, however, I must forgo at this place. As with the

[p. 23]

number 2, so it is with the remaining whole numbers, for in all of them what matters is to join units in such a way that they remain held apart. Curiously, it is not the custom in logic to point to the apparent contradiction that lurks in every number-concept of the natural series of numbers, although the very solution of this apparent contradiction is to be regarded as the proper logical task in the treatment of the number-concept. The distinction of the one as subject-concept and the one as predicate-concept gives the key to the problem; this distinction, in turn, points back to the fact that judgments are double experiences. Thus the double operation of joining and separating, which is continually applied in all counting, is rooted in the end in the general double nature of judging as such.

Now, however, the question may be raised with what right the arithmetician wishes to make the particular one in the subject and the general one in the predicate recognizable by identical signs, in that he creates the formula 1 = 1, which offers no guarantee that the double concept of one be strictly distinguished in its two component concepts. Indeed, that formula seems directly to work toward the confusion of the two twin concepts.

But it is precisely this question that is to lead us to the characterization of counting judging. In counting we place ourselves on principle at a standpoint where the difference between the particular one and the general one is i n d i f f e r e n t to us. For if in counting we were to lay weight upon holding fast the particularity of each individual thing, counting would be impossible. As little as it is possible to count a dog and a cat together, if one holds fast to the particularity of the objects to be counted, so little would counting be possible at all, if with every one we wished to add also some particular mark by which it can be distinguished from the

[p. 270]

other one. We do indeed think that every one that we count is a quite particular, unique one, but it is absolutely a matter of indifference to us what its uniqueness rests upon, and we do not on principle concern ourselves with what attributes may belong to that one, which just make it a unique thing. The absolute indifference which we bring to everything particular in the one makes it possible for us to count another one together with it, for we find in both ones just this much, that each of them is one, that they are therefore of the same kind.

It is a quite peculiar and most admirable feat that the human mind accomplishes in counting. The particular that is found in a thing is grasped as something general that belongs to every thing. What, now, is that quite particular thing which, although it is something quite particular, yet belongs to all things without exception? This quite particular thing in every thing is its u n i q u e n e s s ; for this is what belongs to every thing. Since, then, in counting we reflect upon the uniqueness of a thing only insofar as it is to be found in all things, and abstract from the attributes in which the uniqueness of a thing may be grounded: counting is a judging in which we on principle wish to make no distinction between the particular one and the general one. The difference between the one as subject-concept and the one as predicate-concept is indifferent to us, that is, in counting judging the difference between subject and predicate is disregarded. Subject and predicate are thereby equalized, and the principle of identity takes on the form of t h e p r i n c i p l e o f e q u a l i t y , which is the fundamental principle of mathematical judging. In place of judgments there appear equations, whose left and right sides are interchangeable, because we on principle abstract from the difference of the subject

[p. 23]
