and predicate in the judgment. That mode of awareness, however, in which in judging we make on principle no distinction between the subject and the predicate of the judging, I call n e u t r a l a w a r e n e s s . Mathematics is the science of neutral awareness.

Now it is also made clear why counting and adding — as the arithmeticians express it — is a c o m m u t a t i v e and a s s o c i a t i v e operation. In counting off things we can think them arranged in any groups we please, and neither will these groups exert an influence on the character of the result of the count, nor can it have any significance for this result in what order we counted the individual things together. For in the sum every individual thing does indeed count as an individual, and yet everything particular whereby the individual is distinguished as such is completely suppressed in the sum. This rests on the fact that in counting we on principle abstract from that whereby the particular one is distinguished from the general one. One can also express this by saying that in counting we give up the difference between the original impression and the memory related to it. In the impression we find (as arithmeticians) the thing as one, in the memory of it likewise as one, and the impression therefore contains nothing more than the memory: they are both equally poor, for of the whole inexhaustible wealth of the original impression we grasp nothing more than what we also have in the memory (following the impression), and in this too nothing more than that there was an individual thing present, about which we could indeed have troubled ourselves, but about which we did not trouble ourselves further. Since impression and memory are thus equalized, the one in the subject and in the predicate of the counting judgment is also equalized. The judgment, which in direct and contrary awareness was, so to speak, something

[p. 272]

living, something organized, because the subject-concept had a different character from the predicate-concept, is now through counting turned into something dead, inorganic; for it is the differentiation of the subject and the predicate that can be designated in the judgment as what is, as it were, alive.

29. In a detailed system of logic a great chapter will have to be devoted to the characterization of neutral awareness, because a close examination of the activities of neutral awareness will exert a fructifying influence upon the mathematical disciplines. The doctrine of neutral awareness is the logical or metaphysical complement to mathematics: it is m e t a m a t h e m a t i c s , and has a right to existence only if it furnishes stimuli to new discoveries in mathematics. Unfortunately I cannot, in this sketch, go further than to formulate the demand for a metamathematics. For this purpose, however, I must illuminate neutral awareness from yet another side.

In neutral awareness we equalize not only subject and predicate of the judgment, we also equalize the object-judgment and the I-judgment, or direct and contrary awareness. For in the counting of things it is wholly a matter of indifference whether we count the things ourselves, or whether someone else, who perceives the utterance of our acts of counting, counts off these acts of counting. It is a matter of indifference whether the things are counted by direct judgments, or these direct judgments by contrary judgments. It is a matter of indifference whether in counting one attends to the particular sensible impression at hand and recognizes it as one, or whether, setting a symbol for the one (a stroke, or the sign 1), one now takes these symbols instead of the original impression as the basis for counting. The difference between judgment on the one hand and the judgment about the judgment on the other: in short,

[p. 23]

the significant difference between object-awareness and self-awareness is equalized. Only in this fact does mathematics betray its highly peculiar character, which separates it from all other sciences in a striking way. Mathematics is, namely, a science in which the difference between object-awareness and self-awareness is disregarded, that is, in which the difference between physics and metaphysics appears to be abolished on principle. It is a matter of indifference whether I count things, or whether in my self-awareness I count off my acts of judgment which were directed upon those things. The symbol which I set in place of the thing has become totally equivalent to that thing itself, and I can now concern myself merely with the symbols instead of with the things. No object-judgment brings this about. There is no object-judgment that could replace the original impression to which it refers. Infinitely many object-judgments would be necessary to exhaust the impression; that is, it is inexhaustible. In arithmetic, however, the counting judgment completely replaces the thing counted, for this judgment can now step into the place of the thing itself and become the object of counting. But this also means that in mathematics the symbol replaces the thing. Symbol and thing are equivalent.

Since, then, mathematics is able to replace things entirely by symbols (as no other science is), it is the symbolic science par excellence. Regarding its own symbols as things, it can advance to symbols of symbols, and it thus raises its neutral awareness to higher powers without end. In spite of its great self-sufficiency, however, it must not give up living contact, on the one hand with physics, on the other with logic (metaphysics), for its neutral awareness withers if it turns away from the world of facts, or if it becomes estranged from the common goal of all science, metaphysics.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.            18

[p. 274]

It is, i n i t s w h o l e e s s e n c e , t h e h a i r l i n e b o u n d a r y b e t w e e n p h y s i c s a n d m e t a p h y s i c s , just as neutral awareness too can be regarded as that boundary where direct and contrary awareness give up their difference. For this very reason we often find significant mathematical talent united with metaphysical genius, as, for example, in D e s c a r t e s and L e i b n i z . On the other hand, mathematicians are very often highly gifted for physics, so that it is not necessary to cite examples of this.

Mathematics wins its special position within the circle of the sciences by practicing great renunciation toward the whole world of impressions, and it may perhaps be regarded as the poorest science. But the greater its renunciation, the loftier is the flight of its thought. It is, in a certain sense, the substitute that is given to us human beings in place of absolute cognition. By absolute cognition I understand one that has no need of time, as human cognition has. As I have set forth, it is given to us human beings only to grasp the transitory through the eternal, or conversely the eternal through the transitory; but never can we cognize the eternal with the elimination of the transitory (of the impression or symbol), that is, through the eternal itself. One can also express this by saying that our awareness requires time. But since we can quite well form the concept of a mode of awareness that is incomparably superior to ours in speed, which is able, for example, to make a perception and pass a judgment in every trillionth part of a second: so we can also arrive at the concept of an infinitely rapid awareness, or more correctly of an awareness that has no need of time at all, and this is what we may call absolute awareness. For it there is no opposition between direct

[p. 23]

and contrary awareness, for this opposition exists only in time: for man, who must supplement his direct awareness (in which he receives impressions of reality) in time by contrary awareness, in which he grasps what has been experienced in symbols, in order to be able to return to his judgment and judge it. Absolute awareness is, in fact, nothing other than the unity of our double awareness, the unity of truth and reality. As a substitute for this absolute awareness we have neutral awareness, that is, a mode of awareness in which we abstract from all attributes of the transitory to such a degree that the difference between direct and contrary awareness need not come into consideration. If we are already incapable of replacing direct and contrary awareness by a uniformly absolute awareness, we help ourselves at least in this way, that we remove from the balloon of our cognition as much ballast as is just necessary in order to be able to see past the opposition of the two modes of awareness and to neutralize them. This is how mathematics comes about: whose loftiness, as we see, is purchased at a very dear price. It is, to express myself hyperbolically, as if someone were to give away house and goods in order no longer to have to fear losing house and goods. In that mathematics, by abstracting from everything obstructive, equalizes subject and predicate, and likewise direct and contrary awareness, it avoids the two great sources of error of our cognition, sensible semblance and equivocation. It would be positively infallible if the symbols it uses were not after all again of a sensible nature, so that the error which one believes already to have excluded airtight might yet again creep in through the little sensible gate. Furthermore, it is well to note that the mathematical train of thought can by no means be entirely reduced to mathematical symbols; so that

18*

[p. 276]

the use of linguistic expressions carries the dangers of equivocation into mathematics as well. Thus even mathematics is not in the state that superficiality supposes. As regards exactness and certainty it is the most perfected science, and in this direction an eternal model. For the logician, however, it has a second and far greater significance; for since mathematics equalizes within itself the physical and the metaphysical method, it supplies the logician with analogies for his metaphysical thinking, and can be used to clarify and to consolidate the method of logic.

In that mathematics sets us above the difference between subject and predicate, as well as above the difference between direct and contrary awareness, it also draws our attention to the fact that the dual structure of a sentence and the doubleness of our awareness stand in the closest connection. That a judgment must consist of a subject and a predicate, and that an object-judgment cannot subsist without an I-judgment, are indeed two theorems of logic, yet can be conceived as one theorem. The first theorem is the principle of identity, the second is the principle of judgment pairs: but the latter is, as I have already remarked, a further elaboration of the former. For if an object-judgment cannot subsist without an I-judgment, this means as much as that we could not form subject and predicate of the object-judgment if we were not capable of forming the supplementary I-judgment. Direct awareness must be supplemented by contrary awareness, in order that the dual structure of the object-judgment may come about. If, then, mathematics neutralizes the difference between subject and predicate, it also neutralizes the difference between direct and contrary awareness.

[p. 24]

24. Space and Time.

30. In the doctrine of neutral awareness the answer to the question what space and time are is also already contained. This question is commonly raised because, in face of the concepts of space and time, one finds oneself in a peculiar predicament of the understanding. One says to oneself, on the one hand, that one has no impressions of space and time, as one has, for example, of the hardness, color or warmth of a body; on the other hand, however, one cannot deny that by touching and looking at a body one also gains an impression of its spatial extension, of its shape and position; indeed, if the body is in motion, one cannot deny that one also receives an impression of this motion, which would have the consequence that even the duration of a motion would make an impression on us. In short, one would like to let space and time count as impressions, and yet again also as no impressions; one would like to represent them as sensed, and yet also as not sensed. Out of this perplexity grows the question what space and time are.

The perplexity just characterized, and likewise the question that springs from it, bear the decided stamp of a sensualist way of thinking. One takes cognizing for sensing, and because one recognizes that the sensed phenomena have a spatiotemporal order, one believes that there must be special sensations for the spatial and temporal aspect of the phenomena, while on the other hand one also tells oneself that there are no such special sensations of space and time. To overcome this contradiction, however, one must above all say to oneself that cognition is no sensation, or also that one must not confound judging awareness with sensing awareness: for only from this standpoint

[p. 278]

can one gain a conception of space and time free of contradiction.

We f i n d space and time in our impressions and memories, but we find them without there being any special sensations of space and time. How is that possible? How can one find the spatial and temporal aspect of experiences in them, if within these experiences there are no special sensations which could help us to find, that is, to cognize, the spatial and temporal in them? There must surely be, within our experiences, some sensible signs that give our cognition the necessary points of support for finding a spatiotemporal arrangement in our experiences! Whoever thinks thus is on the way to the finely spun theory of local signs of L o t z e . But however delicate the ideas one may form of such local signs, they remain after all always sensible signs, that is, one assumes within the impressions a new class of most delicate impressions which are destined to orient us as to the spatial or even temporal position of the former. What are we to think of this? Let us suppose that an impression a contains within it another impression a1, destined merely to orient us as to the spatial position of a; then it must at once strike the eye that, by the same principle, the impression a1 too would in turn have to contain within it an impression a2, in order to inform us of its position in space; similarly a2 would have to carry with it an impression a3, this a new impression a4, etc., ad inf., as local sign, so that with the help of an incalculable host of local signs we should still not be oriented even as to the spatial position of a single impression. Thus we see that in an impression there can never be another that could serve us as a sign for the spatial or temporal position of the former.

[p. 24]

If, then, it cannot be a sensible sign that could indicate to us the spatiotemporal position of an impression a: what can there be in a that informs us of its spatiotemporal position? Nothing specially appointed for this at all! In that our cognizing glance finds the impression at all, it finds it as unique, and because it finds it as this unique impression, that is, as one that may not be confounded with any other impression in the realm of all possible impressions: this impression receives a distinct, immovable, eternal position in the realm of all possible impressions, and this its eternal position is what we find as the spatiotemporal character of the impression. Must, then, I ask, the uniqueness that belongs to an impression be marked in it by a special sign appointed expressly for this? Certainly not. Uniqueness belongs to every impression, by virtue of which it is this impression and no other; and absolutely no special aids are needed to secure to the impression its uniqueness. In its mere existence its uniqueness already lies. And the cognizing glance that finds the existence of an impression finds in the existence also the uniqueness belonging to it.

Could we find uniqueness in an impression if it were not there? By no means. No more than we can find the hard, the red or the warm in the impression if it is not there. The uniqueness of an impression behaves in this respect exactly like a sensible property of the impression. But there is no need at all of a sensible sign appointed expressly for this. Nor do we find in the one impression its uniqueness otherwise than in any other whatever, for it is always only the existence of the impression, that is, its immovable eternal position, that we need to find. The uniqueness of an impression behaves in this respect like a concept of uniqueness. We know very well that in the boundlessly

[p. 280]

composite nature of the impression its giving itself to us as unique must be grounded; here, however, we do not concern ourselves at all with this its own nature, for its bare existence, without regard to its constitution, already vouches for us its uniqueness. Since, then, we remain indifferent to the particularity of its nature, and preserve the same indifference toward all impressions, we do justice to the particularity of all possible impressions in a perfectly equal way. This attitude of our awareness, in which we remain indifferent to everything particular, precisely for the purpose of doing justice to everything particular in an equal way, I called the neutrality of our awareness. Since, then, we cognize the existence of our impressions in the light of neutral awareness, we cognize their eternal spatiotemporal order.

Herewith it is also set forth why we can adhere neither to the Leibnizian conception, that space and time are mere concepts, nor to the Kantian conception, according to which they are intuitions of form or forms of intuition. Both thinkers lack a distinct concept of the neutral attitude of our mind, in which we abstract from all particularities of individual things in order to do justice to them all in an equal way, in which, therefore, the particularity of the individual thing interests us very well, but only insofar as we find the same particularity in a l l individual things. If we hold fast to the concept of neutral awareness, then for us there is no longer any dispute as to whether space and time are concepts or intuitions: for concept and intuition are in this case neutralized. From the logical nature of neutral awareness it can then also easily be derived why space and time must be conceived as homogeneous, continuous, boundless manifolds consisting of points. Into these details, however, I cannot enter.

[p. 24]

At the same time, however, we also see how little founded is that doctrine of Kant’s, that it is we who carry space and time into the world of our sensations; and that some forms or other dwell in our mind which bring it about that the matter of sensation, devoid of space and time, stands before us cast in spatiotemporal form. That such theories find great response is easily comprehensible. It flatters our vanity that powers dwell in our mind which bring the unshaped world into a shape; it flatters us to hear that that boundless sensible manifold which we call the world of phenomena is the contrivance of our mind, and that the forms which dwell in our mind are the lawgivers of this contrivance. For it pleases us human beings when we can “make” something, and when we are told that we make the whole world of phenomena, we feel ourselves demigods. We do not consider that that objectivity by which our glimpse of eternity is distinguished, and on which alone our so-called likeness to God rests, forbids that in cognizing we should “make” something, should carry into the world of sensations something foreign to them and thereby falsify our world. It is the lust for falsification that misleads us into conceiving the world of phenomena as made by us. The vain desire to seem what we are not makes us take pleasure in falsification, and robs us by this very means of our true human dignity. This dignity, however, rests on our being conscious that by our cognizing glance we add nothing falsifying to the world of phenomena, but only find what is to be found in it, namely the eternal. That we find the eternal in the phenomena, on this our humanity rests, and that is our eternal patent of nobility. In behaving cognitively we do nothing other than that we let the eternal in the world of phenomena shine through us,

[p. 282]

and the less vain we are of this, the more clearly does the eternal shine through our mind. This capacity of making ourselves a vessel of the eternal each one of us possesses; it is not the special monopoly of individual men. One need only open one’s eye, and one sees the world in space and dwells in its temporal stream; thus the lifting of the eyelid suffices to find the eternal order.

31. The demonstration that we find space and time in the world of phenomena by virtue of the neutral cognizing glance is, however, only the first and easier step toward a theory of space and time. Much more significant and more difficult is the second step, which we have to take by asking ourselves in what relation space and time may stand to one another. For they are two orders which we find in the world of phenomena, and if we name the one, we involuntarily think also of the other, so that their belonging together is already indicated by the mere mention of them together. Space and time form a unitary double order, and the question arises of what kind the bond is that, despite their twofoldness, stamps them into one unitary order. It is just at this question that people are wont, in the theory of space and time, to pass by with a certain indifference, quite as one does not ask what significance it has that our judgments show a twofold articulation, although in a single judgment we wish to present merely one truth. Of a theory of space and time there can be no question at all so long as one does not raise the fundamental question, in what relation the two orders stand to one another?

The answer to this question I have already attempted to give in my work, “New Theory of Space and Time” (1901), and still earlier in a Hungarian treatise in the journal “Jelenkor.” I started out there from the

[p. 24]

thought that the concept of space cannot be formed without the aid of the concept of time. One conceives space as consisting of a three-dimensional manifold of points, and this concept cannot really be thought out unless one adds that all the points of that three-dimensional manifold are there simultaneously, that is, in every now. If one wished to take absolutely no account of the fact that all points of space are simultaneous, one would lose the concept of space. Thus the concept of space points to the concept of time, and without regard to this pointing it is unthinkable. If one takes the totality of the points of space as the subject of a sentence, then it can be predicated of them that they are now, or also that they are in every now. Being-now is a predicate of the totality of all points of space. Space and being-now are related to one another as subject and predicate in a judgment. In this the nature of that bond which connects space and time with one another finds expression. Only so far as we can understand the relation of subject and predicate in a sentence will the relation of space and time also be logically explicable; for the two relations are one and the same. We can say outright that in all judgments of fact a subject and a predicate must be present for the same reason for which the world of phenomena presents itself in a spatiotemporal double order. O u r s e n t e n c e s a r e b u i l t dually for the same reason for which the order of phenomena is a dual (spatiotemporal) o n e , for space and time stand to one another in the relation of subject and predicate.

This principle is already contained in my work mentioned above. We can, however, now go further still, because we recognized that the duality of the structure of judgment must already be prepared in sensing awareness. The

[p. 284]

sensation is a double experience and consists of a half-wave of impression and a half-wave of memory. The duality of the sentence sets forth in conceptual symbols the duality of our sensing; and if one may say that the twofold articulation of the sentence is rooted in the cleavage of our sensing, one may with the same right assert that t h e d u a l o r d e r o f space and time is also grounded in the double e x p e r i e n c e o f i m p r e s s i o n a n d m e m o r y. The impression signals to us the here, the immediately following memory the now, of a fact. We separate the here and the now for the same reason for which we need two concept-symbols (subject and predicate) in order to express merely one fact; and we connect the here with the now in one fact for the same reason for which we must connect the subject-concept with the predicate-concept in order to present a fact judgingly. The relation of space and time is therefore rooted in the mysterious connection between impression and memory, in the enigmatic relation of the two half-waves of our sensation. The deeper scientific and logical research penetrates here, the more significant discoveries may we expect concerning the connection of the whole spatiotemporal world of phenomena.

Against the starting point of these considerations the objection has been raised that we need not necessarily bring the concept of space into connection with the concept of time. One arrives at the concept of space, it is said, precisely by taking no account of time at all (abstracting from it), and in the same way the concept of time has nothing to do with that of space. They are i n d e p e n d e n t concepts, and one should leave them in their independence. Oddly enough, this objection came not merely from the rationalist but also from the sensualist

[p. 24]

side. I believe, however, that this objection rests on mere semblance. What danger lies in the “making independent” of concepts I have already pointed out in the earlier discussions. I permit myself to raise here the question what one would think of a concept so “independent” as to refuse altogether to be squeezed into the yoke of a judgment. Only imagine there were a concept that could be used in no act of judgment! It is perhaps not too bold if I say that no thinking man would still allow such a concept to count as a concept. One expects of a concept that one can use it in sentences. The concept exists only in order to assume a function in sentences, and it proves its worth only through this function. If it is unable to prove itself in this way, then it is no concept. This is the principle of the dependence of concepts.

We are indeed able to make concepts independent, but even such a making independent does not take place without judgments. If I become aware that I have grasped a concept, then the concept has been made independent, and this making independent occurs through an I-judgment of the form: I have this or that concept. Without such I-judgments the hypostasis of concepts cannot be carried out, from which it is to be seen that concepts are hypostatized only by means of contrary awareness. They then lose that character which stamped them as adjectives or verbs, and assume wholly the form of substantives. But in direct awareness there are as yet no concepts made independent, but every concept still has the specific character which belongs to it by virtue of its function in judgment. That making independent of concepts has indeed a great importance for our thinking, indeed it is of as fundamental importance as the contrary awareness from which it springs. On the other hand there lies,

[p. 286]

in the uniformization of concepts through their making independent, one of the greatest dangers that threaten logical thinking at all. For it seduces to the singular thought that concepts exist for themselves outside every judgment, and can have a complete sense in themselves outside every judgment. This conception manifests itself chiefly in the Aristotelian principle that concepts do not fall under the point of view of truth or falsity as judgments do. Concepts stand beyond all truth or falsity. That is unfortunately quite consistently thought, for if concepts are something altogether independent, then they do indeed lie beyond all truth or falsity, since they contain no assertion.

Thus one need only make a concept entirely independent in order to raise it above the world of the true and the false. This famous principle the word-philosophers or scholastics have accordingly applied abundantly, and thereby so damaged the scientific standing of logic and philosophy generally that it may count as a wonder if among truth-loving men philosophy is still mentioned at all. To counteract the scholastic abuse of the unwarranted making independent of concepts, we must lay weight upon declaring concepts dependent under all circumstances, thus even when they have been stuck into the uniform of the noun by contrary awareness. From this follows for us the principle that we must subject concepts just as much as any judgments whatever to the point of view of true and false. We arrive at concepts by behaving judgingly, and without judging behavior it is altogether impossible to form concepts. Judgment is the cradle of concepts. We have therefore to trace back every concept to its cradle, that is, to seek out the judgment or

[p. 24]

more correctly the judgments by which it was called to life and formed. If these judgments have been false, we have to do with an equivocal concept or with a semblance-concept; from true judgments, on the other hand, only true concepts can have sprung. The scholastic will retort to me what then is to be thought of such concepts as a “winged horse,” or a “centaur,” or a “twelve-headed dragon,” etc., which after all are concepts too. To this I have only to answer that this class of concepts does not belong in logic. We must not carry over into logic Olympus, the mythology of the various peoples, the world of fairy tales, the concepts of superstition, etc. Our cognizing glance forms a realm of concepts which relate to our world of feeling and fantasy; the realm of these concepts belongs for the good part in aesthetics, which may be designated as a logic of the life of feeling and fantasy of mankind. Likewise the realm of moral concepts does not belong in logic but in ethics, which can be conceived as a logic of practical action. Logic therefore falls from the outset into three sciences, whose foundation, however, is formed by the logic of cognizing, or of the experiences of eternity.

Every concept has, as was said, to justify itself in logic, and this justification occurs through the demonstration of all those judgments which are necessary to the fixing of the concept. Just for that reason one is wont to define concepts, which means nothing other than to collect the judgments by which the concept can be fixed. A concept made independent is nothing other than an indication of a bundle of judgments by which the concept is fixed. Just for that reason the making independent of concepts also occurs, because it is impossible to introduce into discourse all those bundles of judgments that would be necessary to define every word that occurs.

[p. 288]

If one applies this to the question of the relation of space and time, one also sees at once that space and time are hypostatized concepts, and that the misunderstandings which have arisen from the making independent of this pair of concepts can be countered only by investigating their relation to one another. If one now traces the concepts of space and time back to their cradle of judgment, one finds that they are related to one another as subject and predicate of a sentence, and further also that the here of a phenomenon is announced by the half-wave of impression, the now, on the other hand, by the half-wave of memory.

32. In order to penetrate more deeply into the relation of space and time we must return to the chief result of our investigations. We found that man is unable, in the course of a second, to become aware of impressions as often as he pleases, and that in this direction he is limited. What might be called the general human or Adamic limitedness, and what has been called his finitude, frailty, or otherwise: this can be brought to a definite concept formulable mathematically, namely to the speed (or, if one prefers, the slowness) of the cognizing glance by means of which we acknowledge impressions. There is indeed also a higher cognizing glance, which manifests itself in judging about judging, thus also in the connecting of judgments, and this too has its speed or slowness which cannot be exceeded; but into the investigation of this higher finding glance — which would lead to a doctrine of the cognizing genius — we could not enter. Of fundamental significance for the intelligence of the human race, however, is above all the speed of perceiving cognition, by means of

[p. 24]

which we are able to pass impression-judgments upon present phenomena.

For a being that has infinitely rapid awareness there are no phenomena at all. This is easy to understand if we consider that phenomena are there for beings that have sensations, that is, that, inhibited by impressions, are able to come to awareness only by fits and starts with greater or lesser speed. For infinitely rapid awareness there is simply no inhibition, no sensing, and also no judging in our sense. A judging takes place only where awareness was inhibited by the impression, and where the cognizing glance had to struggle through the sensation in time in order to be able to light up.

Phenomena are there only for beings of finitely swift awareness, as we human beings are; and if they are there for such a being, they must be there both in space and in time. This is a principle of eminent importance, and it is to be emphasized quite especially because most of the philosophical errors of modern times (reckoned, say, from Descartes on) are to be traced to the failure to recognize this truth. It is expedient to resolve it into two principles:

a) N o p h e n o m e n o n c a n t a k e p l a c e m e r e l y i n s p a c e . It is not known to me that this principle has been doubted anywhere; it is accepted as a self-evident matter. But since there is often nothing less reliable than what men call “self-evident,” it is necessary to give oneself a closer account of the truth of the principle before us. If a phenomenon is to take place merely in space, what is meant by this is that it has no temporal duration: that it therefore exists merely in the mathematical point of time. But in order that we human beings might perceive an extended phenomenon in a mathematical point of time, we should have to have infinitely rapid

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.               19

[p. 290]

awareness, for absolutely no time should be allowed to elapse between the being-here of that phenomenon and our awareness of it. But since we have only finitely swift awareness, there can be for us human beings no phenomena in the mathematical point of time, that is, no merely spatial phenomena. For a being, however, that possesses infinitely rapid awareness, there are no phenomena at all. Consequently there can be no merely spatial phenomena, which may also be expressed thus: there are no phenomena in the mathematical point of time.

One might, however, also interpret “merely spatial phenomena” to mean that they are phenomena which indeed take place in space, but never in the course of time, that is, exist in no empirical point of time. By this, however, it is also said that they could never be perceived in the course of time, that is, do not exist for judging beings.

b) N o p h e n o m e n o n c a n t a k e p l a c e m e r e l y i n t i m e : this principle is directed directly against the impressionist way of thinking, and it expresses the polemical main tendency of my whole work. The impressionist way of thinking distinguishes two classes of phenomena: spatiotemporal or physical, and temporal or psychological phenomena. But what is one to understand by merely temporal phenomena? I believe that two interpretations are possible here. Either one speaks of temporal phenomena that take place nowhere in the space of the world, or one thinks of temporal phenomena that are without extension, that is, take place in a mathematical point of space. A third interpretation there is not; and I shall first examine the second.

That we could perceive phenomena that take place in an extensionless point of space, hardly anyone will want to assert. One need only point to the fact

[p. 24]

that bodily particles of very small dimensions elude our perception; further, that the so-called molecules and atoms of the chemists are inaccessible to our perception. A phenomenon, however, that is to be altogether without extension would then have to remain concealed forever from every human power of perception. This popular proof will not satisfy sharper thinkers, because it shows only that mathematically punctual phenomena are imperceptible, but leaves open the possibility that such phenomena may nonetheless exist. But if they exist, the psychologist is readily at hand with the explanation that they are imperceptible only externally, but accessible to the miraculous “inner perception.” I shall therefore try another proof, which is to show directly the nonexistence of mathematically punctual phenomena.

Let us suppose there were a merely temporal phenomenon at the mathematical point of space a. In the spatial surroundings of this point a there must then in any case also be some extended or spatial phenomena which take place simultaneously with the phenomenon at the point a. Now if the phenomenon that takes place at the point a is to be perceived by itself, it must not appear within those extended phenomena that form its surroundings, because it would then be a component of a spatial phenomenon, and thus could represent only a vanishing member of a manifoldly qualified spatial phenomenon. But since the phenomena of the spatial surroundings force themselves upon our perception simultaneously with the phenomenon a, the separation of the phenomenon a can take place only by our becoming aware of it either earlier or later than of the phenomena of its nearest spatial surroundings. It suffices, however, to consider only the earlier or swifter awareness, because the proof is easily

19*

[p. 292]

also be transferred to the case of the later or slower awareness. We can therefore separate the point a from the simultaneous phenomena of its nearest surroundings only by bringing to it specially a swifter awareness than we are able to bring to spatial phenomena at all. The general human swiftness of awareness would have to be exceeded in order that we might be able to separate the phenomenon at the point a from the simultaneously occurring spatial phenomena of the surroundings. But it is also easy to see that with the mere acceleration of our awareness beyond every human measure little would yet be gained. For even for awareness thus accelerated the point a would still have a simultaneously occurring spatial surrounding of phenomena: though of a different kind than we human beings with unarmed senses are able to perceive. In order that the point a be lifted out of this new spatial surrounding too, perceiving awareness would have to undergo a still newer acceleration, and so on without limit. In short, only an infinitely rapid awareness could perceive a phenomenon at the mathematical point of space a. For such awareness, however, there are no phenomena at all; so we may state the principle: t h e r e are no phenomena in the mathematical point of space.

It remains now to refute that mode of conception which holds that phenomena could take place merely in time and stand simply outside every spatial order. Let us suppose, then, that there is a phenomenon A which exists merely in time and stands outside every spatial order. To decide whether it is possible, one need only consider how different persons might behave toward it: say a person P1, who is in Europe, a person P2, who is in America, a person P3, who is on the Moon,

[p. 24]

and a person P4, who is staying on Mars. All these persons stand to the phenomenon A, which we wish to suppose is taking place just now, in a perfectly equal relation; for the phenomenon A lies outside every spatial order, and can be neither nearer nor farther to the person P1 than to the other three persons. If it is present to the one, it would have to be present also to the remaining persons, for there is absolutely no reason why it should be present only to the one and not to the other person as well. Indeed, were it located in space, it would at once be intelligible why it might possibly be perceived by the inhabitant of Mars, but not also by the inhabitant of the Earth. A phenomenon, however, that stands outside the spatial order and exists only in the now, must stand in equal relation to all judging beings of the spatial universe, and must be present to them all in the now. It would be an omnipresent phenomenon. Now psychology wishes to speak of phenomena insofar as they are present merely to a single person: is it not comical, then, that it supposes itself to have to do with merely temporal phenomena, where merely temporal phenomena would have to be present to all persons, that is, could not exist at all.

We have thus arrived at the insight that all phenomena must belong both to the spatial and to the temporal order; that space and time therefore form a unitary double order of the world of phenomena. This is the core of my d o c t r i n e o f the unity of space and time.

33. But what is one to think of such phenomena as, like joy, sorrow, longing, anxiety, rapture, abhorrence, admiration, indignation, etc., arise in time and perish in time? Is one to ascribe extension to all of these? An area, a spatial

[p. 294]

volume? It cannot indeed be denied that there are “psychical phenomena” that are merely temporal, and to which one cannot ascribe spatial extension without making oneself ridiculous! I shall now try at least to indicate the way in which the truth in this conception is to be separated from the error with which it is mixed.

It is a matter of t h e v i t a l i s t i c p r o b l e m . One is wont not to distinguish mechanical from vital phenomena correctly and confounds them with one another, with the consequence that one then also fails to separate vital phenomena from experiences of eternity, so that a hopeless confusion runs through biology, psychology, and logic: in consequence of which biology is taken for psychology, and this in turn for logic.

Vital phenomena present themselves to the man who lives them merely in time; or, more correctly speaking, they present themselves to him with insufficient, mysterious localization; they may also dispense with all localization. On what does this rest? We can receive impressions not only from foreign bodies, but parts in the interior of our body are able to exert impressions on parts in the interior of the same. In short, there are inner impressions, whereby I take the word “inner” in no metaphorical sense at all. A n i n n e r i m p r e s s i o n arises when a part in the interior of our organism evokes an impression in another part of the interior of our organism. In such inner impressions the part from which the impression proceeds is distinguished from the part that receives it only most indistinctly or not at all, because giver and receiver are parts of the same organism. With this it is connected that from inner impressions f e e l i n g s arise which are localized indistinctly or not at all.

In order to form a picture of what is to be thought of inner impressions, a very simple

[p. 24]

procedure suffices: one lays, for example, the hand on the forehead. The surface of the forehead is thereby covered by the inner surface of the hand, and the two surfaces touching one another then give us, as it were, a picture of a surface that is situated in the interior of the organism. One now asks oneself, while pressing the hand against the surface of the forehead, what is really felt: the forehead or the hand? One will perhaps say that predominantly the forehead announces itself in feeling, intermittently the hand also: in any case, however, one will admit that what is felt and what feels are mixed in a bewildering way. How much more must such a mixing occur when what feels and what is felt have grown together organically, that is, when they are interwoven parts of an organism. It is not necessary to go into the various kinds of inner impressions in more detail and to offer considerations on their complications, for it suffices here fully if we see that what is felt and what feels, if both are inserted into the same life-process, are not able to step apart at all (or not properly). From this consideration there results for us a logical distinction between feeling and sensation; at the same time, however, the latter stands before us in a new light. We saw that sensation consists of two half-waves: of the half-wave of impression and of the half-wave of memory. Now we see that every sensing is nothing other than the reaction of a vital process to a mechanical event. The intervention of the mechanical event in the vital process furnishes the half-wave of impression, the reaction against this intervention is the half-wave of memory. But now the intervention in the vital process need not always come from outside, but can originate from the interior of the organism itself: and in this case sensing becomes feeling.

Sensation and feeling now illuminate one another

[p. 296]

reciprocally, and become intelligible only through each other. If a mechanical event intervenes in the vital process, sensation arises at first. If, however, the vital process intervenes in the vital process in such a way that it thereby replaces for itself the intervention of a mechanical event, then a feeling arises. Now it is also comprehensible that sensations can very easily be accompanied by feelings, indeed that they must more or less always have a feeling-tone. For in that a mechanical event intervenes in the vital process, it is unavoidable that the intervention evokes further reactions within the vital process which are no longer occasioned directly by the external impression. In short, the vital process then already intervenes in itself, and feelings arise.

However interesting and fruitful it would be to spin out this conception further, we must nonetheless not permit ourselves to do so. Here it is only a matter of seeing that vital events are of just as spatiotemporal a nature as mechanical processes. But if vital events are to give the cognizing glance news of themselves, they can only announce their taking place in time, and can give only very indistinct signals of their whereabouts. Quite otherwise is the case when vital events are to signal external mechanical interventions, because then impression and memory stand out from one another, so that the now can be referred to a distinct here. I n f e e l i n g , h o w e v e r, t h e v i t a l p r o c e s s m u s t g i v e n e w s o f i t s e l f, a n d t h e r e i t i s u n a b l e , b e c a u s e i t c o n f r o n t s i t s e l f, t o g i v e o f i t s e l f a n y d i s t i n c t s p a t i a l i n f o r m a t i o n . The vital process itself is spatiotemporal, but it gives the cognizing glance spatiotemporal signals only from the processes located outside the body, while of itself, that is, of the life-process within the body, it is able to furnish only predominantly temporal signs of information.

[p. 24]

Hence arises the semblance that vital events are of merely temporal nature, whereas, despite our great ignorance of them, they are localized within the body just as exactly as any mechanical events can possibly be localized outside the body.

Now, however, one confounds the vital process with the t a k i n g c o g n i z a n c e of it, and this is the actual cause of the impressionist error. We have seen that sensualism (or psychologism) is continually inclined to take sensing for a cognizing already; likewise it also takes feeling for a cognizing. It does not distinguish between our glimpse of eternity on the one hand, and the vital and mechanical events on the other. Now the fundamental distinction that underlies all our judgments is the separating of the eternal from the transitory and their relating to one another (principle of identity). Vital events are just as transitory as mechanical events, and what is transitory belongs to the spatiotemporal double order. However profound thoughts we may have or however exalted feelings we may harbor: all these thoughts and feelings are first of all vital events, and as such they are of spatiotemporal nature. But all these thoughts and feelings are thoughts and feelings only because an eternal sense lies in them; and it is this sense that confers on them the extra-spatial but also extra-temporal character. It is not this that makes vital phenomena into psychical phenomena, that they would be merely in time and not also in space; phenomena are psychical only in that the vital events (which are spatiotemporal) contain an eternal, thus extra-spatial and extra-temporal, sense.

Psychologism therefore fails to recognize 1. the nature of vital phenomena, in that it takes them for merely temporal,

[p. 298]

and thereby falsifies the biological natural sciences; but it fails also to recognize 2. the nature of psychical phenomena, in that it does not see that psychical phenomena are nothing other than vital phenomena insofar as an eternal sense dwells in them. And because it does not see this, it falsifies logic also.

34. That space and time, despite their twofoldness, constitute only one world-order, is for the logician beyond doubt. We have proved that there can be neither merely spatial nor merely temporal phenomena, and have thereby shown to be untenable that dualism which wishes to assign the physical and the psychical world of phenomena to two different orders, of which the one is to be spatiotemporal, the other merely temporal. But we had also to go more closely into the semblance that misleads psychologism into assuming a merely temporal world of phenomena, and there it was revealed that this semblance rests on the difference between sensation and feeling, and that this difference points to the opposition between mechanical and vital events. For our cognizing glance can receive news of any other events a l w a y s o n l y t h r o u g h v i t a l e v e n t s : it is therefore in a different situation toward mechanical events than toward the vital process with which it is connected. Herein lies the ground of the fact that the cognizing glance can be differently oriented about mechanical events of the world outside the body than about the life-process with which it is connected. If we receive news through vital events of the phenomena of the world outside the body, it is possible that these phenomena are localized to a high degree distinctly; but if vital events give us knowledge of other vital events, then it can easily happen that we are very badly oriented in spatial respect, or not oriented at all. The problem how it comes about

[p. 24]

that we are able to localize a certain class of phenomena only indistinctly or not at all is, however, by no means solved thereby. We see only which way we have to take in order really to grasp the problem; and we recognize that the question of the relation of space and time leads over into the question of the relation between mechanical and vital events. If one considers now how exceedingly difficult this latter problem is — it has, after all, the whole riddle of life for its content — one comprehends also why philosophers like to evade the question of the relation of space and time. There is a kind of philosophizing which indeed raises problems, but, having arrived at a point, drops them under all sorts of pretexts: in this comfortable position is found especially positivism, which, when it runs out of breath, resorts to the effective means of abusing logic or metaphysics. The consequence of this positivist procedure is that the logical connection of all scientific problems is lost, and that of a monistic (unified) conception of things there can no longer be any question. Against this dismemberment of the understanding we must lay great weight on the demonstration of the connection of the problems. Thus we have shown, for example, that the duality of the structure of the sentence is in closest connection with the dual order of space and time, that, further, both point to the duality of impression and memory, and still further to the duality of mechanical and vital phenomena.

But the problem of the relation between space and time is still in closest connection with other most difficult problems, and namely with the question of the difference between r e s t a n d m o t i o n . For if vital events seduce us into assuming phenomena that have merely temporal character, then it is

[p. 300]

peculiar to mechanical events, in turn, that they lead us astray into thinking of merely spatial phenomena. Chiefly it is the s t a t e o f r e s t of bodies that feigns to us that there is something that is merely in space. One asks, for example, about Mont Blanc not when it is, but merely where it is? Descartes accordingly did really speak of two substances: of an extended and of a thinking substance, and it is really this dualism that appears in recent philosophy in all manner of stylistic turns (so, for example, also in the Kantian Critique of Reason). Descartes set the extended over against the non-extended, and conceived the non-extended as a thinking thing. Now in Locke’s train came the impressionist thinkers, and these conceived thinking as a merely temporal occurrence, so that now the temporal was set over against the spatial and two classes of phenomena were established. Admittedly this comical dualism stammered from the very beginning and was always inclined to swallow half its own words, for that there can be no merely spatial phenomena lay far too much on the surface. Over against this I must, however, emphasize that phenomena at rest give us exactly the same right to speak of merely spatial phenomena as vital phenomena give us to speak of merely temporal phenomena. The semblance is as compelling in the one case as in the other; admittedly our logical awareness also compels us to recognize both kinds of semblance as such.

It is also just as difficult a task to explain why certain phenomena make on us the impression of rest as to explain why vital events seem to present nothing but an unrest that takes place merely in time. The problem of rest is not a hair easier to solve than the problem of vitality. One usually contents oneself with the statement that between rest and motion there is only a r e l a t i v e

[p. 24]

difference: but it is by no means clear what is meant by this relativity, since after all every difference is a relative one. Only by bringing an a into relation to a b does one distinguish the a from the b. One must then keep asking further in what the relativity is actually grounded.

So too with rest. If I look, for example, at a red color-patch at rest, then the half-wave of impression is followed, in imperceptible time, by the half-wave of memory; they form a unitary sensation-wave, which is followed in uninterrupted series by a 2nd, 3rd, 4th . . . sensation-wave. But now I am not at all able to hold these individual sensation-waves apart, but receive from their totality a sensation as if it were merely a matter of one wave. Suppose I receive 10 sensation-waves in one second; then these form just as much only a single sensation as if I had received 5 waves in half a second, or 20 waves in two seconds. Although an o c c u r r e n c e is evidently taking place here, and in two seconds twice as much happens as in one, all this occurrence is as good as lost for my cognition, for the renewal of the sensation-waves has announced itself to me by nothing. I am unable to perceive this renewal of the sensation-waves, and see before me nothing but that color-patch at rest, from which a renewed occurrence seems not to proceed at all. Were it possible for me to separate one sensation-wave from another, I should no longer speak of rest: the phenomenon would have a temporal pulse. Such a temporal pulse would be present, for example, if the red color-patch were to change its quality, for example to pass from red into a yellow, into a green and violet, etc. Of actual motion there would admittedly be no question even then, but neither could actual rest any longer be

[p. 302]

spoken of, because a change after all comes to light in the phenomenon; and we set rest with full right in opposition not only to motion but to change in general. Rest is nothing other than the semblance of eventlessness. If, for example, I look at the unchangingly red color-patch for 10 seconds, the semblance arises for me that just as much has happened as in a looking that lasts merely a second. The semblance arises as if a time interval had elapsed empty, and such an emptiness plays a role in every rest, whether the rest signifies merely the lack of a motion or of a change in general.

How, now, are we to name that incapacity by virtue of which we are unable to separate temporally a temporal series of sensation-waves, so that they form only one (so to speak, timeless) sensation? It is easy to see that this incapacity forms a counterpart to that other incapacity by virtue of which we are unable to localize feelings. As there is a lack of capacity for localization, there is also a lack of capacity for temporization. As feelings are localized very indefinitely, so sensations can be t e m p o r i z e d very indefinitely. Ten sensation-waves of a red color-patch give us just as much only o n e sensation of a color-patch at rest as do a hundred sensation-waves that follow one another. All the effort of the sensation-waves to make their temporal succession manifest is entirely lost; we remain blind toward this temporal succession. We do not temporize the successive waves. Only when after a while we proceed from the sight of the red color-patch to another sight do we also say to ourselves that a time must certainly have elapsed during which we had always only one sensation.

There is no doubt that in the sight of a

[p. 24]

resting color-patch: a r e p e t i t i o n of the impression necessarily takes place, because otherwise the color-patch would have to vanish suddenly from our eyes. But there is also no doubt that we notice absolutely nothing of a repetition; so that, if we were to yield to this semblance, we could boldly assert that this color-patch is not in time at all, but merely in space. For if it were in time, something would after all have to be going on with it, but we perceive nothing of such a process. Since, however, our thinking convinces us completely that on gazing longer at the color-patch we must receive a plurality of like sensation-waves: the conviction also becomes fixed in us that we suffer from a peculiar incapacity to separate the series of these temporally successive sensation-waves member by member, that is, to temporize them. This incapacity to temporize certain enduring, resting phenomena is exactly the counterpart of the incapacity to localize so-called psychical phenomena. Only by setting over against the non-localized psychical phenomenon a non-temporized physical phenomenon is the untenability of psychological dualism fully demonstrated. For there would now have to be, by the impressionist recipe, three worlds of phenomena: a purely temporal, a purely spatial and a spatiotemporal world of phenomena.

Curiously enough, the concept of temporization has not been developed at all in logic and psychology in parallel with the concept of localization, which seems to me to furnish a proof that we neglect the concept of time altogether and cling more to the spatial. How readily it strikes us that vital phenomena lack localization; how little heed, however, is paid to the fact that mechanical (physical) phenomena, on the contrary, lack temporization. But a painter will understand me

[p. 304]

at once, if I tell him: although a color-patch is not temporized, the painter must be able to endow it artificially with a temporization, by letting, for example, one layer of color shine through the other.

To come to the aid of the fantasy as well, I use the manner of speaking that sensation-waves which proceed from a resting color-patch c o v e r one another. This, however, is not meant as an explanation of the facts in question. The expression “cover” has only a metaphorical value, in that it reminds us that there are spatial relations between phenomena, in accordance with which one phenomenon stands in the light of another. If, for example, we see the front surface of an opaque body, there will always be a rear surface of it as well, which cannot be grasped by the same glance. If we take this analogy to our aid, our incapacity to separate the individual sensation-waves which proceed from a resting color-patch will stand quite vividly before our eyes, and we shall be able to use, without danger of misunderstanding, the expression that sensation-waves can cover one another. I n d e e d , w e s h a l l b e able to say of ever y resting phenomenon that the sensation-waves through which it is there c o v e r o n e a n o t h e r . Nothing else is meant by this than that, in the face of resting phenomena, we lack the capacity of temporizing.

It may be left undecided whether the expression “temporize” will satisfy linguistic or other requirements. I formed it ad normam of the expression “localize,” and believe I have made its sense clear. One temporizes an event by grasping the empirical point of time of its taking place; just as one localizes a phenomenon when one assigns it an empirical place. Since, then, in the face of a

[p. 24]

resting color-patch we are unable to establish the empirical point of time of the individual sensation-waves which proceed from it, because we do not become conscious of any occurrence at all, I say that on this occasion we are unable to temporize the succession of sensation-waves. It is evident, moreover, that this incapacity is only the reverse side of that capacity by virtue of which we grasp a quality as a uniformly existing quality. For let us suppose we had the capacity, in the face of a color-patch, to constate each sensation-wave that proceeds from it for itself; this could be brought about only by our perceiving, in the transition from one sensation-wave to another, a change, a new quality. It would then be impossible for us to persist at one quality; rather we should have to advance without cease to ever new changes of quality. But it would not suffice to do this only in the tenth and the hundredth part of a second, because there would still be a persistence of the quality; rather we should in the end have to be able to perceive ever new changes of quality in infinitely small particles of time. We should, in other words, have to have an infinitely rapid awareness, that is, our picture of the world would dissolve. In short, it is grounded in the finite speed of our awareness that, in the face of resting phenomena, we lack temporization.

Hereby a new light falls also on the doctrine of sentence structure. We now understand why in judgments of fact we must necessarily develop two kinds of predicate-concepts, adjectival and verbal concepts. For where temporization is lacking in the perception of things, there adjectival or quality-concepts appear in the statement; but if the perception is a temporized one, then the predicate must necessarily take on a verbal character.

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.            20

[p. 306]

35. Since we see that every uniform quality that we perceive anywhere contains within it an occurrence which escapes our perception, we must also be concerned to devise means for guarding methodically against the error which, from lack of the capacity of temporization, threatens us everywhere. For this purpose it is best to temporize cosmic space itself from the outset, that is, to think of it as engaged in continual self-renewal. For if space continually renews itself, the world of phenomena that is in it renews itself too, and then even the seemingly resting things receive that temporal pulse which they really possess, and which we are merely unable to notice.

If we return to our example of the resting color-patch, it stands before us, by virtue of our habitual conception of space, in a wholly false light of cognition. For the incessantly repeating sensation-waves which bring it about that the color-patch is there for us are wholly overlooked. It is as if our sensing were standing still, and as if after the first wave no more need follow. It is as if this wave had grown rigid in our mind, and this mind itself, and with it the world too, were altogether dead. But does the sensation-wave stand still? Has the life in us given way to death? As certainly as our sensation has not died away, and as certainly as it continually renews itself: so certainly are we also in error if we think of space as rigid and temporally pulseless. If, however, we assume space from the outset as renewing itself, then every spatial phenomenon too will have a share in this renewal, and the false semblance of rest will have vanished from the entire world of phenomena. Everything is then drawn into the temporal swing, for even the resting qualities receive the character of rhythmic repetition which they also really have.

[p. 24]

This mode of conception is nothing other than a generalization of the Copernican thought. For what do we do when we put the heliocentric standpoint in place of the geocentric? We do justice to astronomical facts, which require us to think of the earth as swinging about the sun. We temporize the phenomenon of the earth, which feigns rest to us, in the sense of the astronomical facts. But where is there anywhere in cosmic space a phenomenon that would not have to be temporized? Nowhere. Does time, then, run on somewhere by itself, without troubling itself about the spatial world; and does it perhaps pass through, leaving the spatial world behind it in timeless standstill? Our current conception of space and time really suggests the thought that standing cosmic space stays behind, while time rushes ahead. I mean by this only to characterize in a popular way the untenable dualism in the prevailing conception of space and time. According to this conception the spatial and the temporal world fall apart; for the one order is a standing one, the other, on the contrary, a flowing one. How is it possible to arrive at a consistent conception of nature, if one has continually to do with two orders which contradict each other? Whoever takes a deeper interest in physics and chemistry soon finds that these sciences, in all their hypotheses and theories, vacillate to and fro between the two poles of a t o m i s m and d y n a m i s m . Whence does this come? The answer to this question is given in our exposition. Atomism and dynamism are at each other’s throats throughout the whole history of the development of recent natural science, because natural science s e e m s to accept the current conception of the dualism between space and time. Natural scientists in whom the inclination toward the merely spatial conception predominates become atomists; subtler thinkers

20*

[p. 308]

however, who have a sense for the temporal pulse of all phenomena, incline toward a dynamism. A settlement of the dispute between these two great historical parties in the history of the mechanical natural sciences cannot come about, however, for the reason that the foundations of our whole conception of nature are subjected to no revision.

Fundamentally, the whole of modern natural science takes the standpoint of that conception of space which I set forth here, and which I called the dynamic theory of space. Nowhere does one close oneself to the truth that there can be no question of absolute rest in the realm of phenomena. Indeed, whoever has gone through the school of the physicists has also grown accustomed to think the material world, in all its smallest parts, as vibrating. But in doing so one apprehends space as standing, and falls thereby into the most contradictory hypotheses and theories about the nature of matter and of forces or kinds of energy.

I believe that a unified working-out of all concepts of natural science is possible only on the basis of a dynamic theory of space. If an occurrence is there everywhere in space, well then, let one really carry the occurrence into space; let one temporize it. Then one will not be in continual danger of bringing a standing and a flowing order of phenomena into contradiction with each other. Let one make of space and time not two world-orders, but one unified double order. Time is immanent in space, or also conversely, space is immanent in time. This reciprocal interpenetration of the two orders is expressed most simply by positing space as continually renewing itself.

But what rhythm shall one give to this self-renewal of space? First of all, that rhythm which

[p. 24]

it really has for the intuiting man. As often as we are able in one second to become aware of facts, so often does space stand before us anew: in other words, the speed of our awareness also fixes the rhythm which the self-renewal of space has for us. Since, however, the endeavor of all science is directed to grasping the world theoretically as it would present itself at a swifter rhythm of awareness: natural science will have to proceed to the assumption of an ever swifter self-renewal of space, in order to investigate the occurrence in ever smaller intervals of time. This it also really does, for it examines the occurrence even in the trillionth part of a second, as for example in optics; only it does not lay at the basis of its investigations that conception of space and time which follows with logical necessity from its own investigations.

36. It will seem strange to the reader for some time if he is to accustom himself to think space, which he always grasped as “standing space,” as one continually renewing itself: but the case here is exactly as with the habituation to the thought that the earth does not stand still, but rotates about the sun. It is really nothing more here, either, than a logical generalization of the Copernican thought. To be sure, with the mere phantasm of a self-renewal of space very little indeed is as yet gained for science; for one has so far only the means in hand of arranging for oneself a new and deepened conception of the world of phenomena, but now it is first a matter of really working out this new conception. One thing, however, one knows already quite certainly in advance, namely that the unified connection of all phenomena can be won only on the basis of a theory which grasps space and time as a unified double order, as

[p. 310]

is done here. By the tearing apart of this double order into a standing space and a flowing time one will only ever fall from contradictions into new contradictions, just as in astronomy one would have to entangle oneself in eternal contradictions if one wanted to assume the earth at rest and the whole starry heavens rotating about it.

The carrying of the dynamic concept of space into mathematics, physics and biology will have as its consequence the development of three new sciences, metageometry, metadynamics and metagenetics, which together make up metaphysics proper. One must, namely, distinguish general logic from special; for although both employ the same method (that of contrary awareness), and thus form only one science: yet in view of their immense extent one must separate the general theory of judgment, whose center is the principle of identity, from the special theory of judgment, whose center is the dynamic theory of space, and which one may call special logic or also metaphysics proper. Into this metaphysics proper, however, I cannot enter within the extremely narrow compass of this sketch, and I content myself here with working out the concept of dynamic space at least so far as can be done without the aid of the mathematical theory of functions of complex variables. The philosophical reader who is not versed in mathematics should at least feel that in the concept of a dynamic space he has a means of grasping the unified connection of all mechanical and vital phenomena far more vividly and free from contradiction than with the aid of static space and flowing time, where the world of phenomena must necessarily fall apart for him into two unconnected orders.

[p. 24]

The concept of dynamic space requires the development of a terminology of its own, which, however, is to avoid every superfluous learned verbiage and, if possible, to serve to enliven the geometrical fantasy of the reader. Since we represent space to ourselves as renewing itself in time, we shall assign to every empirical point of time a space corresponding to it, and accordingly speak of a “now-space,” further of a series of successive now-spaces. We shall also say that every now-space “covers” all the preceding ones, by which metaphor it is only indicated that in reality we never perceive a renewing space, but always refer phenomena to a standing space. Our conception of dynamic space, however, becomes living only when we start from a quite determinate point of the now-space and seek out the points corresponding to it in the series of the following now-spaces. To be sure, in reality the whole series of such corresponding points presents itself to us merely as a single standing point; but we must temporize this point, that is, think of it as renewing itself, rejuvenating itself, in time. One can also come to the aid of the fantasy by the following schematic figure:

The straight line R1 represents here a now-space, the straight lines R2, R3 . . . represent renewals of space in the course of time. Correspondingly the point P1 renews itself in the corresponding points P2, P3 . . . etc.; likewise the point Q1 renews itself in the corresponding points Q2, Q3 . . . etc. In short, one temporizes every point of space in the same way as one formerly temporized

[p. 312]

the whole space. And as we said earlier that every renewal of space “covers” all the earlier ones, so we shall also be allowed to use the expression that every renewal of a point covers all the earlier renewals of the same. In the above graphic-symbolic representation the renewals of a point lie along a straight line, which is nothing other than the symbolic time-line. We can therefore — without being misunderstood — also use the metaphorical expression that every point of space “moves on” along the time-line. By this “moving on,” however, only the self-renewal of every point of space in the course of time is meant. The whole cosmic space “moves on” in the time-line, that is, it renews itself in every moment of time. Through every point of space “a time-ray passes,” that is, every point of space goes through, together with all the other points, the self-renewal in the course of time.

By such metaphorical turns of speech one accustoms oneself to conceive time in a manner immanent in space. One no longer thinks of a stream of time which flows along separated from space, but one represents it to oneself as flowing in every point of space. Only with this mode of conception does it also become intelligible why everyone symbolizes the course of time by a straight and not by a curved line. For among all lines only the straight line has that distinguished property that it shortens itself to a single point when one holds it toward the eye so that it falls in its whole length in the direction of our looking. One may define the straight line as a line of which a single point can cover all the others. (This is the optical definition of the straight line: but this optical explanation one ought also to lay at the basis of the geometrical treatment.) Since now a point of space which renews itself in the course

[p. 24]

of time “covers” all its earlier renewals, it behaves exactly as if it were moving along a straight line which remains always covered to us by a single point. In short, the stream of time has an analogy with the straight line, namely this, that one of its points covers all the others; the difference between the time-line and the geometrical straight line is that in face of the latter we can take up a position from which it reveals its dimension, whereas such a placing (of our body) in face of time is impossible: its now-point inexorably covers for us all its other points. Only through memory-judgments are we able to make the course of events present to ourselves; only in judging awareness do we see at all that the now-point of time covers all preceding (as well as succeeding) points of time.

If one now wants an application of the dynamic concept of space as well, I will point to one at once. Only the dynamic theory of space makes intelligible G a l i l e o ’s principle that a body upon which no force acts any longer must be in a uniform motion along a straight line — in the so-called motion of inertia. According to our conception every point of space has a “motion of inertia,” that is, it renews itself incessantly along the time-line with the uniform velocity of the stream of time itself. Galileo has really done nothing else by setting up the law of inertia than temporize cosmic space, that is, carry the pulse of time into space. To be sure, he did this not in the manner of the logician or metaphysician, but with the physicist’s eye of genius. It is not space and time that interest him, but his concrete research turns to the motion of bodies. Thus he temporizes not so much

[p. 314]

space itself as the whole corporeal world of phenomena in space, and expresses this by thinking of every body, even when no action of force is any longer exerted on it, as engaged in a uniform eternal motion. This principle of inertia, which forms the foundation of the whole Galilean dynamics, is, however, fundamentally hard or even impossible to understand if one does not represent space to oneself as flowing (renewing itself). For only in flowing space does rest too become a kind of uniform motion along the time-line. The schematic figure which we used just before can also be employed to make the Galilean principle of inertia intuitive:

Stream of time The resting point P1 presents itself in the next now-space as P2 and in the following one as P3, etc.; it describes the time-line, that is, it is in an ideal uniform motion, each stage of which covers all the previous ones, so that the point is for this very reason called resting. If, however, the point has received by a slight impulse a velocity in a certain direction, it no longer is projected on the corresponding points of the spaces R1, R2, R3 . . . , but traverses the spaces somewhat as the following figure symbolizes:

Stream of time

[p. 24]
