But what we are really able to perceive of this motion is not the diagonal P1 P2 P3, but merely its respective projections in the now-space, that is, p2 P2, then p3 P3, etc. Only this conception shows us the inner kinship of rest with a uniform motion of inertia: for the whole difference between the two consists in this, that the point in the first case continually covers itself, because it moves merely along the ideal dimension of time, while in the second case it does not cover itself, because its motion takes place along a real dimension of space. The mathematician will readily bring these relations also into formulas; but even the nonmathematician comprehends that the whole principle of inertia has no other meaning than the assumption of a self-renewing or flowing space.

But not only the principle of inertia, but all the principles of analytical mechanics gain a deepening and unification if one refers the moving bodies not to standing space but to dynamic space. Indeed, the treatment of the doctrines of analytical mechanics on the basis of dynamic space must not only show the principles hitherto won in a new and unified light, but may prove to be a lever to new discoveries in the domain of analytical mechanics. The theory of functions of complex variables will find an application to dynamics hitherto unsuspected, and thereby itself advance to a newer stage of development. In my conviction logic or metaphysics has a right to exist only if it exercises a fertilizing influence on all the sciences, and thus takes an active part in every progress of the human race.

[p. 316]

25. Concluding Reflections.

37. If we ask what relation obtains between space and time, or what connection prevails between spatial and temporal order, so that the two make up only one order: this question really goes to the connection of all phenomena which are ranged in that double order, that is, what we are really concerned with is the establishment of a universal law which governs the totality of the world of phenomena, endless in space and time. The law of identity is, to be sure, such a universal law, for it asserts that every phenomenon is the phenomenon of an eternal; yet this law cannot by any means satisfy us, for its whole content is nothing other than the cognition that an eternal world-order really exists. Without this cognition nothing, of course, can be accomplished, for whether we make it distinct to ourselves or not, whether we assent to it, once it has been made distinct, or not: it nevertheless lurks, even against our will or without being distinctly grasped, in all our judgments.

But with the constating of an eternal world-order only now is the task set of characterizing this world-order. To be sure, the principle of identity already contains the guidance for this, for it says that we, in order to be able to fit even o n e fact into the eternal order, must develop t w o concepts, which rest on a double experience (impression and memory) and point to a double order (space and time) of phenomena. If it were therefore possible for us to characterize the connection between spatial and temporal order of the world of phenomena, then we should also have found a general law of the world to which every phenomenon without exception is subject. We should then have a principle which forms a necessary complement of the principle

[p. 25]

of identity, which by itself can satisfy no one, because it contains within it the demand to investigate the nature of that double order which presents itself in space and time. As in general logic we recognized the principle of identity as the universal law of our judging, so in special logic we must look for a universal connection between spatial and temporal order, because otherwise we should lack the link between logic (or metaphysics) and the special sciences. For if we already know that the eternal appears to us in a double order, we must also go on to ask how space and time are related to each other.

A first step in the direction of this question we have already made, namely by thinking of space as renewing itself in time (or flowing in time). But for the further development of this dynamic conception it is expedient to introduce also a reverse manner of expression from the one we have so far used. We can, without having to fear misunderstandings, also say that it is time that continually unfolds itself in a new now-space; whereas just before we always used only the manner of expression that it is space that repeats itself in time. We must, in order not to fall into one-sidedness, give equal consideration to both turns of the dynamic conception: if we thought, then, that time is immanent in space, we must also accustom ourselves to the reversal of this thought, namely, that space is an emanation of time. We have, after all, assigned a now-space to every point of time, so we may also say that this now-space is an unfolding (emanation) of the point of time. A continuous series of points of time will unfold itself in a continuous series of now-spaces: it is the one dimension of time that continually unfolds itself into the three dimensions of

[p. 318]

space. The one ideal dimension of time unfolds itself in the three real dimensions of space, and it is this conception alone that suggests to us the intimate connection of space and time.

Nothing can now appear more enigmatic to us than that the unfolding of time requires precisely three dimensions. Modern mathematics has in fact been astonished at this to the highest degree, and on the one hand attempts have been made (as for example by B e r n a r d B o l z a n o ) to furnish proofs that space must necessarily have three dimensions, but on the other hand one has also advanced to mental constructions of spaces of four or more dimensions. The speculations on non-Euclidean geometry, as B o l y a i furnishes them, R i e m a n n ’s theory of manifolds and kindred achievements, belong from the mathematical point of view certainly to the most interesting that the human mind has devised, but they may be regarded only as mathematical speculations. They easily lead to misunderstandings, as I shall show presently.

If one regards cosmic space as consisting of nothing but points, or, to speak with the mathematicians: if one regards the point as the element of space, then space is three-dimensional; there is in it a threefold infinite manifold of points. But this very same space becomes, in the mathematician’s sense, a four-dimensional space if one takes not the point but the straight line as its element. For there is in space a fourfold infinite manifold of straight lines, of which even the non-geometer can easily convince himself. In order to find all straight lines which are possible at all in space, let one assume any plane in space, because this facilitates orientation. Every straight line of space must necessarily pierce this plane in a point; at most the

[p. 25]

lines parallel to the plane may present a difficulty for a moment, but these too must be conceived as if they cut the plane (in infinitely distant points). One finds all the straight lines of space, then, by starting from any plane, and thinking of all possible straight lines as drawn from each of its points. It remains, then, still to consider more closely all those straight lines which radiate from o n e point; they form a bundle of rays, and their number is as great as there are points in a plane, because from one point a straight line can be drawn to every point of a plane, and herewith all the straight lines that can proceed from a point are exhausted. Summing up what has been said, there are in space as many distinct bundles of rays as there are points in a plane, and in each bundle again as many rays are contained as there exist points in a plane. Since now the points in a plane form a twofold infinite manifold, it is clear that the straight lines of space constitute a fourfold infinite manifold. For this very reason the mathematician may say of our space that it is four-dimensional with respect to the straight line. If we were to regard a surface of the second degree, such as an ellipsoid, as the element of space, our space would be nine-dimensional, because there is in it a ninefold infinite manifold of surfaces of the second degree. In short, the mathematician relativizes the concept of dimension, so that it depends on the element which is taken as the starting point what number of dimensions pertains to any manifold. But if one takes the point as the element of space, then it is three-dimensional, and then a four-dimensional manifold of points, that is, a four-dimensional space, is unimaginable.

But may the concept of dimension be relativized? Certainly: this depends on the pleasure of the thinker.

[p. 320]

Only one must remain mindful of the path which led us to the formation of the concept of dimension. Originally we understand by a dimension an infinite continuous series of points, of which one can cover all the others (as in the straight line of space), or must cover them (as in time). Further we understand by a two-dimensional geometrical structure one which can be covered by the one-dimensional structure (that is, by the straight line). The two-dimensional structure is therefore the plane, for one can place oneself in front of it so that an edge covers the whole plane. Space, however, we must regard as three-dimensional, because it can be covered by a two-dimensional structure, namely the plane.

But once the concept of dimension has been fixed in such a way, abstract mathematics can soar to generalizations of it, and decide, for example, to conceive any continuous manifold of elements as o n e dimension and other manifolds which are composed of the arbitrarily assumed fundamental manifold as multidimensional. This is the good right of mathematics, and if it pleases it to soar to manifolds even of infinitely many dimensions, one could only pay such boldness the tribute of admiration. No less is the mathematical theory of manifolds free, in the interest of rendering sensible and facilitating its abstract speculations, to use the most fundamental manifold, namely space, as a kind of model: that is, to conceive abstract manifolds of the 4th, 5th, . . . nth dimension ad normam of the threefold manifold of space, and to transfer the technical terms which the doctrine of space employs to abstract higher manifolds. Yet it must remain conscious that these transferences, as

[p. 25]

for example the transference of the concept of surface curvature to space or to “multidimensional spaces” — are thoroughly m e t a p h o r i c a l in nature, and serve only to render higher mathematical speculation sensible and to facilitate it.

We must not, accordingly, be misled by the metaphorization of the fundamental geometrical concepts (in the interest of an abstract theory of manifolds) into admitting the possibility of spaces of four or more dimensions. This would be an error similar to the one we should commit if, conceiving the whole of human society ad normam of a single individual, we were seriously to speak of a general lung or a general liver of mankind, indeed wanted to find these general organs of mankind really somewhere in space. As little as this would make sense, so little does it make sense to speak of a “space” which would really be a space, that is, the order of the world of phenomena, and could have more than three dimensions.

38. We must not let the concept of dimension be confused and logically ruined by the metaphorizations of higher speculation. The concept of dimension is drawn from the spatiotemporal intuition of phenomena and irrevocably fixed by our determined or sensing awareness. It is determined on the one hand by the concept of the stream of time, on the other by the concept of the straight line. And what I should like to stress particularly here: it is a double concept, wholly corresponding to the spatiotemporal double order of the world of phenomena. There is, as I explained, neither a merely spatial nor a merely temporal world of phenomena, that is, there could be no spatial order without a temporal one and, conversely, no temporal order without a spatial one. Correspondingly there is also no real dimension if there is no ideal one; that is, dimensions of space and dimensions of time condition one another reciprocally. This is

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.               21

[p. 322]

what is meant when one makes use of the metaphor that the one dimension of time unfolds itself in the three dimensions of space, or that the three dimensions of space combine into the one dimension of time. I keep returning to the unity in the spatiotemporal double order, for it is a matter, after all, of working out the dynamic concept of space on all sides so that it can form the foundation of all research into nature, and lead to the cognition of the universal law of nature. Here now I want to illuminate the dynamic concept of space from the standpoint of the concept of dimension.

To this end let us first imagine that space has merely one dimension, or in other words that a judging being were wholly dependent on perception in one dimension. What would be the consequence? This one dimension would have to shorten itself, to the cognizing glance of that being, to a mathematical point. That means, however, that the one dimension of space would be given to it as a dimension of time, for it is precisely the dimension of time that is always given only through the now-point. If there were, then, only a single real (spatial) dimension, this would have to stand there as an ideal (temporal) dimension; space and time would not diverge, there would be no double order of the world of phenomena. The world in merely one dimension would always present itself as a mathematical point, which one could call a spatial as well as a temporal point, because space and time are just not separated. Since, however, I have already explained earlier that there can be no phenomenon in the mathematical point, or, what amounts to the same, that only the infinitely rapid awareness cognizes the world in the mathematical point (that is, outside space and outside time), it is clear that a merely one-dimensional world of phenomena cannot exist, and so too no beings

[p. 25]

can exist who would be in a position to grasp such a world of phenomena.

This consideration is of moment because it strongly suggests to us the most intimate unity of space and time. A dimension of space which was the only one would properly have to be the dimension of time. It would be the dimension of time which does not unfold into space, and which therefore, as a dimension of time too, would remain shortened to a point. It would be the world-order gathered into a point outside space and time. It would be the perfected unity of the world. This, however, is what we are never able to attain, and what we must set up as the eternally unattainable ideal of cognition, so that it may occur to no one to want really to attain this ideal. Only for an infinitely rapid awareness is the difference between space and time abolished, and the world-order gathered together into an extramundane point. Our thinking becomes extravagant when we would construct the world out of that extramundane point of unity, as though we could take up that truly Archimedean, that is, divine, standpoint. To us human beings, of finitely rapid awareness, it is given only to know that the infinitely rapid awareness is the foundation of our world: in the sense of the principle of identity of logic. For the rest, in consequence of our finitely rapid awareness, the unattainable perfected unity of the world must unfold itself for us into a spatio-temporal double order. If, then, we strive for a doctrine of the unity of space and time, it is not done in order to abolish the difference between them, and to construct the world as though from an extramundane standpoint. It is precisely this purpose that these considerations of dimension are meant to serve: to show what an extravagant doctrine of unity, a conceited, sophistical monism, intends, so that we may be on our guard against such intentions.

But how does it stand now with a world of phenomena

21*

[p. 324]

that would occupy only two dimensions of space, or with a judging being who could confine his perception to a mathematical plane? The answer to this question is, at bottom, already contained in the preceding consideration. A being that confines its cognizing glance to a mathematical plane grasps this plane in the image of a straight line, somewhat as we get to see only the edge of a knife-blade when we hold the blade before our eye in the horizontal plane of vision. To the glance banished into the plane, therefore, only o n e spatial dimension is ever present, while the other dimension becomes for it the dimension of time, because it always shortens to a point. The peculiar consequence of these relations is that the dimensions of space and of time become interchangeable. What at one time is a dimension of space to the cognizing glance becomes for it at another time a dimension of time, and what is now a dimension of time to it becomes at another time a dimension of space. The following figure may serve to illustrate this state of affairs:

The two straight lines crossing at right angles may represent the two dimensions of a plane. If the hypothetical being of which we are speaking moves in the dimension (Z Z), this dimension shortens for it to a point, that is, it is for it the ideal dimension, while the straight line (R R) represents the real dimension presenting itself to it.

[p. 25]

But if the being in question moves in the dimension (R R), this becomes for it the ideal dimension and (Z Z) the real one. It now emerges clearly that this case is properly only the twofold repetition of the preceding case, in which we thought of a hypothetical being as confining itself to a single dimension. For here too it is a question of an infinitely rapid awareness, as in the preceding case. To the being in question, namely, the dimension (Z Z) shortens to a point; it grasps the dimension (R R) in the mathematical point of time, which only an infinitely rapid awareness is capable of doing. (Such an awareness would, of course, grasp not only the dimension (R R) but the universe in a mathematical point of time.) It is the purest superfluity to transpose such a being into the dimension (R R) as well, so that it may now, conversely, grasp (Z Z) too. In general it must be maintained that a being which is able to grasp anything in a mathematical dimension must possess an infinitely rapid awareness, and that for such a being there is no world of phenomena. Hence, at bottom, it makes no sense at all to speak of a world of phenomena in merely one or merely two spatial dimensions. If I have nevertheless gone into it, it was only in order to show that space and time cannot confront each other so long as there is talk of a merely one-dimensional or merely two-dimensional world, because the grasping of a merely one-dimensional or merely two-dimensional world requires an infinitely rapid awareness, for which there are then no dimensions at all any more.

It is therefore not at all the case, as one might be inclined to believe at first glance, that the restriction to one and two mathematical dimensions would be possible only to beings lower than we are. We must bear in mind that we are unable to grasp the world, whether in the mathematical point or in

[p. 326]

the line and plane, because this would involve an infinite acuteness of perception, that is, an infinitely rapid awareness, and because in extensionless points, and consequently in mathematical lines and planes, there can be no phenomena. We human beings have only c o n c e p t s of mathematical points, lines and surfaces, because we relate phenomena, which present themselves to us in empirical places, to the eternal, and want to assign to every smallest particle of a phenomenon a perfectly exact position in the eternal order. But are we able to specify this perfectly exact position of any phenomenon whatever? By no means! For in order to be able to specify the absolutely exact position of a phenomenon in the eternal order of things, we should already have to know this eternal order thoroughly, that is, we should have to have absolute cognition, and to stand not within the world-order but above it, where, however, there is no longer any spatio-temporal captivity. Our understanding demands for the world of phenomena, in all its smallest parts, a perfectly determinate position (in space and time), and therefore forms for itself the concepts of mathematical dimensions, but nowhere is our perception or our measurement able to penetrate into mathematical dimensions. There would also be, as has been said, no world of phenomena any more for such an infinitely sharp glance; that is, such a glance would not be caught in a world of phenomena, but would stand outside it as the author of the world.

If our world is not one-dimensional, not two-dimensional, but precisely three-dimensional, this expresses merely the Adamic weakness of our awareness. For if we were able to grasp the world in one or two mathematical dimensions, the world would be altogether dimensionless for us, space and time would not part asunder, and our being as well as our cognition

[p. 25]

would be an absolute one. It is only in this that the finite rapidity of our awareness finds expression, that we behold the unified world in a double order of space and time, and the opposition of two such orders is possible only within three dimensions. Only sensualist short-sightedness misleads us into thinking of the world in the fourth dimension as a higher one, in which higher beings too are at home. Is the slavery of the three dimensions not enough? Does one want a still higher slavery?

Three dimensions are necessary in order that a judging being may be caught in a world of phenomena; one or two dimensions do not suffice for this, because they do not let space and time confront each other, and at bottom are only abstractions from our three-dimensional world; so that they would vanish at once if the third dimension vanished, and nothing would remain but the dimensionless absolute being, complete in itself, for which infinite space and infinite time are exactly as much as an extensionless and durationless point.

Only a three-dimensional space permits an ideal dimension to confront its real dimensions, and so a spatio-temporal double order to be present. We have seen that in the plane an opposition of space and time is not possible, because the one dimension, just as well as the other, can assume both the role of the real and that of the ideal dimension, which amounts to saying that there is no opposition between the simultaneous and the non-simultaneous (or between space and time). Only when one can confront the two (simultaneous) dimensions of a plane from a third dimension has the opposition of space and time also become possible. In order to be able to throw proper light on this, we must go into the nature of our sensations more closely than has hitherto been possible.

[p. 328]

39. The doctrine of the different modes of sensation of our organism suffers, despite the great advances of biological optics and acoustics and despite all the efforts of psychological research, from an almost impenetrable confusion, which has been occasioned chiefly by Locke and his school. When it is a question of the modes of sensation that come into consideration in the first rank for cognition, I believe that such modes of sensation as smell, taste, and the senses of warmth and cold, which stand in the most intimate connection with nutrition and the process of assimilation, and show more the character of stimulations of the organism than that of sensations proper, may be excluded from the inquiry. It suffices here to take into consideration the three “higher senses,” touching, seeing and hearing, and to ask ourselves what significance it has that precisely these modes of sensation (and precisely a triad of them) are destined to shape our picture of the world?

As for touching in the first place, this mode of sensation functions in such extraordinarily manifold variations that it is exceedingly difficult to furnish a unified characterization of it. Fortunately, however, the data of the sense of touch have a fundamental significance for a very exact science, namely mechanics, because we should be absolutely unable to form the concepts of the mechanical action of bodies upon one another if we did not form them by analogy with their mechanical action on our body. One can say in a certain sense that mechanics is properly nothing but an exact doctrine of the sensations of touch transferred to the external world. The concepts of impact and counter-impact, action and reaction, without which the development of a mechanics is impossible, are evidently borrowed from the world of our sensations of touch.

[p. 25]

Precisely as a G a l i l e o or a N e w t o n apprehended the impressions of touch, when, guided by their genius, they translated their conception of them into the mechanical and the mathematical: just so, and wholly in their sense, must we too hold fast to the type of the sensations of touch. Accordingly we shall declare every touching to be a double sensing, which under all circumstances consists of a direct sensation and a counter-sensation. I believe one need only state this principle in order for it to be found evident at once. By touching we find not only the foreign body but also our own body, and this double finding is what constitutes the character of touching. One can make the two components of the sensation of touch intuitive by imagining that we reach for a body, and conversely someone else reaches for our body, or also that we strike against something and conversely something strikes against us. Both kinds of function of touch contain a double sensation, but in the one the direct sensing of the foreign body predominates, in the other, on the contrary, the counter-sensation of our own body. Even when a body rests on the palm of our hand, we must think of our sensing as resolved into a direct and a contrary sensation, as if rhythmically now the body were pressing against our palm, now our palm against the body, and this so rapidly that sensation and contrary sensation flow into one another. This is also the actual state of affairs, and this conception too is what was decisive in the development of the mechanical theories.

Curiously, the elementary principle that touching is a double sensing and that this constitutes its essence has not been stated in psychology, which I trace chiefly to the influence of Locke. Recent psychology has, to be sure, strongly emphasized the significance of the so-called

[p. 330]

“sensations of motion,” which seems to be an approach to the principle stated here. Nevertheless it has not been emphasized — so far as I am aware — that in the function of touch a direct and an inverse sensing are contained. This seems to me very regrettable, because the doctrine of sensation at once gains a certain transparency if we set forth the fundamental mode of sensation, namely touching, as a double sensing, and in this way distinguish it from seeing and hearing.

By means of the direct sensing in touching we perceive two dimensions of space, by means of the inverse sensing, on the other hand, we perceive a resistance and hence the third dimension of space. If, for example, we lay some thin little plate on our palm, the sensation of surface predominates, while the inverse sensing is, so to speak, only dawning. The inverse sensation emerges distinctly, however, when we lay a greater weight on the palm, and the pressure of the weight as well as the counter-pressure of the hand come noticeably into play. That in the direct sensation we perceive only two dimensions of space, moreover, is attested by the seeing of colors. The seeing of patches of color is a merely direct sensing, a counter-sensing being contained in it only in a quite imperceptible manner. Whereas in touching we find not only the foreign body but also our own body, seeing is characterized by the fact that through it we are indeed able to find other bodies, but not our own eye or our own retina. Only when, for example, the light acting upon it is too intense, do counter-sensations appear noticeably in the eye in the form of unpleasant feelings; in normal seeing, however, only the foreign body is so exclusively present, and every possible counter-sensing recedes so far, that one may fairly say that only a direct

[p. 25]

sensing is in play.*) This is precisely what has from of old given seeing its most mysterious character. We involuntarily expect — because we hold touching fast as the type of sensing in general — that a sensation will be paired with a counter-sensation; but lo, in perceiving through the eye we have (at least approximately) only sensations, without any paired counter-sensations being present as well. It really looks as though we saw without any contribution of our own, and as though the images which we see were images even unseen. Natural awareness readily falls into this error, for it would notice its own activity of seeing only if this were bound up with counter-sensations as lively as, for example, the lifting of weights.

At all events we now see clearly that the difference between touching and seeing is to be sought in the counter-sensing. For touching is a double sensing, composed of direct and inverse sensation, while seeing represents, approximately, a merely direct sensing, to which circumstance it owes its wonderful objectivity. Because seeing, then, almost lacks the inverse sensation, the world presents itself to it (roughly speaking) only in two dimensions. Only the circumstance that we see with two eyes, further that we move the eyes, and that during seeing we can move the head and in general make changes of place, and finally that we connect the function of sight in the closest way with the function of touch, brings it about that our visual images too acquire a third dimension.

These simple expositions already show that at the base of our

*) I believe that the doctrine of direct and contrary sensing might cast a new light on the theory of “after-images,” as well as on the theory of color in general.

[p. 332]

different modes of sensation lies a quite definite plan of organization, without which their existence remains absolutely unintelligible. Psychological research of our day suffers from a great confusion for this very reason, that it disregards the teleological point of view, or does not sufficiently emphasize it, precisely where without its guiding thread one has to grope about in an impenetrable darkness. Our whole sensibility is of necessity so constituted that we must arrive at precisely those conceptions of a spatio-temporal world-order at which we do in fact arrive. If we find in our judging awareness that space has three dimensions, we may conclude with apodictic certainty that our sensing awareness, by virtue of its whole constitution, unavoidably determines that we must perceive the phenomena in three dimensions. Guided by this thought, it appears at first glance that the sensation of touch is resolvable into a sensation of surface and a feeling of resistance, or more correctly into a direct and an inverse sensing: wholly in the sense of the exact mechanical conceptions of action and reaction. It results for us without any constraint that the sensation of touch, in virtue of its double nature, represents the proper full sensing, and accordingly is able to shape the world of phenomena in all its three dimensions. Without any constraint we recognize further that seeing, according to its main constitution, insofar as it is a sensing of patches of color, contains within itself only the one component of the full sensing, namely the merely planar direct sensation, and that it in general lacks the inverse sensation, that is, the self-sensation of the retina. Left to itself, however, seeing could not be a function of sense at all, for a mathematically planar world of phenomena does not exist at all and cannot

[p. 25]

exist. Only by working in conjunction with the sense of touch is seeing able to develop into a mode of sensation of its own, and for this reason too it is attuned, by its whole constitution, to cooperation with the sense of touch.

As for hearing, it is a mode of sensation that forms the counterpart of seeing, in that it seeks to furnish as little knowledge as possible of the external world, and brings to expression the predominance of the one-dimensional inverse sensing over the two-dimensional direct sensing. Like seeing, hearing too has its quite peculiar mystical character, for whereas the former offers a direct sensing which takes place approximately without counter-sensations, the latter on the contrary supplies only inverse sensations and lets the direct sensing go under almost entirely. Since we may regard touching as the typical mode of sensation, and in it counter-sensations cannot occur without a lively accompaniment of direct sensations, nothing strikes us as more wonderful than the sensing of tones, since in them counter-sensations are so given that the corresponding direct sensations become more or less imperceptible. Through sound we have only a faint indication of a spatial direction or of a bodily constitution, and if we are able to recognize the constitution of many bodies by their peculiar sound, this rests on the cooperation of other modes of sensation. By its own constitution, however, hearing is a pronounced inverse sensing, whose mystical charm rests on our sensing without being troubled by objects through which the sensation is occasioned. A tone thus acts the more mysteriously, the less it betrays an origin from anywhere, that is, the more it can be sensed, in its whole content, inversely. Through the receding of the direct sensations we have in music the peculiar illusion

[p. 334]

as though we ourselves were resounding, and as though its rhythms were nothing but the rhythms of our own pulse of life.

These few indications may suffice to bring out the inverse character of the sensations of tone. I believe that in the above exposition I have characterized in broad outline the plan that underlies the structure of our three higher senses. The full sensing of touch is supported by the direct sensing of seeing and the inverse sensing of hearing. All together are so constituted that our world of phenomena must necessarily be a three-dimensional one. But touching by itself already suffices for the shaping of a three-dimensional picture of the world. Seeing and hearing are properly not wholly independent modes of sensation, for they suppress the one half of the full sensation. To be sure, there can never be any question in seeing of a complete elimination of the inverse, nor conversely in hearing of a complete elimination of the direct sensing. Both can subsist only because the organization of the eye and of the ear is so designed as to make possible, to a certain degree, the inverse and the direct sensing respectively. In seeing the inverse sensing is, so to speak, only atrophied, but not wholly suppressed, and conversely in hearing not only the inverse sensation plays a part, but a field of activity is reserved also for the direct sensing.

In this conception of our three higher modes of sensation the positive refutation of the Kantian theory of space is already included. In Kant's sense it is not at all prescribed in our modes of sensation that we must apprehend the world of phenomena in three dimensions of space: indeed, if we are to give credence to his theory, the whole realm of our sensations is entirely innocent of its being squeezed into the three dimensions of space. Our mind brings a priori the form

[p. 25]

of space to meet the impressions, and the innocent sensations must put up with being thrust into the straitjacket of the three dimensions. Does that not mean standing the world-order on its head? Sensations, considered in themselves, would have to be unspatial and untemporal, that is, not sensible! We human beings would be blessed with having non-sensible, extraspatial and extratemporal sensations, if that malicious form of space did not dwell in our mind, which catches our heavenly, spaceless and timeless sensations in the ignominious earthly dungeon of the three dimensions and debases them to common sensible sensations! What is one to think, then, of that metaphysical specter of a “form of space”? Does it not make the impression of a supersensible bailiff, appointed by some world-police administration to thrust the refractory sensations, which romp about spaceless and timeless, into the dull dungeon of the three dimensions?

40. From our considerations on the problem of dimensions it follows that there could be no world of phenomena in fewer than three dimensions, because only the infinitely rapid awareness could be at home in mathematical points, lines and surfaces: and this knows no dimensions at all. Only a finitely rapid awareness distinguishes space from time, and unfolds the ideal dimension of the latter into three real dimensions of space. A being that, unlike us, lived in a space of more than three dimensions would in any case have to stand below us; indeed I believe that such a being could no longer have any awareness at all, or, in other words, I believe that a world in more than three dimensions is a logical impossibility. The proof of this is as follows: In order to arrive at a fourth dimension of space, the ideal dimension of time would itself have to become a real dimension of space, that is, in the world of four dimensions

[p. 336]

there would be no time, and hence nothing of the nature of awareness either. — For this reason I hold it a thoroughly unfortunate idea to transpose beings more highly developed than we human beings are into worlds of four and more dimensions. It is not the number of dimensions in which our sensibility is caught that furnishes the standard for the level of our intelligence, but solely the speed of our awareness that is the properly Adamic mark of our mental capacity. Beings more highly developed than we are would have to have a more rapid awareness than we, but time and space would be there for them just as for us, and their space would accordingly have the same number of dimensions as ours. Incidentally, I confess that I speak of the phantasmagoria of “higher beings” only with inward repugnance. We ourselves are those higher beings, who seek to supplement their slow awareness with a theoretically quicker one; for all human science seems to me to amount to nothing else than to accelerating our awareness at least in theory, in order then, of course, to place the higher cognition thus won in the practical service of the welfare of mankind. —

I come now to the concluding question of a universal law of the whole world of phenomena. If one persists in the standpoint of the dualistic doctrine of space and time, according to which space and time are two independent orders which have nothing to do with each other, then of a universal law of nature there can of course be no question at all; indeed it is then inconceivable how in the world of phenomena there can be any connection and any law at all. But if one starts from a doctrine of the unity of space and time, then the dominion of a general law of the world of phenomena — which, to be sure, is for the present unknown — is thereby already established. The cognition of this law, however, has been fully prepared by

[p. 25]

the dynamic theory of space, for the fundamental thought of this theory is that we can form two aspects of the world of phenomena, in both of which aspects one and the same reality presents itself. The unity of these two aspects of the world constitutes the content of the universal law of nature.

Whereas the dualistic theory of space knows only two syntheses, namely the gathering of all points of time into the time-line and of all points of space into cosmic space: the dynamic theory of space adds to these two syntheses, in the first place, two fundamental syntheses more, in that it gathers together: 1. all points of time into one point of space, 2. all points of space into one point of time, whereby a bond between space and time is first established. It is only these two new syntheses that are able to furnish two different aspects of one and the same real world, and only the presence of such aspects makes it possible to formulate a general law for the world of phenomena. But if we stop at the two syntheses of the dualistic theory of space, then it is impossible to gain two aspects of the world of phenomena, for in mere time phenomena are just as impossible as in mere space. If, however, one adds the two new syntheses just named to the two syntheses of the dualistic conception of space, one gains, so to speak, two views of the world, for the phenomena present a different view when one learns to gather together all points of time in one point of space, and conversely all points of space in one point of time.

In order to come at once to the aid of the reader’s intuitive fantasy as well, it suffices to point to a very simple geometrical example. If, for example, the geometer examines the shape of a cylindrical body, he can lay cross-sections through it on the one hand, and longitudinal sections on the other, and use the sectional figures so obtained for the

Pa l á g y i , Logic at the Crossroads.            22

[p. 338]

characterization of the geometrical nature of this body. The body is considered in two kinds of aspect, and only thereby does one come to know it. The representation of a building in ground-plan and elevation is so familiar to everyone that I may perhaps make myself best understood by saying that in the investigation of the general law of the world it is a matter of a ground-plan and an elevation of the whole edifice of the world. Such metaphors are apt to set the power of intuition in motion, but I must at once warn against any extravagant application of them. The perfected unified conception of the world is forever denied to the human being, and indeed to any “superhuman” being whatever; for it exists only for an infinitely rapid, that is, divine awareness. It is given to us only to strive after that unified conception, and indeed always only through the artificial coupling together of dualistic opposites. Once we have made it clear to ourselves that we are really able to grasp the world of phenomena from two different perspectives, a twofold result is thereby achieved: 1. that there really is a general law of the world of phenomena, for the same reality supplies us with two kinds of view, which therefore necessarily hang together, 2. the path too is marked out which we must follow in order to investigate the general law of the world, for it is merely a question of consistently developing the double glance for the two perspectives of the world of phenomena.

We gain the one perspective, as has been said, by thinking of the time-line in dynamic space as gathered together in a point of space. Of course we must not stop at this merely geometrical conception, that is, we must replace the mathematical point of space by the physical one. Thereby we obtain the picture of a material molecule, which moves onward in the endless course of time. The complementary picture arises

[p. 25]

when we think of the points of space as gathered together in one point of time. If we pass from the mathematical standpoint to the physical, we must replace the mathematical point of time by a very small interval of time. What then presents itself to the theoretical glance is nothing other than the space-filling ocean of ether, on which waves are propagated. Viewed from the perspective of space, the picture of a moving molecule presents itself at any empirical point of space; from the perspective of time, on the other hand, we find the ocean of ether with the waves ceaselessly trembling in it. The twofold order of space and time compels us, namely, to lay at the base of the whole world of phenomena two substrata: a material and an ethereal one; just as the physicists have in fact done, out of an inspiration of genius, without however giving themselves an account of the logical necessity of their construction. Since we think of the whole world of phenomena in time as flowing (renewing itself), it is an unavoidable consequence of this conception that we must think of substance itself as resolved into two substrata, of which the one, the material substance, is that which renews itself, while the other, the ethereal substance, represents the milieu in which the renewal takes place. From the perspective of space we find the material substance, from that of time, on the other hand, the ethereal substance as the foundation of all transformations of the world of phenomena. Curiously, it is precisely the dualistic conception of space and time that is unable to penetrate to any concept of the double substance, for since it separates time from space, there can be for it no substance in time, and precisely in consequence of its dualistic character it must content itself with a “dead” spatial substance, which rests in eternal inertia in space. Only monistic thinking is able to grasp the duality in the whole world of phenomena too,

22*

[p. 340]

for only he who presses for unity knows that he has everywhere to do with polar opposites. But whoever believes that the assumption of two substances, matter and ether, contradicts the spirit of monism, simply does not understand monism, for he will also hold that in the sense of the doctrine of unity a sentence must not consist of subject and predicate. Monism consists not in the abolition of opposites, but in their union. It would be extravagance to lay the one divine substance directly at the base of all phenomena, for only if we were in possession of an infinitely rapid awareness could and might we do this. For a finitely rapid awareness the one eternal substance necessarily falls apart into two substances: into the material and the ethereal, of which we may call the former — if it so pleases — also the substance of space, the second, on the other hand, the substance of time.

The law, then, which rules over the whole world of phenomena may be formulated as follows: No changes can take place in the perspective of space of the phenomena without changes in the perspective of time also having to take place; or conversely: the changes in the perspective of time are necessarily also accompanied by changes in the perspective of space. Both kinds of change are equivalent to one another, because they offer only two aspects of one reality.

Against the formulation of this law the objection will perhaps be raised that one cannot see clearly what is meant by the two aspects of the world of phenomena. I will therefore point out what is to be understood by the two modes of view in immediate intuition. In the one mode of view one sees bodies which change their spatial position more or less, in the other mode of view, on the other hand, one sees the qualities or states of bodies in continual

[p. 25]

change, more or less noticeable. The general law that we are seeking, applied to these intuitive relations, therefore runs as follows: no changes can occur in the spatial arrangement (in the state of motion) of bodies without changes also taking place in the qualities of bodies. Changes of spatial position and changes of quality are only two aspects of actual occurrence, and therefore equivalent to one another.

For the sake of further clarification I should like to remark, moreover, that one finds the qualities of bodies from the perspective of time. For what do we understand by the perspective of time? Nothing other than the view which the world of phenomena would afford if one could suddenly arrest the flow of time in order to hold fast what takes place in the course of an extremely short interval of time. Within such a view there would be no motion of bodies, for one can make the interval of time so short that during it every body would seem to stand still. What is meant by the perspective of time can therefore best be symbolized by an instantaneous photograph. Within such an instantaneous photograph of phenomena there is no motion of bodies, but merely qualities, which are distributed in space. On the other hand, it need not be explained that from the perspective of space one finds precisely what one did not find within the perspective of time, namely the motion of bodies. The general law, therefore, which we want to set up, brings the changes of place of bodies and their changes of quality into a necessary connection.

To be sure, this general law can be demonstrated in a manner satisfying to the mathematician and physicist only through the exact elaboration of the concept of dynamic space. This much, I believe, the

[p. 342]

reader will nevertheless feel: that what is at issue here is nothing other than J u l i u s R o b e r t M a y e r ’s law of energy. When I say that the world of phenomena can offer two kinds of view, the same is meant by this that is otherwise understood by the transformation of one kind of energy into another. What is called the conservation of energy, however, is nothing other than that it is one and the same reality which is grasped in different modes of view. I believe, therefore, that the concept of dynamic space is suited not only to establish the modern doctrine of energy logically, but also to lead it toward a further development.
